## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/database-systems-en

Most database courses teach you what SQL does. This book teaches you why the database does it that way — and why any other way would be worse. The difference matters the moment you face a slow query you can't explain, a transaction that silently loses data under concurrent load, or a schema that worked fine at 10,000 rows and fails at 10 million.

Three storylines weave through all 23 chapters: a university registration system that keeps breaking in instructive ways; the four-table JOIN above, which sheds 99% of its runtime chapter by chapter as you add indexes, update statistics, and understand the optimizer; and a bank account with $1,000 that gets withdrawn twice because two concurrent transactions read the same balance simultaneously. None of these are contrived — by the end, they are the same problems your production system will have.

The book is unusually honest about failure modes. Every "Common Misconception" section documents a real mistake engineers make — not strawmen. The chapters on transactions and MVCC are particularly unsparing: writing BEGIN / COMMIT is not enough; the wrong isolation level lets anomalies through even in a well-structured transaction. The chapter on normalization explains when to deliberately not normalize. The final chapter walks through a complete data architecture for a realistic AI-learning platform — schema, indexes, isolation levels, caching, replication, analytics pipeline, GDPR deletion — and asks you to defend every choice, tradeoff by tradeoff.

By the last chapter, the 20-minute query that opened the book is no longer a mystery. You know exactly why it was slow, exactly what the optimizer was doing wrong, and exactly which facts about storage, indexes, statistics, and join algorithms produced the speedup. That understanding is portable to any relational engine — and to any new engine you encounter afterward.

**Target audience**：CS undergraduates (juniors/seniors) taking a database systems course; software engineers who have used ORMs and SQL but never understood what is underneath; backend engineers who want to reason about database performance with confidence.

**Prerequisites**：Basic programming ability. Familiarity with data structures (arrays, trees, hash tables). SQL knowledge is helpful but not required — taught from scratch in Chapter 4.

**Study hours**：90–110 hours (roughly 4 hours per chapter, plus exercise work). Readers who engage deeply with exercises and Socratic dialogue should expect 110+ hours.

## Introduction

Most introductions to database systems teach you how to use a database. This one teaches you how to reason about one.

The difference matters. If you have ever written an ORM query that worked beautifully on 10,000 rows and fell over on 10,000,000; if you have ever read about "eventual consistency" and nodded, but could not confidently say what it actually gives up; if you have ever heard "just add an index" and wondered which one, on which columns, in what order — this book is for you. It is a book about the _why_. Every chapter is organized around the tradeoffs a database designer has to make, and why those tradeoffs exist at all.

**The central conviction of the book:** every database feature is a tradeoff. Normalization trades query cost for update simplicity. Indexes trade write throughput for read speed. Strict isolation trades concurrency for correctness. MVCC trades memory for lock-free reads. Replication trades consistency for availability. Columnar storage trades point-lookup latency for scan bandwidth. LSM trees trade read amplification for sequential writes. No engine is strictly better than another — each occupies a point on some tradeoff curve, and the job of a database engineer is to know which point fits which workload.

**What you will find in this book:**

The twenty-three chapters progress through a deliberate arc.

The opening chapters (1-2) establish the problem: what happens to information when we try to manage it with ad-hoc files and application logic? You will see, concretely, how a small university registration system fails at 100 students and breaks at 10,000.

The relational core (Chapters 3-8) builds the foundation: relations, SQL (both fundamental and advanced), ER modeling, normalization, and the relational algebra that underlies every query planner.

The engine chapters (9-12) take you inside: storage layouts, buffer pools, B+ tree indexes, query processing algorithms (nested-loop, sort-merge, hash joins), and the cost-based query optimizer that chooses between them.

The concurrency chapters (13-17) are where most engineers' mental models are weakest and where real systems fail most subtly. We build up transactions, ACID, isolation levels, two-phase locking, multi-version concurrency control, and crash recovery through ARIES — always starting from a concrete failure and showing what the mechanism prevents.

The scale chapters (18-23) extend to distributed systems (CAP and replication), columnar stores for analytics (OLAP), document and key-value stores, wide-column stores and LSM trees, polyglot persistence, and a capstone design review that uses every concept the book has built.

**Three storylines run throughout:**

* **Thread A — The University Registration System.** A small database of students, courses, and enrollments that grows in complexity across the book. The same domain is re-examined through each new lens: CSV files in Chapter 2, normalized tables in Chapter 6, indexed for performance in Chapter 10, transacted in Chapter 13, recovered after crash in Chapter 17, scaled to millions of learners in Chapter 23.
* **Thread B — The Join That Didn't Come Back.** A single 20-minute query that we return to again and again — through relational algebra, indexing, join algorithms, cost-based optimization, and columnar storage — watching it shrink from 20 minutes to 2 seconds to 100 milliseconds as we apply each new idea.
* **Thread C — Two Users, One Account.** A bank account and two concurrent withdrawals. This tiny scenario threads through every concurrency control chapter, revealing lost updates, dirty reads, write skew, lock conflicts, snapshot isolation, and partition-tolerance tradeoffs — all on the same 1000 USD balance.

**How to use this book:**

Each chapter contains:

* **Definitions** boxes that give the precise technical statement of each new concept.
* **Common Misconception** boxes that call out the places where intuition betrays engineers most often.
* **SQL examples throughout** — not confined to the SQL chapters. Indexing shows `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`. Transactions show `BEGIN/COMMIT`. MVCC shows version chains. Storage chapters show how a `SELECT` becomes page reads.
* **Tradeoffs Summary** at the end of every chapter — the point of the book condensed to 4–7 bullets.
* **Exercises in three tiers** : _Concept Check_ questions that verify you absorbed the definitions; _Application Problems_ that ask you to apply concepts to realistic situations; _Think Deeper_ problems that reach across chapters and do not have a single clean answer.
* **Hint blocks** on the harder exercises — Socratic nudges, not answers.

**A note on exercise answers.** Chapters 1–7 include inline worked answers for every exercise, because the foundational material (relations, SQL, ER, normalization) rewards immediate verification. From Chapter 8 onward, exercises are posed without inline answers — the material has more defensible approaches, and working through problems Socratically (with the Socratopia AI companion, a study partner, or a workshop group) is where the learning actually happens. The AI companion has access to reference solutions and can guide you toward them step by step rather than hand them over. Resist the urge to look up "the answer"; the path _is_ the point.

Mathematical notation uses `\(…\)` for inline math and `\[…\]` for display math, compatible with standard LaTeX renderers.

**A note on real systems:**

The book refers constantly to real database engines — PostgreSQL (MVCC, extensibility), MySQL/InnoDB (clustered B+ trees, next-key locking), SQLite (embedded simplicity), Redis (in-memory data structures), Cassandra (wide-column + LSM), DuckDB (embedded columnar), MongoDB (documents), ClickHouse and Snowflake (columnar OLAP), Spanner and CockroachDB (distributed SQL). The goal is not vendor expertise but _system literacy_ : when you finish this book, you should be able to open the documentation for any production database, recognize the design decisions, and predict the tradeoffs they imply.

**Prerequisites:**

You need basic programming ability and familiarity with data structures at the level of a typical undergraduate data structures course (arrays, trees, hash tables, Big-O). SQL knowledge helps but is not required — Chapter 4 teaches it from scratch. No prior database experience is assumed; no operating systems course is assumed.

**Why this book exists:**

Database systems are not a mystery. Every mechanism in a modern DBMS was designed by engineers solving a specific problem, and the design decisions are almost always the right ones for the tradeoffs they targeted. But the reasoning is rarely explained in the places most engineers first encounter databases (tutorials, ORMs, "pick this database" blog posts). By the end of this book, you should see the whole landscape — row stores and column stores, B+ trees and LSM trees, 2PL and MVCC, synchronous and asynchronous replication — as a single coherent map of tradeoffs, not a bag of unrelated tools.

## Chapter 1: Why Databases?

Open a text editor. Type out a list of fifty books you have read: title, author, year, your rating. Save it as `books.csv`. Congratulations — you have just built a database.

Or have you?

Now imagine three things happen this afternoon. First, your friend asks to look at the list _while you are editing it_. Second, your laptop's battery dies mid-save. Third, you realize three of the authors appear under different spellings ("J.R.R. Tolkien", "JRR Tolkien", "Tolkien, J.R.R.") and you want a count of unique authors. How well does `books.csv` hold up?

This chapter is a tour of what breaks, why it breaks, and what the rest of this book will teach you to build instead. We will not write any SQL yet — we will diagnose the patient before we prescribe.

### 1.1 The Problem a Database Actually Solves

A database is not a place to _store_ data. Plenty of things can store data: disks, files, memory, envelopes. A database is a system that lets you **query** , **modify** , and **protect** data _reliably_ even when:

  1. The data is too large to fit in memory.
  2. Multiple users or programs are accessing it at the same time.
  3. The machine or program might crash at any moment.
  4. The data has internal structure that must not be violated (every `enrollment` must refer to a real `student` and a real `course`).

If you are not in any of those four situations, you do not need a database. A config file, a CSV, or a Python pickle is fine. Databases exist because all four situations are the norm, not the exception, in real software.

Let's make this concrete with an example we will return to in every single chapter of this book.

#### Our Running Example: The University Registration System

A small university wants to track students, courses, and enrollments. The data looks innocent:

Students | Courses | Enrollments  
---|---|---  
id, name, email | id, title, credits | student_id, course_id, grade  
  
The university has 10,000 students, 500 courses, and each student takes roughly 8 courses per year. That's 80,000 rows of enrollment data. Every semester the data changes: new students enroll, grades are assigned, courses are added. Multiple staff work on this data simultaneously. If the machine crashes during registration, the university cannot afford to lose enrollment records.

This is our "Thread A" scenario. We will return to it throughout the book — first as CSV files that break, then as clean relational tables with SQL, then as a data model, and eventually as a live system with indexes, concurrent users, and crash recovery. By the end of the book, the same innocuous three-table schema will have taught you most of modern data management.

> ### Definition: Database

A **database** is an organized, persistent collection of related data, together with the software — called the **Database Management System (DBMS)** — that lets applications query and modify that data correctly in the presence of concurrency, crashes, and structural constraints.

In plain language, this means: a database is data _plus_ the software that guarantees the data stays sensible even under pressure. When people say "use a database", they rarely mean the raw data. They mean the DBMS — the layer of correctness-preserving machinery sitting between applications and bytes on disk.

### 1.2 The Storage Hierarchy (and Why It Dictates Everything)

We cannot talk sensibly about why databases are designed the way they are without understanding _where data lives_. Every single design decision we will see in this book — why B-trees are shaped that way, why query plans count I/Os instead of CPU cycles, why recovery algorithms are written the way they are — descends from a physical reality: **storage is a hierarchy** , and each level is dramatically slower and larger than the one above.

Here are the approximate characteristics of the modern storage hierarchy on a typical server in 2026. The exact numbers change every year; the _ratios_ don't.

Level | Typical size | Access time | Relative cost | Volatile?  
---|---|---|---|---  
CPU registers | < 1 KB | ~0.3 ns | ∞ / byte | Yes  
L1 cache | ~64 KB | ~1 ns | Very high | Yes  
L2 cache | ~1 MB | ~3 ns | High | Yes  
L3 cache | ~32 MB | ~10 ns | High | Yes  
DRAM (RAM) | ~64–512 GB | ~100 ns | Moderate | Yes  
SSD (NVMe) | ~1–4 TB | ~10–100 µs | Low | No  
HDD (spinning) | ~4–20 TB | ~5–15 ms | Very low | No  
Tape / cold | Unlimited | seconds+ | Lowest | No  
  
Focus on two ratios. Register-to-DRAM is about 300×. DRAM-to-SSD is about 1000×. DRAM-to-HDD is about 100,000×. A CPU can execute on the order of **30 million** register operations in the time an HDD returns a single page (§1.6 Q2 works out the arithmetic: 10 ms ÷ 0.3 ns ≈ 3.3 × 10⁷). On SSD the ratio is smaller but still six orders of magnitude — roughly 100,000–200,000 CPU ops per page read.

This tells us three things that shape every chapter ahead:

  1. **Databases are bigger than memory.** Most useful databases do not fit in DRAM. We must assume data lives on SSD or HDD and be brought into memory in chunks. Those chunks are called **pages** — a concept we will use from Chapter 9 onward.

  2. **I/O is the cost that matters.** When we analyze query costs, we count I/Os, not CPU instructions. The ratios above are why. A join algorithm that takes 100 million CPU operations but 1 page read wins easily against one that takes 1 million CPU operations but 100 page reads.

  3. **Volatility is a life-or-death distinction.** Everything above SSD in the table is **volatile** — the data vanishes on power loss. Anything the database promises to "remember" (the D in ACID) must reach non-volatile storage before the promise is kept. This is why crash recovery is one of the four core DBMS responsibilities.

#### Common Misconception

New engineers often think "SSDs are fast, so disk-vs-memory doesn't matter much anymore." This is wrong in two ways. First, the DRAM-to-SSD gap is still ~1000×, which is enormous — slow enough that a query that touches SSD 1000 extra times will feel visibly slow to a human user. Second, SSDs have _different_ performance characteristics (random reads are fast, random writes wear out cells, sequential writes are much faster than random writes), which is why write-optimized engines like LSM trees optimize specifically for sequential writes. Getting "disk doesn't matter anymore" wrong leads directly to queries that run 100× slower than necessary.

### 1.3 What a File System Cannot Do (A Preview of the Pain)

Let's return to our CSV files. We said "congratulations, you built a database" and then hinted that three things would go wrong this afternoon. Let's enumerate them precisely — not to solve them yet (that is Chapter 2's job), but to name the four problems a DBMS must solve.

**Problem 1: Query language.** Suppose you want "the average rating of all books I read in 2022 with more than 300 pages." In a CSV, you write a Python script, or you read the file line by line in your head. The expressivity of the question is bounded by your patience. A database lets you write `SELECT AVG(rating) FROM books WHERE year = 2022 AND pages > 300`, and the DBMS figures out how to execute it efficiently.

**Problem 2: Concurrent access.** Two processes open `books.csv` at the same time. Process A is appending a new book. Process B is editing an old entry. They both write back — and one of them silently clobbers the other's changes. In a file system, the only way to prevent this is to lock the entire file, which stops anyone else from reading it. A database lets a thousand concurrent users read and modify different rows without stepping on each other's work.

**Problem 3: Crash recovery.** Your laptop dies while saving the file. With 50 books, maybe you lose a few. With 80,000 enrollment records mid-semester, you have a crisis. A database guarantees that either a transaction's changes are fully present after the crash or fully absent — never half-applied.

**Problem 4: Data integrity.** The `enrollments.csv` says student 4218 is in course 907. But there is no student 4218 — it was a typo. There is no course 907 — it was deleted last year. A file system cannot enforce referential integrity because it does not know what a reference is. A DBMS does.

> ### Definition: The Four Promises of a DBMS

A DBMS exists to make four promises that a file system cannot:

  1. **Query language** — a declarative way to ask questions about structured data, with the engine figuring out _how_ to answer efficiently.
  2. **Concurrency control** — correct results even when many users read and write at once.
  3. **Crash recovery** — committed data survives; uncommitted data disappears cleanly.
  4. **Integrity enforcement** — structural rules (keys, references, types) are machine-enforced, not human-hoped-for.

In plain language: a database is the software that keeps data sane under the four most common things that corrupt data — bad queries, concurrent users, crashes, and typos.

This framing is the skeleton of the entire book. Every chapter addresses some part of some promise:

* Query language: Chapters 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 11, 12.
* Concurrency: Chapters 13, 14, 15, 16.
* Recovery: Chapters 9, 17.
* Integrity: Chapters 3, 5, 6.

And we revisit all four under distributed systems (Chapter 18), column stores (Chapter 19), and NoSQL (Chapters 20, 21, 22).

### 1.4 Anatomy of a DBMS: From SQL to Bits and Back

Let's open up the black box. Here is what happens, at a conceptual level, when you type a SQL query and press enter in `psql` or a similar client.

  1. **Parser.** The text of your SQL is turned into an abstract syntax tree (AST). Syntax errors get caught here.
  2. **Rewriter.** The AST is transformed — views are expanded, subqueries are sometimes unnested, some obviously equivalent rewrites are applied.
  3. **Optimizer.** The rewritten query is converted into a **query plan**. This is the "how" — which operators (scan, join, sort, aggregate) to run and in what order. The optimizer chooses the plan with the lowest estimated cost (Chapter 12).
  4. **Executor.** The plan is run. It calls into the lower levels of the engine to fetch data.
  5. **Buffer pool.** The executor needs data from disk? The buffer pool is consulted first — if the page is already in memory, return it directly; otherwise, issue an I/O to load it. When memory runs out, the buffer pool evicts pages using a replacement policy (Chapter 9).
  6. **Storage manager.** Pages are read from and written to disk (or SSD). Writes go through the **log** first (the WAL) so that recovery is possible (Chapter 17).
  7. **Transaction manager.** Running underneath all of this, the transaction manager decides when to grant locks (Chapter 15) or issue snapshot visibility decisions (Chapter 16), and when a transaction is allowed to commit.

That is most of a DBMS. Not all — we are skipping authentication, the network layer, the client protocol, the system catalogs, vacuuming, replication. But if you understand those seven layers, you understand 80% of what makes PostgreSQL PostgreSQL.

#### Real Systems at a Glance

Four systems will appear throughout this book. Meet them briefly:

* **PostgreSQL** — a full-featured relational DBMS with MVCC (Chapter 16), extensibility, and strong SQL compliance. Our primary example for the relational world.
* **MySQL (InnoDB engine)** — a full-featured relational DBMS with clustered B-tree indexes keyed by the primary key (Chapter 10) and a different isolation-level implementation (Chapter 14) from PostgreSQL. Our primary example for "same goal, different design choices".
* **SQLite** — an embedded relational DBMS that lives inside your application process. Uses a B-tree for _everything_ (including the table data, not just indexes). Our example for "minimalism is a valid design".
* **Redis** — a key-value store used as a cache or, less often, as a primary datastore. Single-threaded, in-memory, blazingly fast. Our bridge to the NoSQL world (Chapter 20).

Later we meet Cassandra (Chapter 21), MongoDB (Chapter 20), and DuckDB (Chapter 19). Each embodies a different answer to the question "what do you want to trade away in exchange for what?" — and each answer is a chapter of this book.

### 1.5 The Central Theme: Every Concept Is a Tradeoff

Before we go any further, one sentence to hang on the wall:

> **Every major database concept is a tradeoff. The tradeoff IS the lesson.**

Throughout the book, when we introduce a mechanism, we will not just ask "what does it do?" We will ask:

* What does it gain?
* What does it cost?
* Under what workload is the trade worth it?
* Under what workload should you reject it?

For previews:

* **Normalization** (Ch 6) trades query simplicity for update safety. A fully normalized schema has no update anomalies but requires more joins to answer common queries.
* **Indexes** (Ch 10) trade write speed for read speed. Every index you add must be updated on every `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, and `DELETE`.
* **Serializable isolation** (Ch 14) trades concurrency for correctness. A SERIALIZABLE system runs as if every transaction ran one at a time, but in exchange loses the performance benefit of overlapping them.
* **Replication** (Ch 18) trades consistency for availability, sometimes. You can always read _something_ even if part of the cluster is down — but what you read might be stale.
* **Column stores** (Ch 19) trade point-lookup speed for analytical query speed. Reading one row costs more; summing a column costs less.

A reader who finishes this book remembers not just the definitions but the _tradeoffs_. The definitions decay; the tradeoffs let you think for yourself when a real system surprises you.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **The storage hierarchy trades capacity for speed.** Registers are fast and tiny; HDDs are huge and glacial. Every layer above SSD is volatile, which is why durability forces writes down to slow media — you cannot have fast and durable for free.
* **A DBMS trades raw file-system flexibility for four guarantees.** You give up "just open the file and write bytes" in exchange for a query language, concurrency control, crash recovery, and integrity enforcement. For throwaway data, files win; for anything worth keeping, the four promises justify the overhead.
* **Counting I/Os instead of CPU cycles trades modeling precision for honesty.** The DRAM-to-disk gap is ~1000×, so a plan that minimizes CPU but adds a single extra page read usually loses. Cost models ignore CPU noise not because CPU is free but because I/O dominates.
* **The buffer pool trades OS-page-cache simplicity for database-aware control.** The DBMS manages its own cache with policies tuned for query workloads (Chapter 9), rather than delegating to the kernel. The cost is duplicated caching logic; the gain is predictable eviction and write ordering needed for recovery.
* **The layered DBMS architecture (parser → optimizer → executor → buffer pool → storage) trades monolithic speed for replaceable components.** Each layer can be reasoned about and tuned independently, at the cost of cross-layer coordination when something needs to change end-to-end.
* **"Every concept is a tradeoff" trades the comfort of one right answer for the realism of workload-dependent design.** PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQLite, and Redis make different choices because different workloads reward different choices — not because one team got it wrong.

### 1.6 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** In your own words, what is the difference between a _database_ and a _DBMS_? Which one does the term "database" usually refer to in industry conversation?

Hint

Go back to the definition in Section 1.1. Is the data itself software?

**Answer:** A **database** is the organized collection of data. A **DBMS** (Database Management System) is the software that manages that data — parsing queries, enforcing constraints, handling concurrency, ensuring crash recovery, and so on. Strictly speaking, when someone says "we use PostgreSQL as our database", they mean "we use the PostgreSQL DBMS to manage our data." In casual industry speech, "database" is routinely used to mean the DBMS. That ambiguity is harmless in most conversations, but sharpen it to "the DBMS" whenever the distinction matters (e.g., when a colleague says "the database is slow" — they almost always mean the DBMS software and its configuration, not the bits on disk).

**Q2.** The storage hierarchy lists CPU registers at ~0.3 ns and HDD at ~10 ms. Roughly how many register operations can a CPU perform in the time it takes to read one page from an HDD? Show your arithmetic.

Hint

Convert both times to the same unit and divide.

**Answer:** Convert to seconds: 0.3 ns = 3 × 10⁻¹⁰ s; 10 ms = 10⁻² s. The ratio is 10−23×10−10≈3.3×107$$\frac{10^{-2}}{3 \times 10^{-10}} \approx 3.3 \times 10^{7}3$$×10−1010−2≈3.3×107 — about 33 million register operations per HDD page read. Even on SSD (say 50 µs = 5 × 10⁻⁵ s), the ratio is still ~167,000 register operations per page read. This is why database cost models count I/Os, not CPU cycles.

**Q3.** Name the four promises a DBMS makes that a file system cannot. For each, give a one-sentence example of what goes wrong without it.

**Answer:**

  1. **Query language.** Without it, every ad-hoc question about your data requires new imperative code; a SQL-like system lets you ask declaratively and lets the engine figure out how.
  2. **Concurrency control.** Without it, two users editing the same data at once silently overwrite each other's changes — the classic "lost update".
  3. **Crash recovery.** Without it, a power failure mid-transaction leaves the database in an inconsistent state (half-written rows, orphaned references), with no automatic way to roll back.
  4. **Integrity enforcement.** Without it, typos, deleted parents, and missing foreign keys quietly corrupt the data until someone notices a nonsense report months later.

**Q4.** Which of the following is NOT a reason modern databases use their own buffer pool instead of relying on the OS page cache?

* (a) The database has more knowledge about access patterns than the OS.
* (b) The database's replacement policy can be tailored to its workload (e.g., LRU-K or 2Q for OLTP buffer pools, which outperform the OS page cache's simple LRU on index-heavy workloads).
* (c) The OS page cache cannot store any data at all.
* (d) Writes must be coordinated with the WAL, which is easier if the DBMS owns the buffer.

**Answer:** (c) is the false statement. The OS absolutely does have a page cache. The real reasons databases manage their own are (a), (b), and (d): the DBMS knows which pages are likely to be reused (e.g., index pages are hotter than data pages), it can pick replacement policies matched to its workload (LRU-K, 2Q, or Clock-Pro variants that outperform the OS's simple LRU on skewed workloads), and it needs tight coordination with the WAL for durability (Chapter 17). The OS page cache knows none of this.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Consider a single-file, multi-user note-taking app. Two users of the same household share one file, `notes.txt`. Describe four distinct things that go wrong — one per DBMS promise from Section 1.3. Be specific: describe a realistic scenario, not just a category name.

Hint

For each of the four promises, imagine the _simplest_ possible scenario that violates it.

**Answer:**

  1. **Query language.** User A wants "all notes I wrote in March that mention 'doctor'". The app has no query layer, so either User A scrolls through the whole file by hand or the app reloads the entire file into memory and scans with a regex each time. As the file grows, this becomes painful and has no way to use an index.
  2. **Concurrency control.** User A is editing note #37 while User B is appending note #52. Both save at the same time; the editor writes the whole file back. Whichever user saved second overwrites the other user's change entirely. (This is the same "lost update" problem we will see in Chapter 13 with bank accounts.)
  3. **Crash recovery.** User A is halfway through a big paste — maybe 200 notes imported from a different app — when the laptop dies. The file may be truncated, or contain a half-written final note. There is no mechanism to say "undo everything this import did" because there was never a transaction boundary. On reopen, the app has no way to know the import was incomplete.
  4. **Integrity enforcement.** The app stores "linked notes" as a field `linked_to: <id>`. User B deletes note #12, but three other notes still say `linked_to: 12`. Nothing in the file system notices or complains. Next time the app tries to follow the link, it crashes, silently drops the link, or shows a ghost note — depending on how carefully the app code handles the error.

**Q2.** The university registration system in Section 1.1 has 10,000 students, 500 courses, and 80,000 enrollments. Each row is roughly 64 bytes. Pages are 8 KB.

(a) How many pages does each table occupy if it is stored as a heap file (rows packed into pages with no wasted space)? (b) If a query asks "find the title of the course student #4218 is enrolled in for course_id 101", and we have no indexes, how many pages at worst must be scanned? (c) If the buffer pool has room for 100 pages, does it make a difference to the worst case? Explain.

Hint

Rows per page = page size ÷ row size. Scanning a heap file means reading every page until the end, because you don't know where your target row is.

**Answer:** (a) Rows per page: 819264=128$$\frac{8192}{64} = 128648192=128 rows per page. So:$$

Table | Rows | Pages  
---|---|---  
Students | 10,000 | ~79  
Courses | 500 | ~4  
Enrollments | 80,000 | ~625  
  
(Round up because partial pages still count as a page.)

(b) Worst case, we must find the specific enrollment row for student 4218 in course 101 _and_ then look up the course title for course 101.

* Scan Enrollments: up to 625 pages.
* Scan Courses to find course 101: up to 4 pages.
* Total: up to 629 pages.

(c) No, it does not change the _worst case_. The worst case is the first time the query runs and nothing is cached — we still read up to 629 pages from disk. Where the buffer pool helps is _subsequent_ queries: if Courses is small enough to stay fully in the buffer pool (4 pages is trivial), the course-title lookup becomes free on the second and later queries. This is the first hint of why buffer-pool management (Chapter 9) and indexing (Chapter 10) are both essential — one helps repeated queries, the other helps the first query.

**Q3.** A junior engineer on your team says: "Let's just use JSON files instead of a database. JSON has a schema, it's easy to read, and we don't need to install anything." Which of the four DBMS promises does this plan concede, and which (if any) does it keep? Under what narrow circumstances could JSON files genuinely be enough?

Hint

Treat each promise independently. Which ones does JSON give you for free? Which ones must you build yourself, poorly?

**Answer:**

* **Query language:** conceded. You will write Python or jq one-off scripts for every question.
* **Concurrency control:** conceded. File locking is all you have, and it's all-or-nothing.
* **Crash recovery:** conceded. A crash during save yields a truncated or half-written JSON file, which may not even parse.
* **Integrity enforcement:** partially kept. JSON has structural schemas (required fields, types, via JSON Schema), but it has no notion of referential integrity between files.

**When JSON is enough:** single-user, mostly-read, small data, no concurrency, no crash-safety requirements. Think: a configuration file, a static dataset shipped with an app, a personal list of 200 bookmarks. The moment your workload gains _any_ of (multiple writers, needing to survive power loss, sub-second query latency over large data, enforced references), JSON stops being enough.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The storage hierarchy table lists "tape" at seconds of access time. Tape is still in wide use in 2026. What specific workloads justify tape over SSD? What does this tell you about how to think about the claim "storage X is obsolete"?

**Discussion:** Tape is used for archival and compliance workloads: cold backups, regulatory retention (many jurisdictions require 7–10 years of financial records), and very-large-scale scientific archives (particle physics data, long-term astronomy). The dollar-per-terabyte cost of tape is still an order of magnitude below SSD, and tape _unplugged_ consumes zero power while still retaining data — a property SSDs do not have. So the workload signature where tape wins is: huge total volume, very infrequent reads (maybe never), long retention, cost-sensitive, no latency requirement.

The deeper lesson: "obsolete" in storage usually means "no longer the right default for the most common workload." It rarely means "no longer useful for any workload." Throughout this book, you will meet older mechanisms (B-trees from 1970, 2PL from 1976, WAL from the 1980s) that are still the right answer for their workload. Always ask "obsolete for whom?"

**Q2.** A startup builds its MVP on a single PostgreSQL instance. Six months later, usage grows and the founders read a blog post arguing they should "immediately migrate to a distributed NoSQL system like Cassandra for scale." What would you advise them to check before they migrate?

**Discussion:** Several things.

First: _is the database actually the bottleneck?_ Many "database is slow" problems are really "we have a missing index" or "we have the wrong isolation level" or "our ORM issues N+1 queries" — all fixable without changing the DBMS family. Chapter 12 on query optimization exists precisely because a huge fraction of apparent "scaling problems" are fixable at the query-plan level.

Second: _what properties does the workload need?_ Cassandra gives you horizontal scalability and tunable consistency, but it costs you ad-hoc queries, joins, and strong transactional guarantees (Chapter 21). If the startup needs transactions across multiple entities (a typical ORM-backed CRUD app), Cassandra is the wrong tool — the migration makes scaling slightly easier and everything else enormously harder.

Third: _how far does vertical scaling take us?_ A single modern PostgreSQL server on beefy hardware can handle billions of rows and tens of thousands of transactions per second. Startups that migrate to distributed NoSQL at 10 million rows routinely regret it. Chapter 22's "polyglot persistence" covers this exact dilemma.

The right first move is usually: measure, index, tune. Consider migration only when you have evidence that you are hitting the specific limits (write throughput, data size) of the current system and not some fixable app-level problem. Premature migration is a very expensive form of yak-shaving.

**Q3.** Throughout this chapter we've said "a DBMS makes four promises". But one could argue there's a fifth: _performance_. Why have we not listed performance as a promise? What is its relationship to the other four?

**Discussion:** Performance is not a promise a DBMS _makes_ in the same way. Correctness (the other four) is binary: either the DBMS preserves integrity under concurrency and crashes, or it does not. Performance is a continuum and is deeply workload-dependent. A DBMS does not promise "your queries will be fast"; it promises "your queries will be correct" and offers a toolkit (indexes, query planners, buffer pools, replication) that you, the engineer, combine for your workload to get performance.

Why structure it this way? Because performance is _your_ job as a database user. The DBMS gives you the levers — the right index, the right isolation level, the right storage format. Turning the levers requires understanding the tradeoffs, which is precisely what the rest of this book teaches.

A good test: if a DBMS could honestly say "yes, your query will always be fast", you wouldn't need to know any of this. You'd just use it. The reason Chapters 10, 11, 12, 18, 19 exist is that performance is a negotiation between you and the engine, mediated by your knowledge of both.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter motivated the existence of databases from first principles. A DBMS exists to make **four promises** that a file system cannot: a declarative query language, correct concurrent access, crash recovery, and integrity enforcement. Those four promises are the skeleton of the rest of the book.

We introduced the **storage hierarchy** — registers, cache, DRAM, SSD, HDD, tape — and noted that the ratios between levels (especially DRAM-to-SSD and DRAM-to-HDD) dictate most of the design decisions we will see. Database cost models count I/Os because I/Os dominate wall-clock time by four to six orders of magnitude.

We met the **DBMS layered architecture** : parser, rewriter, optimizer, executor, buffer pool, storage manager, transaction manager. Every chapter from now on zooms into one of these layers. Chapters 3–8 build out the query language (parser to executor at the SQL level). Chapter 9 opens the buffer pool and storage manager. Chapters 10–12 build the optimizer's toolkit. Chapters 13–17 are the transaction manager's story. And Chapters 18–22 generalize to the distributed and non-relational worlds.

We also introduced **Thread A (University Registration System)** , the running data example we will return to in every chapter where it helps. We named the **tradeoff theme** : every concept in this book will be framed as what you gain and what you give up, because the tradeoff is the lesson.

Next chapter: we build the university database as CSV files and watch every one of the four promises get broken within half an hour of real use. By the end of Chapter 2, the anomalies will be so obvious that the relational model of Chapter 3 will feel inevitable.

---

## Chapter 2: The Naive Approach — Files, Anomalies, and the Birth of the Relational Model

At the end of Chapter 1 we promised to break a CSV-based database within half an hour. Let's make good on that.

This chapter is a guided tour of failure. We build the university registration system from Chapter 1 as plain CSV files, we use it for a bit, and we watch it corrupt itself in ways that each have a name: **update anomaly** , **deletion anomaly** , **insertion anomaly**. These three anomalies are the reason the relational model (Chapter 3) exists. By the time we finish this chapter, the next chapter's solution will feel inevitable — not as an invention, but as the only sane response to pain we have already felt.

Threading A starts here. We will carry the university registration system forward through 10+ chapters. The specific CSV schema we wreck in this chapter will be rebuilt (cleaner) in Chapter 3, queried in Chapter 4, re-modeled in Chapter 5, normalized in Chapter 6, stored on pages in Chapter 9, indexed in Chapter 10, and made crash-safe in Chapter 17. The point is not that CSVs are bad — the point is that every correction we apply in later chapters maps directly to a pain we discovered in this one.

### 2.1 The Schema We're About to Regret

The registrar's office at a small university needs to track three things: students, courses, and which student is enrolled in which course (with the grade, once assigned). Someone on the staff — let's call her Registrar — says "let's just keep it as a spreadsheet." She produces three files:

**`students.csv`**
    
    
    id,name,email
    1001,Alice Wong,alice.wong@uni.edu
    1002,Bob Khan,bob.khan@uni.edu
    1003,Carlos Silva,carlos.silva@uni.edu
    

**`courses.csv`**
    
    
    id,title,credits,instructor
    101,Introduction to Databases,4,Dr. Chen
    102,Operating Systems,4,Dr. Park
    103,Linear Algebra,3,Dr. Navarro
    

**`enrollments.csv`**
    
    
    student_id,course_id,grade
    1001,101,A
    1001,102,B+
    1002,101,A-
    1002,103,B
    1003,102,A
    

Notice this is _already_ a small improvement over one giant file. Registrar has, by intuition, separated her data into three tables by topic. If she had put everything in a single CSV — one row per enrollment with student name, email, course title, instructor, credits, grade — her first problem would have been finding anything. Splitting by topic is the first move toward the relational model, and Registrar made it for free.

But this structure is not yet safe. Let's watch.

### 2.2 The Update Anomaly

Three weeks into the semester, Dr. Park publishes a paper and changes his preferred name to "Dr. Soo-min Park". Registrar opens `courses.csv` and is about to change row 102... then she notices something terrifying.

Wait, where does Dr. Park's name appear? Let's grep the files.

In Registrar's actual production files — not our tiny sample above — a year later, the enrollments data has accumulated to 80,000 rows. Sensibly, to save the registrar from needing to open two files every time someone asks "what courses is Alice enrolled in?", an earlier staffer "denormalized" the enrollments file. They added convenience columns:

**`enrollments.csv`** (the real production version)
    
    
    student_id,student_name,course_id,course_title,instructor,credits,grade
    1001,Alice Wong,101,Introduction to Databases,Dr. Chen,4,A
    1001,Alice Wong,102,Operating Systems,Dr. Park,4,B+
    1002,Bob Khan,101,Introduction to Databases,Dr. Chen,4,A-
    1002,Bob Khan,103,Linear Algebra,Dr. Navarro,3,B
    1003,Carlos Silva,102,Operating Systems,Dr. Park,4,A
    ... 79,995 more rows ...
    

Why did they do this? Because every time anyone ran a report ("who is enrolled in Operating Systems?"), they didn't have to consult multiple files — all the information is in this one file. This is the most natural thing in the world to do when you only have a text editor and no query engine. Chapter 11 will have _a lot_ to say about why this instinct exists and why a proper database makes it unnecessary.

Now back to Dr. Park's name change. His name appears in:

* `courses.csv`, once, in row 102.
* `enrollments.csv`, in **every single row** for every course he has ever taught. If Dr. Park has taught 300 students across six semesters, that is 300 rows.

To correctly update Dr. Park's name, Registrar must edit 301 rows. Miss a single one and her database now asserts two contradictory facts: Dr. Park's name is "Park" according to rows 1–200 of his enrollments, and "Soo-min Park" according to rows 201–301. Any report she generates will depend on which file it reads.

> ### Definition: Update Anomaly

An **update anomaly** occurs when a single real-world fact is stored in multiple places and at least one of those places is not updated when the fact changes, producing contradictory data.

In plain language, an update anomaly is "the same fact in many rooms" — change it in one room, forget another, and now the database cannot decide what's true. It is not a mistake by the editor; it is a structural property of how the data is laid out.

The update anomaly grows with _redundancy_ — the number of times the same fact is stored. If Dr. Park's name appears once in the entire database, no update anomaly is possible. If it appears 301 times, update becomes a 301-operation transaction, and a lapse anywhere in those 301 operations breaks integrity.

This is the first concrete appearance of the tradeoff theme from Chapter 1. The staffer who denormalized `enrollments.csv` _gained_ query convenience (one file contains all the information) at the _cost_ of safety against update anomalies. The same tradeoff — normalization versus denormalization — reappears formally in Chapter 6.

### 2.3 The Deletion Anomaly

Semester ends. The Linear Algebra course, 103, is discontinued. Dr. Navarro is the only instructor who ever taught it, and the single enrolled student, Bob Khan, has graduated and been removed from `students.csv`.

Registrar does the natural thing: she deletes the row for course 103 from `enrollments.csv`. And in the "production" denormalized version, that means she deletes Bob Khan's row — which was the only row containing Dr. Navarro's information.

Dr. Navarro, along with the course title "Linear Algebra" and credits "3", has now vanished from the database _even though she is still employed and might teach again next year_. The problem wasn't that we wanted to forget Dr. Navarro — we wanted to forget Bob's enrollment in 103. But because Dr. Navarro's information was co-stored with Bob's enrollment, deleting the enrollment silently deleted the instructor.

> ### Definition: Deletion Anomaly

A **deletion anomaly** occurs when removing one piece of information forces the deletion of other, logically independent information that was only stored in the same row.

In plain language, a deletion anomaly is "forgetting more than you meant to." Because Bob's enrollment row was the only place Dr. Navarro appeared, deleting Bob's enrollment silently deleted the instructor.

The pattern to notice: deletion anomalies arise when one logical fact ("Bob is enrolled in 103") and a different, independent fact ("Dr. Navarro is an instructor") share the same storage row. If those two facts lived in separate rows — or better, separate tables — we could delete one without touching the other.

This is the core insight Chapter 3 will formalize: facts about _different entities_ should live in _different tables_. Mixing them, as our denormalized `enrollments.csv` does, is structurally dangerous.

### 2.4 The Insertion Anomaly

Spring semester is about to start. The university hires Dr. Lam to teach a brand-new course, 104, "Database Internals", credits 4. Registrar wants to record this in her spreadsheet.

But where?

* `enrollments.csv` (denormalized) has a row per enrollment. With no students enrolled in the course yet, there is no enrollment to write.
* `courses.csv` exists and could hold the course — but then we've split Dr. Lam across two places (appearing in `courses.csv` but not in `enrollments.csv` until a student signs up). In the single-file version, or in any system that has fused courses with enrollments, there is no way to record "course 104 exists, taught by Dr. Lam" without also inventing a phantom enrollment. Registrar writes:

    
    
    NULL,NULL,104,Database Internals,Dr. Lam,4,NULL
    

Now the database thinks there is an enrollment with no student and no grade. This is ugly but it's worse than ugly: it also breaks every query. "Count the number of enrollments in 104" says 1 when the truth is 0. "Who teaches 104?" returns "Dr. Lam" but via a phantom enrollment row that shouldn't exist.

> ### Definition: Insertion Anomaly

An **insertion anomaly** occurs when a new fact cannot be added to the database without inventing artificial related data — typically `NULL` values or placeholder rows — because the schema forces unrelated facts into the same row.

In plain language, an insertion anomaly is "I cannot tell you about the instructor without telling you about a student" — the schema demands two independent facts to travel together even when you only have one of them.

#### Common Misconception

Students sometimes think NULLs are "just a database thing" with no cost beyond a storage bit. Wrong. NULLs interact with query logic in three-valued ways — an expression comparing to NULL is `UNKNOWN`, not `TRUE` or `FALSE`. This means aggregate functions, joins, and `WHERE` clauses can behave counterintuitively when NULLs are present. Chapter 3 will formalize this; for now, notice that the NULL Registrar was forced to invent _above_ carries real semantic damage: "the number of students enrolled" and "the number of enrollment rows" now disagree by 1.

The three anomalies — update, deletion, insertion — are not three different kinds of bugs. They are **three symptoms of one disease** : data redundancy driven by schemas that mix unrelated facts into the same rows. Cure the disease (by splitting facts across multiple tables, as we will in Chapter 3) and all three symptoms disappear together.

### 2.5 Referential Integrity Without a DBMS

So far our examples have been purely about mutating data. Now consider a subtler failure.

Registrar, in a hurry, types an enrollment row:
    
    
    1099,Alice Wong,109,Advanced Cryptography,Dr. Boerner,4,
    

There is no student 1099 (Alice is 1001), and there is no course 109. Both are typos. In a relational DBMS, this row would be _rejected at insert time_ — the foreign keys from `enrollments.student_id` to `students.id` and from `enrollments.course_id` to `courses.id` would prevent the write.

A CSV file has no such guard. The row enters the file. Tomorrow, when a report pulls "all courses Alice is enrolled in", it finds her in course 109 that does not exist — a **dangling reference**. The report either silently drops her, errors out, or displays "Advanced Cryptography" as a real course even though the system has no other record of it.

> ### Definition: Referential Integrity

**Referential integrity** is the property that every reference in the database points to a row that actually exists. When table T has a foreign key referring to table U, referential integrity means: for every row in T, the referenced row in U is present, not deleted, not typo'd.

In plain language, referential integrity is "no dangling references" — every pointer actually goes somewhere real. A DBMS enforces this automatically through foreign-key constraints (Chapter 3); a file system does not, because it has no language for expressing the constraint in the first place.

Referential integrity is the fourth DBMS promise from Chapter 1 made concrete. A CSV file cannot make this promise because it does not know what a reference is. The DBMS does.

### 2.6 Concurrent Access, File-Style

Two staffers, Xia and Yannick, are processing enrollment edits on a Monday morning. Both open `enrollments.csv` in their favorite editor.

* Xia edits the grade for student 1001 in course 101 from A to A+.
* Yannick, in parallel, adds a new row for student 1050 in course 104.

Both save the file back. Whoever saves second wins. If Yannick saves second, Xia's grade edit is silently erased — because Yannick's editor loaded the file before Xia's save, modified it (adding the new row), and wrote the whole file back. Xia's grade change never made it into the version Yannick was editing, so Yannick's save reverses it.

This is the **lost update** problem, and it is exactly the bank-account scenario we will return to in Chapter 13 as Threading C. The CSV version and the bank-account version are the same structural bug: two readers loaded the _same_ data, made independent changes, and wrote back, with the second write silently clobbering the first.

The "solution" at the file level — take an exclusive lock on the whole file during any edit — scales catastrophically. If Xia is editing for five minutes, Yannick is blocked for five minutes. In the university's actual workflow with twelve staffers during enrollment week, this means eleven people are always waiting. Single-writer files cannot scale to multiple users. This is why Chapter 13 onward takes concurrency so seriously.

### 2.7 Crash Recovery, File-Style (It Doesn't Exist)

Registrar is processing the weekly enrollment batch — a legitimate 2,000-row insert into `enrollments.csv`. Halfway through, the server's power supply fails.

What is the state of the file?

Probably: a partially-written final row (`1843,Sarah Lee,10`). Possibly: the file is truncated — the OS had buffered the write and only flushed some of it. Possibly: nothing at all — the OS flushed none of it. Registrar has no way to know without reading every row and checking it parses.

Worse: suppose the 2,000-row batch was supposed to be _atomic_ — either all 2,000 rows are present or none are. There is no way to express that guarantee over a file. If the crash hit after row 1,500, Registrar has to figure out, by hand, which 1,500 actually landed, reverse them, and redo the batch. Without careful manual accounting, she will end up with duplicates or gaps.

A DBMS turns this from "manual forensics" into a single API call (`BEGIN TRANSACTION`, then 2,000 inserts, then `COMMIT` — or on crash, automatic rollback). Chapter 17 will show how this is actually implemented with a Write-Ahead Log. The key takeaway here: **atomicity is a structural property the storage layer must support.** A bare CSV file cannot. Even a CSV file plus the OS's sync calls cannot, because there is no language for grouping many writes into one all-or-nothing unit.

### 2.8 Why a Query Language Matters

Let's return to one of our original questions from Chapter 1: "The average grade of all students enrolled in courses taught by Dr. Chen."

In a proper relational database, this is one sentence:
    
    
    SELECT AVG(e.grade)
    FROM enrollments e
    JOIN courses c ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE c.instructor = 'Dr. Chen';
    

In a CSV world, this is:

  1. Write a script (Python, awk, spreadsheet) that reads `courses.csv` and filters to Dr. Chen's courses.
  2. Take the resulting `course_id`s and filter `enrollments.csv` to only those.
  3. Average the grades — but grades are letter grades; first convert to a numeric scale, which requires another table (`grade_points.csv`? that doesn't exist yet).
  4. Return the number.

Each of those steps is doable but requires _imperative code_ that describes _how_ to compute the answer. Every new question demands a new script.

The relational model plus SQL is **declarative** : you describe _what_ answer you want, and the engine decides _how_. This is the single biggest win of switching from files to a database. It is what makes a DBMS feel like a programming language for data, not a storage system.

#### Common Misconception

Some engineers (especially those who learned SQL after deep experience with imperative code) think declarative querying is "just convenience" — "I could write the same thing in Python, it would just be longer." This underestimates the win. The optimizer (Chapter 12) uses the _freedom_ of declarativity to pick a plan that is sometimes 1000× faster than the most obvious Python. You could _in principle_ write the same plan in Python, but you would have to re-derive it for every query, for every change in data size, for every new index. Declarativity is not a quality-of-life improvement; it is the interface that lets the engine do the optimization work that humans cannot do at scale.

### 2.9 The Relational Shape of the Problem

Look back at Registrar's three original files at the start of this chapter:

* `students.csv` — facts about _students_.
* `courses.csv` — facts about _courses_.
* `enrollments.csv` — facts about the _relationship between a student and a course_.

This decomposition was the right instinct. The anomalies we explored in this chapter arose when a later well-meaning staffer _added redundancy_ to `enrollments.csv` — duplicating the student's name, the course's title, and the instructor's name into the enrollment rows. Undo that denormalization, and:

* **Update anomaly disappears.** Dr. Park's name lives in `courses.csv` and nowhere else. One edit to one row.
* **Deletion anomaly disappears.** Dr. Navarro lives in `courses.csv`; Bob's enrollment lives in `enrollments.csv`. Deleting Bob's enrollment doesn't touch the courses file.
* **Insertion anomaly disappears.** Course 104 with Dr. Lam is inserted into `courses.csv` without any enrollment row needed.

What Registrar has just derived, by negative example, is the **relational principle** : store each kind of fact in its own table, and link tables by references (foreign keys) rather than by repeating data.

Three things are still missing before this is a real database:

  1. **A way to enforce referential integrity** — the CSVs can still contain `student_id = 1099` for a nonexistent student. Chapter 3 introduces foreign-key constraints.
  2. **A query language** over the normalized tables — otherwise every report is a fresh Python script. Chapter 4 introduces SQL.
  3. **Concurrency control and crash recovery** — because even perfectly normalized CSVs still suffer the multi-writer and crash-mid-write problems. Chapters 13–17 tackle these.

But the _shape_ of the solution — "split your data into tables, each representing one kind of thing, and link them by references" — is now visible. That shape is called the relational model, and it is the subject of Chapter 3.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Denormalized CSVs trade update safety for read locality.** Keeping a student's name and instructor's name inside every enrollment row makes a single-file report trivial but guarantees update, deletion, and insertion anomalies the moment the data changes.
* **File-level locking trades concurrency for simplicity.** You can prevent lost writes on `books.csv` by locking the whole file, but then no one else can read it either — the only safe multi-writer file system is a single-writer file system.
* **Whole-file rewrites trade crash safety for implementation effort.** Writing a temp file and renaming is easy and almost atomic, but mid-write crashes still leave you torn between the old and new version with no bounded recovery story. A DBMS spends Chapter 17's machinery specifically to avoid this.
* **Putting integrity in application code trades enforcement guarantees for flexibility.** Every caller must remember every rule; one forgotten check leaves an orphan row. A DBMS moves the rules into the schema so that no caller can bypass them.
* **Ad-hoc Python scripts trade a query language for raw control.** You can compute anything, but every new question is a new program. SQL gives up some imperative power in exchange for a declarative interface the engine can optimize.
* **The relational decomposition trades query-side join cost for write-side anomaly immunity.** Splitting facts into separate tables means reports need joins; it also means one edit touches one row, not hundreds. For anything that gets updated, this trade almost always pays.

### 2.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** In one sentence each, define the update anomaly, deletion anomaly, and insertion anomaly. Your definitions should make clear what is common to all three.

Hint

Try to identify the single structural cause underlying all three.

**Answer:**

* **Update anomaly:** when the same fact is stored in many rows, changing the fact in one row while missing others produces contradictory data.
* **Deletion anomaly:** when deleting one fact (an enrollment) unintentionally erases another logically independent fact (the instructor) because they were co-stored in the same row.
* **Insertion anomaly:** when a new fact cannot be recorded without inventing NULLs or phantom related rows because the schema forces unrelated facts to live together.

Common cause: _redundancy driven by schemas that mix unrelated facts into the same row._ All three anomalies are symptoms of the same underlying disease; removing redundancy cures all three at once.

**Q2.** Which of the following does a CSV-based "database" _not_ provide on its own? Mark each as provided / not provided and give a one-line reason.

(a) Schema-level type checking (e.g., "`credits` must be an integer"). (b) Prevention of duplicate primary keys. (c) Atomic multi-row updates. (d) Human readability. (e) Referential integrity (foreign-key enforcement). (f) Cross-platform portability.

**Answer:** (a) **Not provided.** A CSV is text. Storing "not a number" in the `credits` column produces no error until something tries to interpret it. (b) **Not provided.** Nothing enforces uniqueness of the `id` column — you can have two students with `id = 1001` and the file system is happy. (c) **Not provided.** No notion of transactions; any multi-row operation is a sequence of independent writes that can be interrupted. (d) **Provided.** CSVs are plain text, open in any editor. (e) **Not provided.** No foreign-key concept; `enrollments.csv` can reference nonexistent students and no one will notice. (f) **Provided.** CSV is about as portable a data format as exists.

CSVs trade almost all correctness guarantees for maximum simplicity and portability. That trade is occasionally the right one — for static data exports, archival, human-editable config — but not for operational data.

**Q3.** Registrar keeps one instructor-email field on each course row in `courses.csv`. One instructor teaches three courses. The instructor changes her email address. How many rows must be updated? What kind of anomaly is this, and what would fix it structurally?

**Answer:** Three rows must be updated, one per course the instructor teaches. This is an **update anomaly** : the same fact (the instructor's email) is stored redundantly across multiple rows. The structural fix is to introduce a fourth table, `instructors(id, name, email)`, and change `courses.csv` to reference `instructor_id` instead of embedding the instructor's name and email. Now the email lives in exactly one row, and editing it is a one-row change. (Chapter 3 introduces foreign keys for this; Chapter 6 proves formally why this arrangement eliminates the anomaly.)

**Q4.** A colleague argues: "We can avoid update anomalies in a CSV just by being careful — whenever we change a fact, we'll grep the whole file and fix every occurrence." Why is this argument weak?

**Answer:** Three problems.

  1. It doesn't scale. One careful person can handle a file of 100 rows; the same person cannot reliably handle 80,000 rows, especially under time pressure. Humans are not atomic transactions.
  2. It doesn't handle concurrency. If two people are running "careful grep-and-fix" in parallel, their edits can interleave and lose changes (Section 2.6).
  3. Most importantly, it moves the correctness property from the system to a human policy. "Being careful" is a policy, not a guarantee. The whole point of a DBMS is to convert policies (things we hope humans will do) into structural guarantees (things the system prevents from going wrong). A properly normalized schema makes update anomalies _structurally impossible_ , which is a different species of guarantee from "we promise to be careful".

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Registrar has a fourth file, `instructor_offices.csv`:
    
    
    instructor_name,office,phone
    Dr. Chen,Building A-214,x4421
    Dr. Park,Building B-105,x4587
    Dr. Navarro,Building A-310,x4309
    

She is considering whether to merge this data back into `courses.csv` (one row per course, with office and phone duplicated for each course the same instructor teaches) "for convenience". Describe two realistic anomalies that would appear within one month if she does so, and describe what a relational DBMS would do differently.

Hint

Think about what changes from month to month: offices, phone extensions, new courses, cancelled courses.

**Answer:** **Anomaly 1 (update).** Dr. Park moves from Building B-105 to Building B-220. His phone extension also changes. In the merged version, his office and phone are duplicated across every course he teaches (say 5 courses) — now we must update 5 rows. Miss even one, and the database says Dr. Park is in two offices simultaneously.

**Anomaly 2 (deletion).** Dr. Navarro's only course this semester is Linear Algebra, 103, which is cancelled for lack of enrollment. Registrar deletes row 103 from `courses.csv`. Because Dr. Navarro's office and phone were only co-stored with that row, they vanish — even though Dr. Navarro is still a faculty member who will likely teach next semester. Dr. Navarro's office now exists only as institutional memory, not in the database.

**What a relational DBMS would do differently.** Keep the schema normalized: `instructors(name, office, phone)` as one table, `courses(id, title, credits, instructor_name)` with a foreign key referencing `instructors`. Dr. Park's office moves: one row changes. Dr. Navarro's course is cancelled: the instructors table is untouched; only the courses row is removed. In both cases, one edit, one row, no redundancy to manage. And the foreign key constraint prevents a typo from creating a dangling reference.

**Q2.** Consider this single row from a hypothetical denormalized `orders.csv` for an e-commerce site:
    
    
    order_id, customer_name, customer_email, item_id, item_name, item_price, quantity, ship_to_address, order_date
    2213, Sofia Ruiz, sofia.r@example.com, 7741, USB-C Cable, 14.99, 2, 18 Elm St, 2026-03-15
    

Identify at least five redundancies. For each, describe the anomaly it exposes and give a one-line example of the anomaly triggering.

Hint

Every time the same fact appears in more than one row of the real data, it's a redundancy.

**Answer:**

  1. **`customer_name`** — appears in every order Sofia places. _Update:_ Sofia changes her last name; five orders must be updated. _Deletion:_ Sofia's last order is cancelled; her customer record vanishes.
  2. **`customer_email`** — same redundancy pattern. _Update:_ email change requires touching every historical order row.
  3. **`item_name`** — appears in every order containing that item. _Update:_ item is renamed from "USB-C Cable" to "USB-C Cable 1m"; hundreds of historical orders must be touched.
  4. **`item_price`** — appears redundantly in every order. _Deletion:_ if the item is discontinued and all its orders are cancelled, the historical price is gone; we cannot compute "what was the average USB-C Cable price in 2025?"
  5. **`ship_to_address`** — likely redundant across a customer's orders. _Update:_ customer moves; which orders' addresses do we update? (Hint: probably _not_ historical ones — the shipping address at the time of the order is a historical fact — which itself suggests item_price and ship_to_address are correctly placed here _for historical orders_. The real schema should separate "the customer's current address" from "the shipping address snapshot at order time", a distinction the denormalized file cannot make.)

**Cross-chapter connection:** this exercise is a preview of Chapter 6 (normalization) and Chapter 23 (the capstone design review). It also raises a subtle point — some "redundancies" are actually desired snapshots of historical fact, which is why normalizing an e-commerce schema requires real judgment, not a mechanical rule.

**Q3.** Write a single Python script that, given the `students.csv` and `enrollments.csv` from the start of the chapter, produces a report "Number of courses each student is enrolled in, sorted descending". Then write the equivalent SQL. Compare the two and discuss: (a) which is easier to write, (b) which is easier to modify if the question changes ("also exclude students with 0 enrollments"), and (c) which is easier for the engine to optimize.

Hint

Count rows per student_id in enrollments.csv, join with students.csv on id, sort by count descending.

**Answer:**

Python (one reasonable version):
    
    
    import csv
    from collections import Counter
    
    with open('enrollments.csv') as f:
        counts = Counter(row['student_id'] for row in csv.DictReader(f))
    
    with open('students.csv') as f:
        students = {row['id']: row['name'] for row in csv.DictReader(f)}
    
    report = sorted(
        ((students[sid], cnt) for sid, cnt in counts.items()),
        key=lambda x: -x[1]
    )
    
    for name, cnt in report:
        print(f"{name}: {cnt}")
    

SQL (the same question):
    
    
    SELECT s.name, COUNT(*) AS course_count
    FROM students s
    JOIN enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    GROUP BY s.name
    ORDER BY course_count DESC;
    

(a) **Easier to write:** SQL, clearly. The Python code must explicitly express _how_ to compute the aggregation (count, join, sort). The SQL expresses _what_ we want.

(b) **Easier to modify:** SQL. To exclude students with zero enrollments, the SQL `JOIN` already does that (it's an inner join). To _include_ them, change to `LEFT JOIN` and the rest is unchanged. In Python, handling zero-enrollment students requires restructuring the code — iterating over `students` rather than `counts`, using `counts.get(sid, 0)`, etc. Every modification of the question requires re-writing the procedure.

(c) **Easier to optimize:** SQL, by a huge margin. A DBMS with an index on `enrollments.student_id` will use that index to make the count fast, while a secondary index and pre-sorted statistics might let it skip the sort entirely. The Python version has no such knowledge and reads every row of both files linearly. As data grows from 3 students to 3 million, the SQL version stays fast and the Python version gets slower linearly. This is the optimization freedom declarative queries give us, and it is the topic of Chapter 12.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** We said "every concept is a tradeoff." The denormalization in Section 2.2 — where the staffer embedded student and course info into `enrollments.csv` — is a tradeoff too: query convenience against update safety. Can you think of a realistic case where denormalization is _correct_? What would need to be true about the workload?

**Discussion:** Yes, several. The most common cases where deliberate denormalization wins:

  1. **Read-heavy, write-rare workloads.** An analytics table that is rebuilt nightly and queried thousands of times during the day. Updates don't happen during business hours; they happen during the nightly rebuild when no one is reading. Update anomalies cannot occur in this workflow because the table is never updated in place — it's rewritten whole. This is the core logic behind star schemas and data warehouses (Chapter 19).

  2. **Historical snapshots.** An `orders` table that records "what was the product's price _at the time of the order_?" — this is denormalized (the price is duplicated from the `products` table), but intentionally, because the historical price is an independent fact that must survive even if the current price changes. Denormalizing here doesn't _cause_ an update anomaly; the field simply is not meant to track the products table.

  3. **Extreme read-latency requirements.** A page that must render in 50ms over a globally distributed database might denormalize to avoid any joins. Cassandra users (Chapter 21) routinely denormalize for this reason.

The pattern: denormalization is correct when either (a) the fact is historical and should _not_ track a source row, or (b) the write path is controlled enough that update anomalies cannot happen. In most OLTP workloads, neither is true, and normalization wins. This is a Chapter 6 topic that you're previewing here.

**Q2.** This chapter framed CSV files as obviously inferior. But CSVs remain very common in 2026 — for data interchange between systems, for exports, for ML training datasets. Why do CSVs persist despite their weaknesses? What does this tell you about when "the best tool" and "the right tool" diverge?

**Discussion:** CSVs persist because their tradeoffs, in specific contexts, are _correct_ :

* **Interchange.** A CSV parses with 20 lines of code in any language. Every database, every spreadsheet, every analytics tool reads them. Portability is maximal. Where the workload is "move data from system A to system B", CSV's lack of concurrency control and crash recovery simply does not matter — we are not doing concurrent writes during interchange.
* **Human editability.** A data scientist wants to hand-correct three rows in a dataset. Opening a PostgreSQL database requires installation, credentials, SQL; opening a CSV in a text editor requires nothing.
* **Simplicity at the small scale.** For a static dataset of 500 rows that changes once a month and has one human editor, the full machinery of a DBMS is overkill.

The deeper lesson: **"best" is always relative to a workload.** A DBMS is unambiguously better than a CSV for operational data — data that is concurrently modified, must survive crashes, and is queried in varied ways. For interchange, archival, and one-editor-small-scale, the CSV's tradeoff profile can be superior: simpler, more portable, no infrastructure dependency, infinitely compatible.

This reflects a principle we will return to throughout the book, especially in Chapter 22 (choosing the right database): _the right tool depends on the workload you actually have, not the workload you imagine._ Many startups over-engineer their initial data architecture because they assume "we'll scale to billions of rows" when the realistic 12-month workload is "50,000 rows, two writers." For that workload, SQLite or even a CSV-plus-backups can be the correct answer.

**Q3.** The three anomalies (update, deletion, insertion) all arise from _redundancy_. Can we conclude "therefore databases should have no redundancy"? Where in this book might you expect to meet deliberate redundancy, and why?

**Discussion:** No, we cannot. Redundancy is a tool, and like all tools, it is right for some jobs and wrong for others. You will meet deliberate redundancy in this book in at least three places:

  1. **Indexes (Chapter 10).** An index is, strictly speaking, redundant storage — the same data, organized differently, so that certain queries are fast. The cost is write amplification: every insert updates both the table and all its indexes. The gain is read speed. This is an intentional space-and-write-cost versus read-speed tradeoff.

  2. **Replication (Chapter 18).** A replicated database stores the same row on multiple machines. That is pure redundancy. The cost is coordination complexity and the CAP-theorem tradeoffs. The gain is availability and read throughput.

  3. **Materialized views and caches (Chapters 8, 20).** Pre-computed query results, stored redundantly, refreshed periodically. Cost: staleness and refresh complexity. Gain: sub-second query response.

The lesson: redundancy is not bad; _uncontrolled_ redundancy is bad. The anomalies of this chapter arose not because of redundancy itself but because the redundancy was _unmanaged_ — no mechanism existed to keep duplicated facts in sync. A DBMS's indexes, replicas, and materialized views are all redundant _by design_ and come with explicit machinery (index maintenance, replication protocols, view refresh) to keep the redundancy consistent. "Controlled redundancy with machine-enforced consistency" is a recurring motif of this book, starting formally in Chapter 10.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter walked through the death of the file-system database. We built the university registration system (Thread A's first appearance) as three CSV files and watched every one of the four DBMS promises from Chapter 1 break within normal use: **update anomalies** (Dr. Park's name), **deletion anomalies** (Dr. Navarro vanishing with the cancelled course), **insertion anomalies** (Dr. Lam's new course without students), **dangling references** (the mis-typed student/course IDs), **lost updates** from concurrent editing, and **no atomicity** on crash.

The three anomalies are not three different problems; they are three symptoms of one disease — **uncontrolled redundancy from schemas that mix unrelated facts into the same rows**. Cure the disease and all three symptoms disappear together. That cure is the **relational model** of Chapter 3.

We also introduced the **query language argument** : declarative queries (SQL) are not merely convenient; they are the interface that lets the engine apply optimization knowledge humans cannot match at scale. This foreshadows Chapter 12.

Key ideas that recur later:

* The update / deletion / insertion anomaly framework returns in Chapter 6, where we prove formally (via functional dependencies and normal forms) when anomalies are structurally impossible.
* The lost-update concurrent-editing problem returns in Chapter 13 as the bank-account scenario and drives Chapters 14–16.
* The crash-mid-write problem returns in Chapter 17 with the ARIES recovery algorithm.
* The observation that "controlled redundancy" is fine — indexes, replicas, materialized views — returns in Chapters 10, 18, and 20.

Next chapter: we fix all of this with the relational model. Rows become tuples, tables become relations, references become foreign keys, and the three anomalies become structurally preventable. Chapter 3 is the formal introduction to the idea this chapter proved by negative example.

---

## Chapter 3: The Relational Model

Chapter 2 left us with wreckage — three anomalies, dangling references, a file-system data layer that corrupts itself under light use. This chapter introduces the idea that quietly replaced the wreckage in the 1970s and still underwrites every mainstream database in 2026: the **relational model**.

The relational model's insight is almost philosophical. Instead of asking "how do I store my data?" (which produces files, structures, pointers, indexes), ask "what is the _shape_ of my data, independent of storage?" Answer: sets of tuples, each tuple a flat collection of values, each value drawn from a known domain. That's it. The relational model says data _is_ a collection of sets of tuples, and anything else — pages, B-trees, buffer pools — is just the storage engine's problem.

This abstraction is what makes databases a discipline rather than a collection of tricks. The same three anomalies that plagued Chapter 2's CSV files become structurally _impossible_ under a properly designed relational schema. That is the claim we will make precise in this chapter and prove (formally) in Chapter 6.

### 3.1 Relations: The Central Concept

A **relation** is, in the mathematical sense, a set. Specifically, a set of **tuples** , where each tuple has the same structure — same attributes, same number of values, drawn from the same domains.

For our university registration system from Chapter 2, the `Students` relation might be written:
    
    
    Students(id INTEGER, name VARCHAR, email VARCHAR)
    

And a particular instance of this relation might be the set:
    
    
    { (1001, "Alice Wong",   "alice.wong@uni.edu"),
      (1002, "Bob Khan",     "bob.khan@uni.edu"),
      (1003, "Carlos Silva", "carlos.silva@uni.edu") }
    

Notice three consequences of "relation is a set":
