  1. **No order.** The three tuples above could be listed in any order — the relation is the same. If you write SQL that depends on row order ("give me the first student"), you have misunderstood the model. (To get order, you ask for order: `ORDER BY`.)
  2. **No duplicates.** Strictly speaking, a relation cannot contain two identical tuples. In SQL — a bag model, not a strict set model — duplicates are possible, but candidate keys and uniqueness constraints exist precisely to let you enforce set semantics when you need them.
  3. **Every tuple has the same attributes.** There is no "row-level schema" that varies per tuple. This is a principled decision — it constrains flexibility but enables everything from type-checked queries to index structures.

> ### Definition: Relation

A **relation** is a set of tuples, all sharing the same schema — i.e., all with the same attributes drawn from the same domains. A relation's schema is the pairing of attribute names with their domains; a relation instance is the particular set of tuples present at a given moment.

In plain language, a relation is a table where rows are unordered, duplicates aren't meaningful, and every row has the same columns of the same types. "Table" is the SQL name; "relation" is the mathematical name. We will use them interchangeably but, when precision matters, "relation" signals we care about the abstract set, not the storage layout.

#### The Three Terminologies

You will encounter three parallel vocabularies for the same concepts. They are used interchangeably in literature, so it pays to know all three:

Mathematical | Logical | Physical (file-system era)  
---|---|---  
Relation | Table | File  
Tuple | Row | Record  
Attribute | Column | Field  
Domain | Type | Field type  
Relation schema | Table schema | File format  
Relation instance | Table contents | File contents  
  
The left column is the formal (mathematical) vocabulary. The middle column is what SQL uses. The right column is how the same concepts were named in the era before databases. If you read a 1972 paper on databases, expect the right column; a 1995 textbook, the middle column; a 2026 theory paper, the left column.

#### Attributes and Domains

Each attribute draws its values from a **domain** — a named set of permissible values. For example, the `credits` attribute of the `Courses` relation might have domain `INTEGER` (or, more strictly, `INTEGER IN [1, 12]` if the registrar wants to enforce that no single course is worth more than a semester's worth of credits).

Domains serve two purposes:

  1. They **type-check** the data at insertion time. A DBMS will reject `'four'` for an `INTEGER` column.
  2. They **constrain queries**. The SQL engine knows that comparing two `INTEGER` columns with `<` is meaningful, but comparing an `INTEGER` with a `VARCHAR` is either coerced or forbidden depending on strictness settings.

Most SQL databases offer a fixed set of built-in domains (INTEGER, VARCHAR, TEXT, BOOLEAN, DATE, TIMESTAMP, NUMERIC(p,s)) plus mechanisms to extend them (ENUM types, CHECK constraints, user-defined types). PostgreSQL's type system is famously rich; SQLite's is famously loose.

### 3.2 The Registrar's Schema, Properly

Let's rebuild the university registration system from Chapter 2 in the relational model. The anomalies of Chapter 2 arose when unrelated facts were co-stored; the relational fix is to put each kind of fact in its own relation.
    
    
    Students(
      id       INTEGER     PRIMARY KEY,
      name     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      email    VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE NOT NULL
    )
    
    Instructors(
      id       INTEGER      PRIMARY KEY,
      name     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      office   VARCHAR(50),
      phone    VARCHAR(20)
    )
    
    Courses(
      id            INTEGER     PRIMARY KEY,
      title         VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
      credits       INTEGER      NOT NULL CHECK (credits BETWEEN 1 AND 12),
      instructor_id INTEGER      NOT NULL REFERENCES Instructors(id)
    )
    
    Enrollments(
      student_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Students(id),
      course_id  INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Courses(id),
      grade      VARCHAR(3),
      PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)
    )
    

This schema encodes, in its very structure, the cure for all three anomalies from Chapter 2:

* **Update anomaly cured.** Dr. Park's name lives in exactly one row of `Instructors`. A name change is one `UPDATE`.
* **Deletion anomaly cured.** Deleting a course does not touch the instructors table. Deleting Bob's enrollment does not touch the courses or instructors tables.
* **Insertion anomaly cured.** A new course with no enrolled students is a straightforward `INSERT` into `Courses` — enrollments are an independent concern.
* **Referential integrity.** A typo `INSERT INTO Enrollments (student_id, course_id) VALUES (1099, 109)` fails at the database level because there is no student 1099 and no course 109.

We will walk through each of these constructs — keys, references, constraints — in Sections 3.3 through 3.6.

### 3.3 Keys: Superkeys, Candidate Keys, Primary Keys

Keys are the relational model's mechanism for identifying tuples. They are the single most important structural feature of a relation, and they come in a hierarchy.

> ### Definition: Superkey

A **superkey** of a relation is a set of attributes whose values, taken together, uniquely identify every tuple. That is: if two tuples have the same values on the superkey's attributes, they must be the same tuple.

In plain language, a superkey is any combination of columns whose values never repeat across two rows.

For the `Students` relation, every one of these is a superkey:

* `{id}`
* `{email}`
* `{id, name}`
* `{id, email}`
* `{id, name, email}`

The superkey concept is permissive: every attribute of `Students` could reasonably be different for every student, so many combinations uniquely identify a tuple. We want a stricter version.

> ### Definition: Candidate Key

A **candidate key** is a superkey _no proper subset of which is itself a superkey_. That is, a minimal superkey — removing any attribute destroys its uniqueness property.

In plain language, a candidate key is a _minimal_ combination of columns that still uniquely identifies a row.

For `Students`, the candidate keys are `{id}` and `{email}`. Neither can lose any attribute and still be unique (they each have only one attribute). `{id, name}` is a superkey but not a candidate key — you can drop `name` and `{id}` still uniquely identifies.

A relation may have more than one candidate key. The relation designer chooses **one** candidate key to be the **primary key** ; the others are called _alternate_ candidate keys.

> ### Definition: Primary Key

A **primary key** is the single candidate key designated by the relation's designer as the canonical identifier for tuples. A primary key must be:

  1. **Unique:** no two tuples share a primary key value.
  2. **Non-null:** the primary key is never `NULL` — this is called **entity integrity**.
  3. **Stable:** the primary key value should not change over the tuple's lifetime. (Changing a primary key forces every foreign-key reference to be updated in lockstep.)

In plain language, the primary key is how the database (and you) refer to a specific row. Entity integrity — "no primary key is NULL" — exists because you cannot have a real entity without a real identity.

#### Natural vs Surrogate Keys

A **natural key** is a candidate key derived from the data's own meaning — e.g., `email` for `Students`, or `ISBN` for books. A **surrogate key** is an arbitrary identifier invented by the database — typically an auto-generated integer or a UUID.

Which should you use? This is a real design question. Arguments:

* **Natural keys** feel elegant and save a column of storage. But they often turn out not to be stable (people change email addresses, books get re-ISBN'd, company names change). When the natural key changes, every foreign-key reference to it must cascade.
* **Surrogate keys** are stable by construction because they carry no real-world meaning. The tradeoff is: you need a second column for the natural key to preserve its uniqueness, and joining by surrogate key is slightly less intuitive than joining by "real" identifiers.

The modern consensus: use surrogate keys (`id INTEGER` or `id UUID`) for the primary key in almost all operational schemas, and add a `UNIQUE` constraint on the natural key if it exists. This gives you stability _and_ preserves the natural key's uniqueness. This is exactly what we did in our `Students` schema above: `id` as primary key, `email` with `UNIQUE`.

#### Common Misconception

Many novices think "the primary key makes the row unique." The primary key doesn't _make_ rows unique — the **uniqueness constraint** enforces uniqueness, and the primary key happens to have that constraint automatically. The distinction matters because you can have many unique columns (each with a `UNIQUE` constraint) even though only one of them is the primary key. The special role of the primary key is that (a) it is non-null, and (b) foreign keys from other tables reference it by default.

### 3.4 Foreign Keys and Referential Integrity

The mechanism that makes the relational model actually coherent — that prevents the dangling-reference bug from Chapter 2 — is the **foreign key**.

> ### Definition: Foreign Key

A **foreign key** in relation `T` is a set of attributes whose values must match the primary key values of some row in another relation `U` (or be `NULL`, if nulls are permitted). The relation `U` is called the **referenced** (or _parent_) relation; `T` is the **referencing** (or _child_) relation.

In plain language, a foreign key is "this column points to a row in that other table, and the database will prevent it from pointing to nothing."

In our schema:

* `Courses.instructor_id` is a foreign key to `Instructors.id`.
* `Enrollments.student_id` is a foreign key to `Students.id`.
* `Enrollments.course_id` is a foreign key to `Courses.id`.

Foreign keys do two things:

  1. **Reject invalid inserts and updates.** If you try to insert `(1099, 101, 'A')` into `Enrollments` and no student 1099 exists, the DBMS refuses.
  2. **Decide what happens on parent delete or update.** If you try to delete student 1001 while they have enrollments pointing to them, what should happen? Three common answers, each chosen at schema-design time:

#### Referential Actions

When a referenced row is deleted (or its primary key is updated), the database can be told to do one of the following:

* **RESTRICT / NO ACTION (default).** The delete is _refused_ if any child row still references the parent. This is the safest default: you must explicitly clean up dependent data before the parent can go.
* **CASCADE.** Deleting the parent also deletes all child rows that reference it. Useful when the child rows are meaningless without the parent (e.g., deleting an order deletes its order-line-items).
* **SET NULL.** The child's foreign key is set to `NULL`, marking the reference as "used to exist but no longer." Requires the foreign key column to allow nulls.
* **SET DEFAULT.** The child's foreign key is set to a default value (e.g., a "deleted-user" placeholder).

Choosing among these is a design decision, and it is often the most consequential one in a schema. Example:
    
    
    Enrollments(
      student_id INTEGER NOT NULL
        REFERENCES Students(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      course_id INTEGER NOT NULL
        REFERENCES Courses(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      ...
    )
    

Here we are saying "don't let a student be deleted while they still have enrollments, and don't let a course be deleted while it still has enrollments." The registrar must explicitly clean up enrollments first. This prevents accidental data loss but requires discipline.

Contrast:
    
    
    OrderLineItems(
      order_id INTEGER NOT NULL
        REFERENCES Orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      ...
    )
    

When an order is deleted, its line items are meaningless, so they cascade. This is safe because line items have no independent identity — they exist only as part of an order.

> ### Definition: Referential Integrity

**Referential integrity** is the property that every foreign key value in the database matches a primary key value in the referenced relation (or is `NULL` where permitted). The DBMS enforces referential integrity automatically on every insert, update, and delete — by rejecting the change or by applying the declared referential action.

In plain language, referential integrity means "no dangling pointers, ever, guaranteed by the system, not by programmer discipline."

This is the answer to the fourth DBMS promise from Chapter 1. The CSV version (Chapter 2) had no way to enforce referential integrity; the relational DBMS does, automatically, by virtue of foreign-key declarations in the schema.

### 3.5 Integrity Constraints More Generally

Foreign keys are one kind of integrity constraint. There are several.

> ### Definition: Integrity Constraint

An **integrity constraint** is a rule that every database instance must satisfy. The DBMS enforces constraints automatically: any operation that would leave the database violating a constraint is rejected (or triggers a cascade).

The five most common kinds of constraints in real schemas:

  1. **Domain constraints** — attribute values must lie within the declared domain. `credits` is an `INTEGER` means you cannot insert `'four'`.
  2. **`NOT NULL` constraints** — attribute must have a value (no nulls allowed). Useful for mandatory fields like `name`, `email`, `created_at`.
  3. **Uniqueness constraints** — the value (or combination of values) must not repeat across tuples. Primary keys are a special case; `UNIQUE` columns and `UNIQUE` multi-column declarations are the general form.
  4. **Referential (foreign-key) constraints** — as above.
  5. **`CHECK` constraints** — arbitrary boolean conditions. `CHECK (credits BETWEEN 1 AND 12)` is a check constraint. `CHECK (grade IN ('A', 'A-', 'B+', 'B', ...))` enforces valid grades.

A well-constrained schema pushes as much of the data's _logic_ into the schema as possible. Business rules expressed as constraints cannot be circumvented by a bug in the application code; business rules expressed only in application code _will_ be circumvented eventually, usually under deadline pressure.

#### Common Misconception

Application developers sometimes argue "we should enforce validation in the application, not the database — that way validation logic lives in one place and uses the language we're comfortable in." This is an expensive mistake. The database is usually accessed by _more than one_ client: the main application, a batch job, a data-migration script, a manual psql session by the on-call engineer, a third-party reporting tool. Any of these can bypass application validation. The only place _all_ clients must funnel through is the DBMS itself. Schema-level constraints are cheap, machine-enforced, and invariant across clients. Duplicate the validation in application code for a nice UX if you like — but do not omit it from the database.

### 3.6 NULL and Three-Valued Logic

NULL in the relational model represents "no value" — unknown, inapplicable, or not-yet-known. It is not zero, not the empty string, not false. It is its own thing, and interacting with it produces surprises unless you understand the rule:

> ### Definition: Three-Valued Logic

SQL's boolean logic has three values: `TRUE`, `FALSE`, and `UNKNOWN`. Any expression involving `NULL` evaluates to `UNKNOWN`. `UNKNOWN` propagates through boolean operations according to strict rules, and it is treated as "not true" in `WHERE` clauses (rows with `UNKNOWN` conditions are filtered out).

The truth tables:

P | Q | P AND Q | P OR Q  
---|---|---|---  
T | T | T | T  
T | F | F | T  
T | U | U | T  
F | F | F | F  
F | U | F | U  
U | U | U | U  
  
Key consequences:

  1. `NULL = NULL` evaluates to `UNKNOWN`, not `TRUE`. To test for null, use `IS NULL`, never `= NULL`.
  2. `grade = 'A'` is `UNKNOWN` when `grade IS NULL`. A query `WHERE grade = 'A'` omits all `NULL` grades, which is usually what you want — but `WHERE grade != 'A'` _also_ omits all `NULL` grades, which is often a surprise.
  3. Aggregate functions skip nulls. `AVG(grade_points)` averages non-null grade points only. `COUNT(*)` counts rows including those with null columns; `COUNT(grade)` counts rows where grade is not null.

NULL is a pragmatic concession — real data is incomplete, and the relational model must handle "we don't know Alice's grade yet" somehow. But every NULL in your schema is a potential source of subtle query bugs. Minimize NULLs where you can. Use `NOT NULL` aggressively. Provide defaults (`DEFAULT 'pending'`) when the semantics of "not yet known" can be captured by a sentinel value.

### 3.7 Thread A: Anomalies Dissolve

Let's trace Thread A — the university registration system — through the three anomalies of Chapter 2 and verify each is now structurally impossible.

#### Update anomaly (Dr. Park's name change)

Chapter 2: Dr. Park's name appeared 301 times (once in `courses.csv`, 300 times in the denormalized `enrollments.csv`). Update required editing all 301 occurrences.

Relational schema: Dr. Park's name lives in exactly one row of `Instructors`.
    
    
    UPDATE Instructors SET name = 'Dr. Soo-min Park' WHERE id = 42;
    

One row changed. Every query that joins to `Instructors` now sees the new name. Anomaly structurally eliminated.

#### Deletion anomaly (Dr. Navarro vanishing)

Chapter 2: Bob Khan's sole enrollment in course 103 was the only row containing Dr. Navarro's info. Deleting the enrollment silently deleted the instructor.

Relational schema: Bob's enrollment lives in `Enrollments`; Dr. Navarro lives in `Instructors`; they are in separate tables. Deleting Bob's enrollment:
    
    
    DELETE FROM Enrollments WHERE student_id = 1002 AND course_id = 103;
    

touches only the enrollments table. `Instructors` is unaffected. Dr. Navarro remains.

#### Insertion anomaly (new course, no students)

Chapter 2: Course 104 could not be inserted without inventing NULL `student_id` / `course_id` values in the denormalized file.

Relational schema: Courses are independent of enrollments. Creating the new course is trivial:
    
    
    INSERT INTO Courses (id, title, credits, instructor_id)
    VALUES (104, 'Database Internals', 4, 71);
    

No enrollment row needed. Anomaly structurally eliminated.

#### Referential integrity (typos)

Chapter 2: `1099,Alice Wong,109,...` could be written into the CSV with no complaint.

Relational schema: the foreign keys on `Enrollments.student_id` and `Enrollments.course_id` catch the typo at insert time:
    
    
    INSERT INTO Enrollments (student_id, course_id) VALUES (1099, 109);
    -- ERROR: insert or update on table "Enrollments" violates
    -- foreign key constraint — Key (student_id)=(1099) is not
    -- present in table "Students".
    

The bad row never lands. The operator sees the error and retries with the correct IDs.

Four Chapter 2 failures, four Chapter 3 structural fixes. This is why the relational model was a revolution.

### 3.8 The Relational Model as a Teaching Device

Throughout the rest of this book, we will sometimes speak of "the relational model" and sometimes of "SQL". These are not the same thing:

* **The relational model** (Codd, 1970) is the mathematical abstraction: sets of tuples, operated on by relational algebra (Chapter 7). It is clean and orthogonal.
* **SQL** (commercial, ~1974–present) is the language implementations converged on. It is not purely relational — it permits duplicates (bag semantics), it has NULLs with three-valued logic, it has procedural features — but it is the interface every mainstream relational DBMS uses.

Why the divergence? Three reasons:

  1. Duplicates are easier to produce and reason about in many common operations. Strict set semantics requires extra work on the engine's part.
  2. Three-valued logic is a concession to the fact that real-world data has holes.
  3. SQL evolved under commercial pressure and accumulated features (window functions, recursive queries, JSON support) that the original relational model did not anticipate.

For the next three chapters (3, 4, 7), we lean on the clean mathematical abstraction when it helps reasoning, and on SQL when we need to show real code. By Chapter 8, SQL and the relational model will diverge further (window functions are not purely relational), and we will be explicit about that.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Set semantics (pure relational model) trades expressive convenience for mathematical cleanliness.** Sets give you simple equivalence laws for the optimizer to exploit; SQL's bag semantics gives you `COUNT` and `SUM` that work the way users expect. Nearly every real system kept the bag.
* **Surrogate keys trade meaning for stability.** A numeric `id` never changes when a student's name, email, or major changes, so foreign keys never dangle — but the key carries no information on its own, and you need a separate uniqueness constraint on whatever attribute the humans actually use.
* **Foreign-key constraints trade write flexibility for referential integrity.** An insert or delete that would produce an orphan is rejected outright; you cannot accidentally point at a nonexistent row, but bulk loads and schema migrations must now respect the dependency order.
* **`ON DELETE CASCADE` trades safety for convenience.** Deleting a student automatically deletes their enrollments, which is what you usually want — and exactly what you catastrophically don't want the day someone deletes the wrong student. `RESTRICT` forces you to acknowledge the fan-out.
* **Three-valued logic (NULL) trades Boolean simplicity for honest handling of missing data.** `x = NULL` is UNKNOWN, not FALSE, so `WHERE` clauses quietly drop rows you might have expected; in exchange, you can represent "we don't know yet" without inventing sentinel values that break arithmetic.
* **Declaring`NOT NULL` where possible trades schema flexibility for query predictability.** Every nullable column is a future three-valued-logic surprise. Make columns nullable only when "missing" is a real state of the world, not just a default.

### 3.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** For the `Students` relation with attributes `(id, name, email, birthdate)`, list: (a) Two superkeys that are _not_ candidate keys. (b) Two candidate keys. (c) State which candidate key you would choose as the primary key, and why.

Assume email is unique per student and that no two students have the same id.

Hint

Candidate keys are minimal superkeys. A superkey that has a proper subset which is also a superkey is not minimal.

**Answer:** (a) `{id, name}` and `{email, birthdate}` are both superkeys but not candidate keys. The first is a superkey because `{id}` alone is a superkey, so adding `name` preserves uniqueness but isn't minimal. The second is a superkey because `{email}` alone is a superkey.

(b) `{id}` and `{email}`. Both uniquely identify, and neither contains a proper subset that does.

(c) Use `{id}` as the primary key. `id` is a surrogate integer: stable, not meant to change, cheap to index, and cheap to reference from other tables (a 4-byte integer is cheaper than a 50-byte email string). `email` becomes a `UNIQUE NOT NULL` column, preserving its uniqueness without dragging around a heavy, user-controlled value as the canonical row identifier.

**Q2.** Consider the `Enrollments` relation with primary key `(student_id, course_id)`. Suppose a student enrolls in the same course twice (say, they failed and re-took it). Does the current primary key support this?

**Answer:** No. The composite primary key `(student_id, course_id)` enforces that each (student, course) pair appears at most once. A second attempt at course 101 by student 1001 would violate the primary key constraint.

Fix: extend the primary key to include a `semester` or `term_id` attribute: `PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id, term_id)`. This lets the same student take the same course in different terms. The schema now models "enrollment of student X in course Y during term Z" rather than the simpler "student X is or was enrolled in course Y." The choice reflects what the business rule actually wants.

**Q3.** Why does SQL have `IS NULL` as a separate construct instead of letting us write `x = NULL`? What would go wrong if `= NULL` returned `TRUE` for rows where x is null?

**Answer:** Because NULL means "unknown", not "the value null". If `NULL = NULL` returned `TRUE`, we'd be asserting "two unknowns are definitely equal" — which is the opposite of what "unknown" means. Two rows with null `grade` might actually have different grades once we find out; we just don't know yet. So comparisons involving NULL must return "unknown" (SQL's third boolean value), and `IS NULL` exists as an explicit test for the "no value" state, separate from comparisons for actual value equality. This is the essence of three-valued logic.

If `= NULL` returned `TRUE`, queries like `WHERE grade = target_grade` would silently match rows where the grade is unknown whenever `target_grade` happens to be null — producing phantom matches that have nothing to do with the actual data.

**Q4.** What are the four possible referential actions on delete, and what does each mean in one sentence? For each, give a one-line example of when it's the right choice.

**Answer:**

* **RESTRICT / NO ACTION:** block the delete if any child row references the parent. _Example:_ don't let a student be deleted while they still have current enrollments.
* **CASCADE:** delete the parent and all child rows that reference it. _Example:_ delete an order and its order line items together — the line items have no meaning without the order.
* **SET NULL:** delete the parent and null out the references in child rows. _Example:_ a user deletes their account; their posts remain but show "deleted user" as author (FK set to null).
* **SET DEFAULT:** delete the parent and set the child references to a default value. _Example:_ a category is deleted; affected products are reassigned to a "Miscellaneous" category.

Choose RESTRICT for relationships where the dependent data must not be accidentally erased; CASCADE for strictly parent-owned data; SET NULL / SET DEFAULT for relationships where the reference is incidental, not essential.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Reread the orders example from Chapter 2's Exercise 2.10 Q2:
    
    
    order_id, customer_name, customer_email, item_id, item_name, item_price, quantity, ship_to_address, order_date
    

Design a normalized relational schema for this data. Include primary keys, foreign keys, `NOT NULL` where appropriate, and a reasonable referential action for each foreign key. Comment briefly on any design decisions (especially around `item_price` and `ship_to_address`).

Hint

The domain has four natural entity types (customers, items, orders, order items). Put each in its own relation.

**Answer:**
    
    
    Customers(
      id       INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      name     VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      email    VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE NOT NULL
    )
    
    Items(
      id       INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      name     VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
      current_price NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL CHECK (current_price >= 0)
    )
    
    Orders(
      id          INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      customer_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Customers(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      order_date  DATE    NOT NULL,
      ship_to_address_snapshot VARCHAR(500) NOT NULL  -- snapshotted at order time
    )
    
    OrderItems(
      order_id      INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      item_id       INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Items(id)  ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      quantity      INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
      price_snapshot NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,  -- snapshotted at order time
      PRIMARY KEY (order_id, item_id)
    )
    

**Design comments:**

* **`price_snapshot`** in `OrderItems` duplicates the item's price at the time of the order. This _looks_ like denormalization, but it's intentional: the historical price is a distinct fact from the item's current price. If the item's price changes next month, we must not revise historical order totals. This is controlled redundancy, not an anomaly source.
* **`ship_to_address_snapshot`** in `Orders` similarly snapshots the shipping address at order time. (We could alternatively model addresses as a separate table with versioning; for most workloads, a snapshot string is simpler and good enough.)
* **`ON DELETE CASCADE`** on `OrderItems.order_id`: deleting an order deletes its line items, which is appropriate — the line items have no meaning without the order.
* **`ON DELETE RESTRICT`** on `OrderItems.item_id` and `Orders.customer_id`: we don't want to silently lose order history if an item or customer is deleted. If you truly need to delete a customer for privacy/compliance reasons, the application must explicitly handle the cascade in code — not let the database silently orphan everything.

**Cross-chapter connection:** This exercise previews Chapter 6 (we will prove formally that this schema is in BCNF) and Chapter 8 (historical snapshots are a motivation for temporal tables and versioning patterns in advanced SQL).

**Q2.** A library database has two relations:
    
    
    Books(isbn, title, author, copies_available)
    Loans(book_isbn, borrower_id, due_date)
    

Identify two integrity constraints this schema needs (beyond the primary keys). Write them as SQL CHECK or REFERENCES constraints. For one of them, describe what goes wrong if you omit it.

Hint

Think about what states are invalid in the real world.

**Answer:** (At least two constraints.)

  1. **Referential integrity on loans:**
         
         Loans(
           book_isbn   VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL REFERENCES Books(isbn),
           borrower_id INTEGER     NOT NULL REFERENCES Borrowers(id),
           ...
         )
         

Without it: a loan can reference a book that doesn't exist — a phantom loan. The library's "outstanding loans" report includes books that aren't in the catalog.

  2. **Copies available is non-negative:**
         
         Books(
           ...
           copies_available INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (copies_available >= 0)
         )
         

Without it: a bug in the application's "check out" logic can decrement `copies_available` below zero — and now the library claims to have minus 2 copies of a book. The CHECK constraint makes this structurally impossible at the database level, so even a buggy check-out script cannot corrupt the count.

Additional constraints worth considering:

* `due_date > CURRENT_DATE` at insert time (usually enforced in application — CHECK constraints with `CURRENT_DATE` are tricky because the constraint is evaluated at insert/update, but the row remains valid later when `due_date` is in the past).
* A consistency constraint linking `copies_available` to the number of active loans — this is _not_ easily expressible as a simple constraint and is usually maintained by triggers or by the application's check-out/return logic.

**Q3.** Consider this SQL snippet:
    
    
    SELECT COUNT(*)     FROM Enrollments;  -- returns 80,000
    SELECT COUNT(grade) FROM Enrollments;  -- returns 62,000
    

What do these two results tell you about the data? Write a query that finds all students with at least one enrollment whose grade has not yet been assigned.

**Answer:** `COUNT(*)` counts every row in `Enrollments`, while `COUNT(grade)` counts only rows where `grade IS NOT NULL`. The difference (18,000) is the number of enrollments with a null grade — most plausibly, enrollments in the current, in-progress semester for which grades haven't been assigned yet.

Students with at least one ungraded enrollment:
    
    
    SELECT DISTINCT s.id, s.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    WHERE e.grade IS NULL;
    

**Cross-chapter connection:** The use of `IS NULL` here (not `= NULL`) is the practical application of Section 3.6's three-valued logic. In Chapter 11, we'll revisit how this query is executed — the `DISTINCT` clause often changes the plan the optimizer chooses.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The relational model insists that every tuple in a relation has the same attributes. Modern "NoSQL" document databases (MongoDB, Chapter 20) allow each document to have different fields. What does this flexibility cost, and what does it gain? When is each the right choice?

**Discussion:** The relational model's uniform-schema requirement costs flexibility: every row of `Students` must have an email attribute even if the email field is empty for some students. Document databases let each student have a different shape — some have emails, some have phone numbers, some have both, some have neither. That flexibility is real, and in early-stage systems where the schema is still being discovered, it removes friction.

What it costs:

  1. **Query-time type checking.** In a relational DB, `SELECT AVG(salary)` fails fast if `salary` turns out to be a string. In a document DB, the query may run and return garbage (or silently skip rows with missing or wrongly typed fields).
  2. **Optimizer knowledge.** The query planner (Chapter 12) relies on statistics about columns — histograms, distinct value counts, null rates. Those statistics are harder to maintain over heterogeneous documents.
  3. **Referential integrity across documents.** Document DBs typically do not enforce foreign keys, so dangling references are back.

When is document-first the right choice? When (a) the schema genuinely varies between documents (think: medical records where some patients have allergies, others have chronic conditions, others have pregnancy history), and (b) you access documents by a known key rather than by ad-hoc queries across shapes. Chapter 20 will take this up in depth.

The deeper point: "schemaless" is a misnomer. There is always a schema — the question is _who maintains it_. In a relational DB, the schema is machine-enforced and lives in the database. In a document DB, the schema lives in the application code and in everyone's head. Neither is wrong — they're different tradeoffs for different workloads.

**Q2.** Codd's original 1970 paper argued the relational model would "free" applications from depending on storage details — the so-called _data independence_ principle. Did this actually happen? What does your answer say about how evolution happens in computing?

**Discussion:** Partly. The relational model did succeed spectacularly in one form of independence: logical-physical independence. The application says `SELECT title FROM Courses WHERE id = 101`; the database decides whether to use a B-tree index, a hash index, a sequential scan, or a clustered table. The app doesn't care. When a DBA adds an index, the app's queries get faster without any code change. This is a huge win and is why relational databases dominate.

But the application is still coupled to the _logical_ schema — if `Courses` gets a column renamed or a table split in two, the app breaks. This is the form of independence the relational model _didn't_ fully deliver: logical-conceptual independence. Modern systems add views (Chapter 8) as a partial answer — an app queries a view, and the view insulates it from schema changes — but in practice, production apps are tightly coupled to the exact relational schema.

Lesson: **abstraction tends to work at the boundary you target most carefully.** Codd cared about storage details and freed apps from them. He didn't focus as much on insulating apps from schema evolution, and that aspect wasn't solved. Similar stories play out in nearly every area of computing — languages abstract away memory but not cache hierarchy, operating systems abstract away devices but not network latency, containers abstract away machines but not filesystem paths. The abstractions that stuck were the ones where the designers specifically worked hard to get that boundary right.

**Q3.** A pragmatic engineer says: "I don't need the relational model — I can represent the same data as a Python dict of lists, and it's faster to query in memory." Under what conditions is this correct? Under what conditions is it catastrophically wrong?

**Discussion:** Correct when:

* The data is small (fits in memory).
* The data is read-mostly (rare writes).
* The workload is single-process (no concurrency).
* The queries are well-known and fixed at development time.
* Crash safety is not required (the dict lives only in RAM).
* Integrity constraints are not required (the application controls all writes and is bug-free, famously a safe assumption).

Under these conditions, a dict-of-lists genuinely is competitive with a database, and often faster. This is why tools like Pandas exist — for interactive data science, "the whole dataset is a dict in memory" is the right mental model.

Catastrophically wrong when:

* Data grows past memory (you need paging and the dict can't do it).
* Concurrent writers exist (you get the Chapter 13 lost-update problem).
* Crash recovery is required (the dict vanishes on restart).
* New queries arise at runtime (you have to re-implement each one in imperative code).
* Multiple applications touch the data (now the dict is serialized through some IPC channel, and it's not really a dict anymore — it's a poorly designed database).

This thought experiment is useful because it makes explicit what a DBMS actually _gives_ you — durability, concurrency, constraint enforcement, declarative queries — beyond simple storage. When the engineer's workload doesn't need those, a dict is fine. When it needs even one of them, a dict is a disaster waiting to happen. Chapter 22 will revisit this as the "polyglot persistence" question: matching workload to tool.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter introduced the relational model as the structural cure for the anomalies of Chapter 2. A **relation** is a set of tuples with a common schema, drawn from declared domains. **Keys** (superkeys, candidate keys, primary keys) give us the mechanism to identify rows and, via **foreign keys** , to express references between relations that the DBMS enforces automatically — **referential integrity**. Along with **domain** , **`NOT NULL`** , **uniqueness** , and **`CHECK`** constraints, this machinery pushes the "rules of your data" into the schema, where the DBMS guarantees them, rather than into application code, where they decay over time.

We traced Thread A through the three anomalies of Chapter 2 and verified each becomes structurally impossible under the relational schema. This is the fundamental win of the relational model — not that it is easier to query (that comes next chapter with SQL) but that it eliminates entire classes of data-corruption bugs by _design_.

We also met **three-valued logic** — NULL as "unknown" interacting with `TRUE` and `FALSE` to produce `UNKNOWN` — and flagged it as a routine source of bugs for engineers who forget that `x = NULL` is not `TRUE`.

Looking ahead: Chapter 4 gives us SQL, the language for writing queries against these relations. Chapter 5 zooms _out_ to ER modeling — how to decide what the relations should be in the first place. Chapter 6 formalizes exactly when a schema is "good enough" via functional dependencies and normal forms — and proves that the anomalies of Chapter 2 disappear if and only if the schema is in a particular normal form (BCNF). Chapter 7 introduces relational algebra, the mathematical operations on relations that SQL is a syntactic sugaring of. And throughout, the relational model's discipline — data as sets, identity via keys, consistency via constraints — will keep paying dividends.

---

## Chapter 4: SQL Fundamentals

Chapter 3 gave us the shape of data: relations, tuples, keys, references. This chapter gives us the language for asking questions of that shape and changing it. SQL (Structured Query Language) is the _lingua franca_ every major relational database speaks — with dialect differences — and it has outlived every competing query language since 1974.

This is not a SQL reference manual. By the end of this chapter you should read and write SQL that handles 90% of real operational queries, and understand **why SQL is shaped the way it is** — the logical evaluation order, the join types, the `NULL` behavior — well enough that no future SQL construct feels arbitrary. Every example anchors in Thread A (the university registration system from Chapters 2–3):
    
    
    Students(id, name, email)
    Instructors(id, name, office, phone)
    Courses(id, title, credits, instructor_id → Instructors)
    Enrollments(student_id → Students, course_id → Courses, grade)
    

### 4.1 The Two Halves of SQL: DDL and DML

SQL has two layers. **DDL — Data Definition Language** (`CREATE TABLE`, `ALTER TABLE`, `DROP TABLE`, `CREATE INDEX`, `CREATE VIEW`) defines the schema. **DML — Data Manipulation Language** (`SELECT`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`) operates on the contents. Day-to-day work is mostly DML, but a careful `SELECT` against a sloppy schema is still wrong.

#### DDL — Creating the Schema

The full DDL for our university registration system, built bottom-up so foreign key references are satisfiable:
    
    
    CREATE TABLE Students (
      id    INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      name  VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      email VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE NOT NULL
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE Instructors (
      id     INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      name   VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,
      office VARCHAR(50),
      phone  VARCHAR(20)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE Courses (
      id            INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      title         VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,
      credits       INTEGER      NOT NULL CHECK (credits BETWEEN 1 AND 12),
      instructor_id INTEGER      NOT NULL REFERENCES Instructors(id)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE Enrollments (
      student_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Students(id) ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      course_id  INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Courses(id)  ON DELETE RESTRICT,
      grade      VARCHAR(3),
      CHECK (grade IS NULL OR grade IN
             ('A+','A','A-','B+','B','B-','C+','C','C-','D+','D','D-','F')),
      PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)
    );
    

A few things to notice in this DDL:

* `REFERENCES Instructors(id)` creates the foreign key. The default action on delete is typically RESTRICT in most databases, but we state it explicitly on `Enrollments` for clarity.
* `CHECK (credits BETWEEN 1 AND 12)` encodes the business rule ("a course is worth 1–12 credits") directly in the schema. No bug in the application can insert an 800-credit course.
* `CHECK (grade IS NULL OR grade IN (...))` uses the three-valued logic from Chapter 3.6: we permit `NULL` (ungraded) _or_ a grade from the valid set.
* `PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)` is a composite primary key. Exactly one row per (student, course) pair.

#### `ALTER TABLE` — Evolving the Schema

Schemas change over time. The registrar decides to track enrollment dates:
    
    
    ALTER TABLE Enrollments
      ADD COLUMN enrolled_on DATE;
    

Existing rows get `NULL` for `enrolled_on`. To require the column going forward, backfill first, then tighten:
    
    
    UPDATE Enrollments SET enrolled_on = '2026-01-01'
    WHERE enrolled_on IS NULL;
    
    ALTER TABLE Enrollments
      ALTER COLUMN enrolled_on SET NOT NULL;
    

Most production schema migrations are a few carefully ordered steps like this, designed to evolve the schema without breaking running applications.

#### `DROP TABLE` — The Most Dangerous SQL
    
    
    DROP TABLE Enrollments;
    

Deletes the table and all its data, instantly and irreversibly, with no confirmation. Production databases should guard `DROP TABLE` behind a migration framework, permissions, or review process — it cannot be undone without a backup, and careless ones have ended careers.

### 4.2 `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`: Writing Data

#### `INSERT`

Single row, multi-row, and `INSERT ... SELECT`:
    
    
    INSERT INTO Students (id, name, email)
    VALUES (1001, 'Alice Wong', 'alice.wong@uni.edu');
    
    
    
    INSERT INTO Enrollments (student_id, course_id, grade) VALUES
      (1001, 101, 'A'),
      (1001, 102, 'B+'),
      (1002, 101, 'A-'),
      (1002, 103, 'B'),
      (1003, 102, 'A');
    
    
    
    INSERT INTO ArchivedEnrollments (student_id, course_id, grade)
    SELECT student_id, course_id, grade
    FROM Enrollments
    WHERE course_id IN (
      SELECT id FROM Courses WHERE credits = 0 -- discontinued courses
    );
    

`INSERT ... SELECT` is how bulk data typically moves between tables — one server-side statement, faster and safer than round-tripping rows through the client.

#### `UPDATE`
    
    
    UPDATE Instructors
    SET name = 'Dr. Soo-min Park'
    WHERE id = 42;
    

A `WHERE` clause is mandatory in any sane production environment. `UPDATE Enrollments SET grade = 'A';` assigns everyone an A — exactly what SQL was told to do. PostgreSQL's `safe-updates` mode rejects unfiltered `UPDATE`/`DELETE` at the client level; use it.

#### `DELETE`
    
    
    DELETE FROM Enrollments
    WHERE student_id = 1001 AND course_id = 102;
    

Same caution: a `DELETE` without `WHERE` removes every row in the table.

#### The Consequences of Missing `WHERE`

A real incident pattern: an engineer means to run `DELETE FROM Customers WHERE id = 47;`, gets distracted mid-statement, and hits Enter at the first `;` — wiping the entire table. If the session was in auto-commit (the default), there is no undo, only backup restore.

#### Common Misconception

"`ROLLBACK` will save me" — only if you were in an explicit transaction. Most SQL clients are in auto-commit mode by default, meaning each statement is its own transaction that commits immediately. To have `ROLLBACK` available, you must start a transaction explicitly: `BEGIN;` before your risky statement. (We'll go much deeper into transactions in Chapter 13; for now, the practical advice is: always wrap dangerous statements in `BEGIN ... COMMIT` blocks.)

### 4.3 `SELECT`: The Heart of SQL

`SELECT` is where you spend most of your SQL hours. Its structure, in full:
    
    
    SELECT   [DISTINCT] select_expressions
    FROM     table_expression
    WHERE    filter_expression
    GROUP BY grouping_expressions
    HAVING   group_filter_expression
    ORDER BY ordering_expressions
    LIMIT    n
    OFFSET   m
    

You rarely use all seven at once, but knowing they exist — and their evaluation order — is the foundation of SQL that doesn't silently do the wrong thing.

#### The Logical Evaluation Order

SQL reads top-to-bottom in the text, but _evaluates_ in a different order. Roughly:

  1. **FROM** — decide which tables and joins provide the row universe.
  2. **WHERE** — filter the rows.
  3. **GROUP BY** — partition surviving rows into groups.
  4. **HAVING** — filter groups.
  5. **SELECT** — compute output columns for each row or group.
  6. **DISTINCT** — remove duplicate output rows.
  7. **ORDER BY** — sort the output.
  8. **LIMIT / OFFSET** — trim the output.

This is a _logical_ order; the real query plan can look wildly different (Chapter 11). But the logical order is what determines _correctness_. For example:

* You cannot use a `SELECT`-list alias in a `WHERE` clause, because `WHERE` is evaluated before `SELECT` computes the alias.
* You cannot use a non-aggregated column in `SELECT` alongside a `GROUP BY` unless the column is in the `GROUP BY` — because by then the row identity is lost.
* `ORDER BY` _can_ use `SELECT`-list aliases, because it is evaluated after `SELECT`.

#### `FROM` and Simple `SELECT`
    
    
    SELECT id, name, email FROM Students;
    SELECT * FROM Students;
    

`*` means "all columns" — handy in ad-hoc queries, dangerous in production: a schema change can break application code or deliver unexpected columns. Name columns explicitly in any query that outlives your terminal.

#### `WHERE`: Filtering
    
    
    SELECT name FROM Students WHERE id = 1001;
    SELECT name FROM Students WHERE name LIKE 'A%';
    SELECT * FROM Courses WHERE credits > 3 AND credits <= 6;
    SELECT * FROM Enrollments WHERE grade IS NULL;
    

Four patterns: exact match (`= 1001`), pattern (`LIKE` with `_` for one character, `%` for any sequence), range (combine comparisons with `AND`/`OR`), and the null test (`IS NULL`/`IS NOT NULL`, never `= NULL`).

#### `ORDER BY` and `LIMIT`
    
    
    SELECT name, email FROM Students
    ORDER BY name ASC
    LIMIT 10;
    

`ASC` is default; `DESC` reverses. Order by multiple columns (`ORDER BY last_name, first_name`) to break ties on the first by the second. `LIMIT` without `ORDER BY` gives "some ten rows, who knows which" — relations are sets with no inherent order.
    
    
    SELECT name FROM Students ORDER BY id LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40;
    

This is the third page of 20. `OFFSET` pagination scales poorly (the engine still scans and discards the first `OFFSET` rows); Chapter 8 discusses keyset pagination as an alternative.

### 4.4 Joins

Joins are where SQL earns its keep. The relational model _relies_ on foreign keys for structure (Chapter 3), and joins are the mechanism for following those references.

Our Thread A question: "What courses is Alice Wong enrolled in?"
    
    
    SELECT c.title, c.credits
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses     c ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE s.name = 'Alice Wong';
    

We followed two foreign key references: `Enrollments.student_id → Students.id`, and `Enrollments.course_id → Courses.id`. Three tables joined produces rows that have _all_ of Alice's enrollments _and_ the course information for each.

#### The Five Join Types

SQL has five join types. They differ in how they handle rows that _don't_ match.

Let's set up two sample tables to make the differences visible:

`A` has rows: `(1), (2), (3)` `B` has rows: `(2), (3), (4)`

Join type | Semantics | Result for `A JOIN B ON A.x = B.y`  
---|---|---  
`INNER JOIN` | Only rows where the join condition holds | `(2,2), (3,3)`  
`LEFT OUTER JOIN` | All rows from A; unmatched rows of A get NULLs for B | `(1,NULL), (2,2), (3,3)`  
`RIGHT OUTER JOIN` | All rows from B; unmatched rows of B get NULLs for A | `(2,2), (3,3), (NULL,4)`  
`FULL OUTER JOIN` | All rows from both; unmatched get NULLs | `(1,NULL), (2,2), (3,3), (NULL,4)`  
`CROSS JOIN` | Cartesian product of A and B; no condition | `(1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (2,2), (2,3), (2,4), ...` (9 rows)  
  
In SQL syntax:
    
    
    SELECT * FROM A INNER JOIN B ON A.x = B.y;
    SELECT * FROM A LEFT JOIN  B ON A.x = B.y;  -- "OUTER" is optional
    SELECT * FROM A FULL JOIN  B ON A.x = B.y;
    SELECT * FROM A CROSS JOIN B;
    

`INNER` and `OUTER` are often omitted: `JOIN` on its own means `INNER JOIN`, and `LEFT JOIN` means `LEFT OUTER JOIN`. The formal names help in mentally distinguishing them.

#### When to Use Each

* **INNER JOIN** — the default choice. "Give me rows where the relationship exists." "Find students _who have_ enrollments."
* **LEFT JOIN** — "Give me all rows from the left side, whether or not they have matches." "Find _all_ students, with their enrollments if any — including students with zero enrollments." Typical for "list X with its related Y" queries where some X's might have no Y.
* **RIGHT JOIN** — the mirror of LEFT. In practice rarely used, because you can always write it as a LEFT JOIN with the table order swapped. Some style guides ban `RIGHT JOIN` outright for consistency.
* **FULL OUTER JOIN** — "Give me everything, matched or not." Used in reconciliation queries: comparing two tables that should match and finding discrepancies.
* **CROSS JOIN** — every row of A with every row of B. Used deliberately for generating combinations (e.g., every student × every course to produce an "eligible enrollment matrix"). Usually followed by a `WHERE` that filters — at which point it is logically equivalent to an INNER JOIN. Prefer explicit `CROSS JOIN` only when that's genuinely what you mean.

> ### Warning: WHERE on the Right Side of a LEFT JOIN Silently Kills the Outer Join
> 
> This is one of the most common and most silent SQL bugs. Once you see it, you will spot it everywhere.
>     
>     
>     -- WRONG: this silently becomes an INNER JOIN!
>     SELECT s.name, e.course_id
>     FROM Students s
>     LEFT JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
>     WHERE e.course_id > 100;   -- ← filters out NULL rows from the left join
>     
> 
> Students with no enrollments have `e.course_id = NULL`. The condition `NULL > 100` evaluates to `UNKNOWN` in SQL's three-valued logic, and `WHERE` removes any row that is not `TRUE` — so all the unmatched students disappear. The `LEFT JOIN` has been silently downgraded to an `INNER JOIN`.
> 
> **Fix: move the right-side filter into the`ON` clause.**
>     
>     
>     SELECT s.name, e.course_id
>     FROM Students s
>     LEFT JOIN Enrollments e
>       ON s.id = e.student_id AND e.course_id > 100;
>     
> 
> Now the filter applies only to matching rows; unmatched students still appear with `NULL` course_id. The rule: **filters on the _preserved_ (left) side go in `WHERE`; filters on the _nullable_ (right) side go in `ON`.**

#### Thread A Example: Students With and Without Enrollments
    
    
    SELECT s.name, COUNT(e.course_id) AS n_enrollments
    FROM Students s
    LEFT JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    GROUP BY s.id, s.name
    ORDER BY n_enrollments DESC;
    

If we had used `INNER JOIN`, we would silently omit students with zero enrollments. The `LEFT JOIN` preserves them with `n_enrollments = 0`. This is a common off-by-one-N bug: junior SQL authors write `INNER JOIN` and wonder why their "students ranked by enrollment count" report doesn't include Alice, who hasn't enrolled in anything yet.

#### Self-Joins

A self-join is a table joined with itself — useful when a single table encodes relationships among its own rows. Given `Employees(id, name, manager_id)`, to pair employees with managers:
    
    
    SELECT e.name AS employee, m.name AS manager
    FROM Employees e
    LEFT JOIN Employees m ON e.manager_id = m.id;
    

`LEFT JOIN` handles the CEO (whose `manager_id` is NULL). Aliases (`e`, `m`) are required — both sides are the same table, so they need distinct names.

### 4.5 `GROUP BY` and Aggregation

Aggregates summarize multiple rows into a single value. The five fundamental aggregates:

* `COUNT(*)` — count of rows.
* `COUNT(expr)` — count of rows where `expr` is not null.
* `SUM(expr)` — sum of non-null values.
* `AVG(expr)` — average of non-null values.
* `MIN(expr)` / `MAX(expr)` — extremes of non-null values.

Plus a few less common but important ones:

* `COUNT(DISTINCT expr)` — number of distinct non-null values.
* `STRING_AGG(expr, separator)` / `ARRAY_AGG(expr)` — concatenate or collect into a list.

Across a whole table:
    
    
    SELECT COUNT(*), AVG(credits), MAX(credits)
    FROM Courses;
    -- 500, 3.42, 6
    

Per group:
    
    
    SELECT instructor_id, COUNT(*), AVG(credits)
    FROM Courses
    GROUP BY instructor_id;
    

One row per distinct `instructor_id`, each row summarizing that instructor's courses.

#### The `GROUP BY` Rule

In a query with `GROUP BY`, every column in the `SELECT` list must be either:

* a column listed in the `GROUP BY`, or
* inside an aggregate function.

Why? Because after grouping, each row in the output corresponds to _many_ input rows. "Select `title`" is meaningless — which of the many titles in the group? "Select `COUNT(title)`" or "select `MAX(title)`" makes sense — the aggregate collapses them into one value.

Most databases reject queries that violate this rule. Historical MySQL did not — it silently picked a random row's value, producing notorious bugs; modern MySQL enforces it by default.

#### `HAVING`: Filtering Groups

`WHERE` filters rows _before_ grouping; `HAVING` filters groups _after_.
    
    
    -- Instructors with at least 5 courses and average credits >= 3
    SELECT instructor_id, COUNT(*) AS n_courses, AVG(credits) AS avg_credits
    FROM Courses
    GROUP BY instructor_id
    HAVING COUNT(*) >= 5 AND AVG(credits) >= 3;
    

`HAVING` can reference aggregates; `WHERE` cannot, because aggregates aren't computed yet when `WHERE` runs. The two clauses genuinely differ in what they see — compare:
    
    
    -- "Instructors who teach at least one course worth >= 4 credits"
    -- (Filter individual rows: keep only 4+ credit rows; then count groups)
    SELECT instructor_id, COUNT(*)
    FROM Courses
    WHERE credits >= 4
    GROUP BY instructor_id;
    
    -- "Instructors whose AVERAGE credits is >= 4"
    -- (Group first; then filter groups by their aggregate)
    SELECT instructor_id, AVG(credits)
    FROM Courses
    GROUP BY instructor_id
    HAVING AVG(credits) >= 4;
    

The two queries answer genuinely different questions; picking the right one is a skill that comes from catching the bug a few times.

#### Common Misconception

Engineers often write:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, e.course_id
    FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    GROUP BY s.name;
    

and get an error or (on loose MySQL) nondeterministic output. The question is ambiguous — "for each name, what course_id?" — and SQL correctly refuses to guess. If you want "one course_id per name", you must tell the query _which_ one: `MAX(e.course_id)`, `MIN(e.course_id)`, or aggregate in some other way. This rule forces SQL authors to be explicit about what a "group" means.

### 4.6 `DISTINCT` and Bag vs Set Semantics

Chapter 3 noted that strict relations are sets but SQL uses bag semantics (duplicates permitted). `DISTINCT` collapses them:
    
    
    SELECT student_id FROM Enrollments;           -- 1001 may appear 8 times
    SELECT DISTINCT student_id FROM Enrollments;  -- each id at most once
    

`DISTINCT` is not free — it forces a sort or hash to detect duplicates. When you only need "does a match exist?", `EXISTS` (Chapter 8) is usually faster:
    
    
    SELECT s.id, s.name FROM Students s
    WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e WHERE e.student_id = s.id);
    

### 4.7 Thread A Worked Example: "Who's in Alice's Database Class?"

Let's walk through a realistic multi-join query, translating from English to SQL step by step.

English question: "List the names and emails of all students enrolled in Dr. Chen's Introduction to Databases course, ordered by last name."

Step 1: Which tables do I need? Students (for names and emails), Enrollments (to know who's enrolled), Courses (to identify the specific course), Instructors (to filter by Dr. Chen).

Step 2: How do the tables connect? Students ↔ Enrollments on student_id; Enrollments ↔ Courses on course_id; Courses ↔ Instructors on instructor_id.

Step 3: What are the filters? `Instructors.name = 'Dr. Chen'` and `Courses.title = 'Introduction to Databases'`.

Step 4: What's the output? `Students.name, Students.email`.

Step 5: What's the order? Last name — but the schema stores full name. For simplicity, assume we can sort the whole `name` string alphabetically (real systems with first/last names would split the column).

Writing it:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, s.email
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses     c ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE i.name = 'Dr. Chen'
      AND c.title = 'Introduction to Databases'
    ORDER BY s.name;
    

This is the archetypal operational query: four tables joined, two filters, simple ordering. You will write hundreds like it. Notice the discipline — every table has an alias, every join has an explicit `ON`, every column is qualified with its table alias — which makes the query readable and maintainable.

**Thread A milestone reached:** we can now answer the "what courses is Alice enrolled in?" question (and many cousins) with one SQL statement. Remember in Chapter 2 this took a custom Python script per question. That is the SQL payoff.

**Thread B preview:** the same joins appear in the 20-minute query we'll meet in Chapter 7 — `SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e JOIN Courses c JOIN Instructors i WHERE s.year = 4`. The _query_ is similar; what changes in later chapters is the _physical execution_ and its performance implications. At 100,000 rows without indexes, this exact join structure takes 20 minutes.

### 4.8 Transactions: A First Taste

Chapter 13 is the full transactions chapter; here's the preview you need before then. A **transaction** groups SQL statements into an all-or-nothing unit — either every statement takes effect, or none does.
    
    
    BEGIN;
      UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1;
      UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = 2;
    COMMIT;
    

If the server crashes between the two updates, neither is visible after recovery. If both succeed and you `COMMIT`, both become visible atomically. You also get rollback on demand:
    
    
    BEGIN;
      DELETE FROM Enrollments WHERE grade = 'F';
      -- wait, did I mean that? Let me check.
      SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Enrollments WHERE grade = 'F';
      -- ah, this would delete 4,000 rows, not what I intended.
    ROLLBACK;
    

The `DELETE` has been "undone" — never visible to other sessions, and now fully reverted.

**Savepoints** give finer-grained rollback inside a transaction: `SAVEPOINT before_x;` marks a point, and `ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT before_x;` undoes only the statements after it while keeping earlier work alive until the final `COMMIT`.

Chapter 13 goes far deeper — isolation, concurrency, ACID. The takeaway for now is that the transaction is the atomic unit, and auto-commit mode has silently destroyed enormous amounts of production data.

### 4.9 Indexes: A First Taste

Indexes get their own chapter (10), but you need a working mental model now. An **index** is a separate data structure the DBMS maintains on a column (or set of columns) to speed up lookups in `WHERE` clauses and joins.
    
    
    CREATE INDEX idx_enrollments_student ON Enrollments(student_id);
    

Now `WHERE student_id = 1001` uses the index instead of scanning all of `Enrollments`. On 80,000 rows this changes 625 page reads (full scan, Chapter 2) into about 4 page reads (B+ tree traversal, Chapter 10).

> **A note on the numbers you will see across chapters.** Thread A mentions several page-read figures; they are not inconsistent, they refer to _different queries_ :
> 
>   * **625 pages** (Ch 2, §2.1): scanning all Enrollments — the naive single-table scan.
>   * **4 pages** (this section): a single indexed lookup of one student's enrollments (the B+ tree descent only).
>   * **8,200 pages** (Ch 9, §9.4): the _full_ "find Alice's courses with titles and instructor names" query with _no_ indexes — a multi-table nested-loop with re-scanning Enrollments for every Student row and so on.
>   * **32 pages** (Ch 10, §10.9): the same full multi-table query _with_ indexes on `Students(name)`, `Enrollments(student_id)`, and the primary keys of `Courses` and `Instructors`.
> 

> 
> Each number is the cost of a different question at a different point in the design. Keep the scope straight when comparing.

Two caveats: indexes are not free — every one slows `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` because it must be maintained alongside the table. And not every query can use an index (e.g. `WHERE email LIKE '%@uni.edu'` cannot use an index on `email`; Chapter 10 covers the rules). Rule of thumb: index every foreign key and every column frequently used in `WHERE` or `ORDER BY`; Chapter 10 refines this.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **DDL rigidity trades schema flexibility for machine-checked structure.** Declaring a column `INTEGER NOT NULL` means a future program cannot insert `"n/a"`; it also means adding a new required attribute is a migration, not a dictionary update. Rigid types are why rows stay sane years later.
* **Declarative SQL trades fine-grained control for optimizer freedom.** You describe _what_ , not _how_ ; the engine picks scan order, join algorithm, and index usage. You lose the ability to hand-tune every plan, and gain plans that improve as statistics and hardware change without code edits.
* **Each index trades write throughput for read speed.** Every `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, or `DELETE` must maintain every index on the table. A wisely chosen index on a foreign key pays for itself on reads; a speculative index on a rarely queried column silently taxes every write.
* **Transaction scope (BEGIN/COMMIT boundaries) trades concurrency for atomicity.** A short transaction holds fewer locks but may split a logical operation across commits; a long one guarantees all-or-nothing but starves other writers. The atomic unit must be as small as correctness allows, not smaller.
* **`DISTINCT` and `GROUP BY` trade CPU work (usually a sort or hash) for duplicate-free output.** When the duplicates are real signal (for `AVG`, `COUNT`) keep them; when they are accidental from a join, `DISTINCT` is the right fix — but recognize you just paid for a sort.
* **Outer joins trade plan simplicity for null-preserving output.** A `LEFT JOIN ... WHERE x IS NULL` is how "rows with no match" is expressed, but it also means every later predicate on the right-hand columns must be null-aware. Many subtle bugs live in this conversion.

### 4.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Rewrite each of these in SQL: (a) Count the number of courses that have exactly 4 credits. (b) Find the student with the largest id number. (c) Find all enrollments with no grade assigned. (d) Find all students whose name starts with 'B' or 'C'.

Hint

(c) requires the null-aware test. (d) either two conditions joined by OR or a pattern.

**Answer:** (a) `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Courses WHERE credits = 4;` (b) `SELECT * FROM Students ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 1;` (c) `SELECT * FROM Enrollments WHERE grade IS NULL;` (d) `SELECT * FROM Students WHERE name LIKE 'B%' OR name LIKE 'C%';` — or equivalently, `WHERE substring(name, 1, 1) IN ('B', 'C');`. Both are correct; the first often uses an index, the second rarely does.

**Q2.** What is the difference between these two queries? Which one is likely to return more rows?
    
    
    -- Query A
    SELECT s.name FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id;
    
    -- Query B
    SELECT s.name FROM Students s LEFT JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id;
    

Hint

What happens to students with zero enrollments in each case?

**Answer:** Query A returns one row per enrollment — so a student with five enrollments appears five times, and students with zero enrollments don't appear at all. Query B returns one row per enrollment _plus_ one row per enrollmentless student (with null `e`-columns). Query B is at least as big as Query A — strictly larger if there are any students with zero enrollments.

Neither is "right" in general — it depends on the question. For "names of students who are enrolled in something" use DISTINCT on Query A. For "names of students, enrolled or not" use Query B. For "count of enrollments per student", you almost certainly want Query B wrapped in a `GROUP BY + COUNT`.

**Q3.** Why does this query fail?
    
    
    SELECT student_id, course_id, COUNT(*)
    FROM Enrollments
    GROUP BY student_id;
    

**Answer:** `course_id` is neither in the `GROUP BY` nor inside an aggregate. After grouping by `student_id` alone, each row of the output corresponds to one student — with many courses. SQL cannot tell which course_id to return, so it rejects the query.

Fixes, depending on intent:

* If you want to count enrollments per student: drop `course_id`. `SELECT student_id, COUNT(*) FROM Enrollments GROUP BY student_id;`
* If you want counts grouped by (student, course): add `course_id` to the `GROUP BY`. (This is equivalent to the enrollments table itself, since (student_id, course_id) is the primary key — so `COUNT(*)` is always 1.)
* If you want one _representative_ course_id per student: `SELECT student_id, MAX(course_id), COUNT(*) FROM Enrollments GROUP BY student_id;`

**Q4.** In the `Orders` schema from Chapter 3's Exercise 3.9 Q1:
    
    
    Customers, Items, Orders, OrderItems
    

Write the SQL to find "for each customer, the total value of their orders, sorted by total descending". Total value = sum of (price_snapshot × quantity) across their order items.

**Answer:**
    
    
    SELECT c.id, c.name, SUM(oi.price_snapshot * oi.quantity) AS total_value
    FROM Customers c
    JOIN Orders o     ON o.customer_id = c.id
    JOIN OrderItems oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
    GROUP BY c.id, c.name
    ORDER BY total_value DESC;
    

Note that we're using an inner join, so customers with no orders don't appear. If we wanted them too (with `total_value = 0`), use a `LEFT JOIN` from `Customers` and `COALESCE(SUM(...), 0)` to replace null with 0.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Write SQL for: "For each course, the average grade (as a GPA on a 4.0 scale) among students who have been graded. Ignore ungraded enrollments. Include only courses with at least 5 graded enrollments."

Use this grade-to-GPA mapping (you may hardcode it in a `CASE` expression):
    
    
    A+=4.0, A=4.0, A-=3.7, B+=3.3, B=3.0, B-=2.7,
    C+=2.3, C=2.0, C-=1.7, D+=1.3, D=1.0, D-=0.7, F=0.0
    

Hint

Use `CASE` inside `AVG`. Use `HAVING` for the "at least 5" filter.

**Answer:**
    
    
    SELECT
      c.id,
      c.title,
      AVG(CASE e.grade
            WHEN 'A+' THEN 4.0 WHEN 'A'  THEN 4.0 WHEN 'A-' THEN 3.7
            WHEN 'B+' THEN 3.3 WHEN 'B'  THEN 3.0 WHEN 'B-' THEN 2.7
            WHEN 'C+' THEN 2.3 WHEN 'C'  THEN 2.0 WHEN 'C-' THEN 1.7
            WHEN 'D+' THEN 1.3 WHEN 'D'  THEN 1.0 WHEN 'D-' THEN 0.7
            WHEN 'F'  THEN 0.0
          END) AS avg_gpa,
      COUNT(e.grade) AS n_graded
    FROM Courses c
    JOIN Enrollments e ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE e.grade IS NOT NULL
    GROUP BY c.id, c.title
    HAVING COUNT(e.grade) >= 5
    ORDER BY avg_gpa DESC;
    

Notes:

* `AVG` automatically skips NULL values, so even without the explicit `WHERE e.grade IS NOT NULL`, ungraded enrollments wouldn't contribute to the average. But the `WHERE` also keeps them out of `COUNT(e.grade)` — actually `COUNT(e.grade)` already skips nulls, so the `WHERE` is optional here. Including it is harmless and clarifies intent.
* The `CASE` expression is a common way to inline a lookup table when the lookup is small. For large lookups, put the mapping in a real table (e.g., `GradePoints(grade, points)`) and `JOIN`.
* A real system would extract the GPA mapping into a view or a proper table so it's not duplicated across queries. Chapter 8 covers views.

**Cross-chapter connection:** this query uses three Thread A tables. Without indexes, at scale (80,000 enrollments, 500 courses), the query does a hash aggregation over the join result — roughly 80,000 hash probes plus 500 output groups. Chapter 11 will reveal the execution plan.

**Q2.** The university wants a roster: "For Dr. Chen's course 'Introduction to Databases', list all enrolled students with their grade (or `(pending)` if the grade is null). Order by student name."

Hint

`COALESCE` replaces nulls with a given value.

**Answer:**
    
    
    SELECT s.name, s.email, COALESCE(e.grade, '(pending)') AS grade
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses     c ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE i.name = 'Dr. Chen'
      AND c.title = 'Introduction to Databases'
    ORDER BY s.name;
    

This is almost identical to the example in Section 4.7 — the only changes are an extra output column with `COALESCE` for the grade, and the enrollment join now provides the grade. `COALESCE(x, y, z)` returns the first of `x, y, z` that is not null; with two arguments it's "x if not null, else y."

**Q3.** Consider this insert of a batch of new enrollments:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    INSERT INTO Enrollments (student_id, course_id, grade) VALUES
      (1001, 201, NULL),
      (1002, 201, NULL),
      (1003, 201, NULL),
      (1099, 201, NULL),  -- student 1099 does not exist
      (1005, 201, NULL);
    COMMIT;
    

What happens? Which rows end up in the table? If we change `BEGIN`/`COMMIT` to auto-commit (remove them), does the answer change?

Hint

The foreign key constraint on `student_id` is triggered when the 1099 row is inserted. What happens to the rest of the batch?

**Answer:** The foreign key constraint on `Enrollments.student_id` rejects the 1099 row. In most SQL databases, a multi-row `INSERT` is a _single statement_ , so it either succeeds entirely or fails entirely — there's no partial result. The 1099 row fails, the whole `INSERT` fails, and the other four rows are not inserted.

Because we're in a `BEGIN ... COMMIT` transaction, the failure rolls back the whole transaction — no rows are added. If we then `COMMIT`, the commit is a no-op (or some clients error on "commit after failed statement"; PostgreSQL errors).

If we remove `BEGIN ... COMMIT` and run in auto-commit mode: each statement is its own transaction, so the `INSERT` still fails atomically on the 1099 row, and still no rows are inserted.

The answer would change if we issued five _separate_ `INSERT` statements instead of one batch:
    
    
    INSERT INTO Enrollments VALUES (1001, 201, NULL);
    INSERT INTO Enrollments VALUES (1002, 201, NULL);
    INSERT INTO Enrollments VALUES (1003, 201, NULL);
    INSERT INTO Enrollments VALUES (1099, 201, NULL);  -- fails
    INSERT INTO Enrollments VALUES (1005, 201, NULL);
    

In auto-commit mode, the first three succeed, the fourth fails, the fifth succeeds. Now the `Enrollments` table has four new rows.

In an explicit transaction, if we handle the error (catch it, continue), the same pattern applies — but on `COMMIT`, we commit those four rows. If we let the error propagate and end with `ROLLBACK`, no rows commit.

The deeper point: transaction boundaries matter, and "insert this batch atomically" vs "insert these one at a time" is a real design decision. Chapter 13 will make the atomicity story precise.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Why does SQL's `ORDER BY` clause evaluate _after_ `SELECT`, but `GROUP BY` evaluates _before_? What would break if the order were swapped?

**Discussion:** `GROUP BY` must operate on source data — it's partitioning rows before they are summarized. If `GROUP BY` ran after `SELECT`, the `SELECT` would have already collapsed the rows (with aggregates) or reshaped them in ways that obscure the grouping keys. You can't group aggregates by an aggregate key that no longer exists.

`ORDER BY`, in contrast, is about presentation — sorting the _output_. It's perfectly sensible to say "compute the output rows, then sort them by the computed values." This is why `ORDER BY` can use `SELECT`-list aliases (`ORDER BY avg_gpa DESC`) but `WHERE` and `GROUP BY` cannot — those run before aliases exist.

If the orders were swapped, you'd get bizarre failures. `ORDER BY avg_gpa DESC` followed by `SELECT AVG(grade) AS avg_gpa` would fail because `avg_gpa` hasn't been computed when `ORDER BY` runs. SQL's logical evaluation order is a carefully designed pipeline where each stage can reference only stages before it.

This is also why `WHERE` cannot reference aggregates and `HAVING` can: `WHERE` runs before aggregation, so aggregates don't exist yet; `HAVING` runs after aggregation, so they do. The constraint is not arbitrary — it's a direct consequence of the pipeline shape.

**Q2.** Consider "find students with no enrollments." There are at least three ways to write this in SQL. List them, and discuss which is likely most performant and why.

**Discussion:** Three common forms:

  1. **LEFT JOIN and check for NULL:**
         
         SELECT s.id, s.name FROM Students s
         LEFT JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
         WHERE e.student_id IS NULL;
         

  2. **NOT IN:**
         
         SELECT id, name FROM Students
         WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT student_id FROM Enrollments);
         

  3. **NOT EXISTS:**
         
         SELECT id, name FROM Students s
         WHERE NOT EXISTS (
           SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e WHERE e.student_id = s.id
         );
         

All three produce the same result (in spirit; see caveat below). Which is fastest depends on the optimizer, but typical behavior:

* **LEFT JOIN anti-join** and **NOT EXISTS** both tend to be optimized into an _anti-join_ — a join algorithm specifically designed to return left rows with no match on the right. These are usually tied for best performance.
* **NOT IN** is the most dangerous. If `Enrollments.student_id` can ever be NULL, `NOT IN` silently returns _zero rows_ (because `x NOT IN (NULL)` evaluates to `UNKNOWN`, filtered out). This is a subtle correctness bug. Performance-wise, `NOT IN` forces the optimizer to materialize the inner set and sometimes can't use an anti-join strategy.

Recommended default: `NOT EXISTS`. It is always correct under nulls, is usually optimized well, and reads naturally ("keep s only if no matching e exists").

**Cross-chapter connection:** Chapter 8 will cover `EXISTS`/`NOT EXISTS` in depth and explain exactly when the optimizer converts them to anti-joins. Chapter 11 covers the anti-join algorithm itself.

**Q3.** A senior engineer says: "Always write your SQL to take advantage of the query optimizer — you don't need to understand how the query executes." A junior engineer replies: "But if I don't understand the execution, how do I know when the optimizer is going to fail me?" Who's right, and what does each perspective capture?

**Discussion:** Both are right, partially.

The senior engineer's point is _mostly_ true: the optimizer in a mature DB (PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server, Oracle) is genuinely sophisticated — it handles most queries well enough that hand-tuning is counterproductive. Spending your career hand-writing execution plans is a waste; the optimizer does it better, and it adjusts as data sizes change. Premature optimization of query execution is a real anti-pattern.

The junior engineer's point is true for the _hard_ 5% of queries. When a query is slow, the optimizer has picked a plan. To fix it, you need to understand (a) what plan it picked (`EXPLAIN ANALYZE`), (b) why the plan is bad (missing index? stale statistics? incorrect join order?), and (c) how to nudge it toward a better plan (add an index, rewrite the query in an equivalent form, update statistics, add a hint).

The resolution: **write SQL declaratively, but learn to read execution plans.** In day-to-day work you treat the database as a black box ("tell it what you want"). When something is slow, you open the box and diagnose. The skill is knowing when to look — mostly, not; occasionally, absolutely.

This is the central philosophy of Chapters 11 and 12: the optimizer is powerful _and_ imperfect, and real engineers need to understand both sides.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter turned the relational model of Chapter 3 into a working language. **DDL** defines the schema, **DML** operates on the data, and `SELECT`'s logical evaluation order (FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → DISTINCT → ORDER BY → LIMIT) determines correctness regardless of how the engine actually executes the query.

**Joins** follow foreign keys; the five types (INNER, LEFT/RIGHT/FULL OUTER, CROSS) control how unmatched rows are handled.
