The most common bug is silently downgrading a LEFT JOIN to an INNER JOIN by putting a right-side filter in `WHERE` instead of `ON`. **GROUP BY / HAVING** is the aggregation mechanism: every non-aggregated `SELECT` column must appear in `GROUP BY`, and `HAVING` filters groups while `WHERE` filters rows. **NULL** permeates every query — handle it explicitly with `IS NULL` and `COALESCE`, because `=` and `!=` silently drop null rows.

We previewed **transactions** (Chapter 13) and **indexes** (Chapter 10) enough that the rest of the book's SQL examples make sense. Thread A milestone: "what courses is Alice enrolled in?" is now one SQL statement instead of a Python script per question. Thread B foreshadowed: the same joins will soon become a "20-minute query" whose cost Chapters 10–12 explain.

Next chapter: good SQL needs good _schemas_. Chapter 5 introduces Entity-Relationship (ER) modeling — deciding what the relations should be _before_ we write DDL. Chapter 6 formalizes when a schema is structurally safe via functional dependencies and normal forms.

---

## Chapter 5: Entity-Relationship Modeling

Chapter 4 taught us the mechanics of writing SQL against an existing schema. But where did the schema come from? We have been acting as though the `Students`, `Courses`, `Instructors`, `Enrollments` decomposition was obvious. It wasn't — someone designed it, probably by thinking before they wrote any DDL.

This chapter is about that step back: how do you design a schema _before_ you type `CREATE TABLE`? The answer, in the mainstream relational tradition, is **Entity-Relationship (ER) modeling**. ER modeling is a way of thinking — and drawing — that separates "what kinds of things are in my domain and how do they relate?" from "what tables should I create?". The first question is a domain-modeling question. The second is a schema-mapping question. Running them together is a classic source of poor schema design.

Thread A reappears in a new role. In Chapters 2 and 3 we _reverse-engineered_ the registration system — we had a CSV, we observed the anomalies, we rebuilt the tables. In this chapter we will _forward-engineer_ it: start from the registrar's description in plain English, produce an ER model, and derive the relational schema mechanically. If everything worked, we should produce something very close to the Chapter 3 schema. The goal is to see the process, not just the result.

### 5.1 Why Model First?

Imagine you are given this description: "Build a system for the university registrar. Track students, their enrollments, the courses, the instructors, the departments, the prerequisites, and the transfer credits."

If you jump straight to DDL, within 20 minutes you will have created eight tables — and halfway through you will realize three of them overlap awkwardly, one of them has fields that should be in a separate table, and you forgot a relationship altogether. You will retract and redo. Multiple times. This is how most bad schemas arise: the schema accretes incrementally, without a design phase, and ends up reflecting the order in which the developer happened to think of things.

ER modeling intervenes before DDL. You identify _entities_ (the "things" in the domain), _relationships_ between them, and _attributes_ of each. You decide cardinalities ("a student can take many courses; a course can have many students"). You decide what has independent existence and what doesn't. Then — and only then — do you mechanically map the ER model to tables, applying a set of standard rules.

This sounds like a lot of process for a simple schema. In practice, for a small system, ER modeling might take 15 minutes on a whiteboard and save you days of refactoring later. For a large system (the Chapter 23 capstone scenario, for example), ER modeling is the difference between a schema you can hand to other teams and one you must constantly defend.

### 5.2 Entities

> ### Definition: Entity

An **entity** is a distinguishable thing in the domain that has independent existence — real-world (a student, a book) or abstract (an order, a reservation, a transaction). An **entity type** (also called an entity set) is a category of similar entities; an **entity instance** is one specific entity of that type.

In plain language, an entity is a noun. Students are entities. Courses are entities. Enrollments are entities too — even though "enrollment" sounds like a relationship, each specific enrollment (Alice's enrollment in course 101) has its own independent identity (a grade, a date).

How do you identify entity types? A practical technique: read the domain description, and list every noun that the system will need to remember. Then ask, for each noun: "Does this thing have its own identity and its own independent attributes? Or is it just describing something else?"

* `student` → yes, has its own identity (id, name, email), persists independently of any course.
* `email address` → no, it's an attribute of a student, not an entity in its own right. (Unless emails have their own life cycle — forwarding rules, aliases — in which case yes.)
* `course` → yes.
* `credit hour` → no, it's an attribute of a course.
* `enrollment` → gray area. In our design, yes — each enrollment has its own grade, date, and possibly withdrawal status. In a simpler system, enrollment might be just a line item with no attributes, in which case it's better modeled as a relationship, not an entity.

#### Attributes

Attributes are the properties of an entity. ER modeling recognizes several kinds:

* **Simple (atomic)** — a single indivisible value. `student.id` (integer), `course.credits` (integer).
* **Composite** — a value with internal structure. `student.name` might decompose into (first_name, middle_name, last_name).
* **Multi-valued** — an attribute whose value is a set. A course can have multiple `prerequisites`. A student can have multiple `phone_numbers`.
* **Derived** — an attribute that is computable from other data, not stored. A student's `age` is derived from their `birthdate` and today's date.
* **Key attributes** — attributes (possibly composite) that uniquely identify the entity. We met these in Chapter 3 as candidate keys.

Why does this taxonomy matter? Because each kind maps differently to relational tables:

* Simple attributes → a column.
* Composite attributes → either one column (storing "Alice Wong") or several columns (`first_name`, `last_name`). Choose based on whether you need to query the pieces independently.
* Multi-valued attributes → cannot be stored in a single column in the relational model (breaks 1NF, Chapter 6). They require a separate table.
* Derived attributes → not stored; computed by queries or views.
* Key attributes → primary key of the resulting table.

#### Weak Entities

Sometimes an entity has no identity of its own — its identity depends on another entity. These are called **weak entities**.

Classic example: an order line item. A line item has (item_id, quantity, price), but "line item 47" on its own is meaningless — you need to know which order it belongs to. The line item's primary key is (order_id, line_number), not line_number alone. The line item exists only as part of its parent order; delete the order, delete all its line items.

> ### Definition: Weak Entity

A **weak entity** is an entity whose existence and identity depend on another entity (the _identifying_ or _owner_ entity). A weak entity's primary key includes the primary key of its identifying entity, plus a _discriminator_ that distinguishes weak entities belonging to the same owner.

In plain language, a weak entity is "a thing that only makes sense in the context of its parent." The parent's key is part of the child's key.

Not every child entity is weak. There are two distinct, related questions:

  1. _"Does this thing have independent identity?"_ — can it be named/identified on its own, or only via its parent's key?
  2. _"Does this thing survive the deletion of its parent?"_ — can it meaningfully exist if we delete the owner?

A **weak entity** answers _no_ to both. A line item's identity is "line 3 of order 17"; delete order 17 and line 3 is meaningless.

#### Domain Modeling Pro Tip

When you are reading a domain description and identifying entities, two questions force clarity:

* _"Does this thing have attributes or behavior of its own, independent of its relationships?"_ If yes, it's an entity. If no, it's probably an attribute or a relationship.
* _"If I delete this thing's parent, what happens to this thing?"_ If it ceases to make sense, this is a weak entity of that parent.

#### Is Enrollment Weak? The Honest Answer

Three modeling choices are all defensible for `Enrollment`, and different textbooks pick differently. Let us be explicit about which we will use and why:

Modeling | When used | Key  
---|---|---  
**(a) Relationship with attribute** : Enrolls is a many-to-many relationship between Student and Course with attribute `grade`. | Classical ER approach when the relationship has few attributes and no lifecycle. | Composite key (student_id, course_id) in the mapping table.  
**(b) Weak entity** of Student _or_ Course (pick one as owner). | When you want ER diagrams to show the lifecycle ("delete a student, delete all their enrollments"). | Composite key with the owner's key.  
**(c) Strong entity** with surrogate PK `enrollment_id`, and two foreign keys. | When Enrollment gains a lifecycle of its own (withdrawal date, partial credit, grade appeals, audit trail). | Surrogate primary key; (student_id, course_id) becomes a UNIQUE constraint.  
  
**This book uses (a)** in the early chapters (clean conceptual mapping) and **(c)** in Chapter 23 (realistic production systems need the surrogate key for audit and workflow). We mostly avoid (b) because it forces an arbitrary choice of owner — enrollments depend on _both_ Student and Course equally, so calling it "weak of Student" is asymmetric.

**What all three agree on:** the composite `(student_id, course_id)` (plus semester/term in real schemas) must be _either_ the primary key or a unique constraint — you cannot have two enrollments of the same student in the same course in the same term. The choice between (a), (b), (c) is about how the conceptual diagram is drawn and whether a surrogate PK exists, not about which rows are allowed.

### 5.3 Relationships

> ### Definition: Relationship

A **relationship** connects two (or more) entities and represents a meaningful association between them. A relationship type is the category; a relationship instance is a specific association between specific entity instances.

In plain language, if entities are nouns, relationships are verbs. _Enrolls_ is a relationship between Students and Courses. _Teaches_ is a relationship between Instructors and Courses. _Enrolls_ may carry its own attributes (grade, date), which is what makes the enrollment example a gray area between "relationship" and "entity".

#### Degree of a Relationship

The **degree** is the number of entity types participating in a relationship.

* **Binary** (degree 2) — the vast majority of relationships. Student _enrolls in_ Course.
* **Ternary** (degree 3) — relationships involving three entities, none of which can be factored out. "Student + Course + Semester" might form a ternary if the relationship attributes depend on all three.
* **Unary** (degree 1, also called recursive) — a relationship of an entity to itself. Employee _manages_ Employee. Course _is a prerequisite of_ Course.

Ternary and higher relationships are often better decomposed into multiple binaries, but sometimes the ternary form is genuinely needed — don't decompose prematurely.

#### Cardinality

Cardinality specifies _how many_ entity instances on one side may be related to instances on the other side. For binary relationships, three cases:

* **1 : 1 (one-to-one)** — each instance on side A relates to at most one instance on side B, and vice versa. Example: `Person has Passport` (one person has at most one passport; each passport belongs to exactly one person). 1:1 is rare in practice; usually the two entities should be merged into one.
* **1 : N (one-to-many)** — each instance on side A relates to many (possibly zero) instances on side B, but each instance on side B relates to at most one A. Example: `Instructor teaches Course` — one instructor teaches many courses; each course has exactly one instructor (in our simplified model).
* **N : M (many-to-many)** — each instance on side A can relate to many instances on side B, and each instance on side B can relate to many A. Example: `Student enrolls in Course` — a student takes many courses; a course has many students.

Side note: "many" usually means "zero or more", not "strictly more than one". Be careful when informal English says "many"; in ER, we mean "any number, possibly zero."

#### Participation

Orthogonal to cardinality is **participation** — whether every instance on one side must participate in the relationship, or may not.

* **Total participation** — every instance _must_ participate. "Every course must have an instructor" → Course has total participation in the Teaches relationship.
* **Partial participation** — instances may exist without participating. "A student may have zero enrollments when they first sign up" → Student has partial participation in the Enrolls relationship.

In diagrams, cardinality and participation are usually shown with arrow styles, boxes, numbers ("0..1", "1..*"), or whatever notation the book or tool uses. The specific notation is less important than being precise about the semantics.

### 5.4 Modeling the Registrar's System Step by Step

Let's walk through a complete ER modeling pass for the university registration system. This is the forward-engineering exercise promised in the introduction.

**Domain description (English):**

> "The university has many students and many instructors. Each student is identified by an ID and has a name and an email. Each instructor has an ID, a name, an office, and a phone number.
> 
> The university offers many courses. Each course has an ID, a title, a number of credits, and a single assigned instructor. Each course is taught by exactly one instructor; each instructor may teach multiple courses.
> 
> Students enroll in courses. A student can enroll in many courses; a course can have many students. When a student enrolls, the enrollment eventually receives a grade. It is possible for a new student to have no enrollments yet, and a new course to have no students yet."

**Step 1: identify entities.**

Nouns with independent attributes and identity:

* Student (id, name, email) — entity.
* Instructor (id, name, office, phone) — entity.
* Course (id, title, credits) — entity. We haven't yet decided where the instructor_id lives.
* Enrollment — attribute "grade"; gray area as discussed. Provisionally, treat as a relationship with its own attribute.

**Step 2: identify relationships.**

* _Teaches_ — between Instructor and Course. "Each course is taught by exactly one instructor; each instructor may teach multiple courses." → 1:N from Instructor to Course. Course has total participation (every course must have an instructor); Instructor has partial participation (an instructor may teach zero courses this semester).
* _Enrolls_ — between Student and Course. "A student can enroll in many courses; a course can have many students." → M:N. Enrolls has an attribute `grade` (nullable). Both Student and Course have partial participation.

**Step 3: identify attributes on each entity and relationship.**

* Student: id (key), name, email. email has a UNIQUE constraint; we'll note that separately from the primary key.
* Instructor: id (key), name, office, phone.
* Course: id (key), title, credits.
* Teaches (relationship): no extra attributes beyond the linkage.
* Enrolls (relationship): grade (nullable).

**Step 4: identify constraints.**

* Course must have an instructor (total participation in Teaches).
* Credits is in [1, 12] (domain constraint).
* Email is UNIQUE (a uniqueness constraint on a non-primary-key attribute).
* Grade, when present, is in a specific set of valid grade strings.

**Step 5: sketch the ER diagram verbally.** Since this book is text-only, here is the structured description:
    
    
    Entities:
      Student [id*, name, email (UNIQUE)]
      Instructor [id*, name, office, phone]
      Course [id*, title, credits]
    
    Relationships:
      Teaches : Instructor ---< Course
        - Instructor side: partial participation, 1
        - Course side: total participation, N
      Enrolls : Student >---< Course
        - Student side: partial participation, M
        - Course side: partial participation, N
        - Attributes on Enrolls: grade (nullable)
    

The `*` marks the primary key. `---<` is "one-to-many"; `>---<` is "many-to-many." (If you're producing a diagram in a real tool, adopt whatever notation the tool uses — Crow's Foot, UML, Chen notation — and be consistent.)

This is the whole ER model. The next step converts it to relations.

### 5.5 ER-to-Relational Mapping Rules

Now the mechanical part. There are five standard rules. Applied consistently, they turn an ER model into a relational schema with no design judgment required at this point — the judgment all happened in the ER modeling phase.

#### Rule 1: Each Strong Entity Becomes a Table

For each strong entity, create a table with one column per simple attribute. The primary key attribute becomes the primary key of the table. Composite attributes decompose (if you want the pieces queryable) or are stored as a single column (if you want to treat them as opaque). Derived attributes are omitted.

From our ER model:
    
    
    Students    (id, name, email)
    Instructors (id, name, office, phone)
    Courses     (id, title, credits)
    

Primary keys are `id` in each case.

#### Rule 2: Multi-Valued Attributes Become Their Own Table

A multi-valued attribute cannot live in a single column. Create a new table with:

* A foreign key back to the owning entity.
* A column for the multi-valued attribute's value.
* A primary key that is (owner_key, value).

Our current domain has no multi-valued attributes, but if a Course could have multiple `keyword` tags:
    
    
    CourseKeywords (course_id REFERENCES Courses(id), keyword, PRIMARY KEY (course_id, keyword))
    

#### Rule 3: 1:N Relationships Become a Foreign Key on the "N" Side

For a 1:N relationship between entities A and B (where "many B's per A"), add a foreign key to B referencing A's primary key. Any attributes on the relationship also go into B.

From our ER: Teaches is 1:N from Instructor to Course. So we add `instructor_id` to Courses:
    
    
    Courses (id, title, credits, instructor_id REFERENCES Instructors(id))
    

If Teaches had a `since` attribute (date the instructor first taught that course), it would go into the Courses table:
    
    
    Courses (id, title, credits, instructor_id, since)
    

Total participation on the Course side (every course must have an instructor) → `NOT NULL` on `instructor_id`.

#### Rule 4: M:N Relationships Become Their Own Table

For an M:N relationship between A and B, create a new table — called a _junction table_ or _associative table_. Its columns are:

* A foreign key to A's primary key.
* A foreign key to B's primary key.
* All attributes of the relationship itself.

The primary key is the pair of foreign keys (or an extended key if the relationship can repeat, e.g., enrollments across multiple semesters).

From our ER: Enrolls is M:N between Student and Course, with attribute `grade`:
    
    
    Enrollments (
      student_id REFERENCES Students(id),
      course_id  REFERENCES Courses(id),
      grade,
      PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id)
    )
    

This is why enrollment is a table, not a column. The M:N nature of the relationship demanded it.

#### Rule 5: Weak Entities Become a Table with a Composite Key

For a weak entity E whose owner is W:

* Create a table for E.
* Include all of W's primary key as a foreign key (forming part of E's primary key).
* Include E's discriminator (the attribute that distinguishes weak entities sharing the same owner).
* Primary key of E's table = (W's primary key) + E's discriminator.

Our current model has no weak entities. An example from e-commerce:
    
    
    Orders (order_id*, customer_id, date, total)
    OrderLineItems (
      order_id    REFERENCES Orders(order_id),
      line_number,            -- discriminator
      item_id, quantity, price,
      PRIMARY KEY (order_id, line_number)
    )
    

An order line item has no identity on its own — "line item 5" has no meaning without knowing its order.

#### Applying All the Rules: Our Resulting Schema

Putting the five rules together on our university registration ER model, we get:
    
    
    Students (id, name, email UNIQUE)
    Instructors (id, name, office, phone)
    Courses (id, title, credits CHECK (1..12), instructor_id REFERENCES Instructors(id) NOT NULL)
    Enrollments (student_id, course_id, grade, PRIMARY KEY (student_id, course_id))
    

This is essentially identical to Chapter 3's schema. The mapping worked.

Notice what the ER process _didn't_ have to choose:

* Surrogate vs natural key — the ER model just says "id"; our DDL chose surrogate integer. That's a DDL-stage decision, not an ER-stage decision.
* Index design — no indexes in the ER; we'll decide those later based on workload (Chapter 10).
* Storage engine (MySQL InnoDB vs MyISAM; PostgreSQL table vs foreign table) — also a DDL-stage choice.

ER is about _shape_. Implementation details live one layer down.

### 5.6 A Harder Example: The Library

To see ER modeling handle more complexity, let's do a quick forward pass on a library system. Domain description:

> "A library has books. Each book has a title, an author, and an ISBN. The library owns multiple physical copies of each book; each copy has a unique barcode and a location on the shelf. Members of the library borrow copies. Each loan has a due date; when the copy is returned, the loan records the return date. A member can have multiple loans simultaneously; a copy is on loan to at most one member at a time (though its loan history has many entries)."

**Entities:**

* Book (ISBN*, title, author) — conceptual, one per title.
* Copy (barcode*, location) — physical, many per Book.
* Member (id*, name, email)
* Loan — has its own attributes (due_date, return_date). Entity.

**Relationships:**

* _Book has Copy_ — 1:N, total participation on Copy side (every copy belongs to a book).
* _Member borrows Loan_ — 1:N from Member to Loan (a member has many loans; each loan belongs to one member).
* _Copy is lent via Loan_ — 1:N from Copy to Loan (a copy has many historical loans; each loan involves one copy).

**Weak-entity consideration: two cases, two different answers.**

* **Case 1: barcodes are globally unique** (typical in modern libraries, where barcodes are issued from a shared pool or printed with the ISBN encoded). Copy's identity stands alone — `barcode` alone locates a copy. Copy is a **strong entity** with `barcode` as PK and `isbn` as a foreign key. This is the model we will use below.
* **Case 2: barcodes are unique only within a book** (older systems where each book was barcoded independently, e.g., "book 47 copy 3"). Copy _is_ a weak entity of Book. Its primary key is the composite `(isbn, barcode)`, and deleting the Book cascades to its Copies.

The distinction is not stylistic — it determines whether a query like `SELECT * FROM Copies WHERE barcode = '100000017'` returns one row (Case 1) or needs to be qualified by ISBN (Case 2). Pick the model that matches the physical reality of your barcode system.

**Deriving the schema (Rules 1–5):**
    
    
    Books   (isbn VARCHAR PRIMARY KEY, title VARCHAR, author VARCHAR)
    Copies  (barcode VARCHAR PRIMARY KEY, isbn REFERENCES Books(isbn), location VARCHAR)
    Members (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name VARCHAR, email VARCHAR UNIQUE)
    Loans   (
      id         INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      member_id  REFERENCES Members(id),
      barcode    REFERENCES Copies(barcode),
      due_date   DATE,
      return_date DATE,  -- NULL until returned
      INDEX (member_id),
      INDEX (barcode, return_date)  -- finds current loan of a copy
    )
    

Design notes:

* We used surrogate integer primary key on `Members` and `Loans`, ISBN on `Books` (ISBN is already a stable, globally unique natural key), barcode on `Copies` (also a stable natural key).
* The "at most one active loan per copy at a time" constraint is harder — it's a condition on the data over time. We cannot easily express it as a simple UNIQUE constraint (because historical loans for the same copy are fine). Modern PostgreSQL supports partial UNIQUE indexes for exactly this case: 
        
        CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON Loans(barcode) WHERE return_date IS NULL;
        

Now no two loans of the same copy can have `return_date IS NULL` simultaneously.
* Alternatively, some designs have a separate `ActiveLoans` table that holds only open loans, and a `LoanHistory` table for closed ones. Both approaches work; the first is simpler, the second is faster for "show me all active loans" queries on large loan history.

The tradeoff theme (Chapter 1) is showing up: "enforce the constraint in SQL with a partial unique index" versus "split into two tables" have different performance and operational profiles. ER modeling alone doesn't force the choice — that's a schema-level tradeoff you make after the ER model is stable.

#### Common Misconception

New engineers often think "the ER model gives you the one right schema." It doesn't. The ER model captures the _shape_ of the domain, but the final schema involves more choices: surrogate vs natural keys, whether to combine several small tables or keep them separate, how to handle historical data, how to express constraints the ER diagram glosses over. The ER model is the _input_ to schema design, not the _output_. Two different engineers given the same ER model may produce slightly different schemas, and both can be correct for different workloads.

### 5.7 When an Attribute Should Become an Entity

One of the most common ER-modeling refactors: realizing that something you first modeled as an attribute should really be its own entity.

Symptom: the attribute starts growing. It has a name, then a name plus a description, then a name plus a description plus a display order. Other entities want to reference it. The same value repeats redundantly across many rows.

Example: in our university model, `Course.credits` is just an integer. But suppose someone says: "Actually, credits are measured differently by department — Arts measures in semester hours, Engineering in quarter hours, Graduate courses have a different scale." Suddenly credits isn't a plain integer but something that must be interpreted in context.

At that point, `credits` should probably become a reference to a `CreditSystem` entity:
    
    
    CreditSystems (id, department, unit_name, scale_factor)
    Courses (id, title, credit_value, credit_system_id REFERENCES CreditSystems(id), ...)
    

The symptom is: "my attribute has started to have attributes of its own." That's the moment to promote it to an entity. In practice, doing this too early adds complexity you don't need; doing it too late forces a migration later. Judgment.

Another example: in an e-commerce system, `order.shipping_address` starts as a string. Then you realize customers have multiple addresses. Then you want to validate against a postal database. Then you want to track delivery-status updates per address. Shipping address has become an entity (`Addresses`), and orders reference it.

This pattern — "attribute grows into entity" — is one of the most common database-refactoring stories, especially in systems that started simple and grew over years.

### 5.8 Thread A, Revisited

We started this chapter with the promise of _forward-engineering_ Thread A. We did:

  1. Read the registrar's description in English.
  2. Identified entities: Student, Instructor, Course. Decided Enrollment was a relationship with an attribute (or, equivalently, a weak entity of both Student and Course).
  3. Identified relationships: Teaches (1:N), Enrolls (M:N).
  4. Applied the five mapping rules.
  5. Produced the Chapter 3 schema with only mechanical work at step 5.

The point of the exercise is not that "you should always ER-model even simple schemas" (for this scale, most engineers can skip the formal step). The point is that **when the schema gets hard, this is the process.** A 30-entity schema for a hospital's electronic medical record system is not going to emerge from a whiteboard session unless someone works through entities, relationships, cardinalities, and mapping rules systematically. And when two teams must share a schema, an ER diagram is the contract — a shared understanding of shape that outlasts any specific DDL.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **ER modeling trades up-front time for schema clarity.** For a five-table schema you can often skip straight to DDL; for a 30-entity system, an ER diagram is the only way to expose missing relationships and wrong cardinalities before they are baked into tables.
* **Surrogate keys trade human-readability for stability.** A numeric `id` protects every foreign key from churn when the "natural" key (email, ISBN, SSN) changes or turns out not to be unique after all. The cost is a column that means nothing to the humans reading the data.
* **Natural keys trade stability for built-in uniqueness and free joins.** A schema keyed on `(isbn)` needs no extra `id` column and joins read naturally, but the day the ISBN format changes — or you discover two editions with the same ISBN — your keys become a migration project.
* **Weak entities trade a standalone identity for ownership semantics.** Modeling `Enrollment` as weak-to-(`Student`, `Course`) says "this row cannot exist without its parents" and lets `ON DELETE CASCADE` do the right thing automatically. You give up the ability to talk about an enrollment that outlives its student.
* **Modeling a relationship with attributes as its own entity trades diagram simplicity for M:N flexibility.** The moment `Enrolls` carries a `grade`, it must become a table; treating it as a pure link loses the attribute. Any relationship of higher than 1:1 cardinality with its own data becomes an entity.
* **ER-to-relational mapping rules trade mechanical rigidity for reproducibility.** Following the five rules gives the same schema every time, which is what you want when two people must produce compatible DDL from the same diagram — at the cost of ignoring workload-specific tuning the rules cannot see.

### 5.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** For each of the following, classify as entity, attribute, or relationship, and justify in one sentence: (a) "User" in a social media app. (b) "Like" on a social media post. (c) "Location" of a user. (d) "Friendship" between two users. (e) "Tag" on a photograph.

Hint

Ask: does it have independent existence and its own attributes? Does it connect two things?

**Answer:** (a) **Entity.** Has identity (user_id), independent attributes (name, email, bio), independent lifecycle. (b) **Entity** (with a relationship flavor). A Like has attributes: timestamp, possibly reaction type. It _relates_ a User to a Post, so it's the archetypal M:N relationship — and in SQL, that becomes a junction table. You can also model it as a relationship with attributes; both are valid. (c) **Attribute** on User, almost always. Unless locations are tracked historically (user moves; old location preserved) or have attributes of their own (time zone, postal code validity), in which case location becomes an entity. (d) **Relationship.** Connects two Users; in the simplest form, it's M:N (unary, since both ends are Users). Often modeled with attributes: friendship_since, status (pending, accepted, blocked), which again pushes it toward "junction table that is itself an entity." (e) **Gray area.** If a Tag is just a string ("#vacation"), it's an attribute on the photo. If tags have attributes (creator, usage count, translations), they're entities referenced via an M:N junction table (PhotoTags).

The pattern: "gray area" is common in ER modeling. The question isn't "what is this really?" but "what does my system need to remember and change about this thing?" If the answer grows over time, what you modeled as an attribute becomes an entity.

**Q2.** A 1:1 relationship exists in a schema. When should you merge the two entities into one table, and when should you keep them separate?

**Answer:** **Merge** if:

* The two entities always appear together (same lifecycle — created together, deleted together).
* No other entities reference them separately.
* Querying usually needs data from both.

**Keep separate** if:

* One side is optional. `User` always exists; `PremiumSubscription` exists for some users. A 1:1 here lets you avoid NULLs on all the premium-only columns for free users.
* The data has different access patterns. If one side is read 1000× more often than the other, separating them reduces the hot table's page size and improves cache behavior.
* The data has different sensitivity. `User` has public fields; `UserSSN` is highly sensitive and access-controlled. Separate for security.
* Historical/versioning differs. One side changes often; the other is nearly immutable.

Most 1:1 relationships in practice fall into the "keep separate" camp for one of these reasons, which is why true "merge-able" 1:1 is rare.

**Q3.** The _Enrolls_ relationship between Student and Course carries the attribute `grade`. If we added a second attribute, `enrollment_date`, and a third, `withdrawn_on` (nullable, set if the student withdraws), would you reconsider modeling Enrollment as an entity rather than a relationship? Why or why not?

**Answer:** Yes — this is exactly the "attribute grows into entity" pattern from Section 5.7. Once the relationship has three attributes, each with its own semantics and own update pattern, it has become a thing worth naming. Treating it as an entity called `Enrollment` (rather than a nameless relationship) makes the model clearer and lets you attach further attributes or relationships to it naturally (e.g., linking an enrollment to a payment record, or tracking the grade change history).

In the relational schema, the end result is the same: a table named `Enrollments` with the four columns. But in the ER diagram — and in conversations with other engineers — "Enrollment is an entity" is more honest to the domain than "Enrolls is a relationship that happens to carry three attributes."

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Forward-engineer a small blog platform. Domain description:

> "Users write posts. Each post has a title, body, publication date, and an author. Users can comment on any post. Comments have a body, a date, and a commenter. Comments can be replies to other comments (threaded). Users can 'like' posts. Posts have tags (free-form strings)."

Produce: (a) The list of entities with their attributes. (b) The list of relationships with their cardinalities and any attributes. (c) The resulting relational schema (DDL-level sketch — column names, primary keys, foreign keys).

Hint

Threaded comments are a unary (self-referential) relationship. Tags start as "free-form strings" but may be ambiguous.

**Answer:**

**(a) Entities:**

* `User` (id*, name, email, joined_date)
* `Post` (id*, title, body, published_at)
* `Comment` (id*, body, commented_at)
* `Like` — entity or relationship? An M:N between User and Post, no attributes beyond "did like / did not like." Modeled as a junction table.
* `Tag` — free-form strings are attributes on Post initially. But if tags could be listed, ranked, or given attributes later, promote to entity.

**(b) Relationships:**

* `User writes Post` — 1:N from User to Post. Every post has exactly one author (total participation on Post side).
* `User comments on Post` — this is a 1:N relationship if each Comment has one commenter and one post. But Comment is an entity with its own attributes, so we express this as two 1:N relationships: _User commented_ and _Post has comment_.
* `Comment is reply to Comment` — unary, 1:N (each comment has at most one parent comment).
* `User likes Post` — M:N, no attributes.
* `Post has Tag` — M:N, no attributes (if tags are free-form strings, no Tag entity; instead just a multi-valued attribute on Post).

**(c) Schema:**
    
    
    Users (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      name VARCHAR NOT NULL,
      email VARCHAR UNIQUE NOT NULL,
      joined_date DATE NOT NULL
    );
    
    Posts (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      author_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Users(id),
      title VARCHAR NOT NULL,
      body TEXT NOT NULL,
      published_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
    );
    
    Comments (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY,
      post_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Posts(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      commenter_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Users(id),
      parent_comment_id INTEGER REFERENCES Comments(id),  -- NULL for top-level
      body TEXT NOT NULL,
      commented_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL
    );
    
    Likes (
      user_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      post_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Posts(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      PRIMARY KEY (user_id, post_id)
    );
    
    PostTags (
      post_id INTEGER NOT NULL REFERENCES Posts(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
      tag VARCHAR NOT NULL,
      PRIMARY KEY (post_id, tag)
    );
    

Notes:

* `Comments.parent_comment_id` self-references. Chapter 7 and 8 will cover how to query recursive structures (Common Table Expressions / recursive queries).
* `PostTags` holds the multi-valued tag attribute as a separate table (Rule 2). If later we wanted tags to have attributes (color, follower count), we'd add a `Tags` entity table and change `PostTags.tag` to `PostTags.tag_id REFERENCES Tags(id)`.
* All the "child" relationships use `ON DELETE CASCADE` where the child is meaningless without its parent.

**Cross-chapter connection:** this schema has no indexes yet. Chapter 10 will return to it and decide which columns benefit from indexes (almost certainly `Posts(author_id)`, `Comments(post_id)`, `Likes(post_id)` for counting likes, `Comments(parent_comment_id)` for threading).

**Q2.** Consider a hospital system:

> "Patients visit doctors. Each visit is at a specific clinic, on a specific date, with a specific doctor. A doctor works at one clinic. A patient can visit many doctors over time; a doctor sees many patients. During a visit, the doctor prescribes medications (zero or more per visit). A medication is identified by a name and dosage; many patients may receive the same medication."

Identify: (a) The entities. (b) Any weak entities. (c) A ternary relationship (if one genuinely exists) and whether it should be decomposed into binaries.

Hint

"Visit" has attributes and carries the relationship — what is it? Is the prescription itself an entity?

**Answer:** (a) **Entities:** Patient, Doctor, Clinic, Visit, Medication, Prescription.

(b) **Weak entities:** `Prescription` is a weak entity of `Visit` — a prescription exists only in the context of a specific visit. Its key is (visit_id, medication_id) or (visit_id, prescription_number) if a visit can prescribe the same medication twice at different dosages.

(c) **Is there a true ternary?** Consider "Patient visits Doctor at Clinic on Date". The naive read says this is a ternary — Patient + Doctor + Clinic all participate in Visit.

But: `Doctor works at Clinic` is already a 1:N relationship (each doctor works at one clinic). So once you know the doctor, you know the clinic. The clinic is functionally determined by the doctor. This means the "ternary" decomposes: Visit is really between Patient and Doctor, with the clinic inferrable from the doctor.

If the rule were different — "a doctor may work at multiple clinics, and a specific visit is at a specific one of them" — then Visit is a genuine ternary between Patient, Doctor, Clinic, and cannot be decomposed without losing information.

This is the kind of subtlety ER modeling forces you to confront. "Are these three things genuinely intertwined, or is one derivable from another?" is a question that only gets asked when you model carefully.

The resulting schema (brief):
    
    
    Clinics (id, name, address)
    Doctors (id, name, clinic_id REFERENCES Clinics)
    Patients (id, name, dob)
    Visits (id, patient_id, doctor_id, visit_date)
    Medications (id, name, dosage)
    Prescriptions (visit_id REFERENCES Visits, medication_id REFERENCES Medications,
                   PRIMARY KEY (visit_id, medication_id))
    

**Q3.** Consider your own favorite app (social media, email client, online banking, game, etc.). Spend 10 minutes sketching the entities and relationships you think it must have internally. You don't need to list all attributes — just entity types and relationship cardinalities. Reflect: where do you think the modeling gets hardest for real-world systems?

Hint

Be concrete; a banking app probably has Account, Transaction, Customer. The hard parts are usually time-varying attributes, historical data, and permissions.

**Discussion (partial answer, demonstrating the reflection step):**

Take an email client. Entities: User (account holder), Email (one specific message), Contact (address book entry), Label / Folder, Thread.

Relationships:

* User has Email — 1:N (User owns many emails). Total participation on Email side.
* Email in Thread — M:1 typically (each email belongs to one thread; a thread has many emails).
* Email has Label — M:N (an email can have many labels; a label has many emails). Junction table.
* Contact references Email (by the address field, perhaps as an attribute).

Where it gets hard:

* **Time-varying state.** Is this email read or unread? Archived? Starred? Each of these is per-user state. If the email itself is shared (e.g., mail server stores one copy, two recipients have their own read-status), you need a per-user-per-email state table.
* **Permissions.** Who can see what. Permissions often cut across entities and are a major source of bugs.
* **Versioning / history.** If a user renames a label, do historical emails still show up under the old name? (Usually not — labels are mutable.) But some labels might have history you want to preserve.
* **Denormalization pressure.** Threads often duplicate the "most recent message preview" into the Thread table for fast listing. This is deliberate redundancy, paid back by dramatically faster inbox queries.

The general point: the surface ER model is a 15-minute exercise. The real complexity in production systems is in the cross-cutting concerns (time, permissions, denormalization) that don't fit cleanly into pure ER and usually require explicit design discussion. This is a recurring pattern you'll see again in Chapter 23's capstone review.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** An ER model expresses structural relationships, but says nothing about how the data will be queried. Should query patterns influence ER modeling? Or should ER be pure structure, with performance considerations applied only at the schema/indexing phase?

**Discussion:** Pure theory says: ER is structure, performance is separate. The argument is clean — if we let performance leak into ER, we confuse two concerns, and the model becomes implementation-coupled.

Pure theory sometimes produces schemas that are painful to operate. Example: a strict ER-derived schema for an e-commerce site might have a `ProductImages` table that stores image URLs, referenced by `Products`. Structurally correct. But every time you list 20 products on a category page, you do 20 extra joins to fetch the primary image. A pragmatic schema collapses the primary image URL into `Products.primary_image_url` as a denormalized convenience column, updated by trigger or by the application when the primary image changes. Impure but vastly faster.

Middle path: **model ER purely, then evaluate the schema under realistic workloads, and denormalize only where the evidence demands it.** This gives you a clean baseline you can justify to a new engineer ("we started here, because this is structurally correct; we deviated here because this query is a known hot path, and the denormalization is maintained by <trigger / application code / nightly rebuild>"). The deviation is documented and explicable, not an accident.

The deeper principle: abstractions and performance are in tension, and in good design you pick abstractions first, then pay the performance rent where you have to. Jumping straight to performance-optimized schemas without an abstraction tends to produce systems that no one fully understands.

**Q2.** Codd published the relational model in 1970; Chen published the ER model in 1976. Why do you think we still teach ER modeling as a separate step, rather than teaching relational modeling directly?

**Discussion:** Two reasons, both practical.

First: the relational model is _too low-level_ for the initial design conversation. When you're sketching "we have students, courses, and enrollments," you don't want to be arguing about whether enrollments should have a composite primary key or a surrogate id. You want to agree on the _shape_ first. ER modeling gives that shape a vocabulary — entities, relationships, cardinalities — that lives above the DDL decisions.

Second: ER modeling produces a diagram, and diagrams are communication artifacts. You can point at an ER diagram in a meeting and say "we're modeling this as a M:N relationship with these two attributes" and everyone in the room understands. The equivalent conversation about relational schemas is "we have tables X, Y, Z, and Z has these two foreign keys" — which requires more context and doesn't communicate cardinalities as cleanly.

The practical consequence: even engineers who have been writing SQL for 20 years still sketch ER diagrams (or some informal equivalent) before DDL on any non-trivial schema. The ER model is a thinking tool that outlasts the "I'll just write the tables" phase of one's career.

**Q3.** In some domains, you can't determine all entities and relationships upfront — new kinds of data arrive over years. What does this mean for ER modeling? Should we ER-model incrementally? Should we use a different approach altogether for open-ended domains?

**Discussion:** This is the central challenge in any long-lived system. Three common strategies:

  1. **Iterative ER modeling.** You ER-model what you know today, then revise the model as the domain reveals itself. The schema gets migrated alongside. This is the standard approach — every production schema evolves this way.

  2. **Extensible attribute columns (EAV, "entity-attribute-value").** One table holds `(entity_id, attribute_name, value)` rows. Each row is one attribute of one entity, and you can add new attributes by adding new rows, no schema change needed. This trades schema rigidity for queryability (EAV queries are painful to optimize and often lose type safety). Used in medical records, CRM customization, where "every customer has different custom fields" is a business reality.

  3. **Document or schema-less storage (Chapter 20).** Store entities as JSON documents; each document has whatever shape it needs. This trades schema rigidity for flexibility even more radically than EAV. Right for genuinely heterogeneous data; wrong for most operational systems where schema gives you integrity.

The right answer depends on _how much_ variation is expected and _how often_ it will be queried ad-hoc. A schema that changes once a quarter (e-commerce) → iterative ER modeling with migration discipline. A schema where every customer has different attributes (healthcare EHR) → EAV or a hybrid. A schema where the data is genuinely document-shaped (user-generated rich content, variable web crawl data) → document store.

ER modeling is not the wrong approach for open-ended domains — it's just not the _complete_ approach. You still need an ER model (for the stable core) plus an extension mechanism (for the variable part). This hybrid is what most mature systems converge to, which Chapter 22 will return to as the polyglot persistence question.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter taught ER modeling — the discipline of designing a schema before writing DDL. We identified **entities** (things with independent existence and attributes), **relationships** (associations between entities, characterized by degree, cardinality, and participation), and the types of **attributes** (simple, composite, multi-valued, derived, key). We walked through the **five ER-to-relational mapping rules** and applied them to forward-engineer Thread A's university registration schema, arriving at essentially the same schema we had already reverse-engineered in Chapter 3.

The key insight: ER modeling separates _shape_ from _implementation_. The shape is what you argue about in design meetings — what things exist, how they connect, what's mandatory. The implementation details (surrogate vs natural keys, index choices, denormalization) are one layer down, informed by workload. Conflating the two produces bad schemas because the DDL-level decisions leak into the abstraction.

We also met the "attribute grows into entity" refactor — one of the most common real-world schema evolutions, where something initially modeled as an attribute accumulates enough attributes of its own that it deserves to be a table.

Looking ahead: Chapter 6 asks a sharper question about our resulting schema. Is it _good enough_? Are there lurking dependencies that would cause anomalies? **Normalization theory** (functional dependencies, minimal cover, 3NF, BCNF) gives us a formal answer, and will let us prove — not just argue — that Thread A's Chapter 3 schema is structurally safe.

---

## Chapter 6: Functional Dependencies and Normalization

In Chapter 2 we found that **uncontrolled redundancy** in a schema produces the update, deletion, and insertion anomalies. In Chapter 3 we fixed the specific anomalies by splitting `enrollments.csv` into separate tables. In Chapter 5 we formalized the design process with ER modeling.

But one nagging question remains: **how do we _know_ a schema is anomaly-free?** Eyeballing it and saying "looks good" works when the schema has four tables and obvious relationships. It does not work for a hospital records system with 150 tables where hidden dependencies lurk in innocuous-looking fields.

This chapter gives us the formal answer. We introduce **functional dependencies** — a precise way of saying "attribute X determines attribute Y" — and **normal forms** , which are conditions on the schema that guarantee specific anomaly classes cannot occur. Along the way we meet Armstrong's axioms, attribute closure, candidate key derivation via closure, the minimal cover, and the two normal forms that matter most in practice (3NF and BCNF).

By the end of this chapter, Thread A's Chapter 3 schema is provably anomaly-free. And the flip side — when denormalization is _correct_ — becomes a principled choice rather than a sin.

### 6.1 Functional Dependencies

> ### Definition: Functional Dependency

A **functional dependency** (FD) from attribute set $X$ to attribute set $Y$, written $X \rightarrow Y$ (sometimes with $R$ as relation context).

In plain language, $X \rightarrow Y$ says "if you know $X$, you know $Y$." Or, equivalently, "any two tuples that agree on $X$ must also agree on $Y$."

Let's make this concrete on our university schema. Consider the (bad) denormalized enrollments table from Chapter 2:
    
    
    Enrollments_Bad(student_id, student_name, course_id, course_title, instructor, credits, grade)
    

Plausible FDs:

* {student_id}→{student_name}\\{$\text{student\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{student\\_name}\\}{student_id}$→{student_name}: if you know the student's id, you know their name. (A student's id uniquely identifies them, and each student has exactly one name.)
* {course_id}→{course_title,instructor,credits}\\{$\text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{course\\_title}, \text{instructor}, \text{credits}\\}{course_id}$→{course_title,instructor,credits}: if you know the course id, you know everything about the course.
* {student_id,course_id}→{grade}\\{$\text{student\\_id}, \text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{grade}\\}{student_id,course_id}$→{grade}: if you know the (student, course) pair, you know the grade. (Each pair has at most one grade.)
* {student_id,course_id}→{student_name,course_title,instructor,credits,grade}\\{$\text{student\\_id}, \text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{student\\_name}, \text{course\\_title}, \text{instructor}, \text{credits}, \text{grade}\\}{student_id,course_id}$→{student_name,course_title,instructor,credits,grade}: if you know (student, course), you know everything, because you can derive student_name from student_id and course_title etc. from course_id.

Two things to notice:

  1. **FDs are claims about _meaning_ , not about the current instance.** "student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$" is a structural rule ("each student has exactly one name") that applies to every valid instance of the database — not just the one currently on disk. An accidentally-consistent instance (where, coincidentally, no two students share a name) does not establish student_name$\text{student\\_name} \rightarrow \text{student\\_id}student_name$ as an FD; the FD must hold for every _possible_ valid instance.

  2. **FDs are directional.** student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$ does _not_ imply student_name$\text{student\\_name} \rightarrow \text{student\\_id}student_name$. (The first is true — each id has one name — but the second is false — two students can share the name "Alice Chen".)

#### Trivial vs Non-Trivial FDs

A functional dependency X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y is **trivial** if Y⊆XY \subseteq XY⊆X. For example, {course_id}→{course_id}\\{$\text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{course\\_id}\\}{course_id}$→{course_id} is trivial; so is {student_id,course_id}→{course_id}\\{$\text{student\\_id}, \text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{course\\_id}\\}{student_id,course_id}$→{course_id}.

Trivial FDs always hold (they are tautologies) and carry no real information. The FDs we care about are **non-trivial** : X→YX $\rightarrow YX$$\not\subseteq XY$

### 6.2 Armstrong's Axioms

Given a set of FDs, what other FDs are logically implied? Armstrong (1974) gave us three rules — Armstrong's axioms — that are _sound and complete_ : from any set of FDs, applying the axioms derives exactly the FDs that are logically implied.

> ### Definition: Armstrong's Axioms

**A1 — Reflexivity.** If Y⊆XY $\subseteq XY$⊆X, then X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y. (Trivial FDs are always implied.)

**A2 — Augmentation.** If X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y, then for any attribute set ZZZ, XZ→YZXZ $\rightarrow YZXZ$→YZ. (If you know X determines Y, adding the same attributes to both sides preserves the FD.)

**A3 — Transitivity.** If X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y and Y→ZY $\rightarrow ZY$$\rightarrow ZX$

Three useful derived rules:

* **Union.** If X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y and X→ZX $\rightarrow ZX$$\rightarrow YZX$
* **Decomposition.** If X→YZX $\rightarrow YZX$$\rightarrow YX$
* **Pseudo-transitivity.** If X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y and WY→ZWY $\rightarrow ZWY$$\rightarrow ZXW$

You will use Union and Decomposition constantly. You will invoke Armstrong's three axioms directly less often, but they are the foundation that makes the derived rules sound.

#### Common Misconception

Students often think Armstrong's axioms are a "computational procedure" for finding all FDs. They aren't — they are logical implication rules. To _compute_ implications efficiently, we don't apply the axioms blindly; we use **attribute closure** (Section 6.3), which is a dynamic-programming algorithm that answers "what does a given attribute set determine?" in linear time.

### 6.3 Closures

#### Attribute Closure

Given a relation schema RRR, a set of FDs FFF, and an attribute set XXX, the **closure of X under F** , written X+X^+X+, is the set of all attributes YYY such that X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y is logically implied by FFF.

Informally, X+X^+X+ answers: "starting from knowing the values of XXX, what else can I deduce?"

#### Algorithm: Computing X+X^+X+
    
    
    X_plus = X
    repeat:
      for each FD Y -> Z in F:
        if Y ⊆ X_plus:
          X_plus = X_plus ∪ Z
    until X_plus stops changing
    return X_plus
    

Worked example. Consider relation R(A,B,C,D,E)R(A, B, C, D, E)R(A,B,C,D,E) with FDs:

$\rightarrow BA$
$\rightarrow DBC$
$\rightarrow ED$

Compute {A,C}+\\{A, C\\}^+{A,C}+:

Start: {A,C}\\{A, C\\}{A,C}. Apply A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B: A∈{A,C}A $\in \\{A,C\\}A$∈{A,C} → add BBB. Now {A,B,C}\\{A, B, C\\}{A,B,C}. Apply BC→DBC $\rightarrow DBC$→D: {B,C}⊆{A,B,C}\\{B, C\\} \subseteq \\{A,B,C\\}{B,C}⊆{A,B,C} → add DDD. Now {A,B,C,D}\\{A, B, C, D\\}{A,B,C,D}. Apply D→ED \rightarrow ED→E: D∈{A,B,C,D}D \in \\{A,B,C,D\\}D∈{A,B,C,D} → add EEE. Now {A,B,C,D,E}\\{A, B, C, D, E\\}{A,B,C,D,E}. No more changes possible.

So {A,C}+={A,B,C,D,E}\\{A, C\\}^+ = \\{A, B, C, D, E\\}{A,C}+={A,B,C,D,E} — which means {A,C}\\{A, C\\}{A,C} determines _every_ attribute of RRR. That makes {A,C}\\{A, C\\}{A,C} a **superkey** of RRR.

#### Using Closure to Find Candidate Keys

Definition (recap): a candidate key of RRR is a minimal superkey. To find one:

  1. Start with a candidate set XXX (often all attributes).
  2. For each attribute A∈XA $\in XA$∈X, check: does X−{A}X - \\{A\\}X−{A} still have closure =R= R=R?
     * If yes, remove AAA from XXX.
     * If no, AAA is essential — keep it.

Worked example, continuing from above (R(A,B,C,D,E)R(A, B, C, D, E)R(A,B,C,D,E) with FDs A→BA $\rightarrow BA$$\rightarrow DBC$

Start with {A,B,C,D,E}\\{A, B, C, D, E\\}{A,B,C,D,E} (all attributes). Clearly this is a superkey. Now prune:

* Drop E? {A,B,C,D}+=?\\{A, B, C, D\\}^+ = ?{A,B,C,D}+=? {A,B,C,D}\\{A,B,C,D\\}{A,B,C,D} already includes DDD, and D→ED $\rightarrow ED$→E, so closure is {A,B,C,D,E}\\{A,B,C,D,E\\}{A,B,C,D,E}. Superkey. Remove EEE.
* Drop D? {A,B,C}+=?\\{A, B, C\\}^+ = ?{A,B,C}+=? A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B already covered. BC→DBC $\rightarrow DBC$→D — {B,C}⊆{A,B,C}\\{B,C\\} $\subseteq \\{A,B,C\\}{B,C}$⊆{A,B,C}, so DDD added. Then D→ED \rightarrow ED→E, so EEE added. Closure === all five. Superkey. Remove DDD.
* Drop B? {A,C}+\\{A, C\\}^+{A,C}+ — we computed this above: all five attributes. Superkey. Remove BBB.
* Drop A? {C}+\\{C\\}^+{C}+ — C alone, no FDs fire, closure is {C}\\{C\\}{C}. Not a superkey. Keep AAA.
* Drop C? {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ — A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B gives {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B}. BC→DBC $\rightarrow DBC$→D requires CCC, so it doesn't fire. Closure is {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B}. Not a superkey. Keep CCC.

Candidate key: {A,C}\\{A, C\\}{A,C}. Minimal superkey — removing either AAA or CCC destroys the superkey property.

If you need to find _all_ candidate keys (not just one), you search systematically: try attribute subsets of increasing size, checking closures. For small schemas, this is feasible by hand. For large schemas, it is done mechanically.

### 6.4 Minimal Cover

When you have many FDs, some of them are redundant. A **minimal cover** (or _canonical cover_) of a set of FDs FFF is a smallest-possible set FcF_cFc of FDs that logically implies the same constraints.

> ### Definition: Minimal Cover

A set FcF_cFc is a **minimal cover** of FFF if:

  1. **Same implications:** every FD implied by FFF is also implied by FcF_cFc, and vice versa.
  2. **Single attribute on the right:** every FD in FcF_cFc has exactly one attribute on the right-hand side.
  3. **No extraneous attributes on the left:** for every FD X→AX $\rightarrow AX$→A in FcF_cFc, there is no proper subset X′⊊XX' $\subsetneq XX$′⊊X such that X′→AX' $\rightarrow AX$′→A would also be implied by FcF_cFc.
  4. **No redundant FDs:** removing any FD from FcF_cFc changes the set of implications.

#### Algorithm: Computing a Minimal Cover
    
    
    1. Decompose each FD X -> Y into separate FDs X -> A for each attribute A of Y.
    2. For each FD X -> A, for each attribute B in X:
         if (X - {B}) -> A is implied by the current FD set, remove B from X.
    3. Remove each redundant FD (one whose removal doesn't change the implications).
    

$\rightarrow BC, B \rightarrow C, AB \rightarrow D\\}F={A$

**Step 1** (single attributes on the right): F1={A→B,A→C,B→C,AB→D}F_1 = \\{A $\rightarrow B, A \rightarrow C, B \rightarrow C, AB \rightarrow D\\}F1={A$→B,A→C,B→C,AB→D}.

**Step 2** (prune LHS extras). Examine AB→DAB $\rightarrow DAB$→D: is it implied by F1F_1F1 with BBB removed? {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ under F1F_1F1: start {A}\\{A\\}{A}, apply A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B add BBB, apply B→CB $\rightarrow CB$→C add CCC. Closure = {A,B,C}\\{A, B, C\\}{A,B,C}. Does it include DDD? No. What about removing AAA? {B}+={B,C}\\{B\\}^+ = \\{B, C\\}{B}+={B,C}. No DDD. Neither side can be removed; AB→DAB \rightarrow DAB→D stays as-is.

**Step 3** (remove redundant FDs). Examine each FD for redundancy:

* Remove A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B? Compute {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ in F1−{A→B}F_1 - \\{A $\rightarrow B\\}F1$−{A→B}. Start {A}\\{A\\}{A}. A→CA $\rightarrow CA$→C gives {A,C}\\{A,C\\}{A,C}. B→CB \rightarrow CB→C doesn't fire (BBB not in set). AB→DAB \rightarrow DAB→D doesn't fire. Closure = {A,C}\\{A, C\\}{A,C}. Missing BBB. So A→BA \rightarrow BA→B is _not_ redundant — keep it.
* Remove A→CA $\rightarrow CA$→C? Compute {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ in F1−{A→C}F_1 - \\{A $\rightarrow C\\}F1$−{A→C}. Start {A}\\{A\\}{A}. A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B gives {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B}. B→CB \rightarrow CB→C gives {A,B,C}\\{A,B,C\\}{A,B,C}. So CCC is derivable via A→BA \rightarrow BA→B then B→CB \rightarrow CB→C. A→CA \rightarrow CA→C _is_ redundant — remove it.
* Remove B→CB $\rightarrow CB$→C? Compute {B}+\\{B\\}^+{B}+ in F1−{B→C}F_1 - \\{B $\rightarrow C\\}F1$−{B→C}. Start {B}\\{B\\}{B}. No FDs fire. Closure = {B}\\{B\\}{B}. Missing CCC. Not redundant — keep.
* Remove AB→DAB $\rightarrow DAB$→D? {A,B}+\\{A, B\\}^+{A,B}+ without it: {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B} grows via A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B, B→CB $\rightarrow CB$→C: closure = {A,B,C}\\{A,B,C\\}{A,B,C}. Missing DDD. Not redundant — keep.

**Final minimal cover:** Fc={A→B,B→C,AB→D}F_c = \\{A $\rightarrow B, B \rightarrow C, AB \rightarrow D\\}Fc={A$→B,B→C,AB→D}.

This cover is what we use for normalization decisions. Normal-form definitions reference the "non-trivial FDs"; having them in minimal form keeps the analysis clean.

### 6.5 Normal Forms: 1NF, 2NF, 3NF, BCNF

Normal forms are progressively stricter conditions on a schema. Each higher form rules out more classes of anomalies by requiring the schema to avoid certain structural patterns.

> ### Definition: First Normal Form (1NF)

A relation is in **1NF** if every attribute value is atomic — no arrays, no repeating groups, no nested relations as column values. Every cell holds a single indivisible value.

In plain language, 1NF is "flat tables only." A column cannot hold `['a', 'b', 'c']` or `{name: 'Alice', age: 20}`. If you need multiple values, you need a separate row or a separate table.

1NF is the baseline for every relational schema. The relational model assumes it. The ER-mapping Rule 2 (multi-valued attributes become their own table) is the operational cure.

Subtle point: SQL databases support ARRAY and JSON column types, which technically violate 1NF. These are pragmatic concessions that work well for _opaque_ data (you store the array as a blob, never query inside it) but undermine relational reasoning when the inside of the array has meaning.

> ### Definition: Second Normal Form (2NF)

A relation is in **2NF** if it is in 1NF and every non-key attribute is fully functionally dependent on the entire primary key (not just part of it).

In plain language, 2NF rules out **partial dependencies** — where a non-key attribute depends on only part of a composite primary key.

2NF is only interesting when the primary key is composite. If the primary key is a single attribute, 2NF is automatic (there is no "part" of a single attribute to depend on).

#### Example of a 2NF Violation

Consider:
    
    
    BadEnrollments(student_id, course_id, student_name, course_title, grade)
    Primary key: (student_id, course_id)
    

FDs:

* {student_id}→{student_name}\\{$\text{student\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{student\\_name}\\}{student_id}$→{student_name} — partial dep (depends only on part of the PK)
* {course_id}→{course_title}\\{$\text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{course\\_title}\\}{course_id}$→{course_title} — partial dep
* {student_id,course_id}→{grade}\\{$\text{student\\_id}, \text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{grade}\\}{student_id,course_id}$→{grade} — full dep (depends on the whole PK)

`student_name` is fully determined by `student_id` alone; it does not need `course_id` to be determined. That is a partial dependency, and it is what causes redundancy — Alice's name appears in every one of her enrollment rows.

**Fix (decomposition):**
    
    
    Students(student_id, student_name)  -- student_name is fully dependent on student_id
    Courses(course_id, course_title)    -- course_title fully dependent on course_id
    Enrollments(student_id, course_id, grade)  -- grade fully dependent on the pair
    

After this decomposition, every table is in 2NF: every non-key attribute depends on the whole primary key.

> ### Definition: Third Normal Form (3NF)

A relation is in **3NF** if it is in 2NF and there are no **transitive dependencies** of non-key attributes on the key. Formally: for every non-trivial FD X→AX $\rightarrow AX$→A in the relation, either:

  1. XXX is a superkey, OR
  2. AAA is part of some candidate key (i.e., AAA is a **prime attribute**).

In plain language, 3NF rules out "non-key attribute determining another non-key attribute" — the transitive dependency pattern.

#### Example of a 3NF Violation

Consider:
    
    
    Courses(course_id, title, credits, instructor_id, instructor_name, instructor_office)
    PK: course_id
    

FDs:

* {course_id}→{title, credits, instructor_id, instructor_name, instructor_office}\\{$\text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{title, credits, instructor\\_id, instructor\\_name, instructor\\_office}\\}{course_id}$→{title, credits, instructor_id, instructor_name, instructor_office} — all full deps on PK
* {instructor_id}→{instructor_name, instructor_office}\\{$\text{instructor\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{instructor\\_name, instructor\\_office}\\}{instructor_id}$→{instructor_name, instructor_office} — a _transitive_ dep: `instructor_name` depends on `instructor_id`, which is not a superkey.

`instructor_id` is not a superkey (it doesn't determine `course_id` and all the course-specific info). So the FD instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$ has:

* LHS not a superkey (fail condition 1).
* RHS (`instructor_name`) not part of any candidate key (fail condition 2; `course_id` is the only candidate key).

3NF violated.

**Fix (decomposition):**
    
    
    Instructors(instructor_id, instructor_name, instructor_office)
    Courses(course_id, title, credits, instructor_id)
    

Now every FD has the "X is a superkey" property:

* In Instructors: instructor_id→instructor_name, instructor_office$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name, instructor\\_office}instructor_id$→instructor_name, instructor_office — LHS is the superkey.
* In Courses: course_id→title, credits, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{title, credits, instructor\\_id}course_id$→title, credits, instructor_id — LHS is the superkey.

Both tables are in 3NF.

> ### Definition: Boyce-Codd Normal Form (BCNF)

A relation is in **BCNF** if for every non-trivial FD X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y, XXX is a superkey.

BCNF is 3NF with the "or RHS is prime" escape hatch removed. It is strictly stronger than 3NF.

#### When Does BCNF Differ From 3NF?

Most practical schemas that are in 3NF are also in BCNF. The difference appears when the schema has **overlapping candidate keys** and an FD where the RHS is a prime attribute but the LHS is not a full candidate key.

Classic example:
    
    
    R(city, street, zip)
    

> **Domain caveat — read this before the example.** The following analysis assumes US 5-digit ZIP codes, where every ZIP lies in exactly one city. This FD (`zip → city`) **fails for UK postcodes, German PLZ (Postleitzahl), Canadian postal codes, and most other postal systems** , where a single code spans multiple localities or municipalities. Functional dependencies are _domain-specific claims_ , not logical truths. Before applying normalization to real data, verify that the FDs you're using actually hold in your region, organization, and time period. The ZIP example is popular in textbooks precisely because it's clean and US-centric; it has misled many international students into designing broken schemas.

Suppose — **assuming a postal system where each zip lies in exactly one city, such as US ZIP codes** :

* {city, street}→{zip}\\{$\text{city, street}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{zip}\\}{city, street}$→{zip} (each address has a unique zip)
* {zip}→{city}\\{$\text{zip}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{city}\\}{zip}$→{city} (each zip lies in exactly one city)

Candidate keys: {city, street}\\{$\text{city, street}\\}{city, street} and {zip, street}\\{\text{zip, street}\\}{zip, street} (both determine all attributes).$

FD zip$\text{zip} \rightarrow \text{city}zip$: LHS `zip` is not a superkey (on its own, zip doesn't determine street). RHS `city` is prime (it's part of the first candidate key). So the FD satisfies 3NF (condition 2) but violates BCNF (needs LHS to be a superkey).

Is this a "real" violation? It still causes redundancy: every row with the same zip must repeat the same city. Change a city's zip assignment and you must update every row.

BCNF decomposition:
    
    
    ZipCity(zip, city)
    StreetZip(street, zip)
    

But now we've lost the original FD {city, street}→{zip}\\{$\text{city, street}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{zip}\\}{city, street}$→{zip} — it cannot be enforced by any single table in the decomposition. This is the tradeoff: BCNF decomposition sometimes loses FDs, which means the decomposition is _not dependency-preserving_ even though it _is_ lossless-join.

#### 3NF vs BCNF: The Pragmatic Choice

* **3NF** is always achievable without losing any FD (there's a guaranteed algorithm).
* **BCNF** is always achievable in a lossless-join way, but may require losing FDs.

Practical consensus:

* Aim for BCNF where you can.
* Settle for 3NF when BCNF would lose a meaningful constraint.
* In either case, most operational schemas built via ER modeling land in BCNF naturally, so the distinction rarely bites you in practice.

### 6.6 Lossless-Join Decomposition and Dependency Preservation

When we decompose a relation to satisfy a normal form, we need two properties of the decomposition to hold.

#### Lossless-Join Decomposition

> ### Definition: Lossless-Join Decomposition

A decomposition of relation RRR into relations R1,R2,…,RnR_1, R_2, $\ldots, R_nR1,R2,$…,Rn is **lossless-join** if for every valid instance of RRR, the natural join R1⋈R2⋈⋯⋈RnR_1 \bowtie R_2 \bowtie $\cdots \bowtie R_nR1$⋈R2⋈⋯⋈Rn reproduces RRR exactly.

In plain language, lossless-join means "we can reconstruct the original without inventing or losing tuples when we join back." This is non-negotiable — any decomposition we use must be lossless-join, or we have changed the data's meaning.

The **lossless-join test** for a two-relation decomposition R→(R1,R2)R $\rightarrow (R_1, R_2)R$→(R1,R2): the decomposition is lossless-join iff the common attributes R1∩R2R_1 $\cap R_2R1$∩R2 form a superkey of _at least one_ of R1R_1R1 or R2R_2R2. That is: R1∩R2→R1R_1 $\cap R_2 \rightarrow R_1R1$∩R2→R1 or R1∩R2→R2R_1 \cap R_2 \rightarrow R_2R1∩R2→R2.

#### Dependency Preservation

> ### Definition: Dependency-Preserving Decomposition

A decomposition is **dependency-preserving** if every FD in the original FFF can be enforced by checking only the FDs that apply within each decomposed relation — without having to join the tables together.

In plain language, dependency preservation means "after decomposition, the database engine can still check every FD using local table constraints." If the decomposition is not dependency-preserving, enforcing some FDs requires joining tables at insert/update time, which is expensive and often impossible to do atomically.

BCNF decompositions are always lossless-join but not always dependency-preserving. 3NF decompositions (via the standard synthesis algorithm) are always both.

### 6.7 Thread A: Proving the Schema is Anomaly-Free

Let's formally verify that Thread A's Chapter 3 schema is in BCNF.
    
    
    Students(id PK, name, email UNIQUE)
    Instructors(id PK, name, office, phone)
    Courses(id PK, title, credits, instructor_id FK → Instructors)
    Enrollments(student_id FK, course_id FK, grade, PK (student_id, course_id))
    

**Students.** FDs:

* id$\text{id} \rightarrow \text{id, name, email}id$ (id is primary key)
* email$\text{email} \rightarrow \text{id, name, email}email$ (email is UNIQUE, so it's a candidate key)

Both LHS's are superkeys. BCNF ✓.

**Instructors.** FD: id$\text{id} \rightarrow \text{id, name, office, phone}id$. LHS is the PK. BCNF ✓.

**Courses.** FD: id→id, title, credits, instructor_id$\text{id} \rightarrow \text{id, title, credits, instructor\\_id}id$→id, title, credits, instructor_id. LHS is the PK. BCNF ✓.

Are there any other FDs? Could `title` determine `instructor_id`? No — two courses can have the same title taught by different instructors over time. Could `title` determine `credits`? Maybe in some domain, but not generally. We assume no other FDs. BCNF ✓.

**Enrollments.** FD: {student_id,course_id}→grade\\{$\text{student\\_id}, \text{course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{grade}{student_id,course_id}$→grade. LHS is the composite PK. BCNF ✓.

Could there be others? grade$\text{grade} \rightarrow \text{student\\_id}grade$? No — many students share the same grade. student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_id}student_id$? No — one student takes many courses. The only non-trivial FD is the one from the PK to `grade`.

All four tables are in BCNF. Therefore, by the 3NF/BCNF anomaly theorems:

* **No update anomaly** — each fact lives in one row.
* **No insertion anomaly** — we can insert facts about students, instructors, courses independently.
* **No deletion anomaly** — deleting an enrollment doesn't erase unrelated facts.
* **Lossless-join** — we can reconstruct any multi-table query by joining.
* **Dependency-preserving** — every FD holds within a single table's PK constraint.

This is exactly what Chapter 2 showed us _needed_ to happen, and what Chapter 3 delivered structurally. Chapter 6 now gives us the formal proof.

### 6.8 When to Denormalize

Normalization theory says "go to BCNF, always." Real engineering says "mostly yes, sometimes no." Let's be specific about when.

#### Denormalization Cases Where It's Correct

  1. **Historical snapshots.** `Orders.item_price_snapshot` denormalizes the current item price into the order — but intentionally, because the historical price at order time is an independent fact. Not an anomaly source, because updates to the `Items` table should _not_ propagate.

  2. **Analytical / OLAP tables.** Star schemas (Chapter 19) deliberately denormalize dimensions into fact tables. The data is rewritten in batch; no concurrent in-place updates; no anomaly exposure.

  3. **Performance-critical read paths.** A product detail page that reads 5 related tables 10 million times a day may materialize the needed data into a denormalized view, refreshed periodically. Cost: write complexity. Gain: 10-100× faster reads.

  4. **Hierarchical closures.** Storing a materialized "ancestors" list on a tree node is redundant (you could recompute from the tree), but makes descendant queries O(1) instead of O(depth).

  5. **Counters.** `Posts.like_count` stored alongside `Posts` is redundant (you could `COUNT(*)` from Likes), but avoids aggregating millions of rows on every page load. Maintained by trigger or app code.

#### Denormalization Cases That Are Usually Bugs

  1. **"It saves a join."** Saving a join is rarely worth taking on anomalies. Most joins are cheap with proper indexes (Chapter 10). Premature denormalization is a common mistake.

  2. **"The ORM is slow."** If your ORM issues N+1 queries, fix the ORM usage or add an index. Denormalizing the schema to hide ORM inefficiency pushes the complexity into a worse place.

  3. **"We might need this data in one place eventually."** Speculative denormalization is speculative design. Wait for a real workload signal.

#### The Principle

**Denormalize with eyes open.** Any denormalization trades anomaly-proof structure for some other benefit. Document the trade. Maintain the redundancy via automated mechanisms (triggers, app logic, batch rebuilds). Test that the denormalized data stays consistent. When you later forget why the redundancy exists, the documentation is what saves you.

#### Common Misconception

"Normalization is a performance sin — real databases should always be denormalized for speed." This is overstated. In a modern relational database with proper indexes, 3NF/BCNF schemas typically _outperform_ naive denormalizations, because normalized tables are smaller, better cached, and joined efficiently. Denormalization wins only for specific workloads (read-heavy, analytical, write-controlled) — not as a default. Starting with normalization and denormalizing only where data shows it's needed is almost always the right posture.

### 6.9 The 3NF Synthesis Algorithm (Optional Depth)

For completeness, here is the canonical algorithm for producing a 3NF decomposition from a set of FDs. It is useful when you need to design a schema rigorously from a given set of constraints.

**Input:** relation RRR with attribute set UUU, FDs FFF.

**Output:** a set of relations, each in 3NF, whose decomposition is lossless-join and dependency-preserving.
    
    
    1. Compute minimal cover F_c of F.
    2. For each FD X -> A in F_c, if X has not yet produced a relation,
       create relation R_X with schema X ∪ A.
       Otherwise merge A into the existing R_X relation.
    3. If no relation created so far includes a candidate key of R, create
       one more relation containing exactly the attributes of a candidate
       key of R.
    4. Eliminate any relation whose attributes are a subset of another
       relation's attributes.
    

The result is a set of relations, each in 3NF, with dependency preservation and lossless-join guaranteed.

For BCNF, there is no universal synthesis algorithm that guarantees dependency preservation — you decompose iteratively, and accept that some FDs may cease to be enforceable within a single relation. Most production schemas choose 3NF-or-better when the choice matters.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Higher normal forms trade query convenience for update safety.** A fully decomposed schema has no update anomalies, but every non-trivial report needs joins. Reports are CPU; anomalies are data loss. The trade is rarely close for transactional systems.
