* **BCNF trades dependency preservation for a stronger anomaly guarantee.** BCNF removes every redundancy caused by a non-trivial FD, but some FDs may span the decomposed tables and require a CHECK or trigger to enforce. 3NF keeps all FDs inside single tables at the cost of allowing a narrow class of redundancy.
* **The 3NF synthesis algorithm trades local optimization for a lossless, dependency-preserving guarantee.** It mechanically produces a correct decomposition from any FD set — the schema may not be the one a human would design, but it is provably sound.
* **Denormalization trades anomaly immunity for read speed or historical accuracy.** A snapshot column (`item_price_snapshot`), a counter (`like_count`), or a materialized closure all deliberately duplicate data. Acceptable when updates do not propagate or are maintained mechanically; dangerous as a casual "saves a join" optimization.
* **Functional-dependency reasoning trades manual inspection for algorithmic closure computation.** Computing X+X^+X+ and checking a minimal cover are mechanical; eyeballing whether a schema is in BCNF is not. Once the FDs are right, everything downstream — key discovery, normalization, decomposition — is a deterministic computation.
* **Normalization as a default trades short-term performance intuition for long-term correctness.** Starting at 3NF/BCNF and denormalizing only where a measured workload demands it almost always beats starting denormalized — the anomalies you avoid never become incidents, and modern engines usually join normalized tables faster than people expect.

### 6.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** For each of the following, say whether it is a functional dependency in the Chapter 3 university schema, and briefly justify. (a) {Students.id}→{Students.name}\\{\text{Students.id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{Students.name}\\}{Students.id}→{Students.name} (b) {Students.name}→{Students.id}\\{$\text{Students.name}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{Students.id}\\}{Students.name}$→{Students.id} (c) {Students.email}→{Students.id}\\{$\text{Students.email}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{Students.id}\\}{Students.email}$→{Students.id} (d) {Enrollments.student_id}→{Enrollments.grade}\\{$\text{Enrollments.student\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \\{\text{Enrollments.grade}\\}{Enrollments.student_id}$→{Enrollments.grade}

**Answer:** (a) **Yes.** Each student id corresponds to one student, who has one name. (b) **No.** Two students can share a name. (c) **Yes.** Email is declared UNIQUE NOT NULL, so each email corresponds to at most one student and thus determines the id. (d) **No.** A student (say id = 1001) has many enrollments in different courses, each potentially with a different grade. `student_id` alone does not determine `grade`; the pair `(student_id, course_id)` does.

**Q2.** Compute {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ for R(A,B,C,D)R(A, B, C, D)R(A,B,C,D) with FDs F={A→B,B→C,C→D}F = \\{A $\rightarrow B, B \rightarrow C, C \rightarrow D\\}F={A$→B,B→C,C→D}.

**Answer:** Start: {A}\\{A\\}{A}. A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B: A is in the set, add BBB. {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B}. B→CB $\rightarrow CB$→C: BBB is in the set, add CCC. {A,B,C}\\{A, B, C\\}{A,B,C}. C→DC $\rightarrow DC$→D: CCC is in the set, add DDD. {A,B,C,D}\\{A, B, C, D\\}{A,B,C,D}. No more FDs apply.

{A}+={A,B,C,D}\\{A\\}^+ = \\{A, B, C, D\\}{A}+={A,B,C,D}. So AAA is a superkey (actually a candidate key, since no proper subset works).

**Q3.** State the definitions of 2NF, 3NF, and BCNF. For each, give a one-sentence description of what kind of redundancy it eliminates.

**Answer:**

* **2NF:** every non-key attribute is fully dependent on the whole primary key (no partial dependencies on composite keys). _Eliminates redundancy from non-key attributes depending on only part of a composite key._
* **3NF:** 2NF plus every non-trivial FD X→AX $\rightarrow AX$→A has either XXX as a superkey or AAA as a prime attribute. _Eliminates redundancy from transitive dependencies (non-key → non-key)._
* **BCNF:** every non-trivial FD X→AX $\rightarrow AX$→A has XXX as a superkey. _Eliminates all "non-superkey determines something" redundancies — stronger than 3NF._

**Q4.** A relation has FDs {A→B,B→C,C→A}\\{A $\rightarrow B, B \rightarrow C, C \rightarrow A\\}{A$→B,B→C,C→A}. What are its candidate keys? Is it in BCNF?

**Answer:** First, compute closures of singletons:

$\rightarrow BA$
$\rightarrow CB$
$\rightarrow AC$

All three singletons are superkeys, and each is minimal (cannot be shrunk — they're already single attributes). So there are **three candidate keys** : {A}\\{A\\}{A}, {B}\\{B\\}{B}, {C}\\{C\\}{C}.

For BCNF: every non-trivial FD must have a superkey LHS.

* A→BA $\rightarrow BA$→B: LHS AAA is a superkey. ✓
* B→CB $\rightarrow CB$→C: LHS BBB is a superkey. ✓
* C→AC $\rightarrow AC$→A: LHS CCC is a superkey. ✓

All FDs have superkey LHS. The relation is in **BCNF**.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Consider the relation:
    
    
    EnrollmentLog(student_id, student_name, course_id, course_title,
                  instructor_id, instructor_name, enrollment_date, grade)
    

with FDs:

* student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$
* course_id→course_title, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_title, instructor\\_id}course_id$→course_title, instructor_id
* instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$
* {student_id, course_id}→enrollment_date, grade\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{enrollment\\_date, grade}{student_id, course_id}$→enrollment_date, grade

(a) What are the candidate keys? (b) Is this schema in 2NF? 3NF? BCNF? Explain. (c) Decompose into BCNF. Check that your decomposition is lossless-join and dependency-preserving.

Hint

(a) Start with the attributes that are never on the right-hand side of any FD — those must be in every candidate key.

**Answer:**

**(a) Candidate keys.** `student_id` and `course_id` never appear on any RHS. They must be in every candidate key. Compute {student_id, course_id}+\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\}^+{student_id, course_id}+:$

* Start: {student_id, course_id}\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\}{student_id, course_id}.$
* student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$: add `student_name`.
* course_id→course_title, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_title, instructor\\_id}course_id$→course_title, instructor_id: add those.
* instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$: add `instructor_name`.
* {student_id, course_id}→enrollment_date, grade\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{enrollment\\_date, grade}{student_id, course_id}$→enrollment_date, grade: add those.

Closure covers all attributes. {student_id, course_id}\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\}{student_id, course_id} is a superkey, and it's minimal (neither alone suffices). So **the unique candidate key is** {student_id, course_id}\\{\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\}{student_id, course_id}.$

**(b) Normal forms.**

* **2NF?** `student_name` depends only on `student_id` (a partial dep on the composite key). Violation. Not in 2NF.
* **3NF?** Fails 2NF, so fails 3NF by inclusion.
* **BCNF?** Fails 3NF, so fails BCNF.

**(c) BCNF decomposition.** Decompose on each problematic FD:

* Separate `student_name` out via student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$:
        
        Students(student_id, student_name)
        

* Separate `course_title`, `instructor_id` via course_id→course_title, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_title, instructor\\_id}course_id$→course_title, instructor_id:
        
        Courses(course_id, course_title, instructor_id)
        

* Separate `instructor_name` via instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$:
        
        Instructors(instructor_id, instructor_name)
        

* Remainder: 
        
        Enrollments(student_id, course_id, enrollment_date, grade)
        

Check BCNF on each:

* Students: only FD is student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$; LHS is PK. ✓
* Courses: course_id→course_title, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_title, instructor\\_id}course_id$→course_title, instructor_id; LHS is PK. ✓
* Instructors: instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$; LHS is PK. ✓
* Enrollments: {student_id, course_id}→enrollment_date, grade\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{enrollment\\_date, grade}{student_id, course_id}$→enrollment_date, grade; LHS is PK. ✓

**Lossless-join:** joining the four tables back on their shared attributes (student_id linking Students ↔ Enrollments; course_id linking Courses ↔ Enrollments; instructor_id linking Instructors ↔ Courses) reconstructs the original relation. Lossless because each decomposition step had a shared attribute that was a superkey of one side. ✓

**Dependency-preserving:** each original FD is enforceable within a single decomposed table:

* student_id$\text{student\\_id} \rightarrow \text{student\\_name}student_id$: enforced in Students.
* course_id→course_title, instructor_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{course\\_title, instructor\\_id}course_id$→course_title, instructor_id: enforced in Courses.
* instructor_id$\text{instructor\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_name}instructor_id$: enforced in Instructors.
* {student_id, course_id}→enrollment_date, grade\\{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{enrollment\\_date, grade}{student_id, course_id}$→enrollment_date, grade: enforced in Enrollments. ✓

**Cross-chapter connection:** This result is nearly identical to Thread A's Chapter 3 schema — which we arrived at by intuition in Chapter 3, by ER modeling in Chapter 5, and now by normalization theory in Chapter 6. Three different paths, one answer. That convergence is confirmation the answer is robust.

**Q2.** The denormalized e-commerce row from Chapter 2 Exercise 2.10 Q2:
    
    
    OrderLog(order_id, customer_name, customer_email, item_id, item_name,
             item_price, quantity, ship_to_address, order_date)
    

with plausible FDs:

* order_id→customer_name, customer_email, ship_to_address, order_date$\text{order\\_id} \rightarrow \text{customer\\_name, customer\\_email, ship\\_to\\_address, order\\_date}order_id$→customer_name, customer_email, ship_to_address, order_date
* item_id$\text{item\\_id} \rightarrow \text{item\\_name, item\\_price}item_id$ (using current item price, not historical)
* customer_email$\text{customer\\_email} \rightarrow \text{customer\\_name}customer_email$
* {order_id, item_id}→quantity\\{$\text{order\\_id, item\\_id}\\} \rightarrow \text{quantity}{order_id, item_id}$→quantity

(a) What are the candidate keys? (b) Decompose into BCNF. (c) The Chapter 3 exercise solution introduced a `price_snapshot` column that duplicates `item_price` at order time. Which FD does `price_snapshot` violate, and why is keeping it still the right design decision?

Hint

(c) asks you to reason about when controlled redundancy is superior to strict normalization.

**Answer:** **(a) Candidate keys.** `order_id` and `item_id` don't appear on any RHS, so they must be in every candidate key. {order_id, item_id}+\\{$\text{order\\_id, item\\_id}\\}^+{order_id, item_id}+: from the first FD, add customer_*, ship_to_address, order_date; from the second, add item_name, item_price; from the fourth, add quantity. Closure is all attributes. {order_id, item_id}\\{\text{order\\_id, item\\_id}\\}{order_id, item_id} is the unique candidate key.$

**(b) BCNF decomposition.**
    
    
    Customers(customer_email, customer_name)           -- from customer_email -> customer_name
    Orders(order_id, customer_email, ship_to_address, order_date)
                                                      -- ship_to_address is arguably per-order, not per-customer
    Items(item_id, item_name, item_price)
    OrderItems(order_id, item_id, quantity)
    

All four are in BCNF (each non-trivial FD has a superkey LHS within its respective table).

**(c)`price_snapshot` and the design decision.** If we add a `price_snapshot` column to `OrderItems`, it duplicates `Items.item_price` at the time of the order. The FD "item_id → item_price" would no longer hold in a combined view (different rows could have different price_snapshots for the same item).

Is this a normalization violation? In the strict sense, yes — `price_snapshot` is derivable from (item_id, order_date) if we maintain price history, so it could be lifted out. But the pragmatic answer: **the historical price is semantically independent from the current price.** Once an order is placed, its item price must not change, even when the item's current price changes. Treating `price_snapshot` as a distinct fact (a per-OrderItem attribute) is not a redundancy mistake — it's a deliberate modeling choice that captures immutable historical state.

A stricter alternative is to maintain a `PriceHistory(item_id, effective_from, price)` table and always look up the historical price by (item_id, order_date). This is more elegant from a normalization standpoint but operationally slower and more complex. Most real e-commerce systems choose the `price_snapshot` approach as a pragmatic trade.

This is a clean example of "denormalization as capture of historical state" — Section 6.8, case 1.

**Q3.** Find a minimal cover of F={AB→C,A→D,D→C,AB→D}F = \\{AB $\rightarrow C, A \rightarrow D, D \rightarrow C, AB \rightarrow D\\}F={AB$→C,A→D,D→C,AB→D}.

Hint

Step 1: single-attribute RHS. Step 2: prune LHS extras. Step 3: remove redundant FDs. Use attribute closure.

**Answer:**

**Step 1.** FDs already have single-attribute RHS. F1={AB→C,A→D,D→C,AB→D}F_1 = \\{AB $\rightarrow C, A \rightarrow D, D \rightarrow C, AB \rightarrow D\\}F1={AB$→C,A→D,D→C,AB→D}.

**Step 2.** Prune extraneous LHS attributes.

* AB→CAB $\rightarrow CAB$→C: Can we drop B? {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ in F1F_1F1: {A}\\{A\\}{A} → A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D → {A,D}\\{A,D\\}{A,D} → D→CD $\rightarrow CD$→C → {A,C,D}\\{A, C, D\\}{A,C,D}. Includes CCC. So A→CA \rightarrow CA→C is implied; drop BBB from the LHS. Replace AB→CAB \rightarrow CAB→C with A→CA \rightarrow CA→C.
* AB→DAB $\rightarrow DAB$→D: Can we drop B? {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ same as above, which includes DDD. So A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D is implied; drop BBB. Replace AB→DAB $\rightarrow DAB$→D with A→DA \rightarrow DA→D (already present).

After Step 2: F2={A→C,A→D,D→C}F_2 = \\{A $\rightarrow C, A \rightarrow D, D \rightarrow C\\}F2={A$→C,A→D,D→C} (with the A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D duplicate removed).

**Step 3.** Remove redundant FDs.

* Is A→CA $\rightarrow CA$→C redundant? {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ without it, in {A→D,D→C}\\{A $\rightarrow D, D \rightarrow C\\}{A$→D,D→C}: {A}\\{A\\}{A} → D → CCC. Includes CCC. **Redundant — remove.**
* Is A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D redundant? Without it, {A}+\\{A\\}^+{A}+ in {D→C}\\{D $\rightarrow C\\}{D$→C}: {A}\\{A\\}{A} (nothing fires). Doesn't include DDD. Not redundant.
* Is D→CD $\rightarrow CD$→C redundant? Without it, {D}+\\{D\\}^+{D}+ in {A→D}\\{A $\rightarrow D\\}{A$→D}: {D}\\{D\\}{D}. Doesn't include CCC. Not redundant.

**Minimal cover:** Fc={A→D,D→C}F_c = \\{A $\rightarrow D, D \rightarrow C\\}Fc={A$→D,D→C}.

Sanity check: does this imply the original AB→CAB $\rightarrow CAB$→C? {AB}+\\{AB\\}^+{AB}+ under FcF_cFc: {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B} → A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D → {A,B,D}\\{A, B, D\\}{A,B,D} → D→CD $\rightarrow CD$→C → {A,B,C,D}\\{A, B, C, D\\}{A,B,C,D}. Yes, CCC is derivable. Check.

Does it imply AB→DAB $\rightarrow DAB$→D? {A,B}\\{A, B\\}{A,B} → A→DA $\rightarrow DA$→D → includes DDD. Check.

Two FDs captured all four originals. This is why minimal cover matters — it exposes the irreducible structure.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Normalization guarantees anomaly-freedom but sometimes produces schemas that require more joins. The query optimizer (Chapter 12) can efficiently execute joins when indexes are present. Is there still a workload where normalization is genuinely worse for performance? Describe it.

**Discussion:** Yes — analytical workloads over very large data.

Consider a data warehouse with a billion rows of fact data, joined to four or five dimension tables (customer, product, date, store). A fully normalized schema means every analytical query — "revenue by product category by month by region" — joins all five tables. With indexes, each join is efficient, but the join overhead multiplies: five joins over a billion fact rows is billions of index lookups.

The **star schema** (Chapter 19) is the OLAP answer: keep the fact table, but _denormalize_ the dimensions' frequently-used attributes into it. Product category lives in the fact table, not just in the Products dimension. Region lives in the fact table, not just in Stores. Now the analytical query reads only the fact table — no joins. The fact table is bigger (more bytes per row), but the query is dramatically faster because sequential scans of a fat fact table beat five-way joins at this scale.

The cost: anomalies on update. But OLAP workloads don't update the fact table in place — they rebuild it nightly from the normalized OLTP source. Anomaly exposure is zero because there are no live updates.

Lesson: normalization is right for OLTP (transactional, update-heavy, correctness-critical). Denormalization is right for OLAP (read-heavy, batch-updated, performance-critical). Chapter 19 shows this explicitly.

**Q2.** A coworker says "normal forms are a 1970s academic exercise — modern databases don't need them because we have indexes and query optimizers." What's the charitable read of this claim, and what's wrong with it?

**Discussion:**

**Charitable read:** modern query optimizers really _are_ much better than 1970s databases. Joins that would have required hand-tuning then are now optimized automatically. The purely-performance argument for denormalization is genuinely weaker than it once was. So the case "we need to denormalize for performance reasons" rings hollow much of the time.

**What's wrong:** normalization is not fundamentally about performance. It's about **correctness** — specifically, anomaly-freedom. A denormalized schema is not "slightly riskier" — it is a schema where certain kinds of data corruption are structurally possible, where a single logical fact may exist in many rows that can disagree. Indexes and optimizers don't help with that. They speed up _queries_ ; they don't enforce _invariants_.

Another way to put it: normalization is a statement about the shape of your data's truth. A normalized schema says "each fact is here, in one row, period." A denormalized schema says "each fact is here and also here and also here, and we hope they agree." The latter requires ongoing enforcement machinery (triggers, application code, batch reconciliation) that the former gets for free.

A mature engineer's posture: **normalize by default, denormalize with cause.** The cause should be a real performance or operational problem, not a speculative one. And when you denormalize, you're explicit about what enforcement replaces the structural guarantee. This posture wasn't available in 1970 because optimizers weren't good enough; it's the correct posture in 2026.

**Q3.** ER modeling (Chapter 5) and normalization (this chapter) can produce the same schema. Do they always agree? When might they disagree, and which should win?

**Discussion:** They usually agree for well-modeled domains, because both are tools for finding the same underlying structure — ER approaches it from "what are the entities?" and normalization from "what are the FDs?"

They disagree when:

  1. **Missing FDs in the mental model.** An ER model might not have explicitly represented a constraint like "zip → city". The schema mapped from that ER model might violate BCNF in a way the ER diagram doesn't capture. Normalization analysis catches it.

  2. **ER ambiguity.** ER notation is coarse — cardinalities and participation are shown, but fine-grained constraints like "subject to this rule, three columns must be unique together" are harder to express. Normalization's FD-based analysis is more precise.

  3. **Semantic vs syntactic thinking.** An ER model says "a Course has exactly one Instructor." Normalization says "course_id$\text{course\\_id} \rightarrow \text{instructor\\_id}course_id$." Both express the same rule, but phrased differently. The difference matters when the rule is subtle: "an instructor's office is their _current_ office, but we also want historical assignments." The ER model might draw the relationship naively; the normalization analysis forces you to decide whether `office` depends on `instructor_id` alone (current) or (instructor_id, time_period).

Which should win? Normalization wins as the _audit_. Use ER for first-pass design (fast, communicable, good for conversations), then audit with FD / normalization analysis (precise, catches subtleties, gives guarantees). If the two disagree, the disagreement is a signal — somewhere in the domain there's a rule that's in your head but not yet on paper. Write it down, then proceed.

The deeper pattern: multiple overlapping tools (ER, FDs, relational algebra, SQL) _should_ converge on the same answer for well-designed schemas. When they don't, you haven't finished understanding the domain yet.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter turned schema design into a formal discipline. A **functional dependency** X→YX $\rightarrow YX$→Y asserts "knowing X determines Y"; sets of FDs generate implication closures via **Armstrong's axioms** (reflexivity, augmentation, transitivity); **attribute closure** X+X^+X+ computes what a set of attributes determines; and **minimal cover** reduces a set of FDs to its irredundant core.

**Normal forms** give us structural conditions that eliminate anomaly classes:

* **1NF** — atomic values; no nested structures.
* **2NF** — no partial dependencies on composite keys.
* **3NF** — no transitive dependencies of non-keys on keys (or RHS is prime).
* **BCNF** — every non-trivial FD has a superkey LHS.

**Decomposition** must be lossless-join (the original is reconstructible by joining) and ideally dependency-preserving (every FD is enforceable locally). BCNF is always achievable as lossless-join, sometimes at the cost of dependency preservation; 3NF is always achievable as both.

Thread A's Chapter 3 schema was formally verified BCNF — every table's non-trivial FDs have superkey LHS, every anomaly class is structurally impossible. The schema that emerged from intuition (Ch 3), from ER modeling (Ch 5), and from normalization theory (this chapter) agrees. That convergence is the validation.

Denormalization is a deliberate trade against anomaly-proof structure, correct in specific workloads (historical snapshots, OLAP, hot read paths) and usually a mistake in others. The right posture: normalize by default, denormalize with documented cause and explicit enforcement.

Looking ahead: the next three chapters are the relational pipeline's deeper machinery. Chapter 7 introduces **relational algebra** , the mathematical operators that SQL compiles to and that query optimizers think in. Chapter 8 covers advanced SQL (subqueries, views, window functions, CTEs). Chapter 9 opens the black box of storage. Normalization is the structural correctness layer; the next chapters tell us how that structure is executed efficiently.

---

## Chapter 7: Relational Algebra

So far we have asked questions of relations in SQL. SQL is the language everyone writes. But SQL is not the language the query _optimizer_ thinks in. When PostgreSQL or MySQL reads your query, it immediately translates it into a more austere, more mathematical language — **relational algebra** — and then reasons about that internal form: "what if I swap these two operators? what if I push this filter down? what if I choose this join order?"

Relational algebra is the lingua franca of the database internals. Every optimization chapter after this one (especially Chapters 11 and 12) will reason in algebraic terms. Every textbook explanation of "why did the query planner choose this plan?" will start with "the algebra is…". Without relational algebra, the rest of the book has no vocabulary.

This chapter is also the first appearance of **Thread B: The Join That Didn't Come Back** — a query that runs for 20 minutes on a 100,000-row database. We will meet it here as an algebraic expression, study its structure, discover it has many logically equivalent forms, and name the specific _equivalences_ a query optimizer exploits. Chapter 11 will trace the naive execution; Chapter 12 will show how the optimizer's algebraic rewriting turns 20 minutes into 2 seconds.

### 7.1 The Operators

Relational algebra has six **primitive operators** and several derived ones. The primitives:

Symbol | Name | Unary/Binary | Intuition  
---|---|---|---  
σ | Selection | Unary | Filter rows by a condition  
π | Projection | Unary | Pick columns; drop the rest  
× | Cross product | Binary | Cartesian product of two relations  
∪ | Union | Binary | Set union  
− | Difference | Binary | Set difference (rows in A but not B)  
ρ | Rename | Unary | Rename a relation or its attributes  
  
And the most important _derived_ operator (it can be built from σ and ×):

Symbol | Name | Binary | Intuition  
---|---|---|---  
⋈ | Join (natural / theta) | Binary | Combine rows from two relations matching a condition  
  
Plus two more useful derived operators:

Symbol | Name | Binary | Intuition  
---|---|---|---  
∩ | Intersection | Binary | Rows in A _and_ B  
÷ | Division | Binary | "Rows in A related to _every_ row in B"  
  
Let's walk through each with examples from Thread A's university schema:
    
    
    Students(id, name, email, year)
    Instructors(id, name, office, phone)
    Courses(id, title, credits, instructor_id)
    Enrollments(student_id, course_id, grade)
    

We'll also introduce the year attribute on Students (a simple integer 1–4) to make some queries more realistic for Thread B.

#### Selection: σ

> ### Definition: Selection (σ)

The **selection** operator $\sigma_\theta(R)$ returns the set of tuples in relation RRR that satisfy the boolean condition $\theta$.

In plain language, selection is the algebraic counterpart of SQL's `WHERE` clause. σyear=4(Students)\sigma_{$\text{year} = 4}(\text{Students})$σyear=4(Students) returns all senior students.

Examples:

* σcredits≥4(Courses)\sigma_{$\text{credits} \geq 4}(\text{Courses})$σcredits≥4(Courses) — all 4+-credit courses.
* σgrade is null(Enrollments)\sigma_{$\text{grade is null}}(\text{Enrollments})$σgrade is null(Enrollments) — all in-progress enrollments.
* σgrade=′A′∧year=4(⋅)\sigma_{$\text{grade} = 'A' \land \text{year} = 4}(\cdot)$σgrade=′A′∧year=4(⋅) — (on a joined relation) seniors with an A grade.

The condition $\theta$ can be any boolean combination of atomic comparisons.

#### Projection: π

> ### Definition: Projection (π)

The **projection** operator $\pi_{A_1, \ldots, A_n}(R)$ returns the relation obtained by keeping only the listed attributes of RRR and dropping the rest. Strictly relational, π also removes duplicate tuples in the result (because relations are sets).

In plain language, projection is the algebraic counterpart of SQL's `SELECT` column list (plus `DISTINCT`). πname,email(Students)\pi_{$\text{name}, \text{email}}(\text{Students})$πname,email(Students) returns just the names and emails, dropping id and year.

Examples:

* πname(Students)\pi_{$\text{name}}(\text{Students})$πname(Students) — all (distinct) student names.
* πtitle(σcredits≥4(Courses))\pi_{$\text{title}}(\sigma_{\text{credits} \geq 4}(\text{Courses}))$πtitle(σcredits≥4(Courses)) — titles of 4+-credit courses.

Notice the composition: πtitle(σcredits≥4(Courses))\pi_{$\text{title}}(\sigma_{\text{credits} \geq 4}(\text{Courses}))$πtitle(σcredits≥4(Courses)) reads from inside out. First filter, then project. (In SQL, you'd write `SELECT DISTINCT title FROM Courses WHERE credits >= 4`.)

Important: strict (set-semantic) projection eliminates duplicates. SQL's `SELECT` does _not_ eliminate duplicates by default; you need `SELECT DISTINCT`. This is one of the places where SQL's bag semantics and pure relational algebra diverge.

#### Cross Product: ×

> ### Definition: Cross Product (×)

The **Cartesian product** R×SR $\times SR$×S of two relations returns the set of all tuples formed by pairing each tuple in RRR with each tuple in SSS. If RRR has mmm tuples and SSS has nnn tuples, R×SR $\times SR$$\cdot nm$

In plain language, cross product is "every combination of left and right". On its own, it's rarely useful — the cross product of two tables is usually enormous. But combined with selection, it produces joins.

#### Union, Intersection, Difference

For R∪SR $\cup SR$∪S, R∩SR $\cap SR$∩S, and R−SR - SR−S to be well-defined, RRR and SSS must have **compatible schemas** : the same number of attributes with pairwise-matching domains. Otherwise the operations are meaningless.

* R∪SR $\cup SR$∪S — tuples in RRR or SSS (duplicates collapsed under set semantics).
$\cap SR$
* R−SR - SR−S — tuples in RRR but not in SSS.

Example. Suppose we have a second table, `AlumniStudents(id, name, email, year)` — schema-compatible with Students. Then:

* Students∪AlumniStudents$\text{Students} \cup \text{AlumniStudents}Students$∪AlumniStudents — everyone, current or alumni.
* Students∩AlumniStudents$\text{Students} \cap \text{AlumniStudents}Students$∩AlumniStudents — students who appear in both (perhaps re-enrolled after graduating).
* Students−AlumniStudents$\text{Students} - \text{AlumniStudents}Students−AlumniStudents$ — students who are not alumni.

#### Rename: ρ

> ### Definition: Rename (ρ)

The **rename** operator $\rho_{A \rightarrow B}(R)$ renames attribute AAA to BBB in RRR. $\rho_{R'}(R)$ renames the whole relation.

Rename is needed primarily to disambiguate attributes when joining a relation with itself, or when joining two relations that share attribute names but the names mean different things. The SQL equivalent is the `AS` alias.

#### Join: ⋈

Join is the workhorse of relational queries. Two flavors:

> ### Definition: Theta Join (⋈_θ)

The **theta join** R⋈θSR $\bowtie_\theta SR$⋈θS is defined as $\sigma_\theta(R \times S)$ — the cross product filtered by the condition $\theta$. When θ\thetaθ is an equality on specific attributes, this is an **equi-join**.

> ### Definition: Natural Join (⋈)

The **natural join** R⋈SR $\bowtie SR$⋈S is the equi-join on all common attributes (attributes of the same name in both relations), followed by projecting out the duplicate copies of the common attributes.

For our schema, Students⋈Enrollments$\text{Students} \bowtie \text{Enrollments}Students$⋈Enrollments naturally joins on `student_id` if we rename one of them (`Students.id → student_id`). Enrollments⋈Courses$\text{Enrollments} \bowtie \text{Courses}Enrollments$⋈Courses naturally joins on `course_id` if we rename (`Courses.id → course_id`).

In most of our algebraic expressions, we will use the theta-join form for clarity, because natural joins require careful attention to attribute naming. Example:

$\text{Students} \bowtie_{\text{Students.id = Enrollments.student\\_id}} \text{Enrollments}$

is the algebra for "all students joined to their enrollments."

#### Outer Joins

Relational algebra also has left, right, and full outer joins (⟕, ⟖, ⟗) corresponding to SQL's LEFT, RIGHT, and FULL OUTER joins. These preserve unmatched tuples by padding with nulls. We will use them when the query demands, but in this chapter we focus on inner joins because they show the algebraic structure most clearly.

### 7.2 Writing SQL as Relational Algebra

Let's translate a handful of SQL queries into algebra.

**Example 1.** `SELECT name FROM Students WHERE year = 4;`

Algebra: πname(σyear=4(Students))\pi_{$\text{name}}(\sigma_{\text{year} = 4}(\text{Students}))$πname(σyear=4(Students))

Read inside-out: filter Students to seniors, then project the name column.

**Example 2.** Thread A query — "courses Alice is enrolled in":
    
    
    SELECT c.title
    FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
                    JOIN Courses c     ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE s.name = 'Alice';
    

Algebra: πc.title(σs.name = ’Alice’(Students⋈s.id=e.student_idEnrollments⋈e.course_id=c.idCourses))\pi_{$\text{c.title}}(\sigma_{\text{s.name = 'Alice'}}($$\text{Students} \bowtie_{s.id=e.student\\_id} \text{Enrollments} \bowtie_{e.course\\_id=c.id} \text{Courses}))$πc.title(σs.name = ’Alice’(Students⋈s.id=e.student_idEnrollments⋈e.course_id=c.idCourses))

Read inside out: join the three tables, filter to Alice's rows, project the title.

**Example 3.** Thread B query — "for senior students, their name, course title, and instructor name":
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c     ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

Algebra: πs.name, c.title, i.name(σs.year=4(S⋈E⋈C⋈I))\pi_{$\text{s.name, c.title, i.name}}(\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4}(S \bowtie E \bowtie C \bowtie I))$πs.name, c.title, i.name(σs.year=4(S⋈E⋈C⋈I))

where the joins have the appropriate predicates.

This is a four-way join. The algebraic expression is compact — four operators: one projection, one selection, three joins (bundled into the big join). But the _execution_ of this expression is where everything gets interesting. Different join orders, different algorithms, different filter placements can change the runtime by three orders of magnitude. We will walk through this carefully in Chapter 12.

#### Thread B: The 20-Minute Query

This query is our Thread B. We will see it again in Chapters 10, 11, 12, and 19. The algebra above is its logical specification — what the query means. Different _physical_ executions of the same algebra can be dramatically different in cost.

Let's state the scenario up front (so Chapter 11 doesn't have to repeat it):

* Students: 100,000 rows (tableid, name, email, year).
* Enrollments: 800,000 rows (student_id, course_id, grade) — average 8 enrollments per student.
* Courses: 500 rows (id, title, credits, instructor_id).
* Instructors: 100 rows (id, name, office, phone).

No indexes beyond primary keys.

Run the query. It takes 20 minutes. Chapter 11 will show why. This chapter's job is to give us the algebraic vocabulary to _discuss_ why.

### 7.3 Algebraic Equivalences

Here is the central idea that makes relational algebra indispensable. The same query has **multiple equivalent algebraic expressions** , and the optimizer's freedom lies in choosing the best one to execute.

#### Equivalence 1: Selection Commutes

$\sigma_{\theta_1}(\sigma_{\theta_2}(R)) = \sigma_{\theta_2}(\sigma_{\theta_1}(R)) = \sigma_{\theta_1 \land \theta_2}(R)$

Filtering by one condition then another produces the same result as filtering by the combined condition — and the order of individual filters doesn't matter.

This is mostly a structural simplification. It's important because cascading filters can be merged and then pushed as a unit.

#### Equivalence 2: Selection Distributes Over Join (Pushdown)

If $\theta$ refers only to attributes of RRR:

$\sigma_\theta(R \bowtie S) = \sigma_\theta(R) \bowtie S$

**This is the single most important equivalence in the optimizer's toolkit.** It says: we can _push a filter down past a join_ when the filter only uses attributes from one side.

Why it matters: imagine joining 1,000,000-row RRR with 1,000,000-row SSS, then filtering to 100 rows. Naive cost: compute the join (possibly 1,000,000 × 1,000,000 in the worst case!), then filter. Pushed down: filter RRR to 100 rows first, then join 100 × 1,000,000. Dramatically cheaper.

This is why `WHERE` clauses that reference only one table are almost always applied as early as possible in the plan — before the join. The optimizer does this rewrite automatically.

#### Equivalence 3: Projection Distributes Over Join (Pushdown)

If we project to attribute set LLL, and LLL includes all attributes that appear in later operators and in the join condition:

$\pi_L(R \bowtie_\theta S) = \pi_L((\pi_{L_R}(R)) \bowtie_\theta (\pi_{L_S}(S)))$

where LRL_RLR and LSL_SLS are the attributes of RRR and SSS, respectively, that are needed for the join condition or the final projection.

In plain language: if we're only going to use 3 of 30 columns, drop the unused 27 before the join rather than after. This reduces the size of rows flowing through the join, which is always beneficial for performance.

#### Equivalence 4: Join is Commutative

$R \bowtie S = S \bowtie R$

The _logical result_ is the same. Execution costs differ — the optimizer's freedom to reorder joins rests on this equivalence.

#### Equivalence 5: Join is Associative

$(R \bowtie S) \bowtie T = R \bowtie (S \bowtie T)$

Again, logical result identical. Join order freedom: combined with commutativity, for a 4-way join there are many distinct orderings to consider. Chapter 12 counts them formally.

#### Equivalence 6: Unions, Intersections, Differences and Set Ops

* Union and intersection are commutative and associative.
* Difference is neither.
* Selection distributes over union, intersection, and difference.
* Projection distributes over union (but not always over intersection or difference — careful).

#### Equivalence 7: Selection After Union / Projection After Union

$\sigma_\theta(R \cup S) = \sigma_\theta(R) \cup \sigma_\theta(S)$

Push selection or projection through a union. This is useful when one side of the union is small and the other large — filter each side independently rather than filtering the combined result.

### 7.4 Thread B Revisited: Algebraic Freedom

Recall Thread B's algebra:

$\pi_{\text{s.name, c.title, i.name}}(\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4}(S \bowtie E \bowtie C \bowtie I))$

Let's apply equivalences to find better forms.

**Apply Equivalence 2 (selection pushdown):**

$\sigma_{s.year = 4}$ only references `S`, so we can push it:

$\pi_L(((\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4}(S)) \bowtie E) \bowtie C \bowtie I)$

Now only senior students participate in the join. If 25% of students are seniors, we've just reduced the left side of the first join from 100,000 to 25,000 rows. This is a direct factor-of-4 win without any other changes.

**Apply Equivalence 3 (projection pushdown):**

We only need s.name, c.title, i.name in the final output, plus the join-condition attributes (ids). Push projections into each subexpression:

πL((πs.id, s.name(σs.year=4(S)))⋈(πe.student_id, e.course_id(E))⋈(πc.id, c.title, c.instructor_id(C))⋈(πi.id, i.name(I)))\pi_L( (\pi_{$\text{s.id, s.name}}(\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4}(S))) \bowtie (\pi_{\text{e.student\\_id, e.course\\_id}}(E)) \bowtie (\pi_{\text{c.id, c.title, c.instructor\\_id}}(C)) \bowtie (\pi_{\text{i.id, i.name}}(I)) )$πL((πs.id, s.name(σs.year=4(S)))⋈(πe.student_id, e.course_id(E))⋈(πc.id, c.title, c.instructor_id(C))⋈(πi.id, i.name(I)))

Now each side of each join carries only the columns it needs. If the original rows were 500 bytes each and we've projected to 50-byte rows, the join flows 10× less data. Combined with pushdown, we might be 10–40× faster.

**Apply Equivalence 5 (join reordering):**

With commutative and associative joins, we have freedom. Some alternatives:

* Join S with E first (large × large).
* Join S with C first — but there's no direct join condition, S and C don't share attributes, so this would be a cross product. Bad.
* Join E with C first (large × small) — maybe cheaper.
* Join I with C first (tiny × small) — even cheaper.

The optimizer's job (Chapter 12) is to cost all these alternatives and pick the best. One promising plan: start with the smallest tables (I, C) and grow outward.

Note what we have done here: the _same algebraic expression_ has been rewritten in several ways, each logically equivalent but operationally different. The optimizer's output (the "query plan") is exactly one of these rewrites, chosen for its estimated cost.

### 7.5 The Bag vs Set Problem

Pure relational algebra works on _sets_. SQL works on _bags_ — duplicate rows are allowed. Where does this matter?

Consider πcredits(Courses)\pi_{$\text{credits}}(\text{Courses})$πcredits(Courses) in strict relational algebra vs `SELECT credits FROM Courses` in SQL. If three courses have 4 credits each, the algebra returns `{4}` (one row), the SQL returns `[4, 4, 4]` (three rows).

Two approaches in real optimizers:

  1. **Work in set semantics, require explicit DISTINCT for duplicate removal.** This is philosophically pure but frustrating — most SQL queries silently rely on duplicate counts (for aggregates like `SUM` and `AVG`, duplicates are information).

  2. **Work in bag semantics (multisets), with the algebra extended slightly.** This is what real optimizers do. The operator rules mostly generalize: $\sigma$ and × on bags are identical; π on bags does _not_ eliminate duplicates; union / intersection / difference have two variants (`UNION ALL` / `UNION`, etc.).

For the rest of this book, we treat relational algebra as set-oriented for clarity of reasoning, but flag when SQL's bag semantics diverges. The divergence most often matters in:

* Projection (π removes duplicates, SELECT does not).
* Union (∪ removes duplicates; `UNION ALL` does not; `UNION` does).
* Aggregates (aggregates operate naturally on bags).

#### Common Misconception

Some engineers think "SQL is relational algebra with sugar on top." It almost is, but the bag semantics and three-valued logic (NULL) push SQL one step away from pure relational algebra. The optimizer still thinks algebraically — but it thinks in a slightly extended "multiset relational algebra" that most textbooks simplify to pure algebra. Real academic papers on query optimization are careful about the distinction; this book, for clarity, will mostly use pure algebra and call out the bag-semantics caveats where they matter.

#### Aggregation: the γ Operator

Aggregate queries — `GROUP BY … SUM / COUNT / AVG / MAX / MIN` — are common but sit outside the original six primitives. Standard practice is to add a seventh logical operator, the **aggregation operator** γ:

> ### Definition: Aggregation Operator (γ)
> 
$\gamma_{G;\, A_1(a_1),\, \ldots,\, A_k(a_k)}(R)$
> 
> Groups the tuples of RRR by the attributes in GGG (the **grouping columns**), then applies the aggregate functions A1,…,AkA_1, $\ldots, A_kA1,$…,Ak to the indicated columns within each group. The result has one tuple per distinct group, containing the grouping columns plus the aggregate results.
> 
> **Notation:**
> 
>   * GGG is a (possibly empty) list of grouping attributes.
>   * AiA_iAi is one of: COUNT,SUM,AVG,MIN,MAX$\text{COUNT}, \text{SUM}, \text{AVG}, \text{MIN}, \text{MAX}COUNT,SUM,AVG,MIN,MAX.$
>   * aia_iai is the column to aggregate (or `*` for COUNT(*)).
> 

> 
> **SQL correspondence:**
>     
>     
>     SELECT dept, COUNT(*), AVG(salary)
>     FROM Employees
>     GROUP BY dept
>     HAVING AVG(salary) > 80000;
>     
> 
> translates to:
> 
> σavg_sal>80000 ⁣(γdept; COUNT(∗), AVG(salary)(Employees))\sigma_{$\text{avg\\_sal} > 80000}\\!\Big(\gamma_{\text{dept};\, \text{COUNT}(*),\, \text{AVG}(\text{salary})}(\text{Employees})\Big)$ σavg_sal>80000(γdept;COUNT(∗),AVG(salary)(Employees))
> 
> The HAVING clause is just a selection (σ) applied _after_ the aggregation — exactly how the optimizer handles it.

**Why this matters for optimization.** The γ operator cannot be pushed below a join — aggregating before joining changes the result. But projecting before aggregating (eliminating irrelevant columns) is safe and reduces working-set size. The optimizer's job is to find placements of γ, σ, and π in the algebra tree that minimize data movement while preserving the answer. Chapter 12 shows concrete examples of this for Thread B's query.

### 7.6 Division: A Powerful but Niche Operator

$\div$

Let R(X,Y)R(X, Y)R(X,Y) be a relation with attribute set X∪YX $\cup YX$∪Y, and let S(Y)S(Y)S(Y) be a relation over attribute set YYY. Then:

$\in \pi_X(R) \mid \forall s \in S,\ (t, s) \in R \,\\}R$

In plain language: the division `R ÷ S` returns all the X-values `t` in `R` such that `t` is paired in `R` with **every** `Y`-value in `S`. Think of `R` as a mapping "which Y's go with each X" and `S` as "the Y's we require"; the division returns the X's that cover every required Y.

#### Example

`Enrollments(student_id, course_id)` maps students to the courses they have taken. `RequiredCourses(course_id)` lists courses required for graduation. Then

$\text{Enrollments} \div \text{RequiredCourses}$

returns the set of `student_id`s who have taken every required course.

#### Why the SQL translation uses double negation

Division expresses **universal quantification** ("for all"). SQL has no direct "for all" quantifier — it has `EXISTS` (existential) and negation. The logical identity

$\in S,\ P(y) \quad\Leftrightarrow\quad \neg\, \exists y \in S,\ \neg P(y)$

lets us rewrite "for every y in S, this property holds" as "there is no y in S for which the property fails." This is the formal justification for the double-`NOT EXISTS` translation. Applied to our example:

* Outer query picks a student `s`.
* _Universal condition_ : for every required course `rc.course_id`, there exists an enrollment `(s.id, rc.course_id)` in `Enrollments`.
* _Double-negated form_ : there is **no** required course `rc.course_id` for which there is **no** matching enrollment.

    
    
    SELECT s.id FROM Students s
    WHERE NOT EXISTS (                             -- no required course...
      SELECT 1 FROM RequiredCourses rc
      WHERE NOT EXISTS (                           -- ...for which no matching enrollment...
        SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e
        WHERE e.student_id = s.id AND e.course_id = rc.course_id
      )
    );
    

The two `NOT EXISTS` layers are not a stylistic choice — they are forced by SQL's lack of a `∀` quantifier. This is one of the most useful patterns in SQL: whenever a question contains "every", "all", or "for each … must", the double-`NOT EXISTS` is usually the right shape. (We revisit the pattern in Chapter 8.)

#### Expressing division via the primitives

For completeness: division is _not_ one of the six primitive operators (σ, π, ×, ∪, −, ρ). It can be constructed from them as

$R \div S = \pi_X(R) - \pi_X\big((\pi_X(R) \times S) - R\big)$

In English: start with all candidate `X`-values (`π_X(R)`); subtract any `X`-value `t` for which some pairing `(t, s)` with `s ∈ S` is **missing** from `R`. What remains is the `X`-values that are paired with _every_ `s`. This construction, not the symbol `÷`, is what query engines actually execute.

**Worked example — verifying the primitive construction.**

Let `R(student_id, course_id)` contain:

student_id | course_id  
---|---  
1 | CS101  
1 | CS201  
2 | CS101  
3 | CS101  
3 | CS201  
3 | CS301  
  
Let `S(course_id)` = { CS101, CS201 } (required courses for graduation).

**Step 1:`π_X(R)`** — all student_ids that appear in R: `{ 1, 2, 3 }`

**Step 2:`π_X(R) × S`** — all _candidate_ (student_id, course_id) pairs: `{ (1,CS101), (1,CS201), (2,CS101), (2,CS201), (3,CS101), (3,CS201) }`

**Step 3:`(π_X(R) × S) − R`** — candidate pairs _missing_ from R: The only pair in the cross product but not in R is `(2, CS201)`. Result: `{ (2, CS201) }`

**Step 4:`π_X(...)` of step 3** — student_ids with at least one missing pairing: `{ 2 }`

**Step 5:`π_X(R) − step 4`** — student_ids with no missing pairings: `{ 1, 2, 3 } − { 2 } = { 1, 3 }`

**Result:** students 1 and 3 have taken both CS101 and CS201; student 2 has not. This matches the definition: `R ÷ S = { 1, 3 }`. ✓

The construction is elegant: rather than checking "does every element of S appear?", it checks "is there any element of S that is missing?" and subtracts those students. The double-negation structure mirrors the double-`NOT EXISTS` SQL translation exactly.

Division is less common in day-to-day SQL but important conceptually: it is the universal-quantification operator, the "for all" beast. Most queries use "exists" (semi-join), which is easier; "for all" (division) is rarer but crucial when a question genuinely asks "every required thing".

### 7.7 Algebra Is What the Optimizer Thinks In

This is the punchline of the chapter.

When you write:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

The optimizer translates this to its internal algebra (as in Section 7.4). Then it _rewrites_ the algebraic expression using the equivalences of Section 7.3. Selection pushdown, projection pushdown, join reordering, elimination of redundant operators — all happen as algebraic rewrites.

Finally, the optimizer maps each _logical_ algebraic operator to one or more _physical_ operator implementations (a join can be nested-loop, sort-merge, or hash — Chapter 11), estimates each option's cost, and picks the cheapest total plan.

This is why relational algebra is _the_ language of the database. Without it:

* There is no way to formally state "these two queries compute the same thing."
* There is no way to generate alternative plans.
* There is no way to reason about optimization at all.

Chapter 11 will trace a specific naive plan for Thread B through its physical execution. Chapter 12 will show how the optimizer uses the equivalences of this chapter to produce a vastly better plan. The 20-minute query becomes a 2-second query, and the transformation is entirely algebraic.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Relational algebra trades human readability for optimizer-friendly rewrite rules.** $\pi$$\sigma$ and ⋈$\bowtie$⋈ are not how you'd like to talk to users, but they are exactly the alphabet the optimizer needs to reorder joins and push down selections without changing meaning.
* **Set semantics (pure algebra) trades duplicate-preserving arithmetic for clean equivalence laws.** In set algebra, $\pi$ is idempotent and $\sigma$ commutes freely. SQL chose bag semantics because `SUM`, `AVG`, and `COUNT` all need duplicates — at the cost of needing an extended bag algebra to state the same optimizer rewrites.
* **Selection pushdown trades no expressive power for (often dramatic) speed.** $\sigma_\theta(R \bowtie S) \equiv \sigma_\theta(R) \bowtie S$ when $\theta$ references only RRR is always a performance win; the pushdown shrinks intermediate results before the join. This is why the optimizer always tries it.
* **Division trades SQL-level brevity for matching "for all" semantics.** R÷SR $\div SR$÷S expresses universal quantification — "students who took every course in SSS" — in one operator, which SQL has no direct form for. You pay by translating division into a double-negated `NOT EXISTS` when writing the query, but the algebraic form is what makes the semantics precise.
* **Join reordering trades a larger plan search space for a cheaper executed plan.** Associativity and commutativity of ⋈$\bowtie$⋈ give the optimizer n!n!n! join orders to consider; combined with cost estimation, the "best" order is often 100× cheaper than the written order. The cost is optimizer time — acceptable because it is paid once per query.
* **Working in logical algebra separately from physical operators trades end-to-end optimization for modular reasoning.** The optimizer first rewrites the algebra for free (no cost model needed), then maps each logical operator to a physical implementation (nested-loop, hash, sort-merge) under a cost model. Mixing the two stages is how bad optimizers get stuck in local minima.

### 7.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Express each of the following in relational algebra for Thread A's university schema: (a) All students in year 3. (b) The names of all instructors with office in 'Building A'. (c) All enrollments with grade 'A' or 'A+' or 'A-'. (d) Pairs of (student name, course title) for enrollments where the grade is null.

Hint

Use σ for filter, π for project, ⋈ for join.

**Answer:** (a) σyear=3(Students)\sigma_{\text{year} = 3}(\text{Students})σyear=3(Students) (b) πname(σoffice LIKE ’Building A%’(Instructors))\pi_{$\text{name}}(\sigma_{\text{office LIKE 'Building A\%'}}(\text{Instructors}))$πname(σoffice LIKE ’Building A%’(Instructors)) (c) σgrade∈{′A′,′A+′,′A−′}(Enrollments)\sigma_{$\text{grade} \in \\{'A', 'A+', 'A-'\\}}(\text{Enrollments})$σgrade∈{′A′,′A+′,′A−′}(Enrollments) (d) πs.name,c.title(σgrade IS NULL(Students⋈s.id=e.student_idEnrollments⋈e.course_id=c.idCourses))\pi_{$\text{s.name}, \text{c.title}}(\sigma_{\text{grade IS NULL}}($$\text{Students} \bowtie_{s.id=e.student\\_id} \text{Enrollments} \bowtie_{e.course\\_id=c.id} \text{Courses}))$πs.name,c.title(σgrade IS NULL(Students⋈s.id=e.student_idEnrollments⋈e.course_id=c.idCourses))

**Q2.** What does $\sigma_\theta(R) \bowtie S$ produce that might differ from $\sigma_\theta(R \bowtie S)$ if the condition $\theta$ references attributes of both RRR and SSS?

**Answer:** The two are not equivalent when $\theta$ references attributes of both — because $\sigma_\theta(R)$ can't even evaluate $\theta$, it only has RRR's attributes. The pushdown equivalence (Equivalence 2) applies specifically when θ\thetaθ references only one side. If θ\thetaθ references both, the selection must be applied after the join, not before.

Example where pushdown fails: σs.name = i.name(Students⋈Instructors)\sigma_{$\text{s.name = i.name}}(\text{Students} \bowtie \text{Instructors})$σs.name = i.name(Students⋈Instructors) — find students whose name matches an instructor's name. You cannot evaluate this selection on Students alone; you need both sides joined first.

When pushdown does apply, the optimizer will always use it; the rewrite can only be performance-winning, never losing.

**Q3.** Explain why πcredits(Courses)\pi_{$\text{credits}}(\text{Courses})$πcredits(Courses) and `SELECT credits FROM Courses` can produce different results. Which is "right"?

**Answer:** In strict relational algebra, projection removes duplicates — the result is a set of distinct values. πcredits(Courses)\pi_{$\text{credits}}(\text{Courses})$πcredits(Courses) on a table with values {3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 6} returns {3, 4, 6}.

In SQL, `SELECT credits FROM Courses` preserves duplicates — the result is a bag: [3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 6]. To get the algebraic behavior, write `SELECT DISTINCT credits FROM Courses`.

Neither is "right" — they are different but both internally consistent. SQL chose bags because most queries actually want duplicate-preserving output (aggregates like `SUM` and `AVG` depend on duplicate counts; `COUNT(*)` measures bag size). Pure algebra chose sets for mathematical cleanliness. Real optimizers work in extended bag algebra, as noted in Section 7.5.

**Q4.** Write the relational-algebra expression for: "All (student_id, course_id) pairs where the student has enrolled in the course."

**Answer:** πstudent_id, course_id(Enrollments)\pi_{$\text{student\\_id, course\\_id}}(\text{Enrollments})$πstudent_id, course_id(Enrollments). No join needed — enrollments already carry the (student_id, course_id) pair.

(A common wrong answer: joining students to enrollments, then projecting. That works, but it's wasteful — we already have both attributes in Enrollments.)

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Consider the Thread B query:

$\pi_{\text{s.name, c.title, i.name}}(\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4}(S \bowtie E \bowtie C \bowtie I))$

(a) Assume 100,000 students, 800,000 enrollments, 500 courses, 100 instructors, 25% seniors. Give a rough cost in rows scanned for two plans:

* Plan A: naive left-to-right evaluation without pushdown: (((S⋈E)⋈C)⋈I)(((S $\bowtie E) \bowtie C) \bowtie I)(((S$⋈E)⋈C)⋈I) first, then filter.
* Plan B: selection-pushed-down: ((σyear=4(S)⋈E)⋈C)⋈I((\sigma_{$\text{year} = 4}(S) \bowtie E) \bowtie C) \bowtie I(($σyear=4(S)⋈E)⋈C)⋈I. (b) Which is better, and by how much?

Hint

Assume a 1:1 match on each join's right side. The join output size equals the left input size times the matching factor.

**Answer:**

**(a) Rough row counts for each plan.**

**Plan A:**

* S⋈ES $\bowtie ES$⋈E: each of 800,000 enrollments matches its single student → 800,000 rows.
* (S⋈E)⋈C(S $\bowtie E) \bowtie C(S$⋈E)⋈C: each of 800,000 enrollments matches its one course → 800,000 rows.
* (S⋈E⋈C)⋈I(S $\bowtie E \bowtie C) \bowtie I(S$⋈E⋈C)⋈I: each of 800,000 rows matches its one instructor → 800,000 rows.
* Finally σyear=4\sigma_{$\text{year} = 4}$σyear=4 filters to 200,000 rows (25%).

Total rows processed by the join stage: at least 3 × 800,000 = 2.4 million (with each row flowing through three joins).

**Plan B:**

$\text{year} = 4}(S)$
* (σ(S))⋈E($\sigma(S)) \bowtie E($σ(S))⋈E: each senior has ~8 enrollments → 200,000 rows.
$\cdot) \bowtie C($
$\cdot) \bowtie I($

Total rows processed: at least 25,000 + 3 × 200,000 = 625,000.

**(b)** Plan B is ~4× cheaper in row traffic. In real disk-cost terms, the difference can be much larger (because the join-output rows are wider and cost more to materialize and hash-probe; we will see this quantitatively in Chapter 11). Selection pushdown is the quickest optimizer win for this query.

**Cross-chapter connection:** Chapter 11 will also show that the _physical_ join algorithm matters. Nested-loop joins without indexes will be much more expensive than the numbers above suggest. Chapter 12 combines pushdown with join ordering and algorithm choice.

**Q2.** Use relational algebra to express: "Students who have taken at least one course taught by Dr. Chen." Give at least two logically equivalent expressions and comment on which seems cheaper to execute.

Hint

Think of two different join orders.

**Answer:**

Both expressions should filter Instructors to Dr. Chen, trace to his courses, to enrollments, to students.

**Expression 1:** Start from Students, join inward:

$\pi_{\text{s.name}}(\text{Students} \bowtie \text{Enrollments} \bowtie \text{Courses} \bowtie \sigma_{\text{name} = 'Dr. Chen'}(\text{Instructors}))$

**Expression 2:** Start from Instructors, join outward:

$\pi_{\text{s.name}}(\sigma_{\text{name} = 'Dr. Chen'}(\text{Instructors}) \bowtie \text{Courses} \bowtie \text{Enrollments} \bowtie \text{Students})$

Both produce the same result. But the _costs_ differ:

* Expression 1 joins all 800,000 enrollments with all 100,000 students first, then narrows via Courses and Instructors. Most of that work is later thrown away.
* Expression 2 starts by filtering Instructors to the single Dr. Chen row, joins to his (say) 5 courses, then to (say) 150 enrollments, then to 150 students. Far less work.

The two are logically equivalent (Equivalences 4 and 5 let us reorder joins). The optimizer would choose Expression 2's order automatically — start from the most selective table, expand outward. This is the core of join-ordering optimization (Chapter 12).

**Q3.** Translate this algebra into SQL:

$\pi_{\text{s.name, g.grade, c.title}}(\sigma_{\text{s.year} = 4 \land \text{g.grade} \in \\{'A', 'A+'\\}}(S \bowtie E_{\rho \text{grade} \rightarrow g.\text{grade}} \bowtie C))$

(Pretend the rename clarifies which `grade` we mean.)

Hint

Start with the FROM/JOIN clauses, then WHERE, then SELECT.

**Answer:**
    
    
    SELECT s.name, e.grade, c.title
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON e.student_id = s.id
    JOIN Courses     c ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE s.year = 4
      AND e.grade IN ('A', 'A+');
    

Straightforward mapping: the projection becomes the SELECT list, the selection becomes the WHERE, the join structure becomes the FROM/JOIN clauses. The rename on Enrollments.grade (to `g.grade`) is implicit in SQL because we qualified with the alias `e`.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Why does the relational algebra have no operator for "sort"? Can you derive such an operator from the primitives, and what would it cost?

**Discussion:** The algebra operates on _sets_ (or bags) of tuples, and sets have no inherent order. A sort operation imposes order, which is outside the set-theoretic model — it's a _rendering_ of the relation, not a new relation. That's why `ORDER BY` in SQL is outside the relational-algebra proper; it's a presentation-layer operation applied at the end.

Can you simulate sort with primitives? Only by introducing an ordinal attribute and using it (e.g., add a `rank` column via arithmetic). This is unnatural and defeats the purpose.

Real query engines treat sort as a physical operator — one of the ways to implement SQL's `ORDER BY` or to produce input for a sort-merge join (Chapter 11). It's not a logical operator because it doesn't change the relation's content, only the presentation.

The deeper point: algebra captures _what_ is computed, not _how_ it's presented. Sorting is about presentation. This separation of concerns is a recurring theme of query processing (Chapter 11).

**Q2.** Algebraic equivalences let the optimizer rewrite queries freely. But some equivalences that hold for sets fail for bags (multisets). Name at least one, and explain why the optimizer has to be careful with bag semantics.

**Discussion:** The cleanest example: $\pi_L(\pi_M(R)) = \pi_L(R)$ when L⊆ML $\subseteq ML$⊆M. In set algebra, both sides return the same set of tuples (no duplicates). In bag algebra, they can differ: `π_L(R)` keeps duplicates that agree on LLL; `π_L(π_M(R))` also keeps them because bag π doesn't dedupe. So for bag π, this equivalence actually holds. Good example.

A harder one: R∪SR $\cup SR$∪S (strict) versus `UNION` in SQL. In set algebra, union is idempotent: R∪R=RR $\cup R = RR$∪R=R. In bag algebra (specifically SQL's `UNION ALL`), R UNION ALL RR \text{ UNION ALL } RR UNION ALL R has _twice_ the tuples of RRR. SQL's default `UNION` (not `UNION ALL`) removes duplicates, restoring set semantics, but this is a different operator.

Implication for the optimizer: when rewriting `UNION ALL`, it cannot freely assume idempotence. When rewriting `UNION`, it can (because duplicates will be removed anyway). The optimizer must track which flavor of the operator is in play.

Another: $\pi_L(R - S)$ versus (πL(R))−(πL(S))($\pi_L(R)) - (\pi_L(S))($πL(R))−(πL(S)). These are not equivalent even for sets, because projection can lose information: a tuple in RRR that appears in SSS on attributes outside LLL might still be subtracted, or might not be. The optimizer must preserve exact information here.

The lesson: bag semantics and multiset-respecting algebras are trickier than set algebras. Real optimizers live in this complexity daily.

**Q3.** Relational algebra dates to the 1970s. Why has no significantly more powerful "query algebra" replaced it for mainstream databases?

**Discussion:** Several reasons:

  1. **It's exactly the right level of abstraction.** Relational algebra is powerful enough to express the queries mainstream applications actually write (filters, joins, aggregates, unions). More-expressive algebras (e.g., Datalog with recursion, graph-traversal algebras) are either subsumed by SQL extensions or serve niches.

  2. **Optimizability.** Algebraic equivalences are the optimizer's toolkit. Any proposed replacement must have its own equivalence theorems that support automatic query rewriting. This is hard to design — turing-complete languages, for example, are unoptimizable by the engine.

  3. **SQL inertia.** SQL is the standard interface; SQL is based on relational algebra; changing the algebra would require changing SQL or introducing a parallel language. The industry has not seen enough upside to pay this cost.

  4. **Extensions where needed.** Features that relational algebra doesn't natively express — recursion, graph traversal, window functions — have been added to SQL as extensions (CTEs, `WITH RECURSIVE`, `OVER` clause). Each extension has its own algebraic companion that the optimizer handles. The algebra has grown incrementally, not been replaced wholesale.

The meta-lesson: long-lived abstractions in computing often survive not because they're optimal in every dimension but because they're _good enough_ and the cost of replacement is prohibitive. SQL/relational algebra is one such abstraction; TCP/IP is another; Unix processes and file descriptors yet another. This "durability of the good-enough" is why most engineering is evolution rather than revolution.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter gave us **relational algebra** — the mathematical language underneath SQL and the internal representation every query optimizer manipulates. Six primitive operators (σ, π, ×, ∪, −, ρ) and key derived operators (⋈, ∩, ÷) cover the expressive needs of standard queries.

The algebra's essential feature is that it supports **equivalence rewrites** : selection pushdown past joins, projection pushdown, join commutativity and associativity, selection through unions. These equivalences are the optimizer's freedom — the same query has many algebraically equivalent expressions, each with a different cost, and the optimizer's job is to find the cheapest.

**Thread B — the 20-minute query — appeared here for the first time** , as an algebraic expression. We showed how selection pushdown alone cuts its row traffic ~4×; Chapter 11 will trace the naive execution plan in detail, and Chapter 12 will show how the optimizer combines pushdown, projection elimination, and join reordering to turn 20 minutes into 2 seconds.

We also noted where **SQL's bag semantics diverges from pure set algebra** — projection not removing duplicates, `UNION ALL` not being idempotent — and flagged that real optimizers work in an extended bag algebra that handles these details.

Relational algebra is the pivot of the book. Everything before (schema design, SQL, normalization) has been about "what queries can I write?" Everything after (storage, indexing, query processing, optimization, transactions) is about "how does the database execute those queries correctly and efficiently?" — and every chapter starts from the algebra.

Next chapter: we pick SQL back up and cover the _advanced_ features — subqueries (correlated and uncorrelated), views, window functions, CTEs, `EXPLAIN` — the tools that make SQL the language the industry actually writes in. Then Chapter 9 opens the black box of storage.

---

## Chapter 8: Advanced SQL — Subqueries, Views, and Window Functions

> **A note on exercise style.** Starting with this chapter, exercises no longer come with inline answers. In Chapters 1–7 we wrote out full solutions so you could verify your understanding of foundational material (relations, SQL syntax, ER, normalization). From Chapter 8 onward, the material is richer and has more defensible approaches; working through exercises _without_ the answer at the bottom of the page — then discussing your reasoning with the Socratopia AI companion, a study partner, or in a workshop — is where the learning actually happens. The companion has access to the reference solutions and can guide you toward them Socratically rather than hand them over. Resist the temptation to look up the "right answer"; the point is the path.

Chapter 4 taught the shape of a SELECT statement: FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, SELECT, DISTINCT, ORDER BY, LIMIT. That vocabulary answers "give me rows that match these conditions and aggregate them this way." It does not, by itself, answer questions that require comparing a row to _another_ row in the same result, or reusing a query as if it were a table, or computing a running total.

This chapter adds three instruments that turn SQL from a filtering language into an analytical language: **subqueries** , **views** , and **window functions**. Each one pays a price. Subqueries give us composability but can explode in cost if the optimizer cannot flatten them. Views give us abstraction but can hide performance cliffs behind innocent-looking names. Window functions give us cross-row computation but force the engine to sort or partition the data in ways a plain GROUP BY does not.

We stay with the same university schema from Chapter 7, and we will write queries that foreshadow what the optimizer will do with them in Chapter 12.

### 8.1 Why We Need More Than SELECT-FROM-WHERE

Consider three questions a registrar might ask.

  1. "List every student whose GPA is above the average GPA of their department."
  2. "For each course, show the enrollment in the previous semester next to the enrollment this semester."
  3. "Give me the top three students per department by GPA."

Try to write the first one with only the Chapter 4 vocabulary. You need the average GPA _per department_ (a GROUP BY aggregate), and then you need to compare each student's GPA to that aggregate. But GROUP BY collapses rows — if you group by department, you lose the individual students. If you do not group, you cannot compute the per-department average. You are stuck.

SQL's answer to questions like these is to let a query appear _inside_ another query. That inner query — a **subquery** — is what we build first.

#### Common Misconception

Beginners often think a subquery is always more expensive than the "equivalent" JOIN. This is true in some dialects and false in others. PostgreSQL, MySQL 8, and SQL Server all routinely flatten subqueries into joins during optimization. The cost difference between `WHERE id IN (SELECT ...)` and `INNER JOIN (...)` is often zero after the planner is done with it. Do not choose syntax based on folklore; read `EXPLAIN`.

### 8.2 Scalar, Row, and Table Subqueries

A subquery returns a value. The shape of that value determines where it can appear.

#### Definition: Scalar subquery

A **scalar subquery** returns exactly one column and at most one row. It may appear anywhere a single value is expected — in SELECT expressions, in WHERE comparisons, in ORDER BY.
    
    
    SELECT s.id, s.name,
           (SELECT AVG(grade) FROM Enrollments WHERE student_id = s.id) AS gpa
    FROM Students s;
    

For each student row, the scalar subquery runs and yields one average. If the inner query accidentally returned two rows, the database would raise a runtime error — "subquery returned more than 1 row."

#### Definition: Row subquery

A **row subquery** returns one row with multiple columns. It is compared against a tuple.
    
    
    SELECT * FROM Students
    WHERE (year, major) = (SELECT year, major FROM Students WHERE id = 42);
    

Row subqueries are elegant but uncommon. Most dialects implement them; tune for readability.

#### Definition: Table subquery

A **table subquery** returns a full relation and must appear in a context where a table makes sense: inside FROM, inside IN, inside EXISTS, inside ANY/ALL.
    
    
    SELECT d.name, t.avg_gpa
    FROM Departments d
    JOIN (
      SELECT s.dept_id, AVG(e.grade) AS avg_gpa
      FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
      GROUP BY s.dept_id
    ) AS t ON d.id = t.dept_id;
    

The table subquery in the FROM clause is often called an **inline view** or a **derived table**. The alias `AS t` is mandatory — a table without a name has no columns anyone can reference.

#### Tradeoff: placement and reuse

A table subquery in FROM runs once and produces a result the outer query scans. A scalar subquery in SELECT _may_ run once per outer row. "May," because the optimizer sometimes lifts constant subqueries out. When the subquery depends on the outer row, the optimizer has less freedom — which brings us to correlation.

### 8.3 Correlated Subqueries

A subquery is **correlated** if it references a column from the enclosing query. The inner query cannot run to completion until it knows the outer row.
    
    
    SELECT s.id, s.name
    FROM Students s
    WHERE s.gpa > (
      SELECT AVG(s2.gpa)
      FROM Students s2
      WHERE s2.dept_id = s.dept_id      -- correlation
    );
    

Read that as: "For each student `s`, check whether `s`'s GPA exceeds the average of the department `s` belongs to."

#### Definition: Correlation

A subquery is **correlated** when it refers to at least one column or alias from the outer query's FROM list. A subquery with no such reference is **uncorrelated** — it can be evaluated once and its result substituted.

#### The naive execution cost

The obvious way to execute a correlated subquery is to run it once per outer row. Ten thousand students means ten thousand runs of the inner query. Each run scans or indexes into `Students`. That is potentially O(n2)O(n^2)O(n2) — catastrophic at scale.

Modern optimizers often rewrite the correlated subquery into a join against a pre-aggregated subquery:
    
    
    SELECT s.id, s.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN (
      SELECT dept_id, AVG(gpa) AS dept_avg FROM Students GROUP BY dept_id
    ) d ON s.dept_id = d.dept_id
    WHERE s.gpa > d.dept_avg;
    

This runs in O(n): one pass to compute the averages, one pass to filter. PostgreSQL and SQL Server call this transformation **decorrelation**. MySQL 8 added it; MySQL 5.7 did not, which is why old forum posts claim "correlated subqueries are always slow." They are not — except when the optimizer can't rewrite them.

#### Common Misconception

"Correlated subqueries are always slow, so use JOINs." The truth is narrower: **un-rewritten** correlated subqueries are slow. When the planner rewrites them, performance is identical. The fix is to read the plan, not to avoid the syntax.

#### When Decorrelation Fails

Decorrelation is not always possible. The optimizer cannot flatten a correlated subquery into a join in several cases:

**1\. Subquery uses LIMIT or OFFSET.**
    
    
    SELECT s.id FROM Students s
    WHERE s.id = (
      SELECT e.student_id FROM Enrollments e
      ORDER BY e.enrolled_at DESC
      LIMIT 1
    );
    

The `LIMIT 1` means the inner query depends on which rows are returned, which changes per-outer-row context. Decorrelation would need to pre-compute "the last-enrolled student for each outer row," which is not a simple group-by — it's a per-group top-N. Some optimizers (PostgreSQL 14+) can handle this with lateral joins; older ones cannot.

**2\. Non-deterministic function inside the correlated subquery.**
    
    
    -- Each outer row must get a different random threshold; cannot pre-compute once.
    SELECT s.name FROM Students s
    WHERE s.gpa > (
      SELECT AVG(g.score) * (1 + random() * 0.1)   -- ← random() inside the subquery
      FROM Grades g
      WHERE g.student_id = s.id
    );
    

The `random()` inside the subquery means the result must differ each time the inner query runs. If the planner decorrelated this into a pre-aggregated join, it would compute `random()` once (at the wrong granularity), changing the per-row semantics. Note: `random()` in the _outer_ WHERE clause (not inside the subquery) would not prevent decorrelation — the optimizer only cares about what is inside the correlated subquery itself.

**3\. Scalar subquery in SELECT that returns NULL on no-match.**
    
    
    SELECT s.name, (SELECT MAX(g.score) FROM Grades g WHERE g.student_id = s.id) AS best
    FROM Students s;
    

This is almost always decorrelated by modern planners (PostgreSQL converts it to a LEFT JOIN with aggregation). But if the inner query has multiple correlated predicates that prevent a clean join condition, some planners fall back to per-row evaluation. Always check the plan.

**The diagnostic rule:** If `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `(cost=…) (actual rows=N, loops=M)` where M equals the number of outer rows and the inner operator is a full scan, decorrelation has not occurred — each outer row is running the full inner query. Rewrite manually as a pre-aggregated join and compare.

### 8.4 EXISTS vs IN vs JOIN — the NULL minefield

Three common ways to express "student has at least one enrollment":
    
    
    -- A: EXISTS
    SELECT s.* FROM Students s
    WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e WHERE e.student_id = s.id);
    
    -- B: IN
    SELECT s.* FROM Students s
    WHERE s.id IN (SELECT student_id FROM Enrollments);
    
    -- C: JOIN + DISTINCT
    SELECT DISTINCT s.* FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON e.student_id = s.id;
    

When `Enrollments.student_id` is declared NOT NULL and has a foreign key to `Students`, all three return identical results and plans after optimization. They diverge — sometimes shockingly — when NULLs are possible.

#### The NULL trap in NOT IN

Suppose `Enrollments.student_id` can be NULL (bad schema, but people ship it). Consider:
    
    
    SELECT s.* FROM Students s
    WHERE s.id NOT IN (SELECT student_id FROM Enrollments);
    

If _any_ row in `Enrollments` has `student_id = NULL`, this query returns **zero rows**. Not "all students without enrollments" — zero. The reason is three-valued logic (Chapter 3): `s.id NOT IN (..., NULL, ...)` reduces to `s.id <> NULL AND ...`, and any comparison with NULL yields UNKNOWN, which the WHERE clause treats as false.

NOT EXISTS has no such trap:
    
    
    SELECT s.* FROM Students s
    WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e WHERE e.student_id = s.id);
    

EXISTS checks for the presence of rows; no NULL comparison is performed.

#### Definition: Rule of thumb

Use **EXISTS / NOT EXISTS** for existence checks. They are NULL-safe and almost always optimize to the same plan as a semi-join. Use **IN** for checking membership against a finite, NULL-free list. Reserve **JOIN + DISTINCT** for when you actually need columns from the joined table.

#### Tradeoff

EXISTS can stop reading as soon as the first matching row is found (a semi-join). JOIN must build the full join result and then de-duplicate it. For tables with many matching rows per outer row, EXISTS is often cheaper. The optimizer will usually convert between them, but giving it a hint through idiomatic syntax never hurts.

### 8.5 Common Table Expressions (CTEs)

A **Common Table Expression** is a named subquery that lives at the top of a query.
    
    
    WITH dept_avg AS (
      SELECT dept_id, AVG(gpa) AS gpa FROM Students GROUP BY dept_id
    )
    SELECT s.id, s.name, s.gpa, d.gpa AS dept_gpa
    FROM Students s JOIN dept_avg d ON s.dept_id = d.dept_id
    WHERE s.gpa > d.gpa;
    

CTEs improve readability. They let you name an intermediate result and use it multiple times in one statement. They also allow **recursion** , which plain subqueries cannot express.

#### Definition: Recursive CTE

A **recursive CTE** computes a fixed point: start from a base case, repeatedly apply a recursive case, and stop when no new rows are produced.
    
    
    -- Prerequisite chain: course → its prerequisites → their prerequisites → ...
    WITH RECURSIVE prereq_chain(course_id, prereq_id, depth) AS (
        SELECT course_id, prereq_id, 1
        FROM Prerequisites
      UNION ALL
        SELECT p.course_id, pr.prereq_id, c.depth + 1
        FROM prereq_chain c JOIN Prerequisites p ON c.prereq_id = p.course_id
    )
    SELECT * FROM prereq_chain;
    

The recursion stops when `Prerequisites` has no further ancestors to contribute. Graph traversals, organizational hierarchies, bill-of-materials expansions — all naturally expressed here.

#### Tradeoff: CTE materialization

Different databases treat CTEs differently. PostgreSQL, before version 12, _always_ materialized the CTE — it ran, wrote its result to a temporary structure, and the outer query read it back. This "fence" prevented the optimizer from pushing predicates down into the CTE, and a CTE that appeared cleaner was often slower than the inline subquery.
