Version 12 changed the default: non-recursive CTEs are inlined unless you write `WITH x AS MATERIALIZED (...)`. MySQL 8 and SQL Server inline by default as well. Know your engine's version.

#### Common Misconception

"CTEs are always faster than subqueries." Sometimes they are slower because of materialization fences. Sometimes identical. Use CTEs for clarity and recursion; trust the optimizer for the rest, and verify with EXPLAIN when performance matters.

### 8.6 Views

A **view** is a named SELECT statement that behaves like a table.
    
    
    CREATE VIEW active_students AS
    SELECT s.id, s.name, s.dept_id, s.year
    FROM Students s
    WHERE s.status = 'active';
    
    SELECT * FROM active_students WHERE dept_id = 3;
    

The engine does not store the result of a view. When you query the view, the engine substitutes the view's definition into your query and optimizes the combined statement. The substitution is called **view expansion** or **inlining**.

#### What views are for

  1. **Abstraction.** A complex join over six tables can be packaged as `enrollment_summary`. Application code reads one view.
  2. **Access control.** Grant a user SELECT on `active_students` without granting SELECT on the full `Students` table. The view filters columns and rows the user is not allowed to see.
  3. **Schema evolution.** Rename a column? Add a view with the old name that maps to the new one. Clients keep working while you migrate.

#### The performance hazard

A view looks like a table but is not one. A query like
    
    
    SELECT * FROM active_students a JOIN Enrollments e ON a.id = e.student_id
    WHERE a.year = 4;
    

expands to a query with `Students s WHERE s.status='active'` joined to Enrollments and filtered by year. The planner then decides whether to push `year = 4` into the scan, whether to use an index on `status`, and so on. A well-written view is invisible to the planner. A view with a non-trivial DISTINCT, a GROUP BY, or a window function creates an optimization barrier — the planner cannot push predicates past aggregation.

#### Definition: Updatable view

A view is **updatable** if UPDATE/INSERT/DELETE against it can be unambiguously translated to operations on the underlying tables. Standard updatable views must:

* Reference exactly one base table,
* Have no DISTINCT, GROUP BY, HAVING, UNION, aggregate, or window function,
* Include the primary key of the base table in the SELECT list,
* Not use derived columns in the INSERT path.

A view over a join is generally **not** updatable — the engine cannot know which base table to write to. Updatable views are convenient when they apply and a footgun when they do not.

#### Materialized views

A **materialized view** stores the view's result as a real table. It must be refreshed when the underlying data changes.
    
    
    CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW dept_stats AS
    SELECT dept_id, COUNT(*) AS n_students, AVG(gpa) AS avg_gpa
    FROM Students GROUP BY dept_id;
    
    REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW dept_stats;
    

#### Tradeoff: read speed vs freshness vs write speed

* **Regular view** : zero storage cost, no staleness, but recomputed on every query. Good for small/cheap queries.
* **Materialized view** : fast reads, can be indexed, but stale until refreshed. Some engines (Oracle, SQL Server indexed views) refresh eagerly on each underlying write, at the cost of every write paying the refresh tax.

Use a materialized view when the underlying query is expensive, staleness is tolerable, and reads vastly outnumber writes. A dashboard showing yesterday's top ten courses: perfect fit. A view backing a live booking system: dangerous.

### 8.7 Window Functions

A **window function** computes a value across a set of rows related to the current row, without collapsing the result into a single aggregate row.

Compare a plain aggregate:
    
    
    SELECT dept_id, AVG(gpa) FROM Students GROUP BY dept_id;
    

with a windowed aggregate:
    
    
    SELECT id, name, dept_id, gpa,
           AVG(gpa) OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id) AS dept_avg
    FROM Students;
    

The second query returns **one row per student** (no collapse) and attaches the department average to each. This is the cleanest answer to the question we opened the chapter with.

#### Anatomy of OVER

`OVER` has three optional clauses:
    
    
    function(args) OVER (
      PARTITION BY col1, col2, ...   -- divide rows into groups
      ORDER BY col3, col4 [ASC|DESC] -- order rows within each partition
      ROWS BETWEEN ... AND ...       -- define the frame
    )
    

* **PARTITION BY** is like GROUP BY but does not collapse rows. Each window evaluation is scoped to the partition.
* **ORDER BY** defines the row sequence within a partition — required for `RANK`, `ROW_NUMBER`, `LAG`, running totals.
* **ROWS BETWEEN** defines the **frame** : which rows, relative to the current row, the function sees. Defaults depend on whether ORDER BY is present.

#### Common window functions

* **Ranking** : `ROW_NUMBER()`, `RANK()`, `DENSE_RANK()`, `NTILE(n)`.
* **Aggregates** : `SUM()`, `AVG()`, `COUNT()`, `MIN()`, `MAX()` — all valid as window functions.
* **Offset** : `LAG(expr, n)` and `LEAD(expr, n)` return `expr` from a row `n` positions before / after the current row within the partition.

#### Example: running total of fees
    
    
    SELECT student_id, term, amount,
           SUM(amount) OVER (PARTITION BY student_id ORDER BY term) AS balance
    FROM Fees;
    

For each student, the balance column shows the cumulative fee through that term. No self-join, no correlated subquery.

#### Example: top 3 per department
    
    
    SELECT id, name, dept_id, gpa
    FROM (
      SELECT id, name, dept_id, gpa,
             RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id ORDER BY gpa DESC) AS r
      FROM Students
    ) t
    WHERE r <= 3;
    

Without window functions, this query is miserable to write (correlated subqueries counting rows with higher GPA in the same department, or self-joins, neither scalable). With window functions it is three lines.

> **Window functions are not allowed in WHERE or HAVING.** The reason is evaluation order: `WHERE` filters rows before the window computation runs. To filter _on_ a window function result, wrap the query in a subquery or CTE, as shown above, and apply `WHERE` on the outer query.

#### RANK vs DENSE_RANK vs ROW_NUMBER

Function | Behavior on ties  
---|---  
`ROW_NUMBER()` | Assigns 1, 2, 3, ... arbitrarily breaking ties.  
`RANK()` | Ties share a rank; next rank skips — 1, 1, 3.  
`DENSE_RANK()` | Ties share a rank; next rank does not skip — 1, 1, 2.  
  
For "top 3," `RANK` may return more than 3 rows when students tie for third. `ROW_NUMBER` always returns exactly 3 but picks one tied student arbitrarily. Choose the one whose semantics match the business question.

#### Frames

Imagine a query over a seven-day sliding window of enrollment counts:
    
    
    SELECT date, COUNT(*) OVER (
      ORDER BY date
      ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW
    ) AS rolling_week
    FROM daily_enrollments;
    

The frame `ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` defines a 7-row sliding window. Without the explicit frame, the default when ORDER BY is present is `RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW` — every row from the start of the partition up to the current row. Different defaults make running totals work "naturally" but can surprise you when you expected a fixed-width window.

#### ROWS vs RANGE: the difference you _must_ understand

`ROWS` and `RANGE` both take the same syntax (`BETWEEN ... PRECEDING AND ...`) but mean fundamentally different things:

* **`ROWS`** is **position-based**. "3 PRECEDING" means "the three rows physically preceding this one in the sort order," full stop. Ties are irrelevant.
* **`RANGE`** is **value-based**. "3 PRECEDING" means "all rows whose ORDER BY value is between (current_value − 3) and current_value, inclusive." Ties in the ORDER BY value all get the same frame.

This matters most at the default. Consider a running sum ordered by score:
    
    
    SELECT name, score,
           SUM(score) OVER (ORDER BY score)                       AS sum_default_range,
           SUM(score) OVER (ORDER BY score ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING) AS sum_rows
    FROM players;
    

Table `players` contains `(Alice, 100), (Bob, 100), (Carol, 90), (Dan, 80)`. Note that `ORDER BY score` is **ascending** by default (add `DESC` for a leaderboard sort); the window function therefore sweeps from the lowest score upward.

name | score | sum_default_range | sum_rows  
---|---|---|---  
Dan | 80 | 80 | 80  
Carol | 90 | 170 | 170  
Alice | 100 | **370** | **270**  
Bob | 100 | **370** | **370**  
  
Look at Alice's row. The default (`RANGE UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW`) includes _every row whose score ≤ 100_ , so Bob (also 100) is pulled into Alice's frame even though he appears after her. With `ROWS`, Alice's frame stops at her physical position in the sort and excludes Bob. **The running total for Alice differs by 100** — a real bug source when you intended a strict running sum. (The ROWS column also depends on the tie-breaking order for equal scores, which is engine-defined unless you specify it explicitly; that unpredictability is a further argument for being explicit about both ROWS/RANGE and secondary ORDER BY keys.)

**Rule of thumb** : if your window is "a running sum where each row sees itself and everything before," use `ROWS UNBOUNDED PRECEDING`. If your window is "everything with a comparable value up to here," use `RANGE`. The default is `RANGE`, which is almost never what you want for running totals.

#### The execution cost

Window functions cost at least one sort per distinct `PARTITION BY + ORDER BY` specification. The engine groups rows by partition key, sorts within each partition, then sweeps the frame. If you have three window functions with the same `OVER` clause, the engine sorts once and reuses. Different OVER clauses mean multiple sorts — watch your plans.

### 8.8 EXPLAIN — reading what the engine actually does

Every DBMS exposes an `EXPLAIN` or `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` command that shows the plan. A plan is a tree of operators — the physical version of the logical expression we saw in Chapter 7.

PostgreSQL output for the "students above department average" query:
    
    
    Hash Join  (cost=25.00..500.00 rows=800 width=40)
      Hash Cond: (s.dept_id = d.dept_id)
      Join Filter: (s.gpa > d.avg_gpa)
      ->  Seq Scan on Students s
      ->  Hash
            ->  HashAggregate
                  Group Key: dept_id
                  ->  Seq Scan on Students
    

Read bottom up. The inner `Seq Scan` feeds a `HashAggregate` computing the per-department averages. The result is hashed. The outer `Seq Scan` probes the hash and applies the filter `s.gpa > d.avg_gpa`. The optimizer decorrelated the subquery automatically.

`EXPLAIN ANALYZE` additionally runs the query and shows actual row counts and timings. It is the single most valuable command a working database engineer learns.

We are not at indexes or cost models yet — those come in Chapters 10 and 12. The point here is cultural: SQL without plan reading is folklore. Every advanced construct in this chapter has a plan. Learn to look.

#### Common Misconception

"EXPLAIN means the query runs." It does not — unless you add `ANALYZE`. Plain `EXPLAIN` shows the estimated plan. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` executes and measures. On production, the difference matters: accidentally adding `ANALYZE` to an `EXPLAIN UPDATE` performs the update.

### 8.9 Putting the pieces together — a realistic query

A registrar asks: "Show me each department's top-3 students by GPA, but only departments with at least 20 students, and attach each student's most recent course grade."
    
    
    WITH dept_large AS (
      SELECT dept_id FROM Students GROUP BY dept_id HAVING COUNT(*) >= 20
    ),
    ranked AS (
      SELECT s.id, s.name, s.dept_id, s.gpa,
             RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY s.dept_id ORDER BY s.gpa DESC) AS r
      FROM Students s
      WHERE s.dept_id IN (SELECT dept_id FROM dept_large)
    ),
    latest_grade AS (
      SELECT e.student_id, e.grade
      FROM (
        SELECT student_id, grade,
               ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY student_id ORDER BY term DESC) AS rn
        FROM Enrollments
      ) x WHERE rn = 1
    )
    SELECT r.name, r.dept_id, r.gpa, g.grade AS latest_grade
    FROM ranked r LEFT JOIN latest_grade g ON r.id = g.student_id
    WHERE r.r <= 3
    ORDER BY r.dept_id, r.gpa DESC;
    

Every construct in this chapter appears: CTEs, a nested window function for "one row per group," a subquery in IN, a LEFT JOIN to preserve students with no grades. The query is long but linear to read — and, crucially, a single EXPLAIN shows the engine's full plan.

### 8.10 Thread B Check-In

Recall the stubborn 20-minute query:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

With the tools of this chapter, we can _rephrase_ the question but not yet fix it. For example:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    WHERE s.year = 4
      AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e
                  JOIN Courses c ON e.course_id = c.id
                  JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
                  WHERE e.student_id = s.id);
    

This existence form returns fewer columns (losing `title` and `name`, which we actually want), but it shows how `EXISTS` trades information for speed when we only care "is there?" The real fix still waits for storage (Chapter 9), indexes (Chapter 10), and join algorithms (Chapter 11). Advanced SQL alone does not make queries fast. It makes them _expressible_. Speed is an engineering problem, not a syntax problem.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Correlated subqueries trade plan efficiency for expressive clarity.** A correlated `(SELECT MAX(gpa) ... WHERE dept_id = s.dept_id)` reads naturally but, if the optimizer cannot decorrelate it, runs once per outer row. When you can write it as a join or a window function, do so; when you can't, accept the nested plan.
* **`NOT EXISTS` vs `NOT IN` trades surprise-freeness for terseness.** `NOT IN` silently returns zero rows when the subquery contains any NULL; `NOT EXISTS` handles NULLs correctly. They are not interchangeable — prefer `NOT EXISTS` whenever the inner column is nullable.
* **Views trade query abstraction for performance opacity.** A non-materialized view is a macro the optimizer expands and pushes predicates into — cheap and transparent. A materialized view trades storage and staleness for precomputed results; pick materialization only when the freshness budget and refresh cost both fit the workload.
* **CTEs trade readability for (sometimes) an optimization fence.** Modern PostgreSQL inlines non-recursive CTEs; older engines and some dialects treat them as an optimization barrier that forces materialization. A clean query with a 10× slowdown is still a slow query.
* **Window functions trade one mandatory sort for row-level context alongside aggregates.** `MAX(gpa) OVER (PARTITION BY dept_id)` gives every row its department's top GPA without collapsing rows — paid for by at least one sort or one hash per partition, unless a covering index pre-sorts the data.
* **Recursive CTEs trade memory footprint for transitive-closure expressiveness.** You can compute a 30-level prerequisite tree in one query, but the engine must keep growing the working set every iteration. For deep hierarchies, schema-side help (closure tables, materialized paths) beats unbounded recursion.
* **`EXPLAIN` trades "SQL as pure declaration" for mechanical honesty.** Reading a plan means you are no longer thinking only in terms of what the query asks — you are thinking about what the engine does. That is the cost of performance work, and there is no way around it.

### 8.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Give a single sentence that distinguishes a scalar subquery from a table subquery. Where in a SELECT statement can each appear?
  2. Explain in your own words why `WHERE x NOT IN (subquery)` can silently return zero rows when the subquery contains NULLs.
  3. Why must a derived table in FROM have an alias, but a subquery in IN does not?
  4. State the difference between `ROW_NUMBER()`, `RANK()`, and `DENSE_RANK()` using a small example: six students with GPAs 3.9, 3.9, 3.8, 3.7, 3.7, 3.5.
  5. Describe one situation where a view improves maintainability and one situation where a view degrades performance.
  6. What is the default frame for a windowed aggregate when ORDER BY is present but ROWS BETWEEN is omitted?

#### Application Problems

  7. Rewrite the following correlated subquery as a join:
         
         SELECT s.id, s.name
         FROM Students s
         WHERE s.gpa = (SELECT MAX(gpa) FROM Students WHERE dept_id = s.dept_id);
         

State whether the rewrite is equivalent in all cases, and what happens if two students in a department tie for the top GPA.

  8. Write two SQL statements that both answer "list every course that currently has zero enrollments," one using `NOT EXISTS` and one using `LEFT JOIN ... WHERE ... IS NULL`. Which is safer when `Enrollments.course_id` is nullable, and why?

Hint

Think about what each form actually checks. `NOT EXISTS` tests whether any matching row exists for this course; `LEFT JOIN ... IS NULL` tests that the joined right side produced no match. Now ask: what does each form do when `Enrollments.course_id` itself is NULL for some row?

  9. Write a single SQL query using a window function that, for each student, shows their current GPA alongside their best GPA ever recorded (assume a `gpa_history(student_id, term, gpa)` table). Use `MAX(gpa) OVER (PARTITION BY student_id)`.

  10. A view `recent_enrollments` is defined as `SELECT * FROM Enrollments WHERE term >= '2025-Fall'`. A query `SELECT * FROM recent_enrollments WHERE student_id = 42` runs. Does the engine scan all of Enrollments or only rows from 2025-Fall onward? Explain the predicate pushdown.

  11. A `CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW` takes eight hours to refresh over a 200 GB transactional database. Propose two strategies to keep the view reasonably fresh without an eight-hour daily outage.

Hint

What part of the 200 GB actually changes between refreshes? If only a small fraction does, the question becomes how to avoid recomputing the rest. Consider partitioning by time and swapping partitions, or maintaining the view incrementally as writes arrive.

  12. Using the schema `Orders(order_id, customer_id, order_date, amount)`, write a window query that returns, for each order, the running total of that customer's spending across orders, ordered by date. Then modify it to a **7-day sliding window sum** instead.

Hint

Both forms share the same `PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY order_date`. The difference is in the frame clause: the default frame for a running total is `ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW`. For the 7-day window, you need a RANGE-based frame measured in time, not in rows.

#### Think Deeper

  13. The optimizer will usually decorrelate a correlated subquery. Construct a correlated subquery that _cannot_ be decorrelated automatically — one where the outer row and inner aggregation genuinely require nested execution. What structural feature blocks decorrelation?

Hint

Decorrelation replaces "run once per outer row" with a group-by-then-join. What kind of inner expression defeats that rewrite? Consider non-equi correlations (inequalities on the outer row) or aggregations whose grouping key depends on an arbitrary expression of the outer row.

  14. Consider two queries that return the same rows:
         
         SELECT s.* FROM Students s WHERE s.id IN (SELECT student_id FROM Enrollments);
         

vs
         
         SELECT DISTINCT s.* FROM Students s JOIN Enrollments e ON e.student_id = s.id;
         

Describe a situation where they differ in cost by more than 10×, and explain which operator in the plan causes the gap.

  15. A developer argues: "We should replace all aggregate queries with window functions because they return row-level information too." Name two costs of that policy — one in execution time, one in readability — that should make you push back.

  16. A materialized view backs a live dashboard. The underlying tables change at 100 writes/second. You have three options: (a) refresh the view every 60 seconds on a timer, (b) refresh incrementally on each write, (c) keep the view as a regular non-materialized view. Sketch the cost/freshness tradeoff for each and name the scenario that would make you pick each one.

  17. Recursive CTEs can compute transitive closures, but deeply recursive chains can blow up memory. Propose a design choice (schema or query-shape) that lets a very deep recursion — say a course-prerequisite tree 30 levels deep — still terminate quickly. Hint: think about what state the engine must keep between iterations.

Hint

A recursive CTE re-derives the closure every time the query runs. What if the closure were stored instead of derived? Look up "closure table" or "materialized path" patterns, and compare the write-side cost (every edge change updates many rows) against the read-side win (one indexed lookup).

  18. Window functions require at least one sort. Indexes can sometimes eliminate that sort. Sketch the condition under which a B-tree index on `(dept_id, gpa DESC)` would eliminate the sort for a query that partitions by `dept_id` and orders by `gpa DESC`. We will see the mechanism in Chapter 10; try to reason it now.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter added the three instruments that make SQL analytically complete. **Subqueries** compose queries as values, tables, or rows; correlated subqueries reference the outer row, and modern optimizers usually decorrelate them. **EXISTS** , **IN** , and **JOIN** look interchangeable but diverge on NULL semantics and on stopping behavior — pick by intent, verify by plan. **CTEs** package named subqueries and enable recursion; whether they impose an optimization fence depends on your engine's version. **Views** abstract and gate access, and materialized views trade staleness for read speed. **Window functions** compute across related rows without collapsing, and cost at least one sort per unique OVER clause.

Every construct here is a logical statement about what to compute. How fast the engine computes it is the subject of Chapters 9–12: storage, indexes, join algorithms, and the optimizer. Thread B's 20-minute join will not get faster through syntax alone. The next chapter opens the engine box.

---

## Chapter 9: Storage, Pages, and the Buffer Pool

Up to this point the database has been a mathematical abstraction. Relations were sets; operators transformed sets into other sets; the optimizer was free to reorder because any logically equivalent plan produced the same answer. That abstraction is beautiful, and it is a lie.

On a real machine, a relation is a sequence of bytes on a disk. The bytes are grouped into fixed-size blocks that move between disk and memory one block at a time. A query that reads one row of a 10-million-row table does not actually read "one row" — it reads a full block containing that row and many others. The cost model of the whole DBMS flows from this fact.

This chapter descends one layer. We follow a single query — Thread A's "list the courses student Alice is enrolled in" — from the SQL layer through the storage manager to the disk and back. Along the way we meet the **page** , the **buffer pool** , the **file organization** , and the cost metric that controls every decision from Chapter 10 onward: the number of I/Os.

### 9.1 The Storage Hierarchy Revisited

Chapter 1 gave the big picture. Now we make it quantitative.

Level | Capacity | Latency | Bandwidth | Relative cost/GB  
---|---|---|---|---  
CPU registers | $\sim 4$ | $\sim 0.3$ | 5 TB/s | astronomical  
L1 / L2 / L3 cache | $\sim 1$ | 1–10 ns | 1–2 TB/s | very high  
Main memory (DRAM) | GB–TB | 100 ns | 50 GB/s | $\sim 5$ 
SSD (NVMe) | TB | $\text{s}$ | 5 GB/s | $\sim 0.10$ 
HDD | tens of TB | 10 ms | 200 MB/s | $\sim 0.02$ 
  
Two numbers matter more than all the others. DRAM is **100,000 times faster** than an HDD and **200 times faster** than a good SSD for a random access. And a block read or write to either SSD or HDD has a **fixed startup cost** (seek, queueing, device latency) that dwarfs the cost of moving the next byte once it has started — once the arm is positioned, you may as well read a whole block.

These two facts dictate everything about physical database design:

  1. Access disk as rarely as possible.
  2. When you do access it, move _blocks_ , not bytes.

#### Definition: The I/O cost model

A DBMS's cost model measures queries in the number of **block reads and writes** it performs. CPU time is a secondary concern — a hundred million CPU instructions cost less than one random block read from spinning disk. Everything that follows — file layouts, indexes, join algorithms — is designed to minimize I/Os.

#### Common Misconception

"SSDs are so fast that the old disk-era algorithms no longer matter." Not quite. SSDs do narrow the penalty, but a random 4 KB read on NVMe is still ~50 $\mu$s, while a main-memory read is ~100 ns — a 500× gap. DBMSs still optimize for block-I/O cost; the _magnitude_ has shrunk but the _shape_ of the tradeoffs has not.

### 9.2 The Page — The Atomic Unit of I/O

A **page** (sometimes called a **block** or a **frame**) is the fixed-size unit a DBMS reads from and writes to disk.

#### Definition: Page

A **page** is a contiguous, fixed-size chunk of disk storage, typically 4 KB, 8 KB, or 16 KB, that a DBMS treats as indivisible for the purposes of I/O. All reads and writes happen at page granularity.

Page sizes by real system:

* PostgreSQL: 8 KB (compile-time constant, rarely changed)
* MySQL InnoDB: 16 KB (tunable at 4/8/16/32/64 KB)
* SQLite: 4 KB by default, configurable up to 64 KB
* SQL Server: 8 KB (fixed)
* Oracle: 8 KB default, tunable per tablespace

Why not 1 byte, or 1 MB? Smaller pages mean more metadata per unit of data and more per-page operations; larger pages mean loading too much irrelevant data when a query wants one row. The OS page size (4 KB on Linux) and the device block size (4 KB on most SSDs) set natural floors. Empirically, 8–16 KB strikes the best balance for transactional workloads; analytical warehouses often use larger pages (64 KB+) because they scan wide swaths anyway.

#### Tradeoff: page size

* **Smaller pages** (4 KB): less wasted memory when reading a row, less contention on a hot page, cheaper to checksum. But more pages per table means larger indexes (more internal nodes in a B-tree) and more per-page overhead.
* **Larger pages** (32 KB+): fewer page reads to scan a big table, denser indexes, better sequential scan throughput. But wasted I/O when you only want one small row, and more contention when many transactions hit the same page.

OLTP systems choose small-to-medium pages; OLAP systems choose large.

#### Page layout — the slotted page

A page is not just a raw array of rows. Rows can have variable length (VARCHAR columns, nullable fields), so the DBMS uses a **slotted page** layout.
    
    
    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ Page Header: LSN, checksum, free space  │
    │ Slot directory: [s1, s2, s3, ...]       │
    │ ...                                     │
    │ free space (grows downward)             │
    │ ...                                     │
    │ Record N                                │
    │ ...                                     │
    │ Record 2                                │
    │ Record 1 (grows upward from the bottom) │
    └─────────────────────────────────────────┘
    

The **slot directory** at the top grows down; records at the bottom grow up. Each slot entry holds an offset (and length) pointing to a record. A row is addressed by `(page_id, slot_id)` — the **record identifier** or **RID**.

The slotted layout gains three properties:

  1. **Variable-length rows** fit without fragmentation — new rows slot into free space.
  2. **Stable addresses** : updating a row's content does not move its RID, because the slot indirects.
  3. **Easy deletion** : mark the slot as deleted; compact later during a vacuum or page reorganization.

When you see an identifier like PostgreSQL's `ctid = (42, 7)`, that means "slot 7 of page 42."

#### Common Misconception

"Updating a column rewrites the row in place." Often true — when the new value fits. When the updated row no longer fits in the page's free space, the DBMS may move it to another page and leave a **forwarding pointer** (Oracle migrated rows, MySQL's overflow pages, PostgreSQL's new row version). A sequence of such forwards can double the I/O cost of scanning a row. Periodic table reorganization or `VACUUM FULL` fixes this.

### 9.3 Heap Files — The Default Organization

The simplest way to store a table is as a **heap file** : a collection of pages, with rows placed into whichever page has free space.

#### Definition: Heap file

A **heap file** is an unordered collection of pages, each containing rows in insertion order or in whatever free-space slot was available. Rows have no global ordering.

When a new row arrives:

  1. The free-space manager identifies a page with enough room.
  2. If no such page exists, a new page is appended to the file.
  3. The row is inserted at the first available slot in the chosen page.

When a row is deleted, the slot is freed; periodically, the DBMS reclaims space and compacts the page.

#### Cost of scanning a heap file

A full table scan reads every page once. For Thread A's `Enrollments` table with, say, 800,000 rows at ~80 bytes each and 8 KB pages:

rows per page≈⌊8192−5080⌋≈100$$\text{rows per page} \approx \left\lfloor \frac{8192 - 50}{80} \right\rfloor \approx 100rows per page$$$\text{pages} \approx \lceil 800{,}000 / 100 \$

A full scan is 8,000 page reads. On SSD at 50 $\mu$s per random read, that is 0.4 seconds. On HDD at 10 ms per random read, 80 seconds. But heap files are read sequentially, not randomly, so the real number is much lower — a sequential SSD read at 5 GB/s can push 8,000 pages in ~13 ms.

Here is the point: for a full scan, a heap file is almost optimal. For a **point lookup** — "find the one row where student_id = 1234" — a heap file is _worst-case_. With no ordering, the engine must read up to every page to find the row. That is the motivation for indexes (Chapter 10).

### 9.4 Thread A: Measuring the Cost of "Find Alice's Courses"

Thread A, Chapter 2, stored the registration system in CSV files. Chapter 3 moved it into relations. Chapter 4 asked:
    
    
    SELECT c.title
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c     ON e.course_id = c.id
    WHERE s.name = 'Alice';
    

Let's count pages. Assume a freshly loaded heap-file table with no indexes.

* `Students`: 10,000 rows × 100 B/row ≈ 1,000,000 B ≈ 125 pages.
* `Enrollments`: 800,000 rows × 80 B/row ≈ 64,000,000 B ≈ 8,000 pages.
* `Courses`: 500 rows × 200 B/row ≈ 100,000 B ≈ 13 pages.

Step 1: find Alice. `SELECT * FROM Students WHERE name = 'Alice'`. Heap file, no index on `name`. The engine scans all 125 Student pages. Worst case: 125 page reads.

Step 2: find Alice's enrollments. Alice has, say, 8 rows in `Enrollments`. With no index on `student_id`, the engine scans the entire `Enrollments` heap — 8,000 page reads.

Step 3: look up each of the 8 course rows by `course_id`. With no index on `Courses.id`, each lookup scans up to 13 pages — worst case 8 × 13 = 104 reads.

Total: about 8,200 page reads to answer one question about one student.

On an 8 KB page and a modern SSD, that is perhaps 400 ms — quick enough to be invisible. On an HDD, 80 seconds. And on a 1-billion-row database, 8,200 becomes 820,000, which is 40 seconds on SSD and half an hour on HDD. This is why indexes exist.

Chapter 10 will add `CREATE INDEX ON Enrollments(student_id)` and drop step 2 from 8,000 reads to ∼$\sim$∼ 3 — a 2,500× improvement on that step alone.

### 9.5 Reading a Page — The Buffer Pool

A page on disk is useless to SQL. To operate on rows, the DBMS must first copy the page into memory. That memory region is the **buffer pool**.

#### Definition: Buffer pool

The **buffer pool** is a fixed-size region of main memory managed by the DBMS that caches recently accessed pages. When a query needs a page, the engine checks the buffer pool first; a cache hit avoids a disk read.

Structure of the buffer pool:

* A pool of **frames** , each the size of one page.
* A **page table** mapping `page_id → frame` (a hash table).
* Per-frame metadata: **pin count** (how many operators currently need this page), **dirty bit** (has the page been modified?), **access timestamps** or LRU-chain pointers.

#### The buffer pool protocol

When an operator asks for page PPP:

  1. The **buffer manager** looks up PPP in the page table.
  2. **Cache hit** : increment pin count, return pointer to frame. Done.
  3. **Cache miss** : choose a **victim frame** (an unpinned, non-dirty one). If the victim is dirty, write it to disk first. Read PPP from disk into the victim frame. Update page table. Pin, return pointer.
  4. When the operator finishes with the page, it **unpins**. If it modified the page, it sets the dirty bit.

#### Pinning

**Pinning** a page marks it as "in use — do not evict." Multiple operators can pin the same page. Only when pin count drops to zero may the buffer manager evict it.

#### Tradeoff: buffer pool size

A larger buffer pool caches more pages and reduces misses. The optimum is roughly "working set" — the set of pages a query mix touches repeatedly. Beyond that, extra RAM helps less and less (diminishing returns). Undersizing the buffer pool is disastrous; oversizing wastes RAM the OS could be using for page cache or query workspace.

A rule of thumb: 25–40% of machine RAM for PostgreSQL's `shared_buffers`, up to 80% of RAM for MySQL InnoDB's `innodb_buffer_pool_size` (because InnoDB bypasses the OS page cache and expects all page caching to happen inside). The difference comes from which layers do the caching — one more place where "it depends on the engine" is the right answer.

#### Common Misconception

"Memory is cheap, just cache everything." When the database is larger than RAM, you _must_ have eviction. Even when data fits in RAM, the buffer pool must still manage pages for correctness — a crashed page that has not been flushed will be recovered via WAL (Chapter 17), and the buffer pool enforces flush ordering. Buffering is not only a performance feature; it is a correctness mechanism.

### 9.6 Replacement Policies — Which Page to Evict?

When a frame is needed and all frames are full, the buffer manager picks a victim. The policy matters enormously: a bad victim is one that the next query would have needed.

#### LRU — Least Recently Used

Keep pages ordered by last access time. Evict the one not accessed for the longest time.

* **Intuition** : "recent use predicts future use."
* **Data structure** : a doubly-linked list of frames + hash index; on access, move to head; on eviction, take the tail.
* **Problem** : a long sequential scan touches every page once, polluting the list — pages the OLTP workload needs get evicted, replaced by scan pages that will not be reused. This is **sequential flooding**.

#### LRU-K

Track the **k kk-th most recent** access per page. Evict pages whose k-th last access is oldest. A scan touches each page once; one access does not displace a page that has been touched multiple times. LRU-2 is standard in many engines.

#### Clock / Second-Chance

A cyclic buffer with an "arm." Each frame has a **reference bit**. When the arm points at a frame: if ref=1, set to 0 and advance; if ref=0, evict. Cheap (no lock-heavy list maintenance), and approximates LRU well.

Most production DBMSs implement a **Clock with frequency counters** : a scan can touch pages without setting the counter as high as a real read, so the counter-based variant resists scan pollution without the full complexity of LRU-K. PostgreSQL's buffer manager is a Clock variant; InnoDB uses a segmented LRU (young + old sublists) with similar goals.

#### Tradeoff

LRU is simple but vulnerable to scans. LRU-K is smart but maintains more state. Clock is cheap and practical. Production systems choose Clock-family policies because under realistic workloads with mixed small reads and large scans, they balance quality and overhead. A buffer policy "perfect" for microbenchmarks can be catastrophic in real workloads.

### 9.7 Writing a Page — Dirty Bits and Flushing

When an UPDATE executes, the engine modifies the in-memory copy of the page and sets the **dirty bit**. The modified page is not written back immediately — that would be catastrophic I/O.

Instead, dirty pages are flushed to disk at selected moments:

  1. When a page is evicted (write-back evict).
  2. When a background process (**checkpointer**) decides to.
  3. When a transaction commits, _its log records_ are forced to disk (Chapter 17 — that is what WAL is about, not the data pages themselves).

#### Definition: Write-back vs write-through

* **Write-through** : every write goes to disk immediately. Simple, crash-safe, catastrophically slow.
* **Write-back** : writes accumulate in memory; flushed later. Fast, but requires a recovery mechanism for crashes.

All serious DBMSs use write-back plus WAL. Chapter 17 explains the correctness guarantee.

#### The checkpoint

A **checkpoint** is a moment where the DBMS flushes some or all dirty pages and records a marker in the log. After a crash, recovery replays only log records after the most recent checkpoint. Checkpoints trade runtime cost (a flush burst) for faster recovery. Too-infrequent checkpoints mean very long crash recovery; too-frequent checkpoints mean the disk is always writing.

### 9.8 File Organizations Beyond Heap

Heap files are the default, but the DBMS can organize a table's pages differently.

#### Sorted (ISAM) file

Rows are stored in sorted order by a key. Point lookups use binary search over pages (log NNN reads); range queries are sequential. Insertions are expensive — a new row in the middle requires shifting or overflow pages. Read-mostly tables benefit; write-heavy tables suffer.

#### Clustered (index-organized) table

The primary index _is_ the table — leaves of the B-tree hold the rows themselves, not just pointers to them. We meet this in Chapter 10.

#### Partitioned file

A large table is split by a partition key across multiple heap files, one per partition. Queries with a filter on the partition key touch only the relevant partitions (**partition pruning**).

#### Tradeoff: organization choice

Organization | Point lookup | Range scan | Insert cost | Use case  
---|---|---|---|---  
Heap | full scan | full scan | O(1) | write-heavy, index-backed reads  
Sorted | log N | sequential | high (shift) | read-mostly, key-ordered  
Clustered | log N | sequential | O(log N) | OLTP workloads with one dominant access pattern  
Partitioned | per-partition | per-partition | O(1) | very large tables with a natural partition key  
  
Chapter 10 focuses almost entirely on the indexes that make these organizations perform.

### 9.9 The Catalog and Metadata Pages

Besides user data, the DBMS stores metadata: which tables exist, which columns each has, what indexes are defined, who owns what. This **system catalog** is itself stored as tables — PostgreSQL's `pg_class`, `pg_attribute`, `pg_index`; MySQL's `information_schema`; SQLite's `sqlite_master`. The parser reads the catalog to type-check queries and to locate the physical files holding each table's pages.

Catalog pages are pinned heavily (every query reads them), usually kept warm in the buffer pool, and very small compared to data. Still, on cold start, the first query of the day is often slower than the second — the catalog is being loaded.

### 9.10 Cost Model for Chapter 10 and Beyond

From here on, every plan we evaluate will be measured in page reads (and writes). A single formula recurs:

$\text{Query cost} \approx \text{CPU cost} + C_{\text{IO}} \cdot (\text{pages read} + \text{pages written})$

where CIOC_{\text{IO}}CIO is much larger than per-row CPU cost. Two quick cost examples for Thread A, no indexes:

* "Count all students": sequential scan of `Students` = 125 reads.
* "Find student whose id = 4221": full scan = 125 reads worst case.
* "All enrollments for student 4221": full scan of Enrollments = 8,000 reads. With an index: 3–5 reads.

The asymmetry between these cases is the whole point of indexing. Sequential scans are cheap per-row but expensive per-selective-query; indexes make highly selective queries fast but have their own storage and write-path cost.

#### Common Misconception

"A good DBMS avoids all disk I/O." The goal is not zero I/O — that would require infinite RAM. The goal is to ensure the I/O you do is amortized over as many rows as possible (a sequential scan that reads a page for 100 rows is cheaper per row than a point lookup that reads a page for one row). Which of these patterns dominates your workload determines your physical design.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Larger pages trade per-query I/O amortization for wasted bytes on selective reads.** 32 KB pages scan big tables with fewer reads and yield denser B-tree internal nodes, but a point lookup drags in extra rows the query never needed and concentrates contention on fewer frames.
* **LRU trades simplicity for vulnerability to sequential flooding.** Pure LRU is cheap to maintain but a single large scan evicts the hot OLTP working set; LRU-K and Clock variants add state or a reference bit to resist this, paying more metadata per frame.
* **Write-back buffering trades crash simplicity for throughput.** Deferring dirty-page flushes until eviction or checkpoint turns many random writes into fewer batched ones, but correctness now depends on WAL ordering and recovery replay (Chapter 17).
* **Heap files trade point-lookup cost for write-path simplicity.** An append-only heap makes INSERT O(1) and full scans near-optimal, but any selective read degenerates to a full scan without an index — the motivation for Chapter 10.
* **Sorted/clustered organizations trade insert cost for log-time lookup and sequential range scans.** Maintaining key order gives O(log N) point lookups and sequential range access, but every mid-file insert requires shifts, overflow pages, or tree splits.
* **A DBMS-managed buffer pool trades duplicated caching for control over flush ordering and pin semantics.** The data may sit in both `shared_buffers` and the OS page cache, but only the DBMS's buffer pool can enforce WAL-before-data ordering and prevent eviction of in-use pages.
* **Frequent checkpoints trade steady-state write bandwidth for short recovery time.** Checkpointing often means the disk is always flushing dirty pages; checkpointing rarely means a crash replays hours of log. Tuning picks a point on this curve.

### 9.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Define "page" in the context of a DBMS. Why is I/O measured in pages rather than bytes or rows?
  2. Describe the slotted page layout. What two properties does the slot directory give you that a flat array of rows does not?
  3. Explain pinning. What would go wrong if an operator could read a page while the buffer manager was free to evict it?
  4. Compare LRU and Clock replacement policies. Name one workload where they diverge significantly.
  5. Name three moments at which a dirty page might be flushed to disk, and identify which of them is triggered by transaction commit.
  6. What is the difference between a heap file and a clustered file? Which is better for point lookups on the primary key, and why?

#### Application Problems

  7. A table has 2,000,000 rows at 250 bytes each. Pages are 16 KB with 100 bytes of per-page overhead. Compute the number of data pages required and the cost in seconds for a full scan on (a) an HDD delivering 200 MB/s sequential and (b) an SSD delivering 3 GB/s sequential.

Hint

First compute usable bytes per page (16 KB minus overhead), then rows per page, then total pages. For elapsed time, total bytes read divided by sequential bandwidth — page count itself is not the right unit when the bottleneck is bytes/second.

  8. A buffer pool holds 1,000 frames. A workload repeatedly scans a 10,000-page table while occasionally performing a lookup on a 50-page `Courses` table. Under pure LRU, describe what happens to the cached `Courses` pages after each full scan. Propose one policy change that fixes it.

Hint

Trace what LRU's "recently used" timestamp does when 10,000 single-touch scan pages flow through 1,000 frames. Which pages end up at the tail? The fix should distinguish "touched once" from "touched many times" — Section 9.6 names the relevant family.

  9. Consider a slotted page with 16 KB capacity and row overhead of 20 bytes. A `Students` row is 120 bytes; an `Enrollments` row is 60 bytes. Compute the maximum rows per page for each and the estimated number of pages for 50,000 students and 400,000 enrollments.

  10. A table has a foreign key `Enrollments.student_id → Students.id`. Without indexes, every insert into `Enrollments` must verify the referenced student exists. If `Students` has 125 heap pages and on average 32 are buffered, what is the expected I/O cost per insert for the FK check? How does creating a primary-key index on `Students.id` change this?

Hint

Without an index, the FK check is a full scan but it can stop on first match — think expected pages read, not worst case, and subtract pages already in the buffer pool. With the PK index, an index probe replaces the scan; what is the depth of a small B+ tree on 10,000 keys?

  11. A row of 1 KB is updated. The updated row is now 1.5 KB. The current page has 0.4 KB of free space. Describe what the DBMS may do. How does this affect subsequent scans?

  12. A DBMS offers page sizes of 4, 8, 16, and 32 KB. A workload is 90% point lookups of small rows (150 B) and 10% full-table scans of a 500-million-row table. Which page size minimizes total I/O cost, and why? (Qualitative reasoning is enough.)

Hint

Weight each page-size choice's cost by the workload mix. A point lookup pays for one page regardless of how full it is; a scan benefits from larger pages. Which side of the 90/10 mix dominates the weighted total?

#### Think Deeper

  13. A colleague proposes eliminating the buffer pool entirely and memory-mapping all database files (`mmap`) so the OS's page cache does the job. List two correctness concerns and one performance concern that a DBMS-managed buffer pool addresses that `mmap` does not.

Hint

Think about WAL: who decides when a dirty data page is allowed to reach disk? With `mmap`, the OS may flush a page at any moment — what does that do to the WAL-before-data invariant? For performance, consider what the DBMS knows about access patterns that the OS does not.

  14. Why do most DBMSs use Clock-family policies rather than "perfect" LRU? Frame your answer in terms of concurrency and lock contention on the buffer pool metadata.

  15. You are given two tables of equal total size, one stored as a heap and one as a sorted file on the primary key. Describe a workload mix (percentages of point lookups, range scans, insertions, and updates) under which the heap file outperforms the sorted file, and one under which the sorted file wins by an order of magnitude.

  16. Thread A, Chapter 2, used CSV files. CSV files are effectively heap files with a parsing overhead. Why is "store rows as CSV in a file system" never the answer even when the queries are very simple? Point to two capabilities of a real DBMS storage layer that CSV lacks.

  17. A "clustered index" means the index _is_ the table — leaf pages contain the rows themselves. What is the performance implication for (a) scans in primary-key order, (b) point lookups by the primary key, (c) inserts when the primary key is a random UUID versus an auto-incremented integer? Reason qualitatively now; we will make it quantitative in Chapter 10.

  18. A buffer pool of size BBB and a workload that repeatedly cycles through a working set of size WWW pages. Argue that hit rate is $\approx 1$ when B≥WB $\geq WB$≥W and $\approx 0$ when B<WB < WB<W (the **cliff**), rather than gradually improving. What real-world property softens the cliff? We will revisit this when we meet the optimizer's statistics in Chapter 12.

Hint

Trace what LRU does on round k+1 of a cycle when B = W − 1: which page is least-recently-used at the moment the next access arrives? Now consider whether _real_ workloads access pages with uniform frequency, or whether some pages are far hotter than others.

### Chapter Summary

The DBMS sits on hardware whose cost structure is fixed: disk is three or four orders of magnitude slower than memory, and I/O happens in block-size chunks. Every design choice from here to the end of the performance chapters follows from this.

A table is a sequence of **pages** in a file, managed under some **file organization** — heap by default, sorted or clustered for specific access patterns. A page is laid out as a **slotted page** : header, slot directory, records growing toward each other with free space in the middle. Rows are addressed by `(page_id, slot_id)`.

The **buffer pool** caches recently-used pages in memory, manages eviction through a Clock or LRU-family policy, and ensures correctness through pinning and dirty bits. Writes accumulate in memory; flushing is decoupled from commit and relies on WAL (Chapter 17) for recoverability.

The cost of a query is, to a first approximation, the number of pages it reads. Thread A's "find Alice's courses" costs 8,000+ page reads without indexes. Chapter 10 will cut that by three orders of magnitude through B+ trees.

---

## Chapter 10: Indexing — B+ Trees and Hashing

Chapter 9 left us with an uncomfortable arithmetic. Thread A's `Enrollments` table has 800,000 rows in 8,000 pages. A question as simple as "what courses is student 4221 enrolled in?" forces a full scan — 8,000 page reads — because the heap file has no ordering we can exploit. Eight thousand page reads to return eight rows is not slow; it is offensively slow. The rows we want are 0.1% of the table. We read the other 99.9% for nothing.

An **index** is the data structure that lets us find rows without scanning. It is the DBMS's most powerful performance tool and its most easily abused one. An unnecessary index costs storage and slows every write. A missing index turns a millisecond query into a minute query. Two wrong indexes can interfere and leave both queries slower than one right index would have made them.

This chapter answers three questions. First, what kinds of access patterns can an index accelerate, and what kinds can it not? Second, how are the two dominant index structures — the **B+ tree** and the **hash table** — actually built and queried? Third, how do we choose among primary, secondary, clustered, covering, partial, and composite indexes?

We stay with Thread A throughout and revisit Thread B's 20-minute query at the end of the chapter with a very different time.

### 10.1 What an Index Is, Precisely

#### Definition: Index

An **index** is an auxiliary data structure that maps values of one or more columns to the physical locations (or direct row copies) of the rows containing those values, so that the DBMS can locate matching rows without reading every page of the underlying table.

Three words in that definition carry most of the weight.

* **Auxiliary** : the index is separate from the table. Losing the index would not lose any data — just the ability to find rows fast.
* **Maps values to locations** : the index takes a search key and returns a **RID** (recall Chapter 9: `(page_id, slot_id)`) or a direct row copy.
* **Without reading every page** : the index's own cost must be, in the common case, log⁡N\log NlogN or O(1). A "faster full scan" is not an index.

An index is nothing more than a database for the database. It must be kept in sync with the table: every INSERT, UPDATE that changes the indexed columns, or DELETE must update the index. That maintenance cost is the price we pay for fast reads.

#### Common Misconception

"An index always speeds up queries." It speeds up _selective_ queries. When a query returns most of the table (say 50%+), the DBMS correctly skips the index and does a sequential scan instead — because following the index back to the heap for every row costs more than just reading the heap. Chapter 12 on the optimizer formalizes this.

### 10.2 Access Patterns and the Indexes That Serve Them

Before choosing a structure, know the query shape.

Query pattern | Example | Ideal index  
---|---|---  
Exact match | `WHERE student_id = 4221` | Hash or B+ tree  
Range scan | `WHERE gpa BETWEEN 3.5 AND 4.0` | B+ tree  
Prefix match | `WHERE name LIKE 'Alic%'` | B+ tree  
Sort avoidance | `ORDER BY credits DESC` | B+ tree (with matching order)  
Multi-column | `WHERE dept_id = 3 AND year = 4` | Composite B+ tree  
Uniqueness enforcement | `PRIMARY KEY`, `UNIQUE` | B+ tree  
Set membership | `WHERE tag IN (...)` | B+ tree  
Containment of large sets | `WHERE id IN (bitmap of millions)` | Bitmap index  
Text containment | `WHERE body LIKE '%bankruptcy%'` | Full-text / trigram  
  
Two observations. First, the B+ tree appears in most rows — it is the default for a reason (range + equality + prefix + sort, all one structure). Second, "LIKE '%substring%'" with a leading wildcard cannot use a B+ tree; it requires a full-text index. Knowing this prevents a common mistake.

### 10.3 Why Not Just a Sorted Array?

The simplest index is a sorted array of `(key, RID)` pairs. Binary search is O(log⁡n)O(\log n)O(logn). Done?

Done for a read-only database. The moment we INSERT a row, we must insert into the middle of a sorted array — O(n) shifts. Even worse, the array does not fit in one page; it spans many pages, and the shift cost is in pages, not bytes.

We need a structure that is **sorted enough** for log-time search and **flexible enough** for log-time updates. The answer is the **B+ tree**.

### 10.4 The B+ Tree

#### Definition: B+ tree

A **B+ tree** of order ddd is a balanced search tree where every internal node holds between ddd and 2d2d2d keys (except the root), every leaf holds between ddd and 2d2d2d entries, and leaves are linked sequentially. All keys are present in the leaves; internal nodes only carry separator keys that guide search.

Three properties matter:

  1. **Balanced** : every root-to-leaf path has the same length. All lookups cost the same depth.
  2. **High fan-out** : each internal node has many children (hundreds to thousands on real page sizes). Depth grows as log⁡dN$\log_d NlogdN$ — for d=128d = 128d=128 and N=109N = 10^9N=109, depth is about 4.
  3. **Leaves are linked** : a range scan follows leaf pointers, no need to traverse back up the tree.

#### Structure on disk

A B+ tree node is one page. For 8 KB pages and a 24-byte entry (16-byte key + 8-byte pointer), a node holds roughly 340 entries. For 16 KB pages and smaller keys, 1000+ entries per node is common.

Leaf layout:
    
    
    [header | (k1, rid1) | (k2, rid2) | ... | (kN, ridN) | next_leaf_ptr]
    

Internal layout:
    
    
    [header | p0 | k1 | p1 | k2 | p2 | ... | kN | pN]
    

where `pi` is a child pointer and `ki` is a separator: keys less than `ki` live in `pi-1`, keys ≥ `ki` live in `pi`.

#### Lookup

Search for key KKK:

  1. Start at root.
  2. At each internal node, binary-search the separator array; descend to the appropriate child.
  3. At the leaf, binary-search for KKK; return the RID.

Cost: O(log⁡dN)O($\log_d N)O(logdN) pages. For 800,000 rows at fan-out 250, depth is ~3$ — three page reads to find any row.

#### Range scan

Search for the start key KloK_{$\text{lo}}Klo. Walk leaves forward until K>KhiK > K_{\text{hi}}K>Khi. The leaves are linked, so traversal is sequential: no tree climb between leaves.$

Cost: O(log⁡dN+R/P)O($\log_d N + R/P)O(logdN+R/P) where RRR is the number of rows returned and PPP is rows per leaf page. For a 1000-row range with 250 rows per page, 4 leaf pages plus 3 internal$ — 7 page reads total.

#### Insert

To insert key KKK:

  1. Descend to the leaf where KKK would live.
  2. Insert into the leaf, keeping sorted order.
  3. If the leaf overflows: **split**. Split allocates a new leaf, moves half the entries, and promotes the smallest key of the right leaf as a separator into the parent.
  4. If the parent overflows: split the parent. Propagate up.
  5. If the root splits: create a new root. This is the _only_ way the tree grows in height.

Cost: O(log⁡dN)O(\log_d N)O(logdN) page reads, plus the writes of any pages we modify. Splits are rare on average because each split leaves both halves with only 50% fill.

#### Delete

Symmetric. If a leaf underflows (fewer than ddd entries), borrow from a sibling or merge. Most DBMSs implement **lazy deletion** : leaf pages are allowed to become sparse and are reclaimed only when they are completely empty. This simplifies concurrency enormously (Chapter 15) at the cost of some wasted space.

#### The tree stays balanced automatically

The key guarantee: height only increases when the root splits, and only decreases during a delete that merges to a root that has become empty. Every other operation preserves balance. Unlike a plain BST, a B+ tree has no pathological shape for pathological insertion orders.

#### Tradeoff: B+ tree vs plain B-tree

In a **B-tree** , internal nodes also hold RIDs, so a lookup can terminate early if it finds the key in an internal node. Beautiful in theory. In practice, B+ trees dominate because (a) internal nodes hold _more_ separators per page (no RID column eats space), so fan-out is higher and depth is smaller; (b) range scans via leaf pointers are a killer feature; (c) simpler concurrency and recovery. "B-tree" in everyday DBMS speak almost always means B+ tree.

### 10.5 Primary, Secondary, and Clustered Indexes

#### Primary index

The index on the primary key. Enforces uniqueness. Usually the first index created and, in many engines, the one that controls physical row layout.

#### Secondary index

Any non-primary index. Maps indexed column values to RIDs. A secondary-index lookup that needs non-indexed columns must then fetch the row from the heap — an extra I/O per matched row.

#### Clustered index

A **clustered index** integrates the index with the table: the leaves of the index _are_ the rows. No separate heap. PostgreSQL does not do this by default (but `CLUSTER` command exists, one-shot physical reorg). InnoDB, SQL Server, and Oracle IOTs use clustered indexes for the primary key.

#### Non-clustered secondary indexes on a clustered table

In InnoDB, the primary key is clustered. A secondary index on column XXX maps X→primary keyX \to $\text{primary key}X$→primary key, not X→RIDX \to $\text{RID}X$→RID. A secondary lookup does two B+ tree descents: one for X→PKX \to \text{PK}X→PK, another for PK$\text{PK} \to \text{row}PK$. Trade-off: slightly more work per secondary lookup, but secondary indexes don't need to be rewritten when a row's physical position changes (rows move around on splits; their PK stays stable).

#### Tradeoff: clustered vs heap+secondary

Aspect | Heap + secondary index | Clustered (index-organized)  
---|---|---  
Primary-key point lookup | 1 traversal + 1 heap fetch | 1 traversal (rows live at leaves)  
Range on primary key | scattered heap reads | sequential leaf walk  
Secondary index lookup | traversal + direct RID | traversal + PK lookup + primary-tree traversal  
Insert | row appended to heap | row inserted into primary tree (may split)  
Primary-key update | heap row stays; PK index updated | Row _moves_ in the tree (very expensive — avoid)  
  
Choose clustered when most access is by primary key, especially range access. Choose heap+secondary when many indexes compete for "primary access pattern" or when PK updates are common.

#### Common Misconception

"PostgreSQL has clustered indexes." PostgreSQL has the `CLUSTER tablename USING indexname` command, which physically sorts the heap once according to an index. After subsequent writes, the ordering drifts. PostgreSQL does **not** have maintained clustered indexes. InnoDB does.

### 10.6 Composite Indexes and the Leftmost-Prefix Rule

A composite index on `(a, b, c)` is a B+ tree whose key is the concatenation of `a`, `b`, `c`.

#### Definition: Leftmost-prefix rule

A composite index on `(a, b, c)` can serve queries that filter on a **leftmost prefix** of the key: `a`, `(a, b)`, `(a, b, c)`. It cannot serve queries that filter only on `b` or only on `c` — not efficiently, at least. The tree is sorted primarily by `a`, then by `b` within each `a`, then by `c`.

Concrete implication for Thread A. Suppose we create:
    
    
    CREATE INDEX enrollments_by_student_term
      ON Enrollments(student_id, term, course_id);
    

Queries that benefit:

* `WHERE student_id = 4221` — uses full index.
* `WHERE student_id = 4221 AND term = '2025-Fall'` — uses full index.
* `WHERE student_id = 4221 AND term = '2025-Fall' AND course_id = 42` — uses full index.
* `WHERE term = '2025-Fall'` — **cannot** use the index. `term` is not the leading column.

The leftmost-prefix rule is the single most common source of "my index isn't being used" posts on Stack Overflow. Always put the most selective column first? Not necessarily — put the column you always filter on first. If every query filters by `student_id`, put it first. If half your queries filter only by `term`, you need a separate index on `term`.

#### Index-only scan (covering index)

If a query only needs columns that are already in the index, the engine can answer without touching the heap at all.
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Students(dept_id, gpa);
    SELECT dept_id, gpa FROM Students WHERE dept_id = 3;
    

The B+ tree leaves already contain `(dept_id, gpa, rid)`. We do not need the RID — we already have the answer. This is an **index-only scan** or **covering index**. The query avoids all heap I/O.

PostgreSQL provides an `INCLUDE` clause:
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Enrollments(student_id) INCLUDE (grade, term);
    

The leading column is the sort key. `grade, term` are stored in leaves but not used for traversal. Covering indexes are one of the biggest available performance wins for narrow reporting queries.

#### Tradeoff

Every additional column in an index grows leaf size, reduces fan-out, deepens the tree by a fraction, and costs write bandwidth on updates. Wide covering indexes pay for themselves for hot queries; they punish write-heavy tables. Measure before adopting.

### 10.7 Hash Indexes

A **hash index** maps a key to a bucket via a hash function. The bucket holds the `(key, RID)` entries for that hash value.

#### Structure

* **Static hashing** : fixed number of buckets. Overflow chains when a bucket fills. Simple; suffers when the load factor is too high.
* **Extendible hashing** : a directory of pointers to buckets. Doubling the directory doubles capacity without re-hashing. PostgreSQL's hash indexes use this.
* **Linear hashing** : grow one bucket at a time, amortizing split cost.

#### What hash indexes do well

Exact-match lookups, in O(1) page reads on average. For N=109N = 10^9N=109, a B+ tree costs 4 page reads; a hash index costs 1–2. In memory-resident hash tables the gap is even larger because no tree arithmetic is needed.

#### What hash indexes cannot do

* **Range scans** : the hash destroys ordering. Impossible.
* **Prefix matches** : impossible.
* **ORDER BY** : impossible.
* **Multi-column search with`=` on only some columns**: impossible unless you also partition the hash function per-column.

The restriction is so severe that most DBMSs default to B+ trees and only offer hash indexes for very specialized use cases. In Redis and memcached — pure key-value — hash is the whole show. In PostgreSQL, hash indexes are a niche.

#### Tradeoff

* **Hash** : O(1) equality, nothing else.
* **B+ tree** : O(log⁡N)O($\log N)O(logN) equality, plus range, prefix, ordered scan. The constant factor is small (3$–4 tree levels typically).

For most workloads, the one-page advantage of hashing is not worth giving up the rest. Use hash only when you are sure equality is the only query.

#### When to use a hash index in PostgreSQL

PostgreSQL hash indexes have been crash-safe (WAL-logged) since PostgreSQL 10 — earlier versions had to be rebuilt after a crash, making them impractical. This removes the main historical objection.

Use a hash index when all three conditions hold:

  1. **High cardinality** : the column holds many distinct values (UUIDs, user IDs, API tokens). Low-cardinality columns benefit more from partial indexes (e.g., `WHERE status = 'active'`) or simply a B+ tree, which the query planner can scan efficiently when selectivity is low.
  2. **Equality-only queries** : every access to this column is an exact match (`WHERE user_id = \$1`). If any query does a range (`BETWEEN`, `<`, `>`) or sorts on this column, a B+ tree is required.
  3. **Index size matters** : hash indexes are typically smaller than a B+ tree on the same column because they store only hashes and RIDs, not the full key ordering structure.

**Canonical example** : a `sessions` table with a UUID `token` column used exclusively for `WHERE token = \$1` lookups:
    
    
    CREATE INDEX sessions_token_hash_idx ON sessions USING HASH (token);
    

If you later need `ORDER BY token` or range access, drop it and create a B+ tree. Most workloads should default to B+ tree unless this tradeoff is consciously evaluated.

### 10.8 Bitmap, Partial, and Other Specialized Indexes

#### Bitmap indexes

For low-cardinality columns (gender, status, country-code), a **bitmap index** stores one bit vector per distinct value. `SELECT ... WHERE gender = 'F' AND country = 'UK'` computes an AND of two bit vectors — extremely fast, and cheap to store. Bitmap indexes are the staple of data warehouses but are poor for high-cardinality columns (a `user_id` bitmap would be mostly zeros).

#### Partial indexes
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Orders(customer_id) WHERE status = 'active';
    

The index only holds entries for rows satisfying the predicate. Smaller index, faster maintenance, perfect for "hot subset" queries (most active orders are a tiny fraction of historical orders). PostgreSQL and SQL Server support them; MySQL does not.

#### Expression / function-based indexes
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Students(LOWER(email));
    SELECT * FROM Students WHERE LOWER(email) = 'alice@uni.edu';
    

The index stores the result of the expression. Without it, the function in WHERE would prevent any regular index from being used (non-SARGable predicates — Chapter 12).

#### Full-text indexes

For `WHERE body LIKE '%bankruptcy%'` we need an **inverted index** : a map from terms to lists of documents containing them. PostgreSQL's `tsvector` and `GIN` indexes, MySQL's FULLTEXT, Elasticsearch's Lucene — all built on this idea. We will not pursue them further, but know that they exist as a separate family from B+ tree / hash.

### 10.9 Thread A: "Find Alice's Courses" with Indexes

Back to the query we measured in Chapter 9. Without indexes: ~8,200 page reads. Now add:
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Students(name);
    CREATE INDEX ON Enrollments(student_id);
    -- Courses already has a primary-key index on id.
    

> **A word on what "B+ tree lookup is 3 reads" actually means.** Earlier sections say things like "log_B(N) ≈ 3 I/Os to reach a leaf." That counts _only_ the tree descent — the page reads that traverse root → internal → leaf levels. It does **not** count (a) reading the actual heap page(s) where the data row lives (unless the index is covering), or (b) reading multiple heap pages if a single key has many matching rows scattered across a heap. So a real indexed query's total page-read cost is:
> 
> **`tree descent` (≈ log_B N, often 3) + `leaf read` (1, sometimes included in the descent) + `heap fetches` (1 per row retrieved, modulated by clustering)**
> 
> For a range query returning _k_ rows, the heap-fetch term dominates (k reads for unclustered, 1–⌈k/rows_per_page⌉ for clustered). Keep this decomposition in mind when reading the step-by-step totals below.

Step 1. `WHERE name = 'Alice'`. B+ tree on `Students(name)`: 3 reads to reach a leaf, then one heap fetch for the row. Total: 4 reads.

Step 2. Look up Alice's 8 enrollments using `Enrollments(student_id)`. B+ tree: 3 reads to a leaf, and the 8 matching rows are contiguous (all with `student_id = alice_id`) — likely 1 leaf page. Then 8 heap fetches. But Enrollments is clustered by nobody in particular, so the 8 RIDs point to 8 different heap pages. Total: 3 + 1 + 8 = 12 reads.

Step 3. For each of 8 course_ids, B+ tree lookup on `Courses(id)`. 3 reads each? No — the root and upper levels are in the buffer pool after the first lookup. Subsequent lookups pay 1–2 reads each. Total: 2 + 7 × 2 = 16 reads generously.

Grand total: 32 reads. Down from 8,200. A **250× improvement** on this one query from adding two indexes.

Now the promise of Chapter 9's arithmetic is concrete. This is why indexes exist.

#### The cost of those indexes

Each indexed column costs:

* **Disk space** : the index is typically 10–30% the size of the indexed column's data, times a small multiplier for tree overhead.
* **Insert cost** : every INSERT into Enrollments updates two structures instead of one — the heap and the index.
* **Update cost** : an UPDATE that changes the indexed column updates the index (delete+insert).
* **Potentially slower TPC-C-style benchmarks** : write-heavy OLTP can slow down 10–20% per additional secondary index.

A rough but useful guideline: _one index per well-defined query pattern_. If two queries hit the same pattern, share an index. Do not create indexes speculatively. Measure which queries dominate, then build indexes for them.

#### Common Misconception

"Index every column, just in case." Over-indexing is a leading cause of slow OLTP systems. Each write pays all the indexes' update costs. Monitor with `pg_stat_user_indexes` or equivalent; indexes that are never read are pure cost.

### 10.10 Index Selectivity and the Optimizer's Decision

The optimizer does not always use an index even when one is available.

#### Definition: Selectivity

The **selectivity** of a predicate is the fraction of rows it retains. `WHERE status = 'active'` on a table where 1% of rows are active has selectivity 0.01. `WHERE status = 'active'` on a table where 90% are active has selectivity 0.9.

When selectivity is below a threshold (often 5–20%, engine-specific), an index scan wins: few rows are read from the heap. When selectivity is high, a full sequential scan beats the index — because following the index to the heap costs one random I/O per row, whereas a sequential scan reads many rows per I/O.

#### The Selectivity Threshold Formula

The crossover point can be derived from first principles. Let:

* `random_page_cost` = the cost of one random I/O (relative to sequential)
* `rows_per_page` = average number of heap rows per 8 KB page

An **index scan** for a query returning `k` rows costs approximately `k × random_page_cost` (one random heap fetch per matching row, unclustered).

A **sequential scan** for a table of `N` rows costs approximately `N / rows_per_page` (sequential reads of the entire heap, regardless of how many match).

The index wins when:

k×random_page_cost<Nrows_per_pagek $$\times \text{random\\_page\\_cost} < \frac{N}{\text{rows\\_per\\_page}}k$$×random_page_cost<rows_per_pageN

Dividing both sides by `N` and noting that selectivity `sel = k/N`:

sel<1random_page_cost×rows_per_page⇔threshold≈1random_page_cost$$\text{sel} < \frac{1}{\text{random\\_page\\_cost} \times \text{rows\\_per\\_page}} \quad\Leftrightarrow\quad \text{threshold} \approx \frac{1}{\text{random\\_page\\_cost} \times \text{rows\\_per\\_page}}sel<random_page_cost$$1⇔threshold≈random_page_cost×rows_per_page1

**Concrete example.** PostgreSQL's default `random_page_cost = 4` (tuned for rotational disks). For an 8 KB page with 100-byte rows, `rows_per_page ≈ 80`. Then:

$$\text{threshold} \approx \frac{1}{4 \times 80} = 0.3\%threshold$$

A query matching more than ~0.3% of rows might use a sequential scan. If you lower `random_page_cost` to 1.1 (for NVMe SSD — much cheaper random I/O), the threshold rises to ~1.1%, so the optimizer uses indexes more aggressively. This is why **tuning`random_page_cost` to match your storage** is important: a database on SSD with `random_page_cost = 4` (HDD default) will under-use indexes.

Thread A example: `WHERE year = 4` matches ~25% of Students (far above any reasonable threshold). Optimizer picks sequential scan. `WHERE year = 4 AND name = 'Alice'` matches 1 row (well below threshold); optimizer picks the name index.

Chapter 12 formalizes how the optimizer estimates selectivity (statistics, histograms) and chooses.

### 10.11 Thread B: The 20-Minute Query Meets Indexes

Thread B's stubborn query:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c     ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

Cost without indexes (cost model from Chapter 11, preview):

* Scan Students: 125 pages.
* For each senior Alice-like student (~25,000 of them), scan Enrollments: 8,000 pages.
* For each matched enrollment, scan Courses: 13 pages; scan Instructors: 5 pages.

Order-of-magnitude: 25,000 × 8,000 × 13 × 5 probes is _astronomical_ under a naive nested loop. Even with a less-naive plan, we are deep in minutes. Hence the 20-minute observation.

With the right indexes:
    
    
    CREATE INDEX ON Students(year);
    CREATE INDEX ON Enrollments(student_id);
    CREATE INDEX ON Courses(instructor_id); -- for the last join
    -- Primary keys on Courses(id), Instructors(id) already indexed.
    

Suddenly:

* Students scan on year: use index, ~200 page reads.
* Each senior → Enrollments index on student_id: ~3 reads + a few leaf entries.
* Each enrollment → Courses by id: ~3 reads, but upper tree cached, so ~1 effective.
* Each course → Instructors by id: ~1 effective.

Total: low thousands of page reads. Under 2 seconds.

This is the 20-minutes-to-2-seconds transformation. Not through clever code. Through indexes. Chapters 11 and 12 explain _which order_ the engine should join in and _how the optimizer decides_. Those optimizations matter — but they are roughly order-of-magnitude improvements; indexes are two or three orders of magnitude.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **B+ trees trade slightly slower equality lookups for everything else.** A hash index resolves equality in 1–2 page reads versus a B+ tree's 3–4, but the tree also serves range, prefix, ORDER BY, and leftmost-prefix composite scans — capabilities the hash structurally cannot offer.
* **Clustered indexes trade expensive PK updates for cheap PK range scans.** Putting the rows at the leaves of the primary B+ tree turns range and PK lookups into one traversal, but moving a row's primary key forces a physical relocation and updates every secondary index that points at it.
* **Composite indexes trade column flexibility for shared-tree economy.** One `(a, b, c)` index serves any leftmost-prefix query in a single structure, but predicates that filter only on `b` or `c` cannot use it — either accept a scan or build another index.
* **Covering (INCLUDE) indexes trade write bandwidth and disk space for zero heap I/O on hot reads.** Adding columns to leaves lets narrow reporting queries avoid the heap entirely, but every UPDATE on an included column rewrites the index leaf and inflates the tree.
* **Secondary indexes trade write amplification for read selectivity.** Each additional secondary index turns one heap write into N+1 structure writes; under TPC-C-style OLTP this can drop throughput 10–20% per index, which is why "index every column" backfires.
* **Partial indexes trade generality for size and write speed.** Indexing only `WHERE status = 'active'` shrinks the tree dramatically and ignores irrelevant updates, but queries outside the predicate get no help and must fall back to a scan.
* **Hash indexes trade ordering and range queries for one less tree level.** O(1) equality is real, but you lose ORDER BY, BETWEEN, prefix matches, and leftmost-prefix composite use — almost always a bad bargain except for pure key-value lookups.

### 10.12 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State the two minimum conditions a structure must satisfy to be called an index.
  2. Explain the leftmost-prefix rule using an index on `(country, city, zip)` and three example queries, one for each: full prefix, partial prefix, non-prefix filter.
  3. What is a covering index? Why does it avoid heap I/O?
  4. Contrast a clustered index with a heap-plus-secondary layout on these three operations: primary-key range scan, secondary-index lookup, primary-key update.
  5. Why does a B+ tree keep all keys in the leaves rather than allowing them in internal nodes?
  6. Name three query patterns a hash index cannot serve.
  7. What is the difference between a partial index and a covering index? Can a partial index also be covering?

#### Application Problems

  8. A B+ tree with fan-out 250 stores 40 million entries. What is the tree height? Approximately how many page reads are needed for a point lookup, assuming the root and first level are in buffer pool?

Hint

Use log⁡dN\log_d NlogdN to find the height. Then count which levels you actually have to read from disk: the root and first internal level are cached, so only levels below them count toward I/O.

  9. A table of 2 million rows is queried with `WHERE status = 'active'`. Status has three values: 'active' (5%), 'pending' (3%), 'closed' (92%). Which of the following should the optimizer choose for each predicate, and why?

     * `WHERE status = 'active'`
     * `WHERE status = 'pending'`
     * `WHERE status = 'closed'`
     * `WHERE status IN ('active', 'pending')`
  10. You have a 500-million-row `events` table. You want to answer two queries fast: "events for user X" and "events in a time range." Propose one or two indexes. Can one composite index serve both? What is the tradeoff?

Hint

Apply the leftmost-prefix rule: which column would `(user_id, ts)` serve, and which would it not? The "one or two indexes" choice is really a question about whether the time-range query alone is frequent enough to justify a second tree's write cost.

  11. A colleague creates the following indexes on `Orders(customer_id, order_date, status, total)`:

    
    
    CREATE INDEX i1 ON Orders(customer_id);
    CREATE INDEX i2 ON Orders(customer_id, order_date);
    CREATE INDEX i3 ON Orders(customer_id, order_date, status);
    

Identify which indexes are redundant and why. What does "redundant" cost?

Hint

A wider composite already serves every leftmost prefix of itself. Ask: does `i3` answer everything `i1` and `i2` could answer? Then count what gets paid on every INSERT and UPDATE for redundant trees.
