  12. An InnoDB table has primary key `(order_id)` (clustered) and secondary index on `customer_id`. A query is `SELECT * FROM Orders WHERE customer_id = 99 ORDER BY order_id LIMIT 10`. Trace the access: which indexes are used, how many tree descents, roughly how many page reads, and in what order rows are returned.

Hint

Recall that InnoDB secondary indexes store the primary key, not a RID. So a "secondary then heap" lookup is really "secondary then _primary tree_." For the ORDER BY: are the secondary-index entries already in `order_id` order, or do you need a sort?

  13. A `Students(name)` index on a table of 1 million rows. `SELECT * FROM Students WHERE LOWER(name) = 'alice'`. Does the index help? If not, what change would make an index usable?

  14. You inherit a table with 9 indexes. Writes are slow; reads seem fine. Describe a process for safely reducing index count without breaking query performance. (Consider: pg_stat_user_indexes, slow query log, testing in a replica.)

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why do most production DBMSs default to B+ trees rather than plain B-trees, given that a B-tree's in-node search could in principle finish a lookup one level earlier?

  16. Construct a scenario where an index _makes a query slower_. Be specific about selectivity, cluster factor, and page counts. This is not hypothetical — bad plans like this happen; Chapter 12 shows how the optimizer sometimes gets it wrong.

Hint

Compare per-row I/O cost: a sequential scan reads many rows per page, while an index scan that returns most of the table pays one heap fetch per matched row. At what selectivity does the index plan's heap-fetch total exceed the sequential scan's page count?

  17. For a workload where inserts and reads are 50/50 on a single-column integer key, would you prefer a clustered index on that key or a heap with a secondary index? Consider insert hotspots (monotonically increasing keys) and lookup locality.

  18. A query `SELECT MAX(salary) FROM Employees` has no WHERE clause. Can an index help? If so, which one and how does the engine execute the query in O(log⁡N)O(\log N)O(logN) instead of O(N)?

  19. Hash indexes give O(1) equality lookups. If RAM is cheap and the whole database fits in memory, why do in-memory databases like Redis use hash tables for most keys but systems like MySQL HeatWave and SAP HANA still use B+ trees internally? Think about what the tree offers beyond point lookup.

  20. Thread A with all the recommended indexes answers "Alice's courses" in 32 page reads. Suppose we also add `CREATE INDEX ON Enrollments(student_id, course_id, term) INCLUDE (grade)`. How many page reads now? What is the new cost of inserting an enrollment row?

Hint

For the read: which heap fetches does the new covering index let you skip? Trace which steps in §10.9 the index now answers without going to the heap. For the write: each additional index that needs maintenance adds one more tree's update path per insert.

### Chapter Summary

An index is a data structure that maps column values to row locations, bought in exchange for storage and write overhead. The **B+ tree** is the workhorse: balanced, high-fan-out, supporting equality, range, prefix, and ordered scans. The **hash index** is a specialist: O(1) equality, nothing else.

**Composite indexes** obey the leftmost-prefix rule: filters must hit leading columns. **Covering indexes** let the engine answer queries without returning to the heap. **Partial indexes** trade generality for size. **Bitmap** and **full-text** indexes exist for low-cardinality and textual data respectively.

Thread A's "find Alice's courses" goes from 8,200 page reads to 32 with two indexes — a 250× improvement. Thread B's 20-minute join collapses to under 2 seconds once `Enrollments(student_id)` and `Courses(instructor_id)` exist. Indexes are the performance engineer's biggest lever, but indiscriminate indexing poisons write paths. The rule of thumb is: one index per well-understood query pattern, reviewed as the workload evolves.

Chapter 11 opens the next layer: given the indexes, how does the engine actually _execute_ a multi-table join? What operators make up a query plan, and how do they stream data? That is where the 2-second version of Thread B comes into focus.

---

## Chapter 11: Query Processing — Operators and the Volcano Model

The SQL parser gives the engine an abstract syntax tree. The algebraic rewriter, Chapter 7, turns it into a tree of relational operators. The optimizer, Chapter 12, picks the physical implementation of each operator. This chapter is about what actually runs.

A database query is not a program in the usual sense. It is a **dataflow** : rows flow through a pipeline of operators, each producing rows for the next. The operators are small, composable, and implemented independently: a scan, a filter, a sort, a join. A plan is a tree of them. The tree is evaluated — typically — by a uniform iterator interface that lets operators be mixed freely without knowing each other.

We will see:

* The **iterator (Volcano) model** that turned query processing into a uniform abstraction.
* The major physical operators: scans, filters, sorts, hashes.
* The three classical **join algorithms** — nested loop, sort-merge, hash join — with their I/O costs.
* How the engine handles GROUP BY, ORDER BY, DISTINCT, aggregate, and set operations physically.
* An alternative execution model — **vectorized processing** — that dominates modern analytical engines like DuckDB and ClickHouse.

We continue tracing Thread B's 20-minute query, this time counting I/Os operator-by-operator.

### 11.1 The Iterator Model

In 1994, Goetz Graefe described a uniform interface that every operator implements:
    
    
    operator.open()      // prepare, open children
    operator.next()      // produce the next tuple (or EOF)
    operator.close()     // release resources
    

The engine pulls tuples from the root operator, which pulls from its children, which pull from theirs. Execution proceeds one tuple at a time, top-down-requested, bottom-up-delivered.

#### Definition: Iterator (Volcano) model

A query execution model in which every operator exposes `open`, `next`, and `close` calls. The root operator is called repeatedly; each `next()` returns one tuple or EOF by pulling tuples from child operators through the same interface. Operators are composable because they only depend on the iterator contract, not on each other's internals.

#### A tiny trace

For `SELECT name FROM Students WHERE year = 4`:
    
    
    Project(name)
       └── Filter(year = 4)
             └── SeqScan(Students)
    

Execution:
    
    
    Project.open → Filter.open → SeqScan.open
    Project.next → Filter.next → SeqScan.next → (id=1, year=2, ...)
                    Filter rejects (year ≠ 4), Filter.next calls SeqScan.next again
                  → (id=2, year=4, ...)
                    Filter accepts, returns to Project
                  Project projects {name}, returns to caller
    ... repeat until SeqScan.next returns EOF ...
    Project.close → Filter.close → SeqScan.close
    

#### Why this model won

* **Compositional** : any operator works with any child. Mix scan with filter with join with aggregate in any tree.
* **Memory-frugal** : the pipeline processes one tuple at a time. No intermediate results materialized.
* **Backpressure built in** : if the consumer is slow, the producer blocks on `next` and does no work.

#### The cost

* **Function-call overhead** : a tuple passing through 6 operators makes 6 function calls. At billions of tuples, this dominates runtime.
* **Polymorphic dispatch** : each `next` is often a virtual call, disabling the branch predictor.
* **Poor cache behavior** : tuples are processed one at a time; CPU L1/L2 caches go cold between rows.

The Volcano model is the right abstraction for OLTP (many small queries, low-latency). For OLAP (few huge queries, throughput), the **vectorized** and **push-based** models (Section 11.10) outperform it by an order of magnitude. Both models coexist in modern engines.

### 11.2 Scans — Sequential, Index, Index-Only, Bitmap

The leaves of every plan read rows from somewhere.

#### Sequential scan

Read every page in order. Throughput king, cost model is simple:

$\text{cost} = N_{\text{pages}} \cdot C_{\text{IO, seq}}$

Sequential I/O is 5–30× faster than random I/O on HDD (the arm does not move) and perhaps 2–4× faster on SSD (prefetching, merged requests). The optimizer treats sequential reads as "free" at small page counts and as the default when indexes cannot help.

#### Index scan

Descend a B+ tree to the first match; walk leaves to the last match; for each leaf entry, fetch the heap row. Cost:

$\text{cost} = H_{\text{tree}} + L_{\text{matched leaves}} + R_{\text{matched rows}}$

where HtreeH_{$\text{tree}}Htree is tree height (3$–4 pages for realistic tables), LLL is leaf pages traversed, and RRR is row fetches. When selectivity is high, RRR dominates and we may overtake the cost of a full scan.

#### Index-only scan

Same as index scan but no heap fetch — the index covers the query. Cost:

$\text{cost} = H_{\text{tree}} + L_{\text{matched leaves}}$

No random I/O per row. Extremely efficient for narrow queries (Chapter 10, Section 10.6).

#### Bitmap (heap) scan

Used when the index returns many matches and the heap rows are scattered. Phase 1: descend the index, build a **bitmap** of RIDs (one bit per heap page, set if the page contains a matching row). Phase 2: read heap pages in ascending page order, filtering rows.

Why bitmap? The heap pages are read **in physical order** , turning random I/O into near-sequential I/O. This is a substantial saving on spinning disks and a meaningful one on SSDs. PostgreSQL's `Bitmap Heap Scan` is the canonical implementation; MySQL and SQL Server have analogs. Bitmap scans bridge the gap between "index useful" and "sequential scan better."

### 11.3 Filters and Projections

#### Filter (σ)

Apply a predicate to each incoming tuple; pass through if true.
    
    
    def next():
      while True:
        t = child.next()
        if t is EOF: return EOF
        if predicate(t): return t
    

Cost is CPU-only — no I/O. The interesting decision is _where in the plan the filter lives_ (Chapter 7 pushed it down; Chapter 12 formalizes).

#### Projection (π)

Strip unwanted columns. Zero cost at runtime in the iterator model — the engine just selects the columns on the way out. For DISTINCT projection, see Section 11.5.

### 11.4 Sort — External Merge Sort

Many operators need sorted input: sort-merge join, GROUP BY (one implementation), ORDER BY, certain distincts, merge-based window frames. In-memory sort is classical O(Nlog⁡N)O(N \log N)O(NlogN); the interesting problem is sorting more data than fits in memory.

#### External merge sort

Phase 1 — **run generation**. Read the input in buffer-pool-sized chunks. Sort each in memory (quicksort / heapsort). Write sorted **runs** to disk. For input of NNN pages and buffer BBB, we produce $\lceil N/B \rceil$ runs.

Phase 2 — **merging**. Open (B−1)(B - 1)(B−1) runs. Read one page from each. Repeatedly pick the smallest front value and write it to output. When a run's buffer empties, read its next page. When an output page fills, flush and refill.

If N/B≤B−1N/B $\le B - 1N/B$≤B−1, one merge pass suffices — **two-pass sort**. Otherwise, intermediate merges combine runs until we can do the final merge. Each pass reads and writes NNN pages.

#### Cost

External sort does one _run-generation_ pass (reads + writes the whole input once), then one or more _merge_ passes (each reads + writes the whole input once). Total I/O is:

$\text{cost} = 2N \cdot \big(1 + \lceil \log_{B-1}(N/B) \rceil\big) \text{ pages}$

The `1 +` accounts for run generation, which the older formula `2N · ⌈log_{B-1}(N/B)⌉` counted only the merge passes. The `⌈log_{B-1}(N/B)⌉` term is the number of merge passes: each merge pass reduces the number of runs by a factor of `B − 1` (one buffer is reserved for output), and we stop once all runs fit in a single merge.

**Worked example** : N=106N = 10^6N=106 pages, B=1000B = 1000B=1000. Then N/B=1000N/B = 1000N/B=1000 runs. One merge pass combines up to `B − 1 = 999` runs, so `⌈log_{999}(1000)⌉ = 1`. Total passes = `1 + 1 = 2`. I/O = 2⋅106⋅2=4×1062 $\cdot 10^6 \cdot 2 = 4 \times 10^62$⋅106⋅2=4×106 pages. (When people say "two-pass external sort," they mean exactly this: one run generation + one merge.)

If `N/B > (B − 1)`, multiple merge passes are needed. For buffer `B = 100`, N=108N = 10^8N=108: `N/B = 10^6`, `log_{99}(10^6) ≈ 3`, so 4 total passes = `8 × 10^8` I/O. This is why large `B` (more memory) is the single best lever for sort performance — doubling `B` roughly halves the merge-pass count for huge inputs.

External sort is the workhorse behind any operator that needs order. Every optimizer estimates its cost with something close to the formula above.

#### Replacement selection

A refinement that produces longer initial runs — roughly 2B2B2B pages per run instead of BBB. Halves the pass count in the common case. Implemented as a heap with active/frozen partitions.

### 11.5 GROUP BY and Aggregates

Two strategies.

#### Sort-based aggregation

Sort by the group key. Scan; emit an aggregate row each time the group key changes. Cost: sort cost + one sequential pass.

#### Hash-based aggregation

Build a hash table keyed by the group key; each slot holds running aggregate state. Scan the input; for each tuple, update the slot. Output at end.

Hash aggregation is typically faster when the group count fits in memory. Sort aggregation is necessary when the group count is huge or when downstream operators need sorted output anyway. PostgreSQL picks between them based on estimated group count versus `work_mem`.

#### Definition: Spilling

A hash aggregate that outgrows `work_mem` must **spill** to disk: evict a batch to a temp file, keep going, merge the spills at the end. Spilling is the single most common reason a surprising query suddenly gets slow — hash memory filled, and the engine started writing to disk.

#### DISTINCT

DISTINCT is a special GROUP BY: group by all projected columns, emit one row per group, no aggregate. Same mechanics.

### 11.6 Joins — The Heart of the Engine

Joins are where the expensive CPU and I/O live. Three classical algorithms.

#### Nested-loop join (NLJ)

The simplest. For every row of outer relation RRR, scan inner relation SSS for matches.
    
    
    for r in R:
      for s in S:
        if r.key == s.key: emit (r, s)
    

Cost (page-oriented):

$\text{cost}_{\text{NLJ}} = \text{pages}(R) + \text{pages}(R) \cdot \text{pages}(S)$

For Thread B, pages(Students)=125, pages(Enrollments)=8,000. A plain NLJ of (Students × Enrollments) is 125+125⋅8000=1,000,125125 + 125 $\cdot 8000 = 1{,}000{,}125125+125$⋅8000=1,000,125 page reads. Ouch.

**Block nested-loop** : read B−2B-2B−2 pages of RRR at a time; for each block, scan SSS once. Cost ≈pages(R)+(pages(R)/(B−2))⋅pages(S)$\approx \text{pages}(R) + (\text{pages}(R)/(B-2)) \cdot \text{pages}(S)$≈pages(R)+(pages(R)/(B−2))⋅pages(S). With B−2=100B-2 = 100B−2=100, Thread B drops to 125+(125/100)⋅8000≈10,125125 + (125/100) $\cdot 8000 \approx 10{,}125125+(125/100)$⋅8000≈10,125 reads — manageable but still bad.

**Index nested-loop** : if SSS has an index on the join key, for each outer row perform an index probe. Cost ≈pages(R)+∣R∣⋅index-probe-cost\approx $\text{pages}(R) +$ |R| \cdot $\text{index-probe-cost}$≈pages(R)+∣R∣⋅index-probe-cost. For Thread B with an index on Enrollments(student_id), each of Alice's seniors costs ~4 reads: 125+25000⋅4=100,125125 + 25000 \cdot 4 = 100{,}125125+25000⋅4=100,125 page reads. Still not great — we need a more aggressive plan.

#### Tradeoff

NLJ wins when one side is very small (hundreds of rows) and can fit in memory. It also wins when the index on the inner side makes per-probe cost tiny. For two big tables without a helpful index, NLJ is the wrong algorithm.

#### Sort-merge join (SMJ)

Sort both sides on the join key; merge.
    
    
    sort R by key; sort S by key.
    cursor_r, cursor_s = R.begin, S.begin
    while both not EOF:
      if cursor_r.key < cursor_s.key: advance R
      elif cursor_r.key > cursor_s.key: advance S
      else: emit all pairs where keys equal, then advance.
    

Cost:

$\text{cost}_{\text{SMJ}} = \text{sort}(R) + \text{sort}(S) + \text{pages}(R) + \text{pages}(S)$

For Thread B with external sort (2-pass each): 2⋅125⋅2+2⋅8000⋅2+125+8000≈40,6252 $\cdot 125 \cdot 2 + 2 \cdot 8000 \cdot 2 + 125 + 8000 \approx 40{,}6252$⋅125⋅2+2⋅8000⋅2+125+8000≈40,625 reads. Worse than indexed NLJ in this toy example — but when both sides are big and no helpful index exists, SMJ beats NLJ handily.

Best use case: both inputs are already sorted on the join key (e.g., inputs come from sorted indexes).

#### Hash join (HJ)

Build a hash table on the smaller side (the **build input**); probe with the larger side (the **probe input**).
    
    
    for r in R: hash_table[r.key].append(r)
    for s in S:
      for r in hash_table[s.key]:
        emit (r, s)
    

Cost, when build fits in memory:

$\text{cost}_{\text{HJ}} = \text{pages}(R) + \text{pages}(S)$

Two sequential scans. Extremely fast. For Thread B: 125 + 8000 = 8125 reads.

When build does not fit, use **Grace hash join** :

  1. Hash-partition both inputs into B−1B-1B−1 partitions on disk by the join key.
  2. For each partition pair, run in-memory hash join.

Cost: ~3× the input size (one write + one read for partitioning, plus final scan). Still linear — the big win over NLJ for large joins.

#### Comparing the three

Scenario | Best algorithm  
---|---  
One side very small (fits in memory) | Block NLJ  
Inner side has index on join key | Index NLJ  
Both sides roughly equal, no index | Hash join  
Both sides sorted by join key already | Sort-merge join  
Range-join (`R.x < S.y`) | Sort-merge (or NLJ)  
Outer joins with large NULL-padded side | Hash (with left hash)  
  
Why not always hash? Hash joins cannot handle range predicates (`R.x < S.y`) — hashes only collide on equality. SMJ handles ranges because the sorted order supports comparisons directly. NLJ handles anything but slowly.

#### Common Misconception

"Hash join is always the fastest." Hash join is the fastest for equi-joins when neither side has a useful index. For small-small joins, NLJ avoids setup overhead. For range joins, it simply doesn't apply. For already-sorted inputs, SMJ costs less and preserves order for a downstream operator. The optimizer chooses per-query based on cost estimates; the engine must know all three.

### 11.7 Pipelining and Blocking Operators

Every operator is either **pipelining** or **blocking**.

#### Definition: Pipelining operator

An operator that can produce output tuples as it consumes input tuples, without waiting for the entire input. Examples: Filter, Project, Index Scan, NL join (per outer tuple), HJ probe phase.

#### Definition: Blocking operator

An operator that must consume _all_ input before producing any output. Examples: Sort, Hash Aggregate, Hash Join build phase.

A plan is segmented into **pipeline stages** at each blocking operator. Between blocking operators, tuples stream; across them, the data is materialized (in memory or on disk).

Pipelining is why the iterator model is memory-frugal. Blocking is where all the memory goes. Query tuning often comes down to: eliminate an unnecessary blocking stage, or shrink its footprint.

#### Limit and early termination

`LIMIT 10` tells the root operator to stop after 10 tuples. In a pipelined plan, this cuts the work: the Filter stops asking the Scan for more. In a blocking plan — e.g., ORDER BY followed by LIMIT — the sort must still see _every_ row to find the top 10. Unless... the optimizer converts it to a **top-k selection** : keep a heap of size 10. Every modern engine does.

### 11.8 Tracing Thread B

Thread B again:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c     ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

Imagine no indexes, plan chosen greedily:
    
    
    HashJoin i.id = c.instructor_id
    ├── SeqScan Instructors (5 pages)
    └── HashJoin c.id = e.course_id
        ├── SeqScan Courses (13 pages)
        └── HashJoin s.id = e.student_id
            ├── Filter year = 4
            │   └── SeqScan Students (125 pages)
            └── SeqScan Enrollments (8000 pages)
    

All three joins are hash joins on the filter-reduced or small sides. Cost: 125 + 8000 + 13 + 5 = **8,143 page reads** plus CPU for hashing. On SSD that is maybe 500 ms. Already dramatically better than the pathological NLJ.

Now add indexes:
    
    
    NestedLoop
    ├── NestedLoop
    │   ├── NestedLoop
    │   │   ├── IndexScan on Students(year=4) → seniors
    │   │   └── IndexScan on Enrollments(student_id=s.id)
    │   └── IndexScan on Courses(id=e.course_id)
    └── IndexScan on Instructors(id=c.instructor_id)
    

For a few thousand seniors, each probe ~3 reads, cached after the first: perhaps 2,000 net page reads. ~100 ms on SSD. **The 20-minute query now runs in 2 seconds.**

The 20-minute version is what happens when the engine picks NLJ on everything _without_ indexes. It is the natural wrong answer for a planner with no statistics and no indexes. Chapter 12 explains how the optimizer arrives at the right answer — when it has the right information.

### 11.9 Set Operators

UNION, INTERSECT, EXCEPT.

* **UNION ALL** : concatenate streams. Pipelining.
* **UNION** (distinct): UNION ALL followed by hash aggregate or sort + unique. Blocking.
* **INTERSECT** : either hash (build on smaller side, probe with larger, emit rows whose key appears) or sort-merge (align sorted streams).
* **EXCEPT** : similar, emit rows from left not present in right.

All reducible to scan + hash/sort primitives. No new machinery.

### 11.10 Vectorized and Push-Based Execution

The iterator model's per-tuple function-call cost makes it slow for analytic workloads. Two reforms:

#### Vectorized execution

Instead of `next()` returning one tuple, return a **batch** of 1024 tuples at a time. Operators work on columnar arrays: apply a predicate to a vector of 1024 values in a tight loop, no function-call overhead per row. Modern CPUs can SIMD-vectorize the inner loop, processing 4, 8, or 16 values per instruction.

Engines: DuckDB, Apache Arrow/DataFusion, ClickHouse, Snowflake, SQL Server columnstore. Speedups of 5–20× over row-iterator are common for scan-heavy aggregations.

#### Push-based execution

Instead of the consumer pulling tuples, the producer pushes them. Each operator registers a callback on its parent; when the scan has a batch, it pushes it through the pipeline. Ideal for JIT compilation — a pipeline compiles to one tight loop with no function-call boundaries. Engines: HyPer, Umbra, PostgreSQL's JIT mode for expressions.

#### The tradeoff

Vectorization and push-based execution crush the Volcano model on analytical workloads (big scans, few rows returned after aggregation). They lose a little on OLTP (small queries, point lookups — the overhead of batching is wasted). Many modern engines run both — DuckDB on scan pipelines, PostgreSQL in tuple mode for OLTP. Expect to see both terms in real systems.

#### Common Misconception

"Vectorized means 'uses SIMD instructions.'" SIMD helps but is not the essence. The essence is **processing batches of values with uniform types** instead of polymorphic tuples. Even without SIMD, vectorization wins because tight loops amortize call overhead and keep data in L1/L2 cache.

### 11.11 Parallel Execution

The modern machine has tens or hundreds of cores. A single query can run on many cores in parallel.

* **Intra-operator parallelism** : split a scan or hash join across threads. Each thread handles a range of pages or a partition of the hash.
* **Inter-operator parallelism** : two independent subtrees of the plan run simultaneously, one per thread.
* **Partitioned parallelism** : for joins, hash-partition both inputs by the join key; N threads do N independent joins on disjoint partitions. Requires a repartition (shuffle) step, which is expensive but enables linear-ish speedup.

Parallelism is the last place performance comes from after indexes and good plans. It rarely helps a single small OLTP query (the setup cost is more than the work). It is transformative for analytical queries over tens of gigabytes.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Nested-loop join trades algorithmic generality for cost sensitivity to outer size.** NLJ handles any join predicate (including ranges and inequalities) and is unbeatable when one side is tiny or the inner has a useful index, but pages(R) × pages(S) explodes when both sides are large and unindexed.
* **Hash join trades equi-join restriction for linear scan cost.** When the build side fits in memory, HJ runs in pages(R) + pages(S) and dominates large equi-joins, but it cannot serve `R.x < S.y` predicates because hashes only collide on equality.
* **Sort-merge join trades upfront sort cost for ordered output and range support.** SMJ handles range-join predicates and preserves order for downstream operators, but the external sort it requires is wasted work if a hash join could have answered the same query.
* **The iterator (Volcano) model trades per-tuple call overhead for memory frugality and composability.** One-tuple-at-a-time pipelining keeps memory use minimal and lets any operator compose with any other, but billions of virtual `next()` calls dominate runtime on analytical queries — which is why vectorized engines win on OLAP.
* **Vectorized execution trades batch latency for throughput.** Processing 1024 tuples at a time amortizes call overhead and keeps L1/L2 cache hot, but the batching overhead is wasted on small OLTP queries that return only a handful of rows.
* **Pipelining trades memory savings for the absence of intermediate materialization.** A pipelined plan streams through memory with no temp tables, but a blocking operator (sort, hash build, hash aggregate) breaks the pipeline and may spill — turning a fast query slow the moment `work_mem` is exceeded.
* **More merge passes in external sort trade I/O for fitting within`work_mem`.** Each extra pass over N pages adds 2N I/Os; one big buffer pool lets the sort finish in two passes, while a small buffer forces many passes and quadratic-feeling slowdowns.

### 11.12 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Describe the iterator model in one paragraph. What does a blocking operator break in the model's memory story?
  2. State the cost formula of external merge sort. When does it complete in two passes?
  3. Compare sort-merge join and hash join in one sentence each. Name one situation where SMJ is preferred.
  4. Define pipelining and blocking operators. Give three examples of each.
  5. Why does a bitmap heap scan sometimes beat both a plain index scan and a sequential scan? What is the key property it exploits?
  6. Contrast the iterator model with the vectorized model. Why is the vectorized model faster for analytical queries?
  7. What is a "spill" in hash aggregation, and why does it dramatically change a query's runtime?

#### Application Problems

  8. A table has 2 million rows / 20,000 pages. The buffer pool holds 500 pages. Compute the cost (page reads + writes) of sorting the table via external merge sort, in two passes if possible.

Hint

First check whether two passes suffice: with B = 500, the merge phase can combine B − 1 runs in one pass, so two passes cover up to (B − 1) × B pages. Each pass reads and writes N pages; total cost is 2 × (passes) × N.

  9. Two tables: R with 5,000 pages, S with 50,000 pages. Buffer pool holds 1,000 pages. No indexes on the join key. Estimate the cost of (a) block nested-loop join, (b) sort-merge join, (c) hash join.

Hint

For BNLJ, use pages(R) + ⌈pages(R)/(B−2)⌉ × pages(S) — note B−2 is the block size, not B. For SMJ, sort each side with the formula from §11.4 then add a sequential merge pass. For HJ, ask first whether the _smaller_ side fits in memory; if not, use the Grace-hash 3× rule.

  10. For the same R and S, suppose S has a B+ tree index on its join key. Each index probe costs 3 pages. Estimate the cost of index nested-loop join with R as the outer.

Hint

Index NLJ cost is pages(R) + |R| × probe-cost, where |R| is the number of _rows_ in R, not pages. Without a row count, use a reasonable rows-per-page estimate to convert. Compare your answer to (a)–(c) of the previous problem to see when the index actually helps.

  11. A query scans 1 billion rows and returns the single top-10 by `created_at`. Describe the execution plan in pipelined form versus the naive "ORDER BY, then LIMIT" plan. What memory is required?

  12. `SELECT department, COUNT(*) FROM employees GROUP BY department` with 1,000,000 employees in 50,000 pages and 500 departments. Should the engine use sort-based or hash-based aggregation, and why?

Hint

Compare working-set size for each strategy. Hash aggregation needs one slot per distinct group — does 500 entries fit in `work_mem`? Sort aggregation pays the full external-sort cost on 50,000 pages regardless of group count. Which constant dominates here?

  13. A plan has the following shape:
         
         HashAggregate(...)
           └── HashJoin (...)
                 ├── SeqScan A
                 └── SeqScan B
         

Describe the pipeline stages and identify where blocking happens. If `work_mem` is too small, which stage is likely to spill?

  14. Thread B's 20-minute query becomes a 2-second query after indexes. Write out the plan trees in both cases (no index and with index). Identify which operator contributes the largest cost difference.

#### Think Deeper

  15. The iterator model has per-tuple function-call overhead. Design an alternative where the overhead is amortized — sketch a batch-at-a-time variant. What changes about the operator interface? What changes about how plans are compiled?

  16. A "late-materialization" optimization in column stores delays reading non-filter columns until after filtering. Why does this help? Describe a query where the savings are large.

  17. When a hash join's build side does not fit in memory, the engine uses Grace hash join. Explain why the partitioning step is cheaper than just paging the build table (why is writing partitions better than random writes)?

  18. For a two-way join R ⋈ S and a three-way join R ⋈ S ⋈ T, describe why the optimizer must consider join _order_ as well as join algorithm. Which of the three classical joins is order-sensitive in ways the others are not?

Hint

Estimate the intermediate result size of (R ⋈ S) versus (S ⋈ T) — they can differ by orders of magnitude depending on selectivities. For order-sensitivity, ask: which algorithm asymmetrically treats one input as "outer" and another as "inner," and how much does swapping them cost?

  19. Modern hardware has 64–256 cores. Why does intra-operator parallelism scale well on aggregates but poorly on a plain sequential scan of a small table? What structural feature of the scan limits parallelism?

  20. Consider a query that reads a 1 TB table and returns one row. A sequential scan on SSD at 3 GB/s would take ~350 seconds. An index descent takes 4 page reads. Why would the optimizer _ever_ pick the scan? Identify two conditions under which scan actually beats index.

Hint

Think about _what_ the index returns versus what the query actually needs. An index that does not cover the projected columns forces a heap fetch — what if the predicate is non-SARGable, or the column is not indexed at all? Also consider what happens to per-row cost when the index is wrong about selectivity.

### Chapter Summary

Query execution is a dataflow: rows stream through a pipeline of operators implemented behind the **iterator** interface (`open`, `next`, `close`). **Scans** feed the pipeline — sequential, index, index-only, bitmap, each with different cost structure. **Filter** , **projection** , and **aggregates** operate per-tuple or per-group, sometimes spilling when memory runs out.

**Joins** are the most expensive operators. **Nested-loop** wins when one side is small or one side has an index; **sort-merge** wins when inputs are already sorted or on range predicates; **hash** wins for large equi-joins with no helpful index. **External merge sort** underpins most blocking operators; its cost is O(Nlog⁡BN)O(N \log_B N)O(NlogBN) pages.

Pipelined stages stream through memory; **blocking operators** (sort, hash build, hash aggregate) materialize and may spill. The choice of operator and order within each stage is the optimizer's job — Chapter 12.

**Vectorized** and **push-based** execution outperform the tuple iterator on analytical workloads by amortizing call overhead and keeping data hot in CPU cache. Production engines often run both for different query shapes. Thread B's 20-minute query, with nested-loop joins against un-indexed tables, becomes a 2-second query with indexed nested-loop joins or even faster with a hash-join plan — the cost difference is plan-level, not merely operator-level.

---

## Chapter 12: Query Optimization

Chapter 11 ended with a riddle. The same query — Thread B's four-way join — can take 20 minutes or 2 seconds depending on the plan. The two plans differ only in which operator scans which side first, and whether to nested-loop or hash-join. Between them lies the **optimizer** : the component that takes a logical relational expression and decides, among thousands of equivalent physical plans, which one to actually run.

The optimizer is where the biggest wins and the most embarrassing losses of a database live. A missed statistic, a stale histogram, a misestimate by a factor of ten, and the system picks a plan that runs a hundred times slower than the right one. Understanding what the optimizer is doing — what information it uses, what choices it faces, where it can go wrong — is the single most valuable skill for a database engineer beyond writing correct SQL.

This chapter is the capstone of the physical-design arc. It weaves together Chapters 7 (algebra), 9 (storage), 10 (indexes), and 11 (operators) into one question: **given the logical expression, which physical plan is cheapest?** We will see how real systems answer it.

### 12.1 What the Optimizer Does

#### Definition: Query optimization

The process of transforming a logically-correct relational expression into an executable plan while considering alternative equivalent expressions, choosing physical operators for each logical operator, and estimating costs to select the cheapest plan.

Three distinct phases happen inside "optimization," often interleaved:

  1. **Rewriting** : apply algebraic equivalences (Chapter 7) — push down selections, push down projections, flatten subqueries, transform EXISTS to semi-join, inline CTEs, rewrite OUTER joins where safe.
  2. **Enumeration** : generate candidate plans — choose join orders, choose access paths (seq scan, index scan, bitmap), choose join algorithms (NL, SMJ, HJ).
  3. **Costing** : estimate the cost of each candidate and pick the minimum.

These are not three sequential stages. A modern optimizer intertwines rewriting and enumeration, using cost estimates to guide rewrite choices.

#### Common Misconception

"The optimizer finds the best plan." It finds a plan that is _probably_ good among plans it considered, based on _estimated_ costs. Both qualifiers matter. It may skip a faster plan because its search heuristic didn't generate it, and it may pick a slower plan because its cost estimates are wrong. Good DBAs know how to read the plan, spot mis-estimates, and nudge the optimizer (hints, statistics refresh, rewritten SQL).

### 12.2 The Cost Model

Every plan candidate has a cost, a scalar number representing expected runtime. The ingredients:

#### I/O cost

* Sequential page read: 1.0 (baseline unit).
* Random page read: 4.0–10.0 (engine-dependent tunable; PostgreSQL's `random_page_cost` defaults to 4.0 on HDD, tune down to 1.1–1.5 on SSD).
* Page write: similar to random read but modulated by WAL batching.

#### CPU cost

The following numbers **are illustrative PostgreSQL-style defaults** (`cpu_tuple_cost`, `cpu_operator_cost` in postgresql.conf); **every engine uses its own constants** , and within an engine they are _tunable_ to match the hardware:

* Tuple processing: 0.01 units per tuple.
* Predicate evaluation: 0.0025 per operator per tuple.
* Hash / sort: higher per-tuple constants.

> **These are PostgreSQL defaults, not universal facts.** MySQL, SQL Server, Oracle, and DuckDB all have their own cost models with incomparable unit systems. The _ratios_ between I/O and CPU costs are what matter pedagogically: I/O is 100× to 1000× more expensive than a single tuple operation, which is why plans that reduce I/O win even if they do more CPU work. When working with a real engine, read its documentation for the actual constants and tune them to your storage (SSD vs HDD) rather than treating any book's numbers as authoritative.

#### Memory cost

Not priced directly — instead, memory allocation (`work_mem`) determines whether a plan fits or spills. Spilling changes the plan's shape (hash aggregate → grace hash, memory sort → external sort), which the cost model handles via the operator's own formula.

#### Startup cost vs total cost

PostgreSQL tracks two numbers per operator: **startup cost** (cost to produce the first tuple) and **total cost** (cost to produce all tuples). For a plan ending in `LIMIT 10`, minimizing total cost is wrong; we want minimum startup-plus-proportional cost. Smart optimizers reason about startup separately.

The raw cost numbers are not in real seconds — they are a unitless quantity that roughly tracks time under default tuning. Calibrating the cost constants is its own art.

### 12.3 Statistics — The Fuel the Optimizer Runs On

Cost estimation needs **statistics** about the data. Without them, the optimizer guesses, and the guesses are often catastrophic.

#### What the optimizer tracks per table

* **Row count** : cardinality of the table.
* **Page count** : size on disk.
* **Per-column** : most common values (MCVs) and their frequencies, number of distinct values (NDV), null fraction, average column width, a histogram of the value distribution.
* **Correlation** : degree to which the logical ordering of the column matches physical ordering on disk (critical for index scan costs — a highly correlated column means sequential heap I/O, uncorrelated means random).

#### Histograms

A **histogram** divides the value range of a column into buckets with roughly equal row counts. For `Students.gpa`, a histogram might say: bucket [0.0–2.5] has 5% of rows; [2.5–3.0] has 20%; [3.0–3.5] has 40%; [3.5–4.0] has 35%.

For the predicate `gpa > 3.5`, the optimizer looks up the histogram: 35% of rows. With 1 million students, expected matches = 350,000. That estimate drives every downstream cost decision.

Two histogram types:

* **Equi-width** : fixed-width buckets, uneven counts. Simple; bad at skewed data.
* **Equi-depth (quantile-based)** : fixed-count buckets, variable widths. Better at skew; the default in PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQL Server.

#### Most common values (MCVs)

Histograms wash out frequent values. For a column like `country` where 40% of rows are "US" and the rest scatter across 200 countries, a bucket covering "US" misrepresents the rest. The MCV list explicitly records the top-K values and their frequencies; histograms cover the remaining long tail.

#### When statistics go stale

Statistics are computed by sampling — PostgreSQL's `ANALYZE`, MySQL's `ANALYZE TABLE`, SQL Server's `UPDATE STATISTICS`. Periodically rerun after major data changes. Stale statistics are the single most common reason a previously-fast query suddenly gets slow: the plan was right for last month's data distribution; today's distribution shifted and the plan did not.

#### Tradeoff: statistics overhead

More buckets, more MCVs, larger sample = better estimates, costlier to collect and store. Default sample sizes (1% for small tables up to a cap around 30,000 rows) are a compromise. Engines let you increase statistics quality for specific columns that matter (`ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... SET STATISTICS 1000`).

#### Common Misconception

"More statistics is always better." Collecting and maintaining very fine-grained statistics is expensive, and diminishing returns set in quickly. A single bad assumption in the optimizer's reasoning often dwarfs the difference between a 100-bucket and 1000-bucket histogram.

### 12.4 Selectivity Estimation

Given statistics, the optimizer estimates the fraction of rows surviving each predicate — the **selectivity**. This number propagates through the plan and drives every cost decision.

#### Single predicate

* `col = const`: selectivity ≈ 1 / NDV(col), or consult MCV list if const is in MCV.
* `col < const`: consult histogram.
* `col BETWEEN a AND b`: subtract histogram fractions.
* `col IS NULL`: null_frac.

#### Conjunction

$\text{sel}(A \land B) \approx \text{sel}(A) \cdot \text{sel}(B) \quad \text{(independence assumption)}$

This is the **independence assumption** : the optimizer assumes predicates are uncorrelated unless told otherwise. It is wrong surprisingly often, sometimes very wrong.

Example: `WHERE city = 'San Francisco' AND state = 'CA'`. Independence says sel(city) × sel(state) ≈ 0.001 × 0.02 = 0.00002. Reality: every San Francisco row is in California, so sel = sel(city) = 0.001. The optimizer underestimates the result size by 20×.

To fix this, modern engines support **extended statistics** : tell the optimizer that city and state are functionally related. PostgreSQL 10+ has `CREATE STATISTICS`, MySQL 8+ has histograms with function-based bindings. These are opt-in — by default the optimizer assumes independence.

#### Disjunction

$\text{sel}(A \lor B) \approx \text{sel}(A) + \text{sel}(B) - \text{sel}(A) \cdot \text{sel}(B)$

Assumes independence again.

#### Join selectivity

For an equi-join on primary-foreign key:

sel(R.k=S.k)=1max⁡(NDV(R.k),NDV(S.k))\text{sel}(R.k = S.k) = \frac{1}{\max(\text{NDV}(R.k), \text{NDV}(S.k))}sel(R.k=S.k)=max(NDV(R.k),NDV(S.k))1

And therefore:

∣R⋈S∣≈∣R∣⋅∣S∣max⁡(NDV(R.k),NDV(S.k))|R \bowtie S| $$\frac{$$|R| \cdot |S| }{\max($\text{NDV}(R.k), \text{NDV}(S.k))}$∣R⋈S∣≈max(NDV(R.k),NDV(S.k))∣R∣⋅∣S∣ 

> **Assumptions behind this formula.** This estimate is correct under three conditions: (1) **primary-foreign-key semantics** — every value on the FK side also appears on the PK side, so no matches are lost; (2) **uniform distribution** — values in both columns are spread evenly across the domain, so each pair of values is equally likely to match; and (3) **independence** — the two join columns are not correlated with other predicates already applied. Violate any one of these and the estimate can be off by orders of magnitude. Real optimizer papers call this the "attribute value independence" (AVI) assumption, and it is the single most common source of bad plan choices.

For Thread A's Students ⋈ Enrollments on `student_id` (10,000 students, 800,000 enrollments):

∣Students⋈Enrollments∣=10,000⋅800,00010,000=800,000| \text{Students} \bowtie $\text{Enrollments}$ | $$\frac{10{,}000 \cdot 800{,}000}{10{,}000} = 800{,}000$$

The join result has the same cardinality as Enrollments — every enrollment matches exactly one student (foreign key guarantee). The formula works here because all three assumptions hold: FK integrity is enforced, enrollment counts per student are roughly uniform, and no correlated predicates have been applied.

**When the formula fails.** For a non-PK-FK join (e.g., `Orders.region = Warehouses.region` where neither column is a primary key), the max-NDV formula assumes each region on the order side matches each region on the warehouse side with equal probability — but in practice some regions dominate. The optimizer may estimate 50 rows and get 50,000. Correlated multi-column predicates compound the error: `WHERE city = 'NYC' AND state = 'NY'` has far fewer rows than `sel(city) × sel(state)` applied independently. Extended statistics (multi-column statistics, functional dependencies) are the standard remedy; they must be declared explicitly in most engines.

### 12.5 Plan Enumeration

Given rewriting and statistics, the optimizer must pick among all equivalent physical plans. The space is huge.

#### The join-order problem

For n-table joins, the number of plan shapes is astronomical. **Left-deep plans** are a restricted subspace where each join has a base table on the right:

$(((R_1 \bowtie R_2) \bowtie R_3) \bowtie R_4)$

There are n!n!n! orderings — 5!=1205! = 1205!=120 for 5-way joins; 10!=3,628,80010! = 3{,}628{,}80010!=3,628,800 for 10-way. Times 3 join algorithms per edge × access paths per table, and the combinatorics explode.

#### System R's dynamic programming

The classical approach, from IBM's System R in 1979, is dynamic programming over left-deep plans:

  1. For each single table, enumerate access paths (seq scan, available index scans); keep the cheapest for each "interesting order."
  2. For each pair (subset of size 2), enumerate joins (choice of join algorithm, which side is outer). Keep the cheapest plan per interesting order.
  3. For each subset of size 3, build by extending a subset of size 2. Continue until all tables are joined.

Cost: O(3n)O(3^n)O(3n) time, O(2n)O(2^n)O(2n) space. Tractable up to ~12–15 tables; beyond that, heuristics kick in.

#### Interesting orders

An "interesting order" is a sort order that might be useful later (for a merge join, a GROUP BY, or ORDER BY). The optimizer keeps the cheapest plan _per order_ , not just the overall cheapest — because a slightly-more-expensive sorted plan might save a downstream sort. This is why the optimizer considers more than one winner per subset.

#### Bushy and right-deep plans

Left-deep is not the only shape.

* **Right-deep** : R1⋈(R2⋈(R3⋈R4))R_1 $\bowtie (R_2 \bowtie (R_3 \bowtie R_4))R1$⋈(R2⋈(R3⋈R4)). Useful when hash joins can pipeline the right side.
* **Bushy** : (R1⋈R2)⋈(R3⋈R4)(R_1 $\bowtie R_2) \bowtie (R_3 \bowtie R_4)(R1$⋈R2)⋈(R3⋈R4). Two subtree joins can run in parallel.

Modern optimizers consider bushy plans at least for some configurations. The search cost is higher but the plan can be substantially better when parallelism or pipelining matches the shape.

#### Heuristics for many tables

When the number of tables exceeds a threshold (PostgreSQL's `geqo_threshold`, default 12), the optimizer switches to a **genetic algorithm** or greedy heuristic. It gives up on optimal and searches for "good enough" within a time budget.

#### Common Misconception

"The optimizer always picks a left-deep plan." Classical ones did; modern ones explore bushy shapes for big queries and for parallelism. The difference can be substantial.

### 12.6 Rule-Based vs Cost-Based Optimization

#### Rule-based (RBO)

Apply a fixed set of rewrites in a fixed order. Push selections down. Pick an index if one matches the WHERE clause. Output plan. No statistics, no cost estimation. Oracle's original optimizer was rule-based.

Advantages: deterministic, fast, explainable. Disadvantages: cannot adapt to data size or distribution. An index is picked even when a full scan would be faster.

#### Cost-based (CBO)

Generate many plans, cost each, pick the minimum. Uses statistics.

Advantages: adapts to data; can catch wins an RBO cannot. Disadvantages: fragile when statistics are wrong; sometimes picks wildly bad plans from mis-estimation.

#### Where real systems sit

All serious engines today are cost-based for joins and access paths. Residual rule-based elements persist for certain rewrites (predicate pushdown is a rule; the rewriter applies it unconditionally because it never hurts). The best optimizers combine: rewrite with rules, enumerate with DP, cost with statistics.

### 12.7 Reading EXPLAIN — A Field Guide

Understanding a plan is more valuable than understanding the optimizer's internals. Here is a PostgreSQL plan for Thread B's query with all indexes present.
    
    
    Nested Loop  (cost=0.42..15302.71 rows=1200 width=80)
      ->  Nested Loop  (cost=0.42..12345.28 rows=1200 width=56)
            ->  Nested Loop  (cost=0.42..8421.55 rows=2500 width=48)
                  ->  Index Scan using students_year_idx on Students s
                        Index Cond: (year = 4)
                        rows=2500 width=24
                  ->  Index Scan using enrollments_sid_idx on Enrollments e
                        Index Cond: (student_id = s.id)
                        rows=0..5 width=24
            ->  Index Scan using courses_pkey on Courses c
                  Index Cond: (id = e.course_id)
      ->  Index Scan using instructors_pkey on Instructors i
            Index Cond: (id = c.instructor_id)
    

Read top-down, executing bottom-up.

Outer loop: `Students(year=4)` — 2,500 matching rows via the year index. For each senior, inner loop: `Enrollments(student_id=s.id)` — an expected 0 to 5 rows via the student_id index. For each enrollment: `Courses(id=e.course_id)` — primary-key lookup. For each course: `Instructors(id=c.instructor_id)` — primary-key lookup.

**Cost breakdown** (PostgreSQL notation): startup..total. The final root estimates 1,200 rows at ~15,300 cost units — about 100 ms on a warm cache.

#### Signals in the plan

* **Seq Scan on a large table** : why no index? Either no index exists, or the optimizer chose not to use it (selectivity too low, index not matching, cost estimate wrong).
* **Hash Join where one side is huge** : normally fine — that is what hash joins are for. But if build side exceeds `work_mem`, look for `Batches: 8 Memory: 4MB` warnings — spilling is happening.
* **Nested Loop over a large outer** : very bad if the inner has no index. Almost always a missing-index signal.
* **Bitmap Heap Scan** : good sign that the optimizer picked a middle path between index and scan.
* **Filter: (...) Rows Removed by Filter: 1000000** : the filter runs _after_ the scan, meaning the scan returned a million rows the query did not need. Can an index evaluate the predicate earlier?

#### Modern plan nodes (PostgreSQL 9.6+)

Newer PostgreSQL versions introduce nodes that do not appear in older textbook examples. Each is annotated with the version that introduced it:

* **Parallel Seq Scan** _(PG 9.6+)_ : PostgreSQL splits the heap into ranges and assigns each to a worker process. You will see `Workers Planned: 4` on the node. If `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` shows `Workers Launched: 0`, the table is too small or `max_parallel_workers_per_gather` is zero.
* **Gather / Gather Merge** _(PG 9.6+)_ : the coordinator node that collects rows from parallel workers. `Gather Merge` preserves sort order (used above a parallel sort); plain `Gather` does not. A `Gather` at the root of a plan is the signal that parallelism was used.
* **JIT: enabled** _(PG 11+)_ : shown at the bottom of `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` output, this means PostgreSQL compiled parts of the plan with LLVM JIT. JIT helps long-running analytical queries but adds compile-time overhead — disable it for short OLTP queries with `SET jit = off` or by raising `jit_above_cost`.
* **Incremental Sort** _(PG 13+)_ : instead of re-sorting the full result, PostgreSQL exploits a partial pre-sort (e.g., an index already sorted on the first key) and sorts only within groups. Shown as `Incremental Sort Key: col1, col2` with `Presorted Key: col1`. Far cheaper than a full sort when the leading key is already ordered.

When you see any of these nodes in a production plan, interpret them the same way as the classic nodes — compare `rows=` estimates to actual rows, look for spill indicators (`Batches`, `Disk Usage`), and check whether worker count matches expectations.

#### EXPLAIN ANALYZE — the essential tool

`EXPLAIN` shows estimates. `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` runs the query and shows actuals. Compare:
    
    
    rows=2500 (expected) vs actual rows=250000 (100× underestimate)
    

That factor-of-100 misestimate cascades — every downstream operator's cost was based on 2500 input rows, not 250,000. The plan is probably catastrophic. The fix is almost always: update statistics, add extended statistics, or rewrite the query to give the optimizer a clearer shape.

### 12.8 Predicate Pushdown, Pruning, and Other Rewrites

#### Predicate pushdown

Move WHERE predicates as far down the plan as possible — before joins, into the scan, into subqueries. Chapter 7 proved this algebraically; Chapter 12 implements it. The rewriter inspects each predicate and asks: does it reference only columns from one side of the join? If yes, push to that side.

#### Projection pushdown

Strip columns as early as possible. Joining only needs join-key columns plus any columns the outer query projects. Heavy text columns (a "body" column in a blogposts table, for instance) should never be carried through the entire pipeline.

#### Partition pruning

For partitioned tables, the optimizer reads the predicate and eliminates partitions. `WHERE created_at >= '2024-01-01'` on a date-partitioned table reads only 2024 and later partitions. A big win — often orders of magnitude — for queries over time-ranged data.

#### Subquery flattening and decorrelation

Chapter 8 hinted at this. The rewriter turns `WHERE id IN (SELECT ...)` into a semi-join operator; correlated subqueries become joined aggregates. When it works, it is transformative. When it doesn't, the user sees a slow query and must rewrite manually.

#### Join elimination

If a LEFT JOIN references columns from the right side that are not used anywhere except NULL-checks, the join can sometimes be removed entirely. The optimizer sees the foreign-key constraint and the projection and concludes the join is a no-op.

### 12.9 Thread B: The Complete Story

Thread B opened Chapter 7 with 20 minutes. It closed Chapter 10 with 2 seconds. Let's trace the arc through the optimizer's eyes.

#### Without indexes, without statistics

Optimizer sees four tables with no statistics (or default "1000 rows each"). No indexes. It picks left-deep NL join arbitrarily. Result: Students$\text{Students} \times \text{Enrollments}Students$ dominated, effectively O(n2)O(n^2)O(n2) scans. 20 minutes on a 100k-row DB.

#### With statistics, without indexes

Now the optimizer knows sizes: Students=10k, Enrollments=800k, Courses=500, Instructors=100. Without indexes, NL join is ruled out (inner scans are too expensive). It picks a hash-join plan, building on the smaller side of each join. Instructors (100 rows) builds first; joined with Courses. Then hashed against Enrollments. Then joined with filtered Students. Cost: ~8,000 page reads. ~500 ms. Huge win already.

#### With statistics, with indexes

The optimizer now has an extra choice: index scan. For the highly-selective filter `year = 4` on Students (25% selectivity), sequential scan is still ~as good. But for the join `Enrollments.student_id = s.id`, with index, per-probe cost is 3–5 reads for 2,500 probes — ~10,000 probe reads cached to ~2,000. Plus another 2,500 for Courses, cached after the first, ~2,500 for Instructors, cached. Total: ~5,000 page reads. ~200 ms. The plan shifts from hash-heavy to index-NL-heavy.

#### With extended statistics for year + major

Suppose we also add `CREATE STATISTICS ON (year, major) FROM Students`. The optimizer can now correctly estimate that all fourth-years in CS (where CS is 40% of seniors) are correlated, avoiding a downstream mis-estimate. For queries that filter on both, the plan improves another 20–30%.

This is the complete arc. The 20-minutes-to-200-milliseconds gap is **6,000×** , but the contributing factors don't decompose cleanly by simple multiplication — they are intertwined (a better join order only helps once statistics reveal the sizes; indexes only pay off once the optimizer stops picking nested-loop with a full outer). A workable apportionment, matched to the narrative above, is: **~2,500× from statistics + hash join (20 min → ~500 ms)** , then **~2.5× from indexes + correct ordering (500 ms → ~200 ms)**. Extended statistics move another ~20–30% on correlated filters. The key takeaway is not the decomposition but the _ordering of interventions_ : feed the optimizer information (statistics) first; only then do indexes buy you the remaining order of magnitude.

#### Common Misconception

"The optimizer fixes slow queries automatically." It fixes queries when (a) the indexes exist, (b) the statistics are fresh and accurate, (c) the query shape is one it understands. When any of those fail, the optimizer picks a bad plan. Most slow-query fixes in production are: update statistics, add missing index, rewrite query. Not: optimizer magic.

### 12.10 When to Intervene

Database engineers spend much of their time tuning optimizers.

#### Refresh statistics

First response to a slow query that used to be fast: `ANALYZE`. Often that alone fixes it.

#### Extend statistics

For correlated columns (`city/state`, `manufacturer/model`), create extended statistics.

#### Add an index

When the plan shows a Seq Scan on a table where only a tiny fraction is selected, add an index on the filter column.

#### Rewrite the SQL

Sometimes the optimizer cannot unify `IN` and `EXISTS` (older engines), or correlates subqueries without decorrelating, or picks a bad join order because an estimate is off. Rewriting can nudge it.

#### Optimizer hints

`/*+ INDEX(t idx) */` in Oracle, `STRAIGHT_JOIN` in MySQL. Tells the optimizer to use a specific join order or access path. **Last resort** : hints age badly. Data changes; the hint doesn't; five years later the hint is causing the slow query.

#### Rebuild bloated indexes

Indexes that have seen many deletions accumulate dead entries. A periodic `REINDEX` or `VACUUM FULL` can restore performance.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Cost-based optimization trades plan stability for adaptiveness.** A CBO that consults statistics can pick the right plan as data shifts, but a stale or misestimated histogram cascades into 100×-wrong cost predictions and the kind of plan flip that turns a 2-second query into a 20-minute one.
* **More statistics buckets and MCVs trade collection cost for estimation accuracy.** Doubling histogram resolution helps with skewed distributions, but ANALYZE itself takes longer and the marginal accuracy gain often loses to a single bad independence assumption.
* **The independence assumption trades correctness on correlated columns for fast predicate combination.** Multiplying selectivities is O(1) and good enough most of the time, but on correlated pairs like `(city, state)` it can underestimate by 20×, blowing up downstream join cost estimates.
* **Optimizer hints trade short-term correctness for long-term fragility.** A `/*+ INDEX(...) */` can rescue a misestimated plan today, but data distributions drift; five years later the hint is the reason a query is slow, and nobody remembers why it was added.
* **Startup cost vs total cost is a tradeoff the planner must price separately for`LIMIT`.** A plan with low total cost but high startup cost is wrong for `LIMIT 10`; the optimizer keeps both numbers and picks differently for "first row fast" versus "all rows fast" workloads.
* **DP enumeration over more join shapes (bushy, right-deep) trades planning time for plan quality.** Considering bushy plans expands the search space and finds better plans for some join graphs, but the search itself can take longer than the query — at large N, engines fall back to greedy or genetic heuristics.
* **Extended statistics on column groups trade upfront DBA work for accurate compound-predicate estimates.** Declaring `(city, state)` as correlated fixes a class of misestimates, but somebody has to know which columns to declare — the optimizer cannot infer correlations on its own.

### 12.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State three things the optimizer's cost model prices and two things it does not.
  2. Explain the independence assumption and give an example where it leads to a large misestimate.
  3. What is an "interesting order"? Why does the optimizer keep multiple winners per join subset?
  4. Distinguish rule-based from cost-based optimization. Name one historical engine that used pure RBO.
  5. Why are statistics stale? What command refreshes them on two engines of your choice?
  6. Describe the difference between predicate pushdown and projection pushdown using one example each.

#### Application Problems

  7. For a table with 1M rows and a column with 200 distinct values, roughly uniformly distributed, estimate the selectivity of `WHERE col = 'X'`. How many rows does the optimizer expect to match?

  8. A query plan shows:
         
         Seq Scan on orders  (cost=0..18000 rows=200000 width=...)
           Filter: customer_id = 42
           Rows Removed by Filter: 999800
         

Actual matches: 200. Estimate: 200000. The optimizer misestimated by 1000×. Propose three fixes.

Hint

A 1000× overestimate on a single equality predicate suggests the optimizer thinks values are nearly uniform when they are skewed. Walk through the decision tree of §12.10: what could the database not know that you could tell it? Consider stats freshness, MCV coverage, and access path.

  9. A `WHERE product_category = 'electronics' AND price > 100` returns ~2% of rows. The optimizer estimates 0.04% (400× underestimate). Explain what assumption failed. What mechanism fixes it?

Hint

Compute sel(category) × sel(price > 100) as if independent and compare to the actual 2%. The gap reveals correlation: are electronics priced like every other category? §12.4 names both the failed assumption and the engine feature that lets you tell the optimizer otherwise.

  10. You have a 10-table join. The optimizer's DP enumeration completes in under a second on 8 tables but times out on 10. Identify what strategy the optimizer likely switches to at 10+ tables and its tradeoffs.

  11. A plan has a Hash Join with `Batches: 16 Memory: 4MB Disk: 512MB`. Explain what happened, what changed to trigger spilling, and two mitigation strategies.

Hint

"16 batches" and "Disk: 512MB" tell you the build side overflowed `work_mem` by roughly the disk-to-memory ratio. Ask: did the build-side estimate jump unexpectedly, or is the actual build size always too big? Mitigations should address either the estimate or the resource limit.

  12. Given statistics: Students=10k (NDV on id = 10k), Enrollments=800k (NDV on student_id = 9500 — some students have no enrollments). Estimate the expected size of `SELECT * FROM Students JOIN Enrollments ON Students.id = Enrollments.student_id`.

Hint

Apply the join-cardinality formula |R| × |S| / max(NDV(R.k), NDV(S.k)) from §12.4. Note that NDV(student_id) = 9500 < NDV(id) = 10000, which matters for which side determines the denominator. The result should match a sanity check based on average enrollments per matched student.

#### Think Deeper

  13. The optimizer uses sampling-based statistics with a default sample size of ~30,000 rows. Describe a data distribution where sampling systematically misleads the optimizer. Propose a remedy.

Hint

Consider what a 30k-row sample misses: rare values, heavy long tails, or correlations across columns. Pick a distribution where the _truth_ is concentrated in a small fraction of rows the sample is unlikely to hit. The remedy might be increasing per-column statistics targets or an explicit MCV-related setting.

  14. Oracle and SQL Server optimizers consider bushy plans; PostgreSQL traditionally did not. Sketch a query where a bushy plan outperforms the best left-deep plan and identify what must be true about the join graph for bushy to win.

  15. A user complains that after adding an index, queries got slower. Propose three plausible explanations, each pointing at a different optimizer phenomenon.

  16. A query runs instantaneously during testing (small data set) and 100× slower in production (large data set). Plans differ. Describe three aspects of the optimizer's input that are different in production. Which is most likely the root cause?

  17. An "adaptive" optimizer checks estimated vs actual row counts mid-query and re-plans if they diverge too much. Sketch when this helps and when it hurts. Describe one production engine that does this.

Hint

Adaptive replanning helps when you can stop a doomed plan early and switch cheaply — but it costs you the work already done and the planning time itself. Ask: at what point in a query is the cost of throwing out partial results lowest? Conversely, when does mid-query replanning thrash without ever beating the original plan?

  18. The optimizer picks plans based on estimated cost, but the **real** runtime also depends on cache warmth, concurrent queries, and hardware load. When and how does the optimizer account for these? When does it not, and what are the implications?

  19. Consider a correlated subquery
         
         SELECT s.id FROM Students s WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM Enrollments e WHERE e.student_id = s.id AND e.term = '2025-Fall');
         

Describe how the optimizer decorrelates this, and what operator it produces. Why is a SEMI JOIN sometimes preferable to an INNER JOIN + DISTINCT?

  20. You have a workload where the same query shape runs with 10,000 different literal values of a filter predicate. The optimizer might produce 10,000 slightly different plans, or one plan reused for all. Describe the tradeoff (plan cache, parameter sniffing) and name the concept in two real databases.

### Chapter Summary

The optimizer converts a logical expression into an executable plan by interleaving **rewriting** , **enumeration** , and **costing**. Rewriting applies algebraic equivalences. Enumeration generates candidate join orders and access paths. Costing estimates, using statistics, the expected work of each candidate.

**Statistics** — histograms, MCVs, NDV, correlation — are the fuel. Stale or inaccurate statistics produce misestimates, which cascade through the plan and break everything downstream. The **independence assumption** is the most common source of misestimates for compound predicates; **extended statistics** mitigate it.

**Plan enumeration** uses System-R-style dynamic programming over left-deep (sometimes bushy) plans, considering interesting orders at each subset. Beyond ~12 tables, heuristics or genetic algorithms take over.

**EXPLAIN ANALYZE** is the engineer's primary tool: it shows the plan, the estimated and actual row counts, and where time was spent. Discrepancies point at statistics problems or structural mis-estimates. Fixes are almost always: refresh statistics, add an index, rewrite the query, extend statistics for correlated columns.

Thread B's arc — 20 minutes to 200 milliseconds — decomposes into contributions from each optimizer component, with indexes providing the biggest single leap but every other piece amplifying it. This closes the physical-design half of the book. Chapters 13 onward leave performance behind and ask a harder question: how do we ensure correctness while many transactions modify the same data simultaneously?

---

## Chapter 13: Transactions and ACID

So far the book has treated a database as a single-user system. One query at a time, one modification at a time, no one else in the room. That is a fiction. Real databases run _many_ concurrent statements from _many_ concurrent clients. Servers crash, networks drop connections, processes are killed mid-execution. And yet, at the end of all this chaos, users expect the database's invariants to hold: balances balance, constraints are enforced, no money appears or disappears.

This chapter is where the book pivots. The previous chapters were about _speed_. The next five chapters are about _correctness_ under concurrency and failure. The central abstraction is the **transaction** : a unit of work that the database promises to execute as if it were the only thing happening, and as if the hardware never failed. The four promises are abbreviated **ACID** : Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability.

The transaction abstraction is one of the most successful in computer science. Applications don't need to reason about races, partial failures, or disk crashes. The DBMS handles all of them. But the abstraction has a price — performance overhead, tradeoffs between isolation and throughput, and subtle edge cases that every backend engineer eventually discovers the hard way.

We introduce the bank account scenario — Thread C, two users one account — that will carry us through Chapters 13 through 16.

### 13.1 Why Transactions Exist

Consider two SQL statements run from two different application sessions simultaneously:
    
    
    -- Session A: transfer $100 from Alice to Bob
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE owner = 'Alice';
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE owner = 'Bob';
    
    
    
    -- Session B: compute total money in system
    SELECT SUM(balance) FROM Accounts;
    

If session B runs its SELECT between A's two UPDATEs, it sees 100 USD of money that does not currently exist (Alice is down, Bob is not yet up). The total is wrong — by 100 USD. Run the report a split second later, the total is right. Is the system broken? From session B's perspective, yes.

The problem is that "transfer money from Alice to Bob" is _not one operation_ at the SQL level. It is two. Between them, the world looks inconsistent. Any observer that reads the database in that window sees an impossible state.

The fix is to let the application declare that these two UPDATEs are **one unit of work** — a **transaction**. The database either applies both, or neither, and no observer sees anything in between.

Three classes of failure the transaction abstraction handles:

  1. **Mid-transaction crash** : the server dies after the first UPDATE but before the second. Without transactions, Alice loses 100 USD and Bob never gets it. With transactions, both UPDATEs are undone.
  2. **Concurrent visibility** : another session reads between the two UPDATEs. Without transactions, it sees inconsistency. With transactions, it sees either the before-state or the after-state but not a mixed state.
  3. **Write-write conflict** : two sessions both try to modify Alice's balance concurrently. Without transactions, last-writer-wins destroys one of the updates silently. With transactions, one of them is serialized (blocked, retried, or aborted).

These three motivations map to three letters of ACID: Atomicity, Isolation, Durability (with Consistency as the invariant they all serve).

#### Common Misconception

"Transactions are about multi-statement updates." They are about any observation or modification that must be atomic across multiple underlying operations. A single UPDATE that touches 1000 rows also needs transaction semantics (the 1000 rows update together or not at all, and no concurrent reader sees a partial set of updates).

### 13.2 The Four Promises (ACID)

#### Atomicity

#### Definition: Atomicity

A transaction either executes fully — every statement takes effect — or not at all. There is no state of the database in which some but not all of the transaction's effects are visible after it has ended.

Atomicity is about _all or nothing_. If a transaction runs 10 UPDATEs and then the server crashes, recovery (Chapter 17) will ensure that either all 10 effects are persistent or none are. No half-completed transactions.

#### Consistency

#### Definition: Consistency

If the database satisfies every integrity constraint before a transaction begins, and the transaction's SQL respects those constraints at commit, then the database satisfies every constraint after the transaction commits.

This is a tautological-sounding promise, because it depends on the application writing correct SQL. The database enforces constraints (primary keys, foreign keys, CHECK, uniqueness); Consistency says these constraints are never violated across transaction boundaries. If you issue an UPDATE that violates a CHECK constraint, the transaction aborts — never committed into an inconsistent state.

Consistency is the weakest of the four in terms of what the database uniquely provides (most of it is enforced by schema constraints) but the most important one for the application — it is what business logic relies on.

#### Isolation

#### Definition: Isolation

Concurrent transactions appear to execute in some serial order, such that the result is equivalent to executing them one at a time.

This is a beautiful theoretical guarantee and, in practice, the hardest to deliver without crushing performance. Full serializability requires that _any_ interleaving of operations between transactions yields the same result as _some_ serial order. Strict implementations of this slow writes to a crawl, which is why databases offer **isolation levels** — relaxations of the full guarantee — that Chapter 14 explores.

#### Durability

#### Definition: Durability

Once the database has acknowledged a transaction as committed, its effects survive any subsequent failure — crashes, power loss, process kills.

Durability is the easiest to understand and the subtlest to implement. Modern drives have write caches; confirmed OS writes may not yet be on the platter. Chapter 17 describes WAL and the sync sequence required to turn "the OS thinks it wrote" into "the data is on spinning rust."

#### The four together

ACID is the promise an application relies on. Most higher-level abstractions — queues, caches, ORMs — depend on it silently. The rest of this chapter describes how Atomicity and Isolation are delivered; Chapter 17 handles Durability.

### 13.3 Transaction Life Cycle

SQL transactions follow a consistent protocol.
    
    
    BEGIN;                       -- transaction starts
    UPDATE Accounts SET ... ;    -- statement 1
    INSERT INTO Log ... ;        -- statement 2
    SELECT ... ;                 -- read within the transaction
    COMMIT;                      -- commit point: effects become durable and visible
    

Or:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    UPDATE ...;
    -- something goes wrong
    ROLLBACK;                    -- all effects are undone
    

#### States

* **Active** : from BEGIN until the first decision point. The transaction may read, write, and is free to abort.
* **Committing** : after COMMIT is issued. The database must now make the changes durable (flush WAL, release locks, publish visibility to other transactions).
* **Committed** : transaction is durable and visible. No rollback possible.
* **Aborting** : after ROLLBACK (or a forced abort due to deadlock, constraint violation, crash). Changes must be undone.
* **Aborted** : fully reverted. Transaction is done; no further operations.

#### Savepoints

SQL allows partial rollback within a transaction via **savepoints** :
    
    
    BEGIN;
    UPDATE ...;
    SAVEPOINT s1;
    UPDATE ...;
    -- bad happened
    ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT s1;   -- undo only the second UPDATE
    COMMIT;                      -- commit the first UPDATE
    

Savepoints are widely supported (PostgreSQL, MySQL InnoDB, SQL Server, Oracle) and are the mechanism behind nested transactions in application frameworks.

#### Implicit vs explicit transactions

In most engines, statements outside an explicit `BEGIN` still run in an implicit transaction scoped to one statement. A standalone UPDATE is atomic — you do not need to wrap it in BEGIN/COMMIT to get atomicity for one statement. Explicit transactions are for multi-statement units of work.

PostgreSQL and SQL Server default to autocommit — each statement its own transaction. Oracle runs in "chained" mode by default — no statement is committed until you issue `COMMIT` explicitly. Important gotcha: a badly-behaved client that opens a transaction but never commits or rolls back holds locks for hours. Every application framework needs to guarantee transaction cleanup.

### 13.4 Thread C: Two Users, One Account

The scenario that will thread through Chapters 13–16 is deliberately small.

**Setup** : `Accounts(id, balance)`. One account with `id = 1, balance = 1000`. Two concurrent transactions each withdraw 800 USD.

#### The statements

Session A:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT balance FROM Accounts WHERE id = 1;   -- reads 1000
    -- application checks balance >= 800, decides to proceed
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 800 WHERE id = 1;
    COMMIT;
    

Session B runs the exact same three statements.

#### Without any concurrency control — the lost update

Interleave like this:
    
    
    A: BEGIN
    B: BEGIN
    A: SELECT → reads 1000
    B: SELECT → reads 1000 (A hasn't committed anything yet)
    A: UPDATE SET balance = 1000 - 800 = 200; writes 200
    B: UPDATE SET balance = 1000 - 800 = 200; writes 200 (overwrites A's update)
    A: COMMIT
    B: COMMIT
    

Final balance: 200. Total withdrawn: 1600 USD. But the account only had 1000 USD. The bank has given away 800 USD it did not have.

This is the **lost update** anomaly. B's computation used a balance that A had already decremented but not yet committed. When B wrote 200 USD, it overwrote A's 200 USD as if A had never happened.

#### A different interleaving — the phantom funds
    
    
    A: BEGIN
    B: BEGIN
    A: SELECT → 1000
    A: UPDATE balance = 200
    A: COMMIT
    B: SELECT → 1000 (but B read it before A committed)
    B: UPDATE balance = 200
    B: COMMIT
    

Same result — but the story is different. Now B read a balance _after_ it had already been decremented by A. B's read was stale. Same end: 1600 USD withdrawn from 1000 USD.

#### A third interleaving — the false rejection
    
    
    A: BEGIN; SELECT → 1000; UPDATE = 200;
    B: BEGIN; SELECT → 200; application says "insufficient funds" and ROLLBACKs
    A: COMMIT
    

Now B sees 200 instead of 1000 and rejects, even though if we had serialized A and B we would have had B see 1000 and A see 200 — B could legitimately withdraw, A could not. The _order_ matters and our uncontrolled interleaving picked the wrong one.

All three anomalies arise from one underlying bug: two transactions both _read_ the balance, then both _wrote_ based on a stale value. Chapter 14's isolation levels classify anomalies like these; Chapter 15 fixes them with locks; Chapter 16 fixes them with MVCC.

#### Common Misconception

"Using a single UPDATE statement avoids the race." Sometimes. `UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 800 WHERE id = 1` _is_ atomic at the SQL statement level in most engines — the read-modify-write is serialized internally. But when the application needs to _check_ the balance first (`if balance >= 800 then ...`), the check is outside the UPDATE and the race returns. Application-level logic is where transactional bugs hide.

### 13.5 Serial, Serializable, and Equivalent

The gold-standard guarantee that transactions produce correct results is **serializability**.

#### Definition: Serial execution

An execution in which transactions run one at a time, with no overlap. Each transaction completes entirely before the next begins.

Serial executions are trivially correct — nobody interferes with anybody. They are also unacceptably slow: no parallelism.

#### Definition: Serializable execution

An execution in which transactions run concurrently, but the final state and the results of every transaction's reads are _equivalent_ to some serial execution.
