Note that different serial orders may give different results. A serializable execution need only match _some_ serial order, not a specific one. Two transactions T1 and T2 that both withdraw from the same account produce different end-balances depending on order — but as long as the concurrent execution matches either (T1 then T2) or (T2 then T1), it is serializable.

#### How "equivalence" is defined

The theory distinguishes several notions of equivalence:

* **Conflict serializability** : two schedules are equivalent if they have the same set of conflicting operations in the same order. Two operations conflict if they belong to different transactions and one is a write to a data item the other reads or writes.
* **View serializability** : weaker — same reads and same final writes. Strictly larger set of schedules, but undecidable in general.

Database engines target **conflict serializability** because it is efficient to check (build a "conflict graph" of transactions; a schedule is conflict-serializable iff the graph is acyclic). Chapter 15 explains how 2PL produces conflict-serializable schedules; Chapter 16 how MVCC does.

### 13.6 Read and Write Anomalies — A Catalog

Before Chapter 14 dissects isolation levels, we need shared vocabulary for the anomalies each level prevents.

#### Dirty read

T1 writes a value, T2 reads the value, T1 aborts. T2 saw data that never officially existed. Real-world analog: reading an email from an Outbox folder before the sender clicks "Send for real."

#### Non-repeatable read

T1 reads x=10x = 10x=10. T2 updates x=20x = 20x=20 and commits. T1 reads xxx again and gets 202020. Two reads of the same row returned different values without T1 modifying it. For a report that performs multiple reads, this means rows "change" while you are looking.

#### Phantom read

T1 reads `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Orders WHERE status='pending'` = 5. T2 inserts a new order with status=pending and commits. T1 re-runs the same SELECT and gets 6. A _row that was not there before_ now appears — a "phantom." Subtly different from non-repeatable read (the same row does not change; _the set of rows_ changes).

#### Lost update

Thread C's first interleaving. Two transactions both read an X, compute new X values, and write — the later write overwrites the earlier without respecting it.

#### Write skew

A tricky one, less famous than the others. Two transactions both read overlapping data, both write disjoint data, and each write is locally sensible — but together they violate an invariant. Classic example: on-call scheduling. Alice and Bob are both on call. The rule is "at least one doctor must be on call." Both transactions run: "If at least one other doctor is on call, I can go off-call." They both read "yes, the other is on call" and both update themselves to off-call. No one is on call. Each transaction's write was individually fine; the pair violated the invariant.

Write skew cannot be reproduced by any serial execution of the two transactions. MVCC's snapshot isolation is vulnerable to it; serializable isolation prevents it.

#### Summary table

Anomaly | Scenario  
---|---  
Dirty read | T2 reads T1's uncommitted write  
Non-repeatable read | T1 reads, T2 commits update, T1 reads again  
Phantom read | T1 reads range, T2 commits insert, T1 re-reads  
Lost update | T1 and T2 both read, both write, second wins  
Write skew | T1 and T2 read overlap, write disjoint, invariant breaks  
  
Chapter 14 shows which anomalies each isolation level permits and prevents.

### 13.7 Atomicity Mechanisms — Undo Logs, Preview

Making a transaction **all or nothing** in the presence of crashes is not trivial. Chapter 17 covers the full recovery story. For now, the mechanism in brief:

  1. When a transaction modifies a page, the DBMS writes a log record describing the change _before_ modifying the page in the buffer pool.
  2. The log record is **ahead of** the data page — hence **write-ahead log (WAL)**. The log is always flushed to disk at commit.
  3. If the server crashes mid-transaction, recovery reads the log, sees the transaction never reached COMMIT, and **undoes** its effects by applying reverse-updates from the log records.
  4. If the transaction reached COMMIT, recovery **redoes** any updates whose data pages did not make it to disk.

This is the skeleton of ACID's A and D. Chapter 17 gives the algorithm (ARIES) and the invariants it maintains.

### 13.8 Isolation Mechanisms — Two Families

Two families of isolation mechanism dominate the industry:

#### Lock-based (pessimistic)

Before reading or writing, acquire a lock. Conflicting operations block until the holder commits. Chapter 15.

#### Multi-version (optimistic)

Every write produces a new version tagged with the transaction's timestamp. Readers see the version valid at their start time. Writers check at commit for conflicts. Chapter 16.

PostgreSQL uses MVCC primarily (with some locking for write conflicts). MySQL InnoDB uses MVCC for reads and row locks for writes. SQL Server offers both (locking by default, snapshot isolation optional). Oracle uses MVCC. Essentially every modern database uses MVCC for reads; pure 2PL is mostly historical.

### 13.9 Durability Mechanisms — WAL, Preview

Once a transaction commits, its effects must survive crashes. The minimal requirement:

**Before acknowledging COMMIT to the client, force the log to stable storage.**

If every log record is on disk, recovery can reconstruct committed changes by replaying the log. The data pages themselves may lag behind — they get flushed opportunistically. This decoupling lets the database run fast _and_ stay durable: one sequential log write per commit beats many random data writes.

The delicate part is what "stable storage" means. Disk write caches, OS page cache, fsync semantics — Chapter 17 turns this into rigorous protocol.

### 13.10 Tradeoffs of ACID

ACID is not free, and alternative systems exist that relax its guarantees for scalability.

#### Strict ACID

* Every commit forces a sync. ~100 μs per commit on SSD; ~10 ms on HDD.
* Isolation mechanisms add contention. High-conflict workloads can see throughput drop by factors of 10× when scaling from 1 to 100 concurrent transactions.
* Distribution across nodes requires consensus (2PC, Raft, Paxos), which is slow — we will explore this in Chapter 18.

#### The "BASE" alternative — Basically Available, Soft State, Eventually Consistent

Cassandra, DynamoDB, Redis, many NoSQL systems offer weakened consistency in exchange for scale. A write is acknowledged by a quorum of replicas; eventual consistency says that if no new writes arrive, all replicas will eventually agree.

* **Atomicity** is usually per-key, not across keys.
* **Isolation** is weak or absent — reads may see stale versions.
* **Durability** holds once the quorum has acknowledged.

BASE systems run orders of magnitude faster and scale to many more nodes, at the cost of forcing the application to reason about inconsistencies. We will revisit this in Chapter 18's CAP discussion.

#### Mixed systems

Modern engines often expose ACID tables alongside relaxed-consistency tables or indexes. Even within a single database, some tables might be replicated with strong consistency and others eventually. The rigid "ACID vs BASE" dichotomy is obsolete; the practical question is what guarantees each operation needs.

#### Common Misconception

"ACID databases don't scale." They scale — they just have a cliff. A single node can handle hundreds of thousands of small transactions per second. A replicated cluster with synchronous commit scales linearly in reads but is bounded in writes by the coordinator's commit pipeline. Distributed ACID databases (CockroachDB, Spanner, YugabyteDB) get most of ACID's promises across nodes with careful engineering. The cost is latency: a commit that synchronously replicates takes 5–10 ms instead of 500 μs.

### 13.11 Transactional Boundaries in Real Systems

Knowing _where_ to put transaction boundaries is application design, not database science. A few rules of thumb:

* **One logical operation = one transaction.** Transfer money, submit order, update user profile — each is one unit.
* **Keep transactions short.** Long transactions hold locks longer, increase deadlock risk, and block vacuum (PostgreSQL MVCC).
* **Do not call external services from inside a transaction.** If you call an HTTP API while holding row locks, a slow API degrades your entire DB. Write the result to a `pending_tasks` table in one transaction, process externally, commit success in a second transaction.
* **Retry on deadlock abort.** Two transactions that deadlock — one will be aborted by the engine. The application must retry. Application frameworks like Rails and Django generate retry wrappers for this.

This is the working knowledge every backend engineer carries. The next chapter formalizes why deadlocks happen and how they are detected.

### 13.12 Thread A Check-In

Back in the university registration system, the main transactional case is course enrollment. Thirty seats in a popular course; 500 students trying to enroll the moment registration opens. Each enrollment is:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT seats_remaining FROM Courses WHERE id = 42;   -- read
    -- application checks > 0
    UPDATE Courses SET seats_remaining = seats_remaining - 1 WHERE id = 42;
    INSERT INTO Enrollments(student_id, course_id) VALUES (?, 42);
    COMMIT;
    

Without concurrency control, two students who both read "seats = 1" can each proceed through the check and both insert — seats go to -1. With transactions plus the right isolation level plus the right locking, the second transaction either sees "seats = 0" (and rejects) or blocks until the first commits (and then sees 0). Chapter 15 will build out this exact workflow with 2PL.

### 13.13 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State the four ACID properties in one sentence each.
  2. Give an example where a single SQL statement is already atomic without BEGIN/COMMIT, and one where multi-statement atomicity is needed.
  3. Describe the lost update anomaly using Thread C's account scenario. Which two database operations are the culprits?
  4. Distinguish non-repeatable read from phantom read. Give an example of each.
  5. What is a savepoint, and what problem does it solve?
  6. Explain why "serial execution" is always correct but not always acceptable. Under what workloads does it fail to scale?
  7. Name two reasons an application should keep transactions short.

#### Application Problems

  8. Write a SQL transaction (BEGIN...COMMIT) for a "buy item" workflow that decrements inventory, charges a credit, and inserts an Order row. Identify where atomicity is essential and what happens if the middle step fails.

Hint

For each pair of statements, ask: "if a crash happened between these two, what invariant would break?" The middle step (charging a credit) often reaches an external system — note where that complicates the all-or-nothing promise (§13.11 has the rule of thumb).

  9. Two transactions update a shared counter with `UPDATE counters SET value = value + 1 WHERE name = 'views'`. Under no-concurrency-control, show a trace where the increment is lost. Then show the trace where both increments land.

Hint

The single-statement UPDATE internally does read-modify-write. Lay out the read of `value` and the write of `value + 1` as separate primitive steps; an interleaving where two reads see the same starting value loses an update. The "both land" trace requires read-write atomicity at the row level.

  10. Thread C's banking scenario: propose an application-level protocol (using only the SELECT/UPDATE primitives and no database-provided locks) that prevents lost updates. What weakness remains?

Hint

A version-column / compare-and-swap pattern lets each UPDATE check that nobody else has modified the row since the SELECT. Walk through what `WHERE version = ?` does to the conflicting writer's `rows_affected`. Then ask: what does this approach do when conflicts are _common_ — does it gracefully degrade or thrash?

  11. Describe a read-heavy workload and a write-heavy workload, and for each, discuss how transaction boundaries should be drawn. Should read-only transactions exist?

  12. An application developer forgets to call COMMIT after a failed write and leaves a connection idle. After 4 hours, queries from other clients start to hang. Describe what's likely happening and the fix.

  13. Given a transaction that transfers 100 USD from Alice to Bob, sketch the log records (undo + redo) the engine must write. How does commit translate to disk writes in WAL?

Hint

For each UPDATE, a log record needs both a _redo_ image (to reapply if the data page didn't reach disk) and an _undo_ image (to roll back if the transaction aborts). The commit itself adds one record. What makes commit "durable" is which write must be `fsync`-ed before the client is told "OK"?

  14. A distributed application wants to implement idempotent transactions (so retries are safe). Propose a schema change to the `orders` table that lets a retried INSERT detect it is a duplicate. How does this interact with ACID?

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why does the **Consistency** in ACID feel like a tautology? Articulate what the database does guarantee and what it pushes back onto the application.

  16. ACID vs BASE: propose three real applications, one where strict ACID is required, one where BASE is acceptable, one where a mix is best. Justify each choice by the failure modes.

  17. Savepoints let an application rollback partial work inside a transaction. Design a workflow in which a savepoint is essential. Could you replicate the same behavior without savepoints? What tradeoff do you accept?

  18. Conflict serializability is weaker than view serializability. Describe a schedule that is view-serializable but not conflict-serializable. Why do engines prefer to check the stronger property?

Hint

Construct a three-transaction schedule with a "blind write" — a write that is later overwritten without being read. The conflict graph may have a cycle even though the final state matches a serial order. For the second part, think about what kind of algorithm decides view serializability and how that scales to thousands of concurrent transactions.

  19. In Thread C, three concurrent withdrawals of 500 USD each are requested against a 1000 USD balance. Under perfect isolation, how many succeed? What information must the database maintain to guarantee the "correct" answer is the same regardless of timing?

Hint

"Perfect isolation" means _some_ serial order. Across the six possible orderings of three transactions, how many of the 500 USD withdrawals would succeed? Then ask: which version of the balance must each transaction see at decision time, and what mechanism ensures it sees that version even under concurrent execution?

  20. The ACID abstraction hides an enormous amount of machinery — locks, versioning, WAL, buffer pool flushing. Pick one other systems abstraction you know (virtual memory, file systems, network sockets) and compare: what does each hide, and at what cost in performance or complexity?

### Chapter Summary

A **transaction** is a unit of work the database treats as atomic, consistent, isolated, and durable — **ACID**. Atomicity ensures all-or-nothing. Consistency ensures integrity constraints are never violated across transaction boundaries. Isolation ensures concurrent transactions produce results equivalent to some serial order. Durability ensures committed transactions survive crashes.

The **lost update** , **non-repeatable read** , **phantom read** , and **write skew** anomalies arise when the database does not enforce isolation. **Serial** execution prevents all of them but has no concurrency. **Serializable** execution preserves concurrency while delivering a result equivalent to some serial order.

Two families of mechanism deliver isolation: **lock-based** (Chapter 15) and **multi-version** (Chapter 16). Both sit atop **WAL** (Chapter 17), which delivers atomicity and durability.

Thread C's banking scenario — two 800 USD withdrawals from a 1000 USD balance — will serve as the running example through the next three chapters, showing how each mechanism prevents the anomalies we cataloged here. Chapter 14 starts by mapping SQL's declared isolation levels to the anomalies they prevent.

---

## Chapter 14: Isolation Levels

The SQL standard defines four isolation levels — READ UNCOMMITTED, READ COMMITTED, REPEATABLE READ, and SERIALIZABLE — and every major DBMS implements some subset with some interpretation. The levels are a contract: each level permits a specific set of anomalies and forbids the rest. An application chooses the level that suits its correctness requirement, and the database chooses the mechanism (locking or MVCC) that delivers the contract.

This is where the ACID abstraction gets operational. "Serializable" sounds absolute; real systems charge a real performance tax for it, and the non-serializable levels exist because many applications can tolerate specific anomalies in exchange for throughput. Picking the wrong level silently produces wrong results — money appears, orders duplicate, reports disagree. Understanding the levels is understanding how to trade correctness for performance intentionally.

This chapter walks through each level, maps it to the anomalies of Chapter 13, shows Thread C's banking scenario at each level, and closes with what real engines actually do. We will see that the standard is a simplification, that "REPEATABLE READ" means different things in PostgreSQL and MySQL, and that "SERIALIZABLE" in production has several non-equivalent implementations.

### 14.1 The ANSI SQL Standard — a Matrix

The 1992 SQL standard defined isolation levels by _which anomalies they permit_.

Level | Dirty Read | Non-Repeatable Read | Phantom Read  
---|---|---|---  
READ UNCOMMITTED | Possible | Possible | Possible  
READ COMMITTED | Prevented | Possible | Possible  
REPEATABLE READ | Prevented | Prevented | Possible  
SERIALIZABLE | Prevented | Prevented | Prevented  
  
Higher levels prevent more anomalies. The SERIALIZABLE row is the strong guarantee of Chapter 13 (equivalent to some serial execution).

#### What this table is missing

The standard was written with lock-based implementations in mind. Its three anomalies — dirty, non-repeatable, phantom — are a subset of what can actually happen. The **lost update** and **write skew** anomalies, which we catalogued in Chapter 13, are either invisible in the standard's framing or require a precise interpretation.

Modern databases describe isolation levels more rigorously, often adding extra levels (SNAPSHOT ISOLATION, READ COMMITTED SNAPSHOT) that do not fit cleanly into the ANSI matrix but match what the engine actually delivers.

#### Common Misconception

"The standard isolation levels mean the same thing in every database." They do not. PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ is actually snapshot isolation (stronger than the standard requires but vulnerable to write skew). MySQL InnoDB's REPEATABLE READ uses next-key locking (also stronger on ranges than the standard requires). SQL Server's REPEATABLE READ is the most faithful to the standard. Writing portable code requires knowing your engine.

### 14.2 READ UNCOMMITTED — Why It Exists, Why You Should Not Use It

#### Definition: READ UNCOMMITTED

The weakest level. Transactions may read _uncommitted_ data written by other transactions. Dirty reads are allowed.

Imagine T1 writes `balance = 500` and T2 reads `balance` — getting 500 even before T1 commits. If T1 later aborts, T2's read was of data that never officially existed. T2 may have acted on phantom data.

#### When is this ever okay?

In a few niche use cases. A dashboard showing "approximate" statistics. A log viewer. A debugger inspecting state. Anywhere the consequences of reading uncommitted data are zero because no one acts on it.

For almost everything else, READ UNCOMMITTED is a footgun. Most databases offer it primarily because the standard requires it. PostgreSQL and SQL Server implement it; MySQL does (with caveats). Some engines (Oracle, Spanner) don't implement it at all — reads are always of committed data in those systems.

#### Cost

Near-zero overhead on the read side. No locks, no version chains, no waits. If you really want throughput at any correctness cost, here it is.

#### Thread C at READ UNCOMMITTED

Session A begins withdrawal, writes `balance = 200`. Session B reads `balance` — gets 200. B decides "insufficient for 800 USD, reject." A aborts. The true balance was 1000 the whole time. B rejected a legitimate withdrawal because of phantom data.

Or worse: B reads 200, decides it can withdraw 100 USD, writes `balance = 100`. A commits, writing 200. Now the account is at 200 (A's successful commit), and B thinks it has withdrawn 100 USD from a 200 balance to leave 100 — but the engine committed B's write of 100, so actually the balance is 100, even though the correct interleaving should give 100 or 200 depending on order. Data is corrupt.

No one runs a real bank at READ UNCOMMITTED.

### 14.3 READ COMMITTED — The Workhorse

#### Definition: READ COMMITTED

Each SELECT statement sees only committed data. Within a transaction, two SELECTs of the same row may see different values if another transaction committed between them. No dirty reads; non-repeatable reads are allowed.

This is the default level in PostgreSQL, Oracle, and SQL Server. In MySQL InnoDB it is an option; the MySQL default is REPEATABLE READ.

#### Implementation (lock-based)

Acquire a short-duration **shared lock** to read a row; release it immediately after reading. Acquire an **exclusive lock** to write; hold until commit. Dirty reads are impossible because you cannot read a row held by another transaction's exclusive lock (you wait). Non-repeatable reads are allowed because the shared lock is released before the transaction ends.

#### Implementation (MVCC)

Each statement gets a snapshot at its start. Reads see all versions committed as of that statement. Successive statements in the same transaction see successively newer snapshots. Writes require the writer to see a version no one else has modified since; otherwise the writer blocks or aborts.

> ### Same Contract, Different Engine: Locking vs. MVCC at READ COMMITTED
> 
> Both implementations deliver the **same observable guarantee** — no dirty reads, non-repeatable reads allowed — but through completely different mechanisms and operational profiles:
> 
> | Lock-based READ COMMITTED | MVCC READ COMMITTED  
> ---|---|---  
> **How reads work** | Acquire short shared lock, release immediately | Read from statement-start snapshot; no lock at all  
> **How writes work** | Acquire exclusive lock; hold until commit | Write new version; conflict detected at commit  
> **Reader blocks writer?** | Briefly — shared lock held only during the read, then released; writer waits only for this short window | No — readers never block writers  
> **Writer blocks reader?** | Yes — reader must wait for the exclusive lock to release | No — reader sees pre-write snapshot immediately  
> **Dirty reads** | Impossible (reader waits for lock) | Impossible (snapshot only contains committed data)  
> **Non-repeatable reads** | Possible (short-duration shared lock released) | Possible (next statement gets a newer snapshot)  
> **Typical engines** | SQL Server default (without RCSI), DB2 | PostgreSQL, Oracle, MySQL (RC mode)  
>   
> The key insight: the **contract** (what anomalies are permitted) is defined by the isolation level. The **mechanism** (locks vs. version chains) is an implementation choice that doesn't change the contract but does change concurrency behavior. Under high read concurrency, MVCC dramatically reduces lock contention — readers never block writers and vice versa. Under high write contention, MVCC may abort and retry transactions rather than blocking; lock-based approaches block instead. Neither is uniformly better; the right choice depends on the workload's read/write ratio and contention pattern.

#### Thread C at READ COMMITTED
    
    
    A: BEGIN
    B: BEGIN
    A: SELECT balance → 1000
    B: SELECT balance → 1000
    A: UPDATE balance = 200 (acquires exclusive lock)
    B: UPDATE balance = balance - 800 → BLOCKS waiting for A's lock
    A: COMMIT (releases lock)
    B: UPDATE proceeds. B reads current balance = 200, sets it to 200 - 800 = -600. COMMIT.
    Final balance: -600
    

Read committed prevents dirty reads but does not prevent lost updates. B's UPDATE eventually ran against the post-A balance, which is correct in a sense — but the application's _decision_ to proceed with the withdrawal was made based on B's earlier SELECT of 1000. The result is a balance of -600 USD, which is wrong.

#### The SELECT FOR UPDATE escape hatch

To fix this at READ COMMITTED, the application can tell the database to acquire a stronger lock on the SELECT:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT balance FROM Accounts WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;   -- exclusive lock
    -- now the row is locked; no other tx can read-for-update or update it
    -- application checks, decides
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 800 WHERE id = 1;
    COMMIT;
    

With `SELECT FOR UPDATE`, B would _block_ on its own SELECT, not just on the UPDATE. B's SELECT would see the post-A balance of 200, correctly reject the withdrawal, and commit without damage.

#### The read-modify-write pattern

`SELECT FOR UPDATE` is the standard way to implement "check and then write" under READ COMMITTED. It manually upgrades the read to a write lock on that row. It is a performance cost — more lock contention — but it is correct, explicit, and widely used.

#### Common Misconception

"READ COMMITTED prevents anomalies." It prevents _dirty_ reads. It does not prevent lost updates, non-repeatable reads, phantom reads, or write skew. If your application assumes a SELECT's result is still valid after a subsequent UPDATE in the same transaction, you need SELECT FOR UPDATE or a higher isolation level.

### 14.4 REPEATABLE READ — Snapshots and Dragons

#### Definition: REPEATABLE READ (standard)

Within a transaction, any row read once will always return the same value on subsequent reads. Non-repeatable reads are prevented. Phantoms — new rows matching a range — are still allowed.

#### Implementation (lock-based, faithful standard)

Acquire a shared lock on each row read; **hold** it until commit. Other transactions cannot modify rows you have read. But the lock is on specific _rows_ , not on ranges, so new rows matching a range predicate can still appear — phantoms.

#### Implementation (MVCC / snapshot isolation)

Every read in the transaction sees a snapshot taken at transaction start. All rows, consistent, throughout the transaction. Phantoms are _also_ prevented because new rows inserted by other transactions after your snapshot are invisible.

This MVCC-based REPEATABLE READ is usually called **snapshot isolation** (SI). It's _stronger_ than the SQL standard's REPEATABLE READ — SI also prevents phantoms. But SI is _weaker_ than serializable — it does not prevent write skew.

PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ is snapshot isolation. Oracle's default (kind of — Oracle calls it "READ COMMITTED" but per-statement; "SERIALIZABLE" in Oracle is MVCC snapshot isolation). MySQL InnoDB's REPEATABLE READ is snapshot for reads plus next-key locking for writes.

#### Thread C at REPEATABLE READ (with snapshot isolation)
    
    
    A: BEGIN (snapshot s_A)
    B: BEGIN (snapshot s_B)
    A: SELECT balance → 1000 (from snapshot s_A)
    B: SELECT balance → 1000 (from snapshot s_B)
    A: UPDATE balance = 200 (new version tagged A)
    B: UPDATE balance = balance - 800 (uses balance=1000 from s_B)
    B tries to commit → DETECTS write-write conflict (A already modified this row)
    B aborts. Application retries.
    A: COMMIT succeeds.
    B: (retry) BEGIN (snapshot s_B2) → sees 200 → insufficient funds → correctly rejects.
    

Snapshot isolation catches the lost update by aborting the second transaction. The application must retry. The final balance is correct: 200 after A's withdrawal; B's second attempt is correctly rejected.

This is a fundamentally different correctness guarantee: **optimistic concurrency**. Rather than blocking (locks), SI lets both transactions proceed on private snapshots, then aborts conflicts at commit.

#### Write skew in SI

SI is not serializable. It cannot detect **write skew** : two transactions read overlapping rows and write _disjoint_ rows. The write-write conflict check does not fire because neither tx modifies the other's written rows.

Classic on-call example:
    
    
    T1: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM oncall WHERE on_duty = TRUE; → 2
    T1: UPDATE oncall SET on_duty = FALSE WHERE doctor = 'Alice';  -- because 2 > 0
    T2: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM oncall WHERE on_duty = TRUE; → 2 (Alice's update not yet visible)
    T2: UPDATE oncall SET on_duty = FALSE WHERE doctor = 'Bob';    -- because 2 > 0
    Both commit. Now on_duty count = 0. Invariant violated.
    

Neither T1 nor T2 modified a row the other modified. The conflict check finds nothing. Both commit. Invariant breaks.

This is a real, not hypothetical, class of bug. The `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` pattern does not fix it unless you know exactly which rows to lock in advance.

#### Common Misconception

"Snapshot isolation means serializable." It doesn't. Snapshot isolation prevents four of the five anomalies (dirty, non-repeatable, phantom, lost update) but not write skew. For the remaining 5%, you need true serializability.

### 14.5 SERIALIZABLE — The Gold Standard

#### Definition: SERIALIZABLE

The execution is equivalent to some serial ordering of the transactions. All anomalies are prevented, including write skew.

#### Implementation A: Strict two-phase locking (2PL)

Every row a transaction reads or writes is locked, and locks are held until commit. Ranges read are protected by **predicate locks** (rare — see Chapter 15 for why they are expensive to implement) or **next-key locks** (common) to prevent phantoms. This is pessimistic; contention grows quickly.

> ### Definition: Next-Key Lock
> 
> A **next-key lock** on B-tree index entry `k` is the combination of two parts:
> 
>   1. A record lock on the row with key `k`.
>   2. A gap lock on the open interval (`prev_key`, `k`) — the empty space between `k` and the previous existing index entry.
> 

> 
> In plain language, a next-key lock locks a row plus the "slot" immediately before it. If a transaction reads all rows with `balance > 500`, the engine takes next-key locks on every matching index entry _and_ on the gaps between them. A concurrent `INSERT` of a new row with `balance = 600` would need to place the new entry in one of those gaps — but the gap is locked, so the insert blocks until the reader commits. This is how InnoDB prevents phantoms without implementing full predicate locking.
> 
> Next-key locks work because B-tree indexes impose a total order on keys: the "gap before `k`" is a well-defined interval, unlike an arbitrary predicate ("all salaries > X AND department = Y") which would require scanning all held locks on every insert. The cost comparison that matters is **per insert** :
> 
>   * **Predicate locks** : each insert must evaluate _every_ held predicate against the new row to decide conflict — **O(n) per insert** where n is the number of held predicate locks in the system.
>   * **Next-key locks** : each insert performs a B-tree probe to check whether it falls into a locked gap — **O(log N) per insert** where N is the index size, independent of how many locks are held.
> 

> 
> A range scan that takes k next-key locks pays O(k log N) total at acquisition time, but the per-insert cost for concurrent writers remains O(log N). That decoupling — "acquisition scales with the reader, conflict-check scales with the index" — is what makes next-key locking viable in production.
> 
> The tradeoff: next-key locks are coarser than strictly necessary (they lock the whole gap, not just the subrange the predicate would actually cover), so concurrency suffers on heavily contended ranges.

Databases using 2PL for SERIALIZABLE: older SQL Server (before snapshot was default); some configurations of DB2.

#### Implementation B: Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI)

Extend snapshot isolation with a check for "dangerous structures" in the conflict graph. If the engine detects that two concurrent transactions could have caused write skew, it aborts one. No locks acquired for reads; only conflict detection at commit.

Databases using SSI: PostgreSQL (since 9.1), MySQL InnoDB (via explicit lock), CockroachDB.

#### Thread C at SERIALIZABLE

Identical to REPEATABLE READ for this example — snapshot isolation already catches the lost update. But for the on-call scenario, SSI detects the dangerous read-write pattern and aborts one of the two transactions. Write skew prevented.

#### Cost of SERIALIZABLE

* 2PL: many locks, possible deadlocks, reduced concurrency.
* SSI: extra bookkeeping per read, occasional aborts, higher retry rate on high-contention workloads.

Both implementations can see 2–10× throughput drops under high concurrency versus READ COMMITTED. For low-contention, single-key workloads, the cost is usually minimal.

#### When to actually use it

* Financial transactions where write skew would be catastrophic.
* Inventory systems where "we have enough stock" is a cross-row invariant.
* Unique constraint enforcement across tables.

Most applications default to READ COMMITTED + SELECT FOR UPDATE and use SERIALIZABLE for the few transactions that really need the stronger guarantee. Very few applications run globally at SERIALIZABLE.

### 14.6 The Per-Engine Reality

#### PostgreSQL

* READ UNCOMMITTED: maps to READ COMMITTED (no true dirty reads in Postgres).
* READ COMMITTED (default): snapshot per statement.
* REPEATABLE READ: snapshot isolation.
* SERIALIZABLE: SSI.

Postgres's snapshot-based implementation means _reads never block writes and writes never block reads_ for the common case. The cost is visible: long-running transactions prevent VACUUM from reclaiming dead rows, and some workloads see catastrophic bloat.

#### MySQL InnoDB

* READ UNCOMMITTED: true dirty reads.
* READ COMMITTED: consistent reads see snapshot at each SELECT.
* REPEATABLE READ (default): snapshot at first read, next-key locking for phantoms on writes.
* SERIALIZABLE: treats all SELECTs as SELECT FOR SHARE.

InnoDB's REPEATABLE READ is stronger than the ANSI standard on ranges (next-key locks prevent phantoms for inserts) but does not guarantee full serializability — write skew is still possible at RR. Serializable requires explicit SERIALIZABLE and is noticeably slower.

#### SQL Server

* READ COMMITTED (default, locking-based).
* READ COMMITTED SNAPSHOT (option, MVCC-based like Postgres's READ COMMITTED).
* REPEATABLE READ (locking-based, ANSI-faithful: non-repeatable prevented, phantoms possible).
* SNAPSHOT (MVCC, like Postgres's REPEATABLE READ).
* SERIALIZABLE (full locking with range locks; expensive).

SQL Server supports _both_ lock-based and MVCC-based for READ COMMITTED and REPEATABLE READ, depending on database settings. A migration between them can silently change behavior.

#### Oracle

* READ COMMITTED (default, MVCC-based, per-statement snapshots).
* SERIALIZABLE (MVCC snapshot isolation — _not_ true serializable; write skew is possible).
* READ ONLY (no writes allowed, consistent-read of a snapshot).

Oracle calls MVCC snapshot isolation "SERIALIZABLE," which is widely understood to be non-standard and occasionally confusing. No true serializable guarantee is built in; application-level locking is required for write-skew-free operation.

#### SQLite

* DEFERRED / IMMEDIATE / EXCLUSIVE transactions — really a coarse-grained locking scheme.
* SQLite is effectively serializable by construction (one writer at a time in the shared-memory journal; WAL mode allows concurrent readers).

#### Summary of defaults

Engine | Default Level | Implementation | Verified as of  
---|---|---|---  
PostgreSQL | READ COMMITTED | per-statement MVCC snapshot | 16 (2026)  
MySQL InnoDB | REPEATABLE READ | MVCC + next-key locks | 8.4 (2026)  
MariaDB | REPEATABLE READ | MVCC + next-key locks | 11.4 (2026)  
Oracle | READ COMMITTED | per-statement MVCC snapshot | 23c (2026)  
SQL Server | READ COMMITTED | locking (RCSI optional) | 2025 GA  
SQLite | SERIALIZABLE | coarse-grained locking | 3.45 (2026)  
CockroachDB | SERIALIZABLE | SSI on top of RocksDB | 24.1 (2026)  
  
**Version note.** Isolation-level defaults and the exact semantics of each level have changed historically. PostgreSQL's SSI arrived in 9.1 (2011); SQL Server's read-committed-snapshot isolation (RCSI) was introduced in 2005 but remains opt-in; MySQL's default was SERIALIZABLE pre-3.23 before being relaxed to REPEATABLE READ. **Always verify your engine's version-specific documentation** rather than relying on any textbook's table — this one included.

The defaults matter because most applications never change them. If you inherit a system, check the default first.

### 14.7 Thread C End-to-End Trace

Let's now run Thread C against each level concretely.

#### Setup
    
    
    CREATE TABLE Accounts (id INT PRIMARY KEY, balance INT);
    INSERT INTO Accounts VALUES (1, 1000);
    

Session A and Session B each attempt:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT balance FROM Accounts WHERE id = 1;       -- statement 1
    -- application checks balance >= 800
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 800 WHERE id = 1;  -- statement 2
    COMMIT;
    

#### READ UNCOMMITTED (hypothetical in PostgreSQL, real in MySQL)

Both see 1000 in the SELECT even after the other's UPDATE. Lost update or double-withdrawal both possible. Incorrect.

#### READ COMMITTED

Depending on interleaving:

* If one finishes UPDATE-COMMIT before the other reads, the other sees 200 and correctly rejects.
* If both read 1000 first and then both UPDATE, the second UPDATE blocks (locking implementations) until the first commits, then decrements from 200 to -600.

The row-level write lock serializes writes but does not protect against read-modify-write races. The application's logic is at fault; the database did what was asked.

Fix: add `SELECT FOR UPDATE` on statement 1. Now the second transaction blocks on the SELECT and, after the first commits, sees 200.

#### REPEATABLE READ / SNAPSHOT ISOLATION (PostgreSQL)

Both see 1000 in the SELECT (snapshots at transaction start). First UPDATE writes; commits. Second UPDATE is a snapshot-isolation conflict: the row was modified after the second transaction's snapshot. PostgreSQL raises `could not serialize access due to concurrent update`. The second transaction aborts; application retries.

On retry, the second transaction's snapshot is newer; SELECT sees 200; application rejects.

This is the correct behavior _without_ requiring SELECT FOR UPDATE. Snapshot isolation converts lost updates into retryable errors.

#### SERIALIZABLE (SSI in PostgreSQL)

Same as REPEATABLE READ for this narrow case. For write-skew scenarios, SSI additionally aborts conflicting transactions.

#### Conclusion

For Thread C's simple lost-update problem, REPEATABLE READ on PostgreSQL is sufficient. READ COMMITTED is sufficient _if_ the application adds SELECT FOR UPDATE. SERIALIZABLE is overkill here but essential for write skew in other scenarios.

### 14.8 Choosing an Isolation Level in Practice

#### The decision tree

  1. **Does this transaction only read committed data and never act on a prior read?** READ COMMITTED is fine.
  2. **Does it read a value, then update based on the read?** Either (a) use READ COMMITTED with SELECT FOR UPDATE, or (b) use REPEATABLE READ / SNAPSHOT ISOLATION and retry on conflict.
  3. **Does it enforce a cross-row invariant (e.g., "at least one doctor on call")?** SERIALIZABLE.
  4. **Is this a long-running report that must see a consistent snapshot?** REPEATABLE READ or SNAPSHOT.
  5. **Is this a financial transaction?** SERIALIZABLE, ideally. If performance forbids, extensive application-level locking with audit trails.

#### Retrying aborted transactions

Any optimistic scheme requires the application to retry on conflict. Real frameworks:
    
    
    # Preconditions:
    #   1. All work inside the `with` block must be idempotent OR have no
    #      side effects outside the database transaction.
    #   2. The database transaction must roll back cleanly on
    #      SerializationError — verify your driver does this automatically.
    # If either is false, do NOT use this retry pattern.
    
    import random, time
    
    for attempt in range(5):
        try:
            with db.transaction(isolation='SERIALIZABLE'):
                # ... work ...
            break
        except SerializationError:
            time.sleep(random.uniform(0.005, 0.05) * (2 ** attempt))  # jittered backoff
            continue
    else:
        raise RetryExhausted("too many retries")
    

> **ORM exception mapping for SerializationError (SQLSTATE 40001).** Each ORM surfaces `SQLSTATE 40001` under a different class: **SQLAlchemy** raises `sqlalchemy.exc.OperationalError` with `pgcode == '40001'` (check `e.orig.pgcode`); **Django** raises `django.db.utils.OperationalError` (or wraps it in `TransactionManagementError` if caught inside `@transaction.atomic`). **psycopg2** directly raises `psycopg2.errors.SerializationFailure`. Look up the exception class your ORM or driver uses before writing the retry handler — catching the wrong exception class will silently swallow non-serialization errors or miss the ones you intend to catch.

> **Idempotency is a precondition, not a suggestion.** The transaction body must be safe to re-execute. Common violations:
> 
>   * **External side effects inside the transaction block** : sending an email, calling a payment gateway, pushing to a message queue. These effects happen _before_ the transaction commits, so they are not rolled back when the transaction aborts — but the retry will trigger them again. Result: duplicate emails, double charges. **Move all external side effects outside the retry loop** , or use the transactional outbox pattern (Chapter 22).
> 
>   * **Non-deterministic inputs read outside the transaction** : reading `now()` in Python and passing it as a parameter means each retry sees a slightly later timestamp. Sometimes acceptable, sometimes silently wrong.
> 
>   * **Application-level state mutation** : e.g., appending to a Python list inside the block. On retry, the list grows again. Reset any mutable state at the top of each attempt.
> 
> 

> 
> Retry storms can form if 100 clients all retry simultaneously. **Exponential backoff with jitter** (shown above) is mandatory for production code — without it, the synchronization of retries keeps contention high and can collapse throughput.

#### Mixed levels in one application

Most real applications use several levels. Reports at REPEATABLE READ (consistent snapshot). OLTP at READ COMMITTED (throughput). Financial at SERIALIZABLE. The database API typically allows per-connection or per-transaction control:
    
    
    SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
    BEGIN;
    ...
    COMMIT;
    

#### Common Misconception

"Use the highest isolation level to be safe." The performance penalty at SERIALIZABLE can be 2–10× and the retry rate on high-contention workloads can be 10–50% of all commits. Always starting at SERIALIZABLE is wasteful. Intentional, per-transaction choice is the discipline.

### 14.9 Thread A Check-In

Back at the university, the enrollment workflow:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT seats_remaining FROM Courses WHERE id = 42;
    -- app checks > 0
    UPDATE Courses SET seats_remaining = seats_remaining - 1 WHERE id = 42;
    INSERT INTO Enrollments(student_id, course_id) VALUES (?, 42);
    COMMIT;
    

At READ COMMITTED without SELECT FOR UPDATE: seats can go negative. Two students read "1 seat left," both decrement, seat count becomes -1.

At READ COMMITTED with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`: the second student blocks on the SELECT until the first commits. Then reads 0, correctly rejects. This is the classic fix.

At REPEATABLE READ (SI): the second student reads 1, tries UPDATE, gets serialization error, retries. On retry, sees 0, rejects.

At SERIALIZABLE: identical to REPEATABLE READ for this case; write skew doesn't apply here (single-row invariant, not cross-row).

The right choice for the university system: READ COMMITTED + SELECT FOR UPDATE if you want predictable throughput; REPEATABLE READ with retry if you want cleaner application code and can tolerate occasional retries. Both are correct.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Higher isolation levels trade throughput for correctness guarantees.** Each step from READ COMMITTED toward SERIALIZABLE prevents more anomalies but adds lock contention or abort retries; production benchmarks show 2–10× throughput drops when running globally at SERIALIZABLE versus READ COMMITTED.
* **Snapshot isolation trades blocking for retry storms.** Readers and writers never block each other under SI, but two concurrent writers to the same row both proceed until commit-time conflict detection aborts one — under high contention the retry rate can swallow the throughput gain.
* **`SELECT FOR UPDATE` trades concurrency for predictable serialization.** Upgrading the read to a write lock at SELECT time eliminates the lost-update race without raising the isolation level, but every concurrent reader on that row now blocks instead of running optimistically.
* **Snapshot isolation prevents four anomalies but trades away protection against write skew.** SI catches dirty/non-repeatable/phantom/lost-update via per-row conflict detection, but cross-row invariants (the on-call doctor example) require SSI or explicit locking — and few developers know to ask for them.
* **Application-level retry trades engine-side simplicity for application complexity.** MVCC retry on `SerializationFailure` keeps the engine fast and lock-free, but the application now needs idempotent transaction bodies and exponential-backoff jitter — failures of either preconditions silently corrupt data.
* **Per-engine semantics trade portability for engine-specific optimization.** PostgreSQL's REPEATABLE READ is SI; MySQL InnoDB's is SI plus next-key locks; Oracle calls SI "SERIALIZABLE." Each choice is sensible in isolation, but code that relies on one definition behaves differently when ported.

### 14.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State in one sentence each: dirty read, non-repeatable read, phantom read, lost update, write skew.
  2. Fill in the ANSI matrix: for each of the four levels, which of the first three anomalies does it allow?
  3. Why does "SERIALIZABLE" in Oracle not mean the same thing as "SERIALIZABLE" in PostgreSQL?
  4. What is snapshot isolation? Is it a named SQL level?
  5. Describe what SELECT FOR UPDATE does and when an application needs it.
  6. Why does snapshot isolation fail to prevent write skew?

#### Application Problems

  7. Your application is a ticket-booking system. Each seat can be booked by at most one customer. Write the transactional code for booking a seat (a) under READ COMMITTED + SELECT FOR UPDATE, and (b) under REPEATABLE READ with retry. Which is more efficient under low contention? High contention?

Hint

For the efficiency question, think in terms of "wasted work per success." Under low contention, optimistic retry rarely retries — almost all attempts succeed. Under high contention, what is the expected number of retries before a transaction wins, and how does that compare to time spent blocked on a lock?

  8. A reporting query on PostgreSQL produces inconsistent totals between different queries in the same transaction. Diagnose: what isolation level is it running at? What should it run at?

  9. Two concurrent transactions both execute:
         
         UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = 1;
         

Does this UPDATE need any isolation-level protection to be correct? What about under READ UNCOMMITTED?

  10. Write a transaction that moves 50 USD from account A to account B. Explain which isolation level is sufficient and why.

  11. The classic "on-call doctor" write-skew example. Write the two conflicting transactions in SQL, run them at REPEATABLE READ, and explain why they both succeed. Rewrite using SELECT FOR UPDATE to prevent the anomaly without upgrading to SERIALIZABLE.

Hint

SI's write-write conflict check fires only when two transactions write the _same_ row. Note that Alice's update and Bob's update touch disjoint rows. To force serialization, the SELECT FOR UPDATE must lock the rows that the _other_ transaction will need to read for its decision — what set of rows is that?

  12. An ORM generates the following sequence for a checkout: 
         
         SELECT * FROM Cart WHERE user_id = 42;
         -- ... application logic ...
         UPDATE Cart SET checked_out = true WHERE user_id = 42;
         

Under READ COMMITTED, what race condition could occur if a user double-clicks "checkout"? Propose a fix.

Hint

Two near-simultaneous sessions both read the cart with `checked_out = false`, both decide to charge, both run the UPDATE — what gets charged twice? The fix should make sure only one of the two SELECTs proceeds; you have several options (FOR UPDATE, conditional UPDATE, idempotency token).

  13. A banking application uses READ COMMITTED. The audit team reports occasional "balance mismatches" where the sum of all account balances changes between two back-to-back SELECT SUM() queries in a reconciliation script. Why does this happen? What is the correct isolation level for the reconciliation?

Hint

At READ COMMITTED, each statement gets a fresh snapshot. Imagine a transfer transaction commits between the script's first and second SUM(). What does the report need: a single snapshot for the entire script, or fresh data per statement? §14.4 names the level that gives one consistent snapshot for the whole transaction.

#### Think Deeper

  14. Snapshot isolation lets reads never block writes and writes never block reads. But the cost is "aborts on conflict." Under what workload pattern does snapshot isolation outperform 2PL? Under what pattern does 2PL win?

Hint

Frame the comparison as "wait time vs wasted work." When conflicts are rare, optimistic SI almost never aborts — every transaction's work counts. When conflicts are frequent, what fraction of SI transactions get aborted, and how much CPU does that throw away? 2PL serializes them through a queue rather than retrying.

  15. Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI) detects dangerous structures at commit time and aborts one transaction. Sketch why this is sufficient (no false negatives) and why it produces false positives (unnecessary aborts). How do engines tune the tradeoff?

  16. MySQL's default isolation level is REPEATABLE READ; PostgreSQL's is READ COMMITTED. Each choice reflects a philosophy. Name one workload style that fits each default and identify applications where switching the default would be catastrophic.

  17. A long-running analytical transaction at REPEATABLE READ holds a snapshot open for hours. In PostgreSQL, this prevents VACUUM. Explain the cascade of performance problems this causes and propose two mitigations.

  18. The SQL standard's isolation levels are defined by "anomalies permitted." Adya (2000) argues this is inadequate because the definitions are phenomenological, not operational. Describe a scenario where two engines both claim "SERIALIZABLE" but produce different results, and argue why this isn't technically a bug.

Hint

"Equivalent to _some_ serial order" leaves freedom: two engines can pick different serial orders for the same concurrent input. Construct two transactions whose outcomes differ depending on which one "logically went first," then note that both serial orders are valid serializations.

  19. Consider a read-only transaction under SERIALIZABLE. How much conflict detection does the engine still need to do? Why can't we always assume read-only transactions are free?

  20. Thread C's bank scenario at READ COMMITTED + SELECT FOR UPDATE: trace in detail, including which lock types are acquired and when they are released. Then explain why this is equivalent (for this scenario) to REPEATABLE READ in PostgreSQL.

### Chapter Summary

**Isolation levels** are contracts: each level permits specific anomalies. **READ UNCOMMITTED** permits dirty reads and is almost never what you want. **READ COMMITTED** prevents dirty reads and is the default in most engines; non-repeatable reads, phantoms, and lost updates are possible without `SELECT FOR UPDATE`. **REPEATABLE READ** as ANSI defines it prevents non-repeatable reads; as most MVCC engines implement it, it is actually **snapshot isolation** , stronger on phantoms, weaker than serializable on write skew. **SERIALIZABLE** prevents all anomalies, either through strict 2PL or through Serializable Snapshot Isolation.

Each real engine interprets the standard differently. **PostgreSQL** defaults to READ COMMITTED and offers SSI. **MySQL InnoDB** defaults to REPEATABLE READ with next-key locking. **SQL Server** offers both lock-based and MVCC variants per setting. **Oracle** calls its snapshot isolation "SERIALIZABLE" without preventing write skew.

Thread C's banking scenario is correctly handled by (a) READ COMMITTED + `SELECT FOR UPDATE`, or (b) REPEATABLE READ with application-level retry, or (c) SERIALIZABLE. The right choice depends on contention, latency, and whether cross-row invariants matter.

Chapter 15 opens the lock-based implementation: how does 2PL actually acquire, hold, release, and detect deadlocks on locks? Chapter 16 opens MVCC: how does an engine version-stamp rows and let readers and writers not block each other?

---

## Chapter 15: Lock-Based Concurrency Control — Two-Phase Locking

Chapter 14 gave the isolation contracts. Chapter 15 shows one way to implement them: **locks**. Every transaction, before it reads or writes a data item, acquires a lock. Conflicts are resolved by making one transaction wait. When the holding transaction finishes, its locks are released, and waiting transactions proceed.

Locking is the pessimistic family of concurrency control. Its central invariant, **two-phase locking (2PL)** , is both simple to state and surprisingly subtle to get right. This chapter walks through 2PL, its strict variant (SS2PL, the one real systems actually use), the lock-mode lattice, predicate and next-key locks for range protection, deadlock detection and prevention, and the performance-tuning work that deadlock-heavy workloads impose on production teams.

We will see why InnoDB's row-level locking is so much more scalable than MySQL's ancestral table-level locking. We'll see why a "SELECT FOR UPDATE" is the right bullet to load for Thread C's two-user-one-account scenario. And we'll see, finally, why locking alone cannot deliver good throughput for read-heavy OLTP — motivating Chapter 16's MVCC.

### 15.1 Locks — Shared, Exclusive, and Compatibility

#### Definition: Shared lock (S)

A lock held by a transaction that intends to _read_ a data item. Multiple transactions can hold shared locks on the same item simultaneously.

#### Definition: Exclusive lock (X)

A lock held by a transaction that intends to _modify_ a data item. Exclusive locks are incompatible with all other locks: only one transaction can hold an X lock at a time, and no other transaction may hold even an S lock on the same item.

#### Lock compatibility matrix

Holding/Requesting | S (Shared) | X (Exclusive)  
---|---|---  
S | Compatible | Blocked  
X | Blocked | Blocked  
  
When a transaction requests a lock and the matrix shows "Blocked," the request waits in a queue until compatible. Queues are typically FIFO with aging to prevent starvation.

#### What a "lock" actually is

In implementation, a lock is a data structure — usually an entry in an in-memory **lock table** keyed by the data-item identifier. Each entry records: current holders (a list of `(transaction_id, mode)` pairs), queue of waiters, and metadata for deadlock detection. Lock-table operations happen under fine-grained mutexes — the lock table itself is one of the hottest data structures in the DBMS.

#### Granularity

Locks can protect items at various granularities:

* **Row-level** : lock individual rows. High concurrency, high overhead (many locks).
* **Page-level** : lock individual pages. Medium concurrency, less overhead.
* **Table-level** : lock entire tables. Low concurrency, trivial overhead.

Modern engines primarily use row-level locking. InnoDB's row locks are one of its historical selling points over MyISAM's table locks. But row locks do not scale indefinitely — a transaction modifying millions of rows can exhaust the lock table. **Lock escalation** (row → page → table) is an engine-level optimization.

#### Common Misconception

"Row-level locks are always better than table-level locks." Row-level locks cost memory per-row and CPU per-acquire. For a query that modifies 10 million rows, acquiring 10 million row locks is slower than taking one table lock. Engines escalate. The right granularity is workload-dependent.

### 15.2 Two-Phase Locking (2PL)

#### Definition: Two-Phase Locking

A locking protocol in which every transaction acquires all its locks before releasing any. Each transaction proceeds through two phases: a **growing phase** where it only acquires locks, and a **shrinking phase** where it only releases them. Once shrinking begins, no new locks may be acquired.

The protocol is elegant because it implies **serializability** : the order in which transactions enter the shrinking phase is a valid serial order equivalent to the concurrent execution. Formal proof appears in Bernstein et al. 1987.

#### Example
    
    
    T1: BEGIN
    T1: LOCK-X(A)      -- growing
    T1: LOCK-X(B)      -- growing
    T1: READ(A), WRITE(A), READ(B), WRITE(B)
    T1: UNLOCK(A)      -- shrinking begins
    T1: UNLOCK(B)      -- shrinking
    T1: COMMIT
    

Note that T1 cannot LOCK-X any additional item after the first UNLOCK. If the application says "oh, I also need C," that is a protocol violation.

#### Why two-phase implies serializability

Intuition: if T1 releases a lock on item A, and T2 then acquires and writes A, T2 _follows_ T1 in any equivalent serial order. T1 cannot subsequently read T2's writes because T1 is already in shrinking phase — no new locks allowed. The order "release before acquire" creates a consistent dependency graph, and the graph is acyclic by induction.

#### Problem: cascading aborts

Basic 2PL allows releasing locks before commit. If T1 releases its lock on A, T2 reads A, and then T1 aborts, T2 read data that never committed. T2 must also abort. If T3 read from T2, T3 also aborts. A **cascading abort**. Not acceptable.

#### Strict 2PL (S2PL)

#### Definition: Strict Two-Phase Locking

A variant of 2PL in which **exclusive** locks are held until commit or abort. Shared locks may still be released in the shrinking phase.

Eliminates cascading aborts for writes: no one reads uncommitted writes because the writer's X lock is held until commit.

#### Strong Strict 2PL (SS2PL)

#### Definition: Strong Strict Two-Phase Locking

All locks (both S and X) are held until commit or abort. The simplest and strictest variant.

This is what almost all production databases implement when they do locking. The "shrinking phase" collapses to a single point: commit/abort releases everything at once.

#### Thread C at SS2PL
    
    
    A: BEGIN
    B: BEGIN
    A: LOCK-S(row) (for SELECT)    -- A holds S
    A: reads balance = 1000
    B: LOCK-S(row) (for SELECT)    -- B also holds S (compatible)
    B: reads balance = 1000
    A: LOCK-X(row) (for UPDATE)    -- A requests X, but B holds S; A blocks
    B: LOCK-X(row) (for UPDATE)    -- B requests X, but A holds S; B blocks
    -- Deadlock!
    

Both transactions hold shared locks and each waits for the other to release before it can upgrade to exclusive. Neither can proceed. Engines detect this and abort one.

The fix is `SELECT FOR UPDATE`, which requests X directly instead of S:
    
    
    A: BEGIN
    A: LOCK-X(row) via SELECT FOR UPDATE
    A: reads balance = 1000
    B: BEGIN
    B: LOCK-X(row) via SELECT FOR UPDATE → blocks waiting for A
    A: UPDATE; COMMIT (releases X)
    B: SELECT proceeds, reads balance = 200
    B: application correctly rejects; COMMIT.
    

No deadlock, correct result. The intent-based upgrade to X at SELECT time is the standard pattern for write-intention reads.

### 15.3 The Full Lock Mode Lattice

S and X are not the only modes. Real engines use a richer lattice that includes **intention locks** for multi-granularity locking.

#### Intention locks

When a transaction plans to modify a row, it should also indicate to the table-level machinery that _some_ row in the table is being modified. Otherwise, another transaction that wants to lock the whole table must scan every row's lock state.

**Intention-shared (IS)** : "I plan to read some row in this table." **Intention-exclusive (IX)** : "I plan to write some row in this table."

The table-level lock is IS or IX before the row-level locks are S or X. Table-scans that need consistent view of the table request S or X at table level — these are blocked by conflicting IX or IS on other rows.

#### Extended compatibility matrix

| Lock type | IS | IX | S | SIX | X |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IS | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ |
| IX | ✓ | ✓ | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ |
| S | ✓ | ✗ | ✓ | ✗ | ✗ |
| SIX | ✓ | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ |
| X | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ | ✗ |
  
SIX = Shared + Intention Exclusive: read the whole table, write some rows.

This is the lattice used in SQL Server, DB2, and InnoDB. PostgreSQL has a different set but with the same principle.

### 15.4 Deadlocks

Two or more transactions, each waiting for a lock held by another, form a **deadlock**. Without intervention, they wait forever.

#### Detection

The lock manager maintains a **waits-for graph** : a directed graph where an edge Ti→TjT_i $\to T_jTi$→Tj means TiT_iTi is waiting for a lock held by TjT_jTj. A cycle means deadlock.

The DBMS periodically scans the graph for cycles. When a cycle is found, pick a **victim** — usually the transaction with the least work done, or the most recent one — and abort it. Its locks release; the other transactions proceed.

#### Prevention

Alternatively, assign each transaction a timestamp at BEGIN. Use one of two protocols:

* **Wait-Die** : if older transaction waits on younger, allow (wait). If younger waits on older, abort younger (die). Older transactions run to completion; younger retry with same timestamp.
* **Wound-Wait** : if older waits on younger, abort (wound) younger. If younger waits on older, wait.

Both prevent deadlock by construction: no waits-for cycle can form because the allowed waits only go one way on the timestamp.

#### Timeout

A simpler hack: set a timeout on lock waits. If a transaction waits too long (default: 50–60 seconds in MySQL; configurable in Postgres), abort it. Declares "probably deadlock" rather than proving it.

#### What real engines use

* PostgreSQL: deadlock detection via waits-for graph. Default check interval: 1 second. When detected, abort youngest.
* MySQL InnoDB: waits-for graph detection, immediate. `innodb_deadlock_detect` option; when disabled (for throughput), uses timeout only.
* SQL Server: graph-based, very fast. `DEADLOCK_PRIORITY` can tell the engine which transaction to prefer keeping.
* Oracle: graph-based. Deadlock abort is the most common transaction error in high-contention Oracle workloads.

#### Common Misconception

"Deadlocks are bugs." Deadlocks are _expected_ in high-contention locking systems. The engine detects them and aborts one transaction; the application retries. A well-written application handles `SerializationFailure` errors as a routine event, not an exception. Deadlock _rates_ , however, are a red flag: if you see 10% of transactions deadlocking, something structural is wrong (conflicting access order, hot row, missing index forcing lock scans).

### 15.5 Deadlock by Example

Consider two transactions that each transfer money between two accounts, but in opposite directions.
    
    
    T1: transfer from A to B       T2: transfer from B to A
    BEGIN                          BEGIN
    LOCK-X(A)                      LOCK-X(B)
    -- update A                    -- update B
    LOCK-X(B)  ← waits on T2       LOCK-X(A)  ← waits on T1
    

Both transactions hold one lock and wait for another. Deadlock. Engine detects; aborts one.

> **Why deadlock is possible at all: locks are acquired incrementally, not atomically.** SQL does not offer a way to request a set of locks as a single atomic operation. Each `UPDATE` or `SELECT FOR UPDATE` in a transaction acquires its locks one at a time, row by row, as the query executes. Between acquiring lock-1 and lock-2, another transaction can slip in and acquire lock-2 first. This incrementality is _the_ structural reason deadlocks exist. An atomic "lock both A and B" primitive (as in some database research prototypes) would remove deadlock entirely but is impractical because the full set of locks a transaction will need is rarely known up front — it depends on what rows the queries return.

#### The fix: consistent lock order

If every transaction that touches accounts A and B acquires locks in a consistent order (e.g., by primary key ascending), the deadlock cannot form. Both transactions would LOCK-X(A), LOCK-X(B) in that order. The second to arrive waits on both; no cycle.

Many deadlock hotspots in real applications come from inconsistent lock-acquisition orders — sometimes by account ID, sometimes by row order of a SELECT, sometimes by hash-map iteration order. Production codebases adopt conventions (lock by primary key ascending) to avoid them.

#### Insertion-order hotspot

Another common pattern: a table where every insert appends to the end of an auto-increment primary key range. InnoDB's next-key locks can cause deadlocks between concurrent inserters that all converge on the "tail" of the index. Solutions: use UUID primary keys (spread inserts), or use a separate sequence with pre-allocated ranges.

### 15.6 Predicate Locks and Phantoms

#### The phantom problem

SS2PL on rows prevents non-repeatable read for _existing_ rows. But a transaction that ran `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders WHERE status='pending'` and got 5 might rerun the same query and get 6 — because another transaction inserted a new row matching the predicate. The newly-inserted row was not among the rows T1 locked; its lock didn't block the insertion.

#### Definition: Phantom

A row that satisfies a predicate in one read of a transaction but did not exist in the prior read, due to another transaction's insertion.

Full serializability requires blocking inserts of rows matching the predicate. Three mechanisms.

#### Predicate locks

Theoretically beautiful, rarely practical: lock _predicates_ rather than rows. When T1 queries `WHERE status = 'pending' AND priority > 5`, it acquires a lock on the predicate itself; any insert or update must check whether the new row matches.

**Why they are expensive.** Suppose at some moment there are `n` held predicate locks across all active transactions. A new `INSERT` arrives. The engine must evaluate **every** held predicate against the new row to decide whether the insert conflicts — an O(n) cost per insert. Worse, the predicates can be arbitrary SQL expressions (including subqueries, function calls, or even user-defined functions), so there is no index-based shortcut; each check is a full expression evaluation. In a busy OLTP system with hundreds of active reader transactions, every insert becomes hundreds of predicate evaluations. This is why predicate locking exists in exactly one mainstream system — PostgreSQL's SSI — and even there it is implemented as approximate _SIREAD_ locks on pages/rows rather than as true predicate matching (with some false positives as the cost of the approximation).

By contrast, next-key locks (below) reduce the problem to "does the new row's index key fall in a locked gap?" — an O(log n) B-tree probe instead of O(n) predicate evaluations. That is the practical reason next-key locks won.

#### Next-key locks (InnoDB)

A B-tree index stores rows in sorted order. Acquire a lock not only on each row matching the predicate but also on the **gaps between** rows. An insert that would land in a locked gap is blocked.

Example: `SELECT ... WHERE status = 'pending'` on an index on `status`. Locks all "pending" rows _and_ the index range on both sides, so an INSERT ... VALUES('pending', ...) would be blocked when it tries to insert into the locked range.

Next-key locks are the reason InnoDB's REPEATABLE READ is stronger than the standard requires. They prevent phantoms on range-based inserts.

#### Predicate evaluation at scan time

SQL Server uses "range locks" on the index; PostgreSQL's SSI uses a combination of row-level and page-level "sIRead" locks plus commit-time conflict checking.

#### Tradeoff

Next-key locks significantly expand the lock footprint. A single SELECT that scans an index can lock tens of thousands of rows and gaps. Under high insertion load, this is a major contention source. Many engines let you switch between gap locking and non-gap locking (`innodb_locks_unsafe_for_binlog` in older MySQL — note the name "unsafe"; removed in 8.0).

### 15.7 The Performance Cost of Locking

#### Throughput under contention

Consider a workload of 1000 concurrent writers, all updating a single hot row. Under SS2PL:

* One transaction holds X.
* 999 wait.
* First commits in 1 ms (fast transaction).
* Next acquires; commits; and so on.
* Throughput: 1000 TPS. Not 1 million TPS despite 1000 clients.

This is the pessimistic locking reality: concurrency is bounded by the rate of the serial critical section. Hot rows — inventory counters, "last ID" sequences, shared counters — are pessimistic locking's Achilles heel.

#### Mitigations

* **Counter sharding** : split the hot counter into N sub-counters; aggregate on read. Increment any random sub-counter; contention drops by N.
* **Queue and coalesce writes** : let writes enter a queue; one worker applies them in a single transaction periodically. Trades latency for throughput.
* **Optimistic paths** : switch to MVCC or compare-and-swap when conflicts are rare.

#### Lock-free data structures inside the engine

The lock table itself uses lock-free or fine-grained-locking structures. A naive global mutex around the lock table would serialize every lock acquisition, crippling the database. Modern engines use hashed lock tables with per-bucket mutexes, or lock-free hashtables for the read path.

### 15.8 Thread C at SS2PL — Full Trace
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT balance FROM Accounts WHERE id = 1 FOR UPDATE;   -- LOCK-X(row(1))
    -- read committed balance = 1000
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = balance - 800 WHERE id = 1;
    COMMIT;   -- release LOCK-X
    

Session A arrives first: acquires LOCK-X(row 1). Reads 1000. Application sanity-checks. Issues UPDATE (already holds X). Commits. Releases.

Session B arrives next: requests LOCK-X(row 1). Blocks on A's lock. When A commits, B's lock request is granted. B's SELECT re-reads balance = 200. Application checks: 200 < 800, rejects. Commits with no changes.

Correct result: balance = 200. The serial equivalent: A ran to completion, then B ran and correctly rejected. No lost update. No phantom. No write skew (not applicable here).

#### What if the application used `SELECT` without `FOR UPDATE`?

Under SS2PL both transactions would take S on the row for the SELECT. Compatible (both S). Both read 1000. Now both try to UPDATE, each requesting X. But the other holds S. Deadlock.

Engine detects. Aborts one. That one retries, sees 200 (after the other committed), correctly rejects.

The SS2PL deadlock detection converts the race into a correctness-preserving abort. It is not elegant; it costs two attempts and one aborted transaction. SELECT FOR UPDATE avoids the deadlock by requesting the stronger lock upfront.

### 15.9 Lock Escalation and Contention Management

#### Lock escalation

Engines may escalate row locks to page or table locks when a transaction acquires many. SQL Server escalates to table at ~5000 row locks. Reduces lock-table memory but drops concurrency: a table lock blocks _all_ other writers.

Lock escalation is one of the sneakier production issues: a routine analytical update that touches 10% of a table escalates, and the entire OLTP system stalls. Partitioning tables, splitting updates into smaller batches, or switching to MVCC reads-around-writes are standard mitigations.

#### Optimistic concurrency retrofits

Many applications hit locking performance ceilings and add optimistic logic on top:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT balance, version FROM Accounts WHERE id = 1;
    -- application decides
    UPDATE Accounts SET balance = ?, version = version + 1
      WHERE id = 1 AND version = ?;   -- fails if someone else updated
    IF rows_affected = 0: ROLLBACK and retry
    COMMIT;
    

This is **optimistic concurrency via a version column**. The database is still at READ COMMITTED, but the application is implementing its own "compare-and-swap" on the version column. Conflicts are detected at UPDATE time (rows_affected = 0), not at lock acquisition. Under low contention, no locks are held between SELECT and UPDATE; no blocking.

Under high contention, it degrades (lots of retries). But for many real-world OLTP workloads, this pattern outperforms SS2PL + SELECT FOR UPDATE by a significant margin. Application-level optimism, backed by DB-level row-write atomicity, is widely used.

### 15.10 When Locking Is the Right Choice

Despite MVCC's widespread adoption for reads, locks remain essential for:

* **Write-write conflicts** — two concurrent writers to the same row. Even MVCC engines use row-level write locks to serialize these.
* **Foreign-key constraint enforcement** — the parent row must exist; other transactions cannot delete it. Handled by shared row locks on the parent.
* **Index maintenance** — B-tree splits and merges acquire short-lived **latches** (not full locks; they are cheaper) to protect structural modifications.
* **DDL operations** — ALTER TABLE, DROP INDEX take table-level exclusive locks (or in modern engines, "online" versions that use shorter locks with copying).

MVCC does not replace locking; it layers on top. Chapter 16 shows how.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Shared locks trade reader-reader concurrency for the cost of an upgrade race.** Multiple S locks on a row let many readers proceed simultaneously, but two readers that each later want to upgrade to X form a classic deadlock — the reason `SELECT FOR UPDATE` exists.
* **SS2PL trades concurrency for the strongest correctness guarantee available from locking.** Holding all locks until commit prevents cascading aborts and yields strict serializability, but the rate of forward progress is bounded by the slowest holder of any contended lock.
* **Finer lock granularity trades lock-table memory for higher concurrency.** Row-level locks let many writers touch the same table, but a transaction that updates 10 million rows can exhaust the lock table — escalation to page or table locks recovers memory at the cost of blocking unrelated writers.
* **Predicate locks trade O(n) per-insert checks for true serializability against phantoms.** Locking arbitrary predicates is theoretically clean, but every INSERT must evaluate every held predicate; this is why next-key locks (an O(log n) B-tree probe) won in production.
* **Next-key (gap) locking trades over-locking for cheap phantom prevention.** Locking the gap before each index entry blocks would-be phantom inserts in O(log n), but it can lock entire ranges that the predicate barely touches, throttling concurrent inserters into the same key range.
* **Deadlock detection trades occasional aborts for liveness without a timestamp scheme.** Periodically scanning the waits-for graph catches real cycles cheaply, but the application must be prepared to retry; the alternative — Wait-Die / Wound-Wait prevention — avoids deadlock entirely at the cost of more aggressive aborts of "wrong-direction" waits.
* **Pessimistic locking trades hot-row throughput for predictable serialization.** SS2PL with `SELECT FOR UPDATE` makes a hot counter run at exactly "1 / commit-time" TPS, no surprise aborts; switching to optimistic compare-and-swap raises the ceiling but introduces retry loops the application must own.

### 15.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State the growing and shrinking phases of 2PL. Why does the protocol imply serializability?
  2. What is the difference between 2PL, Strict 2PL, and Strong Strict 2PL?
  3. Fill in the basic compatibility matrix for S and X. Explain why two S locks are compatible.
  4. Describe what an intention lock is and why it exists.
  5. Define deadlock. Describe two mechanisms a DBMS can use to handle it.
  6. Explain why `SELECT FOR UPDATE` can prevent a deadlock that a plain SELECT + UPDATE would have caused.
  7. What is a next-key lock? What problem does it solve?

#### Application Problems

  8. Two transactions both execute: 
         
         UPDATE Inventory SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE product_id = 42;
         

Trace the locks acquired under SS2PL. Does this pair deadlock? What if instead they both issued `SELECT stock FROM Inventory WHERE product_id = 42 FOR UPDATE` first?

Hint

A bare UPDATE goes straight to LOCK-X — there is no S-then-X upgrade race. Compare to a plain `SELECT` followed by `UPDATE`: now both transactions hold S simultaneously and each needs X. Trace which version produces the deadlock pattern from §15.2.

  9. Design a banking transfer operation. Two accounts: source and destination. The application calls it with `(from_id, to_id, amount)`. Write the SQL and specify the lock-acquisition order needed to prevent deadlocks between concurrent transfers in opposite directions.

Hint

The deadlock in §15.5 comes from inconsistent lock-acquisition order — T1 locks A then B, T2 locks B then A. The fix is a _total order_ every transaction agrees on, regardless of which direction the transfer flows. Sort by primary key before issuing the locks.

  10. A high-frequency trading system sees frequent deadlocks at SS2PL. Propose three architectural mitigations, ordering them by implementation cost.

  11. `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM Orders WHERE status='pending'` at REPEATABLE READ in InnoDB. What locks does it acquire? How does a concurrent INSERT into Orders behave?

Hint

InnoDB's REPEATABLE READ relies on next-key locking (§15.6) for range-based reads. Trace what gets locked: not just the matching index entries, but also the gaps between them. A concurrent INSERT must place its new row in some gap — what happens if that gap is locked?

  12. Under SS2PL, a long analytical query running for 10 minutes acquires many shared row locks. Describe three different problems this could cause.

  13. An application uses the optimistic pattern with a version column. Describe a scenario where it can produce a lost update if the application doesn't check `rows_affected`.
