  14. For Thread A's enrollment workflow, write the SQL with explicit locking at SS2PL to handle the "30 seats left, 500 students clicking" scenario. Verify no lost seats are possible.

Hint

The race is between read and check — the application reads `seats_remaining`, then decides, then writes. To make the read part of the same exclusive critical section as the write, request the X lock at SELECT time. Then trace what the 31st student sees and how the engine guarantees they see it.

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why does SS2PL guarantee strict serializability (in fact, the stronger "order-preserving serializability" where commit order matches lock order)?

  16. A real lock manager must handle millions of lock requests per second. Describe the structure of a lock table you would design. Where would the hot contention be, and how would you mitigate it?

  17. Consider a workload where writes are rare (1% of transactions) but reads are very frequent (99%). Compare SS2PL and MVCC. How does SS2PL behave? Why is MVCC so much better here?

Hint

Even if writes are 1%, what does an SS2PL X lock do to the 99% of readers that touch the same row at the same time? Then ask: what does MVCC do differently for those readers — do they wait, or do they see an older version?

  18. Design a deadlock detection algorithm that runs in O(V + E) time on the waits-for graph. When should it run: continuously, periodically, or on-demand? What are the tradeoffs?

  19. SS2PL holds all locks to commit. Some theoretical protocols (basic 2PL, strict 2PL) allow earlier release but have correctness caveats. Discuss one real-world situation where releasing locks early (knowingly accepting cascading aborts) would be a sensible engineering choice. Be specific.

  20. Thread C's "two users one account" at SS2PL: identify the minimal change to the schema that would let the engine serialize both transactions without holding row locks. (Hint: think about partition keys or sharding.)

Hint

The contention is structural: one balance row, many writers. What if the balance were _split_ across multiple rows whose sum equals the balance? Then reads aggregate across shards, and writers can pick a random shard. Trace what happens if a withdrawal needs more than one shard's worth.

### Chapter Summary

**Locks** serialize conflicting accesses. **Shared** locks permit concurrent reads; **exclusive** locks are acquired for writes and block all other access. The **two-phase locking** protocol (growing then shrinking) guarantees serializability; **Strong Strict 2PL** , the industry standard, holds all locks until commit to prevent cascading aborts.

**Deadlocks** are expected, not pathological: the engine detects them via a waits-for graph and aborts a victim. Applications must handle `SerializationFailure` and retry. Consistent lock-acquisition order across transactions dramatically reduces deadlock rates.

**Next-key locks** and **range locks** prevent phantoms by locking not only rows but index gaps. Different engines implement this differently; InnoDB's implementation is the source of MySQL's REPEATABLE READ being stronger than the ANSI standard.

**Lock escalation** , **intention locks** , and **lock-table design** all exist to manage the memory and contention cost of fine-grained row-level locking at scale. Despite optimizations, locking plateaus: hot rows bottleneck throughput regardless of hardware.

Thread C at SS2PL works correctly with `SELECT FOR UPDATE`: the second withdrawal blocks on the first's lock, reads the post-commit balance, and correctly rejects. Without `FOR UPDATE`, the two transactions deadlock and one is aborted; after retry it reads correctly.

Chapter 16 introduces **MVCC** : a radically different concurrency model where readers never block writers and vice versa, at the cost of multi-version storage and different conflict patterns.

---

## Chapter 16: Multi-Version Concurrency Control (MVCC)

Chapter 15 left us with a tradeoff we could not escape. Strong Strict 2PL is correct but pessimistic: every read acquires a shared lock, every write an exclusive lock, and concurrent reads of a row being updated block until the writer commits. For read-heavy workloads, this is a catastrophe. Ninety-nine percent of queries are reads; under 2PL, each read that touches a hot row queues behind every concurrent write. Throughput collapses even though there is no actual correctness conflict — two readers do not need to block each other, they just need a consistent view.

MVCC (multi-version concurrency control) rethinks this from scratch. Instead of locks, the database keeps **multiple versions** of each row, each tagged with the transaction that created it. Readers see the version valid as of their start time; writers create new versions without removing the old ones. The key property:

**Readers never block writers. Writers never block readers. Write-write conflicts are handled separately — typically with row-level locks.**

This is the concurrency model of PostgreSQL, Oracle, MySQL InnoDB (for reads), SQL Server's snapshot modes, and every modern distributed database. It is the single most important performance innovation in the transaction-processing history of the last 30 years. And it comes with a new set of problems — **bloat** , **vacuum** , **snapshot retention** , **write skew** — that shape the operational reality of every MVCC database.

This chapter explains the mechanism, traces Thread C through the version chain, discusses the engineering tradeoffs, and closes by showing why "Serializable Snapshot Isolation" (SSI) extends MVCC to true serializability.

### 16.1 The Core Idea — Versions, Not Locks

#### Definition: Row version

A **row version** is one specific value of a row at a point in time, tagged with the transaction that created it (XMIN) and optionally the transaction that deleted or updated it (XMAX).

A row is not a single tuple in MVCC. It is a **version chain** — a linked list of tuples, from oldest to newest.
    
    
    Account id=1:
      version 1: balance=1000, xmin=T1 (creator), xmax=T3 (deleter)
      version 2: balance=200,  xmin=T3, xmax=null   ← current
    

When T3 updated balance from 1000 to 200, T3 did not overwrite the row. T3 created a new version (balance=200, xmin=T3) and marked the old version as deleted by T3 (xmax=T3). The old version is still there, readable by any transaction that started before T3 committed.

#### Snapshot

Each transaction, at BEGIN time (or at each statement time in READ COMMITTED), gets a **snapshot** : a set of transaction IDs that were committed as of that moment. When the transaction reads a row's version chain, it returns the version whose xmin is in the snapshot and whose xmax is either null or _not_ in the snapshot (i.e., "my snapshot saw this created but not deleted").

This is the whole trick. Reads are served from a snapshot; writers never disturb readers; writers create new versions.

#### Thread C at MVCC
    
    
    Account id=1, version 1: balance=1000, xmin=T0, xmax=null
    
    A: BEGIN (snapshot S_A = {T0})
    B: BEGIN (snapshot S_B = {T0})
    
    A: SELECT balance → 1000 (version 1, xmin=T0 ∈ S_A, xmax=null, visible)
    B: SELECT balance → 1000 (same)
    
    A: UPDATE balance = 200
      → creates version 2: balance=200, xmin=T_A, xmax=null
      → updates version 1: xmax = T_A
    A: COMMIT
    
    B: UPDATE balance = balance - 800
      → reads current version from B's snapshot = 1000
      → attempts to write new version; discovers version 1 has xmax=T_A
      → T_A is NOT in B's snapshot but IS committed
      → "write-write conflict" detected
      → B aborts with serialization error
    

B retries: new snapshot sees T_A committed, reads version 2 (200), application rejects. No lost update.

Compare with SS2PL from Chapter 15: there, B's UPDATE _blocked_ on A's lock and then proceeded with the new value. Here, B's UPDATE _aborts_ on conflict detection and the application retries. Different shape; same correctness.

#### Common Misconception

"MVCC keeps two copies of each row." MVCC can keep _many_ copies of each row — as many as there are overlapping transactions that have seen different versions. After all those transactions complete, the old versions can be garbage-collected. Bloat is real (Section 16.5).

### 16.2 How Versions Are Stored

There are two main approaches.

#### In-place plus version chain (PostgreSQL, CockroachDB)

The row's most recent version lives in the heap. Older versions are also in the heap — as extra rows marked with `xmax` pointers. Each version has its own physical location; there is no in-place overwrite.
    
    
    Heap page of Accounts:
      [row 1: balance=1000, xmin=T0, xmax=T_A, next_version_pointer=row 2]
      [row 2: balance=200,  xmin=T_A, xmax=null, next_version_pointer=null]
    

All versions occupy heap space. When none of the live transactions can see a version (it is "dead"), **VACUUM** reclaims the space by marking its slot as free.

Advantages:

* Reads don't need to jump across structures.
* Indexes can point to any version.
* Visibility checks are inline.

Disadvantages:

* Heap bloat: under heavy update load, the heap doubles or triples in size until vacuum catches up.
* Index entries must be updated on update (every secondary index gains a new entry; the old one is dead until vacuumed). Mitigations: heap-only tuples (HOT, PostgreSQL).
* Long-running transactions block vacuum globally.

> **Heap-Only Tuples (HOT) — how PostgreSQL reduces index bloat on updates.** When a row is updated, PostgreSQL normally writes a new version to the heap _and_ adds a new entry to every secondary index. HOT avoids the index-write cost when two conditions both hold:
> 
>   1. **No indexed column is changed.** The UPDATE touches only columns that no index covers (e.g., updating a `bio` text field when the only indexes are on `email` and `created_at`).
>   2. **The same heap page has free space.** PostgreSQL writes the new version on the same physical page as the old one, then stores an intra-page pointer chain: the index continues pointing to the old slot, which redirects to the new one.
> 

> 
> When both conditions hold, a HOT update writes exactly one heap page and zero index pages — dramatically less write amplification on tables with many secondary indexes and updates that avoid indexed columns.
> 
> When either condition fails (the page is full, or an indexed column changes), the update falls back to the normal path: a new heap tuple is written elsewhere, and every secondary index gets a new entry pointing to it. Index entries for the old location remain as dead tuples until vacuum cleans them up.

#### Undo-log-based (Oracle, InnoDB, MySQL)

The heap holds only the most recent version. Older versions are reconstructed from the **undo log** : the transaction's record of reverse-operations.
    
    
    Heap:
      balance=200 (most recent)
    
    Undo log for T_A:
      when applied: balance 200 → 1000 (reverse delta)
    

A reader at an older snapshot starts from the current row and applies undo deltas backward until reaching the version visible at its snapshot.

Advantages:

* No heap bloat from versions (though the undo log grows).
* Current reads (snapshot = now) are cheapest.
* Vacuum problem replaced by undo log rotation.

Disadvantages:

* Older snapshots cost more: multiple undo applications.
* Long-running transactions grow the undo segment (Oracle's "ORA-01555 snapshot too old" is the famous failure mode).
* More complex to implement correctly.

Oracle has used undo-log MVCC since the early 1990s; InnoDB adopted it. PostgreSQL's "in-place versions in heap" has distinct operational characteristics. Both are MVCC; the storage tradeoff differs.

### 16.3 Visibility Rules in Detail

When a transaction T, with snapshot S, reads a row version V:

  1. If V.xmin is _not_ committed (per T's catalog): invisible.
  2. If V.xmin is committed but _not in S_ (committed after T's snapshot): invisible.
  3. If V.xmin is in S (committed and visible to T): a. If V.xmax is null: visible. b. If V.xmax is in S: invisible (someone else already deleted or superseded). c. If V.xmax is not in S: visible.
  4. If V.xmin = T itself and V.xmax = T: invisible (T's own in-progress deletion).
  5. If V.xmin = T and V.xmax = null: visible (T's own insert).

"In the snapshot" encodes "visible to me": the snapshot is a list of committed-at-snapshot-time transaction IDs, with room for own-transaction adjustments.

#### Read Committed vs Repeatable Read

* **READ COMMITTED** : each statement takes a fresh snapshot. Two SELECTs in one transaction can see different versions.
* **REPEATABLE READ / SNAPSHOT** : one snapshot at transaction start, used for all reads.

This is why the same transaction sees different values across statements at READ COMMITTED — the snapshot advances.

#### Common Misconception

"MVCC eliminates all conflicts." MVCC eliminates _read-write_ conflicts: a reader can read while a writer writes, and neither blocks. **Write-write** conflicts still exist and must be resolved by either aborting one writer (optimistic, PostgreSQL RR) or locking (InnoDB). MVCC is complementary to locking, not a replacement for all of it.

### 16.4 Write-Write Conflicts

Two transactions attempt to update the same row under MVCC.

#### InnoDB approach: locks for writes

T1 updates: acquires row-level X lock, writes new version, holds lock until commit. T2 attempts to update the same row: blocks on T1's X lock (classical 2PL on writes). When T1 commits: lock released; T2 reads current version, applies its update.

This is **MVCC for reads, 2PL for writes**. The common pattern in industry. Writes are serialized; reads are concurrent.

#### PostgreSQL REPEATABLE READ approach: first-committer-wins

T1 updates: creates new version (no lock in read-committed sense, but row-level write lock briefly). T2 attempts to update: sees T1's new version; discovers a write conflict at commit time. T2 aborts; must retry.

This is **optimistic concurrency** : concurrent writers proceed without blocking until one commits and forces the other to abort. Throughput wins when conflicts are rare; retry storms happen when conflicts are common.

#### SELECT FOR UPDATE under MVCC

`SELECT FOR UPDATE` still acquires row-level write locks in MVCC engines. It is the bridge between "read a value" and "update based on the value" — the lock ensures no one else can modify the row between the SELECT and a subsequent UPDATE. Useful for read-modify-write at READ COMMITTED when you want pessimistic behavior.

### 16.5 Bloat and Vacuum

The existential problem of PostgreSQL-style MVCC: dead versions accumulate.

#### Definition: Dead tuple

A row version whose xmax is in the past of every live transaction's snapshot. No one can ever see it again. It occupies space until reclaimed.

#### VACUUM

A background process (PostgreSQL's autovacuum) scans tables and reclaims space from dead tuples. Two modes:

* **Regular vacuum** : mark dead slots as free; can be reused by new inserts. Does not shrink table file.
* **Vacuum FULL** : rewrite the entire table, keeping only live tuples. Shrinks the file. Requires exclusive lock; blocks everything.

Autovacuum is tuned to run automatically based on dead-tuple thresholds. Under heavy write workload, autovacuum can fall behind: the table grows faster than vacuum cleans.

#### Quantifying Bloat Rate

How fast does dead space actually accumulate? A back-of-envelope calculation:

* A table receiving **100 UPDATE rows/sec** generates 100 dead tuple versions per second (the old version is immediately dead once the transaction commits under READ COMMITTED).
* If each row is 360 bytes (a typical OLTP row), dead space accrues at 100 × 360 = **36,000 bytes/sec = ~36 KB/sec**.
* Over **1 hour** (3,600 seconds): 36 KB × 3,600 = **~130 MB of dead space**.
* Over a **business day** (8 hours without vacuum): ~**1 GB of dead space per table**.

For a large high-write table (e.g., an `events` table at 10,000 writes/sec with 100-byte rows), dead space reaches ~1 GB in **16 minutes** if vacuum doesn't keep up.

This growth is bounded by `autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit` and the table's dead-tuple threshold (`autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor`, default 20%). A table must be 20% dead before autovacuum wakes up — for a 100 GB table, that means 20 GB of dead space triggers a vacuum. At 36 KB/sec, you reach 20 GB in ~6.5 days. The practical lesson: the defaults work for tables with low-to-moderate update rates; for write-intensive tables (orders, events, sessions), reduce `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor` to 0.01–0.05 so vacuum triggers earlier and more frequently.

#### Long-running transactions block vacuum

A critical problem. Vacuum can only reclaim a version if _every_ live transaction's snapshot cannot see it. A transaction that has been running for hours has an old snapshot; any version deleted after that snapshot started cannot be vacuumed. A 12-hour analytical query can cause table bloat to snowball.

Famous failure: a dashboard that opens a transaction and never closes it. VACUUM stalls. Tables grow unbounded. Disk fills. Database stops.

Mitigations:

* Limit transaction duration (query timeouts, statement_timeout).
* Use READ COMMITTED for long queries (per-statement snapshots advance).
* Avoid long-running transactions; break them into smaller chunks.

#### Oracle/InnoDB equivalent: undo segment

Undo-log MVCC does not have heap bloat but does have undo-log bloat. Old undo entries can be purged only if no transaction's snapshot needs them. Long-running transactions similarly pin undo; when the undo segment overflows its allotted space, the engine throws "snapshot too old" (Oracle) or falls back to sync mode (InnoDB).

#### Tradeoff

Both implementations trade storage for concurrency. MVCC _must_ keep extra versions to be correct; the question is _where_ (heap vs undo log) and _how long_ (until vacuum vs immediate). Every MVCC engine has opinions; every production MVCC database has stories about bloat.

### 16.6 Snapshot Isolation Is Not Serializable — Again

Chapter 14 introduced write skew as the anomaly that snapshot isolation cannot prevent. MVCC is snapshot isolation at its heart; therefore pure MVCC is vulnerable.

Classic example revisited. On-call schedule, two doctors Alice and Bob, both "on call."
    
    
    T1: snapshot at t0, sees Alice=oncall, Bob=oncall
    T1: UPDATE Alice.oncall = false  (new version of Alice's row)
    
    T2: snapshot at t0 (or anytime before T1 commits), sees Alice=oncall, Bob=oncall
    T2: UPDATE Bob.oncall = false (new version of Bob's row)
    
    T1: commits. T2: commits. No write-write conflict (disjoint rows).
    Final state: no one on call. Invariant violated.
    

Neither transaction touched the other's written row. The write-write conflict check fires only on the same row. The invariant requires _a cross-row constraint_ — "at least one oncall" — that MVCC's per-row conflict detection cannot see.

#### Fixes

* **SELECT FOR UPDATE** : T1 reads Bob's row FOR UPDATE to materialize the write-read dependency. T2 would block (InnoDB) or abort (PostgreSQL).
* **Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI)** : track read dependencies; abort on dangerous patterns. See Section 16.8.
* **Serializable via locks** : upgrade to full 2PL for this transaction. Slow.

In practice, most applications learn of write skew the hard way, and developers either (a) sprinkle SELECT FOR UPDATE everywhere, (b) upgrade to SERIALIZABLE where the invariant matters, or (c) accept the risk and add a reconciliation process.

### 16.7 Latches vs Locks

A digression important for understanding MVCC implementation.

#### Definition: Latch

A short-duration synchronization primitive protecting a specific in-memory data structure — a page in the buffer pool, a B-tree node during split, a slot in the lock table. Latches are held for microseconds, not seconds. They are implemented as mutexes or spinlocks.

#### Definition: Lock (in this sense)

A long-duration logical synchronization primitive protecting a row, page, or table for transactional purposes. Held for the duration of a transaction. Managed by the lock manager.

MVCC uses latches internally (the version chain needs protection during concurrent insertions) but avoids locks for reads. The operational pattern: very-short latches guard structural changes; transaction-level locks only for writes. This is why MVCC engines scale so well on read-heavy workloads.

### 16.8 Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI)

PostgreSQL's SSI, added in 9.1, upgrades MVCC to true serializability by tracking a specific pattern.

#### The dangerous pattern

Two concurrent transactions T1 and T2. T1 reads some data D1, writes D2. T2 reads D2 (or reads a value that depends on what T1 would write), writes D3. The read-write dependency from T1→T2 plus the read-write dependency from T2→T1 forms a cycle; serializability is impossible in this schedule.

SSI detects the formation of this cycle and aborts one transaction.

#### Implementation sketch

Each transaction maintains a list of:

* **predicates it read** (to detect writes that invalidate the reads).
* **rows it wrote** (to cross-reference).

At commit time, the engine checks: is there a committed concurrent transaction T' whose writes invalidate _our_ reads, AND does our writes invalidate _T' or another concurrent transaction's_ reads? If yes, dangerous structure — abort.

#### Cost

False positives: some aborts happen when no actual anomaly would have occurred. The engine is conservative. Under low-contention, SSI's abort rate is near zero; under high-contention serializable workloads, it can be 5–10%.

#### When SSI beats 2PL

* High-concurrency workloads where most transactions do not conflict.
* Read-heavy workloads.
* Workloads where the write-skew invariants are real but rare.

#### When 2PL beats SSI

* Very high-conflict workloads where most transactions would abort under SSI; 2PL serializes without retry storms.
* Long-running transactions where aborting is wasteful.

PostgreSQL's SSI is the leading production-quality SSI implementation. CockroachDB uses a similar approach. SQL Server and Oracle do not offer true serializable on top of their MVCC; they use 2PL for serializable mode.

### 16.9 MVCC in Thread A

Thread A's enrollment workflow under PostgreSQL (MVCC REPEATABLE READ):
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT seats_remaining FROM Courses WHERE id = 42;
    -- say, returns 1
    -- application checks > 0
    UPDATE Courses SET seats_remaining = seats_remaining - 1 WHERE id = 42;
    INSERT INTO Enrollments(student_id, course_id) VALUES (?, 42);
    COMMIT;
    

Two students A and B, both see seats=1 in their snapshots. A updates first; commits. B tries to update; sees the row was modified by a transaction not in its snapshot; aborts with "could not serialize."

On retry, B's snapshot sees seats=0 (A's committed update). Application checks 0>0 = false. Rejects. Commits without damage.

The application must handle the serialization error and retry. Most application frameworks provide this automatically.

#### Alternative: READ COMMITTED with SELECT FOR UPDATE
    
    
    BEGIN;
    SELECT seats_remaining FROM Courses WHERE id = 42 FOR UPDATE;
    -- row-level X lock acquired
    -- read fresh value
    -- check and proceed
    

B's SELECT now blocks on A's lock. When A commits, B sees the new value. If seats = 0, application rejects.

This avoids the retry but serializes writes on the row. Under 500 concurrent enrollments for 30 seats, throughput is bounded by "one transaction per lock release." The MVCC retry approach lets more transactions try in parallel, at the cost of extra work on conflicts.

Which is better? Depends on contention. Low contention: MVCC optimistic is faster. Very high contention: 2PL pessimistic might be faster because retry storms exceed the cost of serialization.

### 16.10 Hybrid Engines

Real engines rarely pick one model. Typical setups:

* **MVCC for reads** , pessimistic locks for writes (InnoDB, SQL Server RCSI, Oracle). Readers always snapshot; writers lock rows.
* **Pure MVCC with optimistic writes** (PostgreSQL, CockroachDB). Writes conflict-detect at commit; failures are retried by the application.
* **MVCC + SSI** (PostgreSQL). Readers snapshot; conflicts detected; serializable upgrade opt-in.

The architecture choice affects operational reality:

* InnoDB's writes are slow under contention (locking) but predictable (no surprise aborts).
* PostgreSQL's writes are fast under low contention but require retry logic.

Applications should know what their database does and write accordingly.

### 16.11 Performance Comparison

#### Read-heavy workload (80% reads, 20% writes, low conflict)

* SS2PL: reads block on writer locks → degraded throughput.
* MVCC: reads never block → near-linear scaling.

MVCC wins by 3–10× under realistic concurrency.

#### Write-heavy workload (50% writes, high contention on hot rows)

* SS2PL: writers serialize on row locks → deterministic throughput.
* MVCC with optimistic writes: writers conflict, abort, retry → retry storms at very high contention.

SS2PL can outperform optimistic MVCC here. Solution: switch to MVCC + row locks for writes (InnoDB-style), getting the best of both.

#### Analytical workload (long-running reads)

* SS2PL: long readers block any writer that touches their read set → production crisis.
* MVCC: long reader snapshots, zero write blockage. But blocks vacuum → bloat.

MVCC wins on throughput but shifts the pain to vacuum operations.

#### Mixed OLTP+OLAP (HTAP)

* Pure SS2PL: impossible. Long OLAP queries starve OLTP writes.
* Pure MVCC: OLAP reads old versions while OLTP writes new ones. The design goal of HTAP engines.

Modern engines universally use MVCC for this reason.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **MVCC trades extra storage for read-write concurrency.** Keeping multiple versions per row lets readers never block writers and writers never block readers — a 3–10× throughput win on read-heavy workloads — but the heap (or undo log) holds dead versions until cleanup runs.
* **In-place version chains trade vacuum complexity for cheap reads.** PostgreSQL-style heap versions keep all data inline so reads need no indirection, but a long-running snapshot blocks VACUUM globally and tables can bloat unboundedly until disk fills.
* **Undo-log MVCC trades current-read speed for old-snapshot cost.** Oracle/InnoDB keep only the latest version in place, making `now`-snapshot reads cheapest, but older snapshots must walk back through undo deltas — and a long transaction can overflow the undo segment ("ORA-01555 snapshot too old").
* **Snapshot isolation trades full serializability for never-blocking reads.** SI prevents four anomalies cheaply, but a cross-row invariant like "at least one doctor on call" can be violated by write skew because conflict detection fires only on same-row writes.
* **Optimistic write-conflict detection (PostgreSQL RR) trades blocking for retry cost.** Concurrent writers proceed in parallel and only one aborts at commit, but under high write contention the abort rate dominates and an InnoDB-style row lock would deliver more steady-state throughput.
* **More aggressive VACUUM trades steady-state CPU for less bloat.** Tightening autovacuum thresholds keeps tables compact and predictable, but vacuum itself reads and rewrites pages — overdoing it eats the throughput MVCC was supposed to deliver.
* **SSI trades occasional false-positive aborts for true serializability without locks.** Tracking read dependencies and aborting dangerous structures gives serializable behavior on top of snapshot isolation, but the tracking is conservative, and high-contention serializable workloads can see 5–10% of transactions abort even when no real anomaly would have occurred.

### 16.12 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Explain the core invariant of MVCC in one sentence (readers, writers, blocking).
  2. What is a row version? What do `xmin` and `xmax` mean?
  3. Contrast in-place MVCC (PostgreSQL) with undo-log MVCC (Oracle / InnoDB). Name two operational consequences of each.
  4. Describe VACUUM. What is "dead tuple bloat" and when does it become a problem?
  5. Why does MVCC not prevent write skew?
  6. Distinguish a latch from a lock. Name one thing each protects.
  7. What is Serializable Snapshot Isolation, and how does it differ from snapshot isolation?

#### Application Problems

  8. Trace a sequence of five transactions on a single row under MVCC: two readers at different snapshots, an inserter, an updater, and a deleter. Draw the version chain and show which version each reader sees.

Hint

Apply the visibility rules from §16.3 step by step. For each reader, ask: which xmin values are in its snapshot, and which xmax values would mark a version invisible? The two readers should land on different versions because their snapshots include different sets of committed transactions.

  9. An OLTP system runs at PostgreSQL's default READ COMMITTED. An analyst opens a connection and runs `SELECT count(*) FROM orders` every hour without ever committing. After two days, disk fills. Diagnose.

Hint

The analyst's transaction never commits, so its snapshot lives forever. Think about what VACUUM is allowed to reclaim — only versions invisible to _every_ live snapshot. Now ask which rows the OLTP writers are dead-versioning over those two days, and what happens when none of them can be cleaned.

  10. You have a table with a hot-row counter incremented by every application request. Throughput under InnoDB is 500 TPS (limited by row lock contention). Propose two architectural changes that boost throughput significantly while staying MVCC.

Hint

The 500 TPS ceiling is "1 / row-lock-hold-time." Two ways to break it: spread the writes across multiple physical rows so contention drops by a factor of N; or batch many increments into one periodic write so the lock is held less often. Each has a read-side cost — what is it?

  11. Thread C at PostgreSQL REPEATABLE READ: two sessions attempt the 800 USD withdrawal concurrently. Show the precise sequence of operations, version-chain state at each step, and the final outcome. Include retry logic the application must implement.

Hint

Walk through §16.1's Thread C trace: at which step does B's UPDATE detect the conflict, and what error does PostgreSQL raise? For the retry, recall §14.8's preconditions — the entire transaction body must be safely re-executable, and the loop needs jittered backoff to avoid retry storms.

  12. A developer writes an autoupdater that increments a `last_seen` timestamp. Under heavy load, the transactions frequently abort with "could not serialize" at REPEATABLE READ. Propose a fix that lets the updater proceed without aborts.

  13. A multi-tenant SaaS uses INSERT to add rows into customer tables. Under PostgreSQL MVCC, does INSERT interfere with concurrent readers of the same table? Explain.

  14. Design a deletion policy for a table where rows are only logically deleted (set `deleted_at = now()`) and never removed. Describe how MVCC bloat would differ from the hard-delete case.

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why do undo-log-based MVCC engines (Oracle, InnoDB) not have the vacuum problem in the same form as heap-version-based engines (PostgreSQL)? What _other_ problem do they have?

  16. Imagine a hypothetical MVCC engine where dead versions are cleaned up _eagerly_ at each transaction commit. What are the performance tradeoffs versus deferred cleanup (vacuum)?

  17. A long-running read transaction prevents vacuum and causes bloat. Propose an engine-level feature that would let the read proceed _without_ pinning versions, perhaps at the cost of reading a slightly-stale view. What is the semantic tradeoff?

Hint

What if the engine took a "frozen" copy of the read set (or used a read replica's snapshot from N minutes ago) so that vacuum on the primary is no longer blocked? Trade off the staleness against the operational pain — for which queries is "data from 5 minutes ago" acceptable, and for which is it not?

  18. Serializable Snapshot Isolation has false-positive aborts. Design a workload where SSI's false positive rate is unusually high, and explain structurally why.

  19. In MVCC, every update creates a new version and invalidates index entries pointing to the old version. PostgreSQL's "HOT updates" try to keep new versions on the same heap page to avoid updating indexes. Describe when this works and when it fails; what is the storage-level condition?

Hint

HOT requires two conditions: enough free space on the same page for the new version, and no indexes touch the changed columns. Walk through what happens when each condition is violated — does the update degrade gracefully, or does it kick off a cascade of index updates?

  20. Hybrid transactional-analytical processing (HTAP) requires concurrent OLTP and OLAP. Argue from Chapter 16 why MVCC is essential. Would a column-store MVCC engine differ from a row-store MVCC engine in how it implements versioning? (Preview Chapter 19.)

### Chapter Summary

**MVCC** stores multiple versions of each row, tagged with the transaction that created (xmin) and optionally deleted (xmax) the version. Readers see the version valid as of their **snapshot** ; writers create new versions without overwriting. The central property: **readers never block writers, writers never block readers**.

Two implementation families: **in-place with dead tuples** (PostgreSQL, CockroachDB) and **undo-log-based** (Oracle, InnoDB). Both pay storage costs for versions; the costs manifest differently — heap bloat vs undo-log growth. **VACUUM** (or equivalent) reclaims space.

**Write-write conflicts** still exist under MVCC and are resolved by either **row-level write locks** (InnoDB) or **optimistic conflict detection** at commit (PostgreSQL). `SELECT FOR UPDATE` remains useful for read-modify-write under READ COMMITTED.

**Snapshot isolation** — what most engines call REPEATABLE READ under MVCC — is vulnerable to **write skew**. **Serializable Snapshot Isolation** (PostgreSQL 9.1+) extends MVCC with conflict-graph tracking to deliver full serializability, at the cost of some false-positive aborts.

Thread C under MVCC: the second withdrawal either aborts and retries (PostgreSQL RR) or blocks (InnoDB with implicit row lock on UPDATE); in both cases the final result is correct and no money is lost.

Chapter 17 closes the concurrency-and-failure arc with recovery: how a crashed DBMS, using the write-ahead log, reconstructs a consistent database from the logs of transactions that committed and discards the partial effects of those that did not.

---

## Chapter 17: Crash Recovery — WAL and ARIES

Every concept in the book so far assumes that when a transaction commits, its effects are permanent. "Durable" — the D in ACID. But permanence is a mirage if the machine can die at any moment. RAM contents vanish when the power drops. Pages in the buffer pool that were never flushed to disk are gone. Half-written pages corrupt into nonsense. Something must survive.

The answer, invented at IBM in the 1970s and refined across 40 years of research and production experience, is the **write-ahead log (WAL)** and an algorithm called **ARIES** that uses it. The log is a sequential, append-only record of every change the database makes. Before any data page is modified in a way that a crash could expose, the change is written to the log first. If the server crashes, a recovery process reads the log and reconstructs a consistent database — applying committed changes that never made it to data files, and rolling back changes from transactions that never reached commit.

This chapter ties together the transactions of Chapters 13–16 with the durability they need. We see what WAL guarantees and what it does not, the three-pass structure of ARIES, how long-running transactions and checkpoints interact, and what goes wrong when any of these pieces falter. Thread A's registration server crashing mid-enrollment finally gets its answer: how the database restarts without losing or duplicating any seats.

### 17.1 Why Recovery Is Hard

Imagine an enrollment that increments `seats_remaining` and inserts into `Enrollments`:
    
    
    BEGIN;
    UPDATE Courses SET seats_remaining = seats_remaining - 1 WHERE id = 42;
    INSERT INTO Enrollments(student_id, course_id) VALUES (4221, 42);
    COMMIT;
    

At the storage level, both operations modify pages in the buffer pool. The changes are not immediately written to disk — writing on every update would destroy throughput (Chapter 9). So the pages are marked dirty and flushed later.

Now suppose the server crashes after `COMMIT`. Did the changes make it to disk?

* **The`Courses` page**: maybe flushed, maybe not.
* **The`Enrollments` page**: maybe flushed, maybe not.
* **Any combination** : all four possibilities exist.

If neither flushed: the commit was acknowledged but the changes are lost. **Durability violated.**

If only one flushed: the database is in an inconsistent state — course has one fewer seat but no enrollment record, or vice versa. **Atomicity violated.**

If both flushed: great, but how do we know without some kind of marker?

The database needs a way to reconstruct the correct final state after any crash. That is recovery.

#### Failure model

The DBMS assumes two kinds of failure:

  1. **Transaction abort** — the application aborts (or the system aborts on deadlock). Mid-recovery is just the transaction system's regular rollback.
  2. **System crash** — the entire DBMS process or the machine dies. The buffer pool is lost. The disk is intact, but pages may be in arbitrary intermediate states.

It assumes that disk writes are atomic at the sector level (usually true), but that a write _in progress_ when the power drops may tear (partial page writes). Mitigations exist; we mention them briefly.

It does _not_ assume the disk itself fails permanently. That is **media failure** — recovered from backup + replay. A separate concern; we focus on crash recovery here.

### 17.2 Write-Ahead Logging — The Three Rules

ARIES and its descendants enforce three rules.

#### WAL Rule 1: Log records describe every change

Before modifying a page in the buffer pool, write a **log record** describing the change:
    
    
    { LSN, transaction_id, page_id, offset, old_value, new_value, prev_LSN }
    

* **LSN** : Log Sequence Number, a monotonically increasing identifier.
* **transaction_id** : who did it.
* **page_id, offset** : what was changed.
* **old_value, new_value** : enough to undo and redo the change.
* **prev_LSN** : the previous log record for the same transaction — forms a backward chain per transaction, essential for efficient undo.

#### WAL Rule 2: Log ahead of pages

For any data page modification with LSN LLL:

**The log record with LSN LLL must be durably on disk before the modified data page is written to disk.**

This is the **write-ahead** invariant. Without it, a crash could expose a page mid-modification to disk, and the log wouldn't know the modification had happened. Recovery couldn't undo.

In practice: every page has a **pageLSN** field recording the LSN of the most recent log record affecting it. Before the buffer manager writes a dirty page, it ensures the log is flushed up to at least pageLSN.

#### WAL Rule 3: Log flush precedes commit

**Before acknowledging COMMIT to the client, the log (up to the transaction's COMMIT record) must be durably flushed.**

This is why COMMITs cost a disk sync. Without it, a crash could lose the commit record; recovery would undo the transaction; the client would have been told "committed" for something that didn't happen.

#### Together

WAL rules (2) and (3) imply that every committed change is recoverable, even if data pages haven't been written. Rule (1) ensures every change can be undone if its transaction aborted.

#### Common Misconception

"WAL writes every byte of data twice — once in the log, once in the heap." Essentially correct, but the log writes are **sequential** , and sequential I/O is much faster than random. The cost of WAL is amortized over many commits per flush; the disk syncs group in-flight commits. This is why real databases sustain tens of thousands of committed TPS despite every commit requiring durability.

### 17.3 Anatomy of a Log Record

Different engines use slightly different log formats. PostgreSQL's WAL:
    
    
    Header: LSN, transaction_id, record_type, length
    Body: varies by record_type
    

Record types:

* **Heap update** : page_id, offset, old tuple, new tuple.
* **Index update** : index page_id, operation (insert/delete), key.
* **Begin** : transaction start marker.
* **Commit** : transaction committed.
* **Abort** : transaction aborted.
* **Checkpoint** : system-level marker; lists active transactions and dirty pages.

ARIES adds a few more:

* **Compensation Log Record (CLR)** : emitted during undo, describes a reverse operation. Essential for making undo itself recoverable — if the engine crashes while rolling back, the CLRs tell it what has already been undone.

#### Physical vs logical logging

* **Physical logging** : old and new raw bytes of the page region. Simple to apply (byte-level redo/undo). Large log records for big changes.
* **Logical logging** : the operation name and its arguments (e.g., "DELETE from table where id=42"). Smaller, but requires the engine to be able to reproduce the operation, which is fragile across versions.
* **Physiological logging** (ARIES): physical within a page (page-level physical, row-level logical). The standard compromise in practice.

PostgreSQL uses physiological logging; MySQL InnoDB similar. SQLite uses journal files with physical pages (less efficient but simpler).

### 17.4 The Steal-and-No-Force Policy

Two buffer-manager policies interact with recovery.

#### Definition: Steal policy

The buffer manager may evict a dirty page even if the transaction that dirtied it has not committed. The evicted page's changes _are_ written to disk (with the WAL log already there, by Rule 2).

#### Definition: No-force policy

The DBMS does _not_ require all of a transaction's dirty pages to be written to disk at commit time. Only the log must be flushed.

Combining steal + no-force is the industry standard. It maximizes throughput (the buffer manager is free; commits are fast) at the cost of making recovery more complex:

* **Steal** means uncommitted changes might be on disk at crash time → recovery must **undo** them.
* **No-force** means committed changes might not be on disk at crash time → recovery must **redo** them.

So a recovery process must do both redo and undo. This is ARIES.

#### The alternatives

* **No-steal, force** : commit flushes all dirty pages; no uncommitted changes on disk. No undo or redo needed in recovery. But terrible performance.
* **Steal, force** : commit flushes; but uncommitted changes may reach disk. Need undo only. Rarely used.
* **No-steal, no-force** : no uncommitted changes reach disk, but committed changes may not be on disk. Need redo only. Limits buffer pool efficiency.

Steal + no-force + WAL = ARIES, the compromise with best throughput.

### 17.5 ARIES — The Three-Pass Recovery Algorithm

ARIES stands for "Algorithms for Recovery and Isolation Exploiting Semantics," from IBM's Mohan et al., 1992. It is the canonical crash-recovery algorithm. Every major DBMS uses ARIES or a direct descendant.

After a crash, the DBMS restarts and runs three passes over the log.

#### Pass 1 — Analysis

Read the log forward from the last checkpoint. Determine, at the moment of the crash:

* Which transactions were **in flight** (started but not committed or aborted)?
* Which pages were **dirty** (modified in buffer but possibly not yet on disk)?

Build two tables:

* **Transaction table** : live transactions, their last LSN.
* **Dirty page table** : pages that may need reexamination, with their earliest LSN (the "recLSN" — LSN at which the page first became dirty).

#### Pass 2 — Redo

From the **earliest recLSN** in the dirty page table, replay forward through the log. For each log record:

  1. If the target page exists in the dirty page table and the log record's LSN is greater than the page's pageLSN, **redo** the change: apply new_value to offset.
  2. Otherwise skip (page is already up-to-date).

After redo, the database is in the state as of the moment of the crash — but with uncommitted transactions' changes still present.

#### Pass 3 — Undo

For each transaction in the transaction table (these are the in-flight transactions), walk the log _backward_ using prev_LSN pointers. For each update log record:

  1. Undo the change by writing old_value back.
  2. Emit a **CLR** (compensation log record) documenting the undo.

Continue until reaching the transaction's begin record. Mark the transaction as aborted.

If the server crashes during undo, recovery restarts. The CLRs ensure no undo work is duplicated — they record what has already been undone.

#### Why this works

* **Redo** brings all pages up to the state they had at the moment of crash (including committed and uncommitted effects).
* **Undo** rolls back the uncommitted effects.
* The result: exactly the state of committed-only transactions. Durability and atomicity both preserved.

#### Common Misconception

"Recovery is slow." Recovery can be slow for enormously large logs, but checkpoints (next section) bound the work to the log written since the last checkpoint. For a system with checkpoints every few minutes, recovery takes seconds.

### 17.6 Checkpoints

Without intervention, the log grows unboundedly and recovery reads all of it. A **checkpoint** records a consistent state and lets recovery skip earlier records.

#### Sharp checkpoint (simple, slow)

  1. Stop accepting new transactions.
  2. Wait for in-flight transactions to commit or abort.
  3. Flush all dirty pages to disk.
  4. Write a CHECKPOINT record to the log.
  5. Resume.

Guarantees: at the checkpoint, the disk matches the log exactly. Recovery starts from the checkpoint. Safe, simple. But unacceptable in production: the "stop the world" pause can be seconds.

#### Fuzzy checkpoint (modern, used by all engines)

  1. Write a CHECKPOINT-BEGIN record including: transaction table snapshot, dirty page table snapshot.
  2. Asynchronously flush dirty pages.
  3. Write a CHECKPOINT-END record when flush finishes.

No pause. Recovery must start from the earliest recLSN listed in the CHECKPOINT-BEGIN record (since some of those pages may not have been flushed yet), redo forward from there, then undo in-flight transactions.

PostgreSQL, SQL Server, Oracle, InnoDB all use fuzzy checkpointing. Checkpoint interval is tunable: PostgreSQL's `checkpoint_timeout` defaults to 5 minutes; many production systems tune down to 30 seconds for fast recovery at the cost of more background flushing.

#### Tradeoff: checkpoint frequency

* **Frequent checkpoints** : bounded recovery time; more background I/O.
* **Rare checkpoints** : less background I/O; recovery can take minutes on a write-heavy system.

Production tuning: pick a worst-case acceptable recovery time (say, 30 seconds) and work backward to checkpoint frequency.

### 17.7 Thread A — Registration Server Crashes Mid-Enrollment

The registration system from Thread A has 500 students enrolling in a 30-seat class. The server crashes partway through. What happens on restart?

#### State at crash

* 200 enrollments committed (reached COMMIT and log was flushed).
* 50 enrollments in-flight (began but COMMIT not yet logged).
* Various buffer pool pages dirty — some related to committed enrollments, some to in-flight ones.
* Some data pages may have been flushed; some may not.

#### After restart

Pass 1 (analysis): reads the WAL since the last checkpoint. Identifies 50 in-flight transactions. Builds the dirty page table.

Pass 2 (redo): replays the log forward. Every committed update that wasn't on disk is reapplied. After redo, the database reflects _all_ changes — including those from the 50 in-flight transactions.

Pass 3 (undo): walks backward through the in-flight transactions' logs. Reverts their changes — decrement any `seats_remaining` that was wrongly decremented, remove any `Enrollments` rows that were wrongly inserted.

Result: 200 enrollments. Exactly what was committed. Exactly what the clients were told. No lost seat (durability). No orphan enrollment (atomicity). No duplicates (atomicity). No consistency violation.

#### Client experience

The 200 committed students see their enrollments. The 50 in-flight students got an error at crash time (their connection dropped mid-request). When they retry, they either find themselves enrolled (the engine redid their commit) or they are not (recovery undid the in-flight state). The retry either succeeds or fails cleanly — no partial state.

This is the ACID abstraction in action. The application doesn't think about any of the machinery. It just calls BEGIN, does its work, calls COMMIT, and trusts.

### 17.8 The `fsync` Story — What "Durable" Actually Means

Rule 3 says "log is durably on disk before commit acknowledgment." What does "durably" mean?

#### The write stack

An application write passes through:

  1. Application memory → kernel page cache (OS buffer).
  2. Kernel page cache → disk controller cache (often volatile, battery-backed if enterprise).
  3. Disk controller → media (platters, NAND flash).

Each layer can fail without the one above knowing. A naive `write()` returns when the kernel accepts the data — not when the disk has it.

#### fsync()

`fsync(fd)` asks the kernel to ensure the file's data is on stable media. Implementations:

* Linux `ext4`, `xfs`: flushes kernel buffers and issues a disk cache flush.
* BSD: similar.
* Mac `HFS+`: historically unreliable; `F_FULLFSYNC` ioctl is needed for true durability.
* Windows NTFS: `FlushFileBuffers`.

Every WAL flush issues an fsync. This is why commits cost milliseconds even on SSDs: the disk must actually commit the sector.

#### Group commit

A common optimization: batch multiple commits into one fsync. Transactions waiting to commit queue briefly; the engine fsyncs once for all of them. Throughput: tens of thousands of TPS where naive commit would give single-digit thousands.

#### Torn writes

A crash mid-write can leave a page half-old, half-new. Recovery must detect and repair:

* **Page checksum** : corrupt-detected pages are recovered by applying the log from scratch (requires full-page images in the log, as PostgreSQL does).
* **Double-write buffer** (InnoDB): every page is written twice, once to a safe scratch area, once to its real location. On recovery, any torn page can be replaced from the scratch copy.

Different engines choose different mitigations. The underlying concern is universal.

#### Common Misconception

"fsync is always reliable." It's as reliable as the disk stack underneath. Consumer SSDs have been shipped with firmware that lied about cache flush completion. File systems have bugs. Between fsync and true durability sits a long chain of components. Real databases monitor for disk errors and use techniques like checksummed WAL to detect corruption before it's too late.

### 17.9 Point-in-Time Recovery and Continuous Archiving

Recovery so far restores the database to the state at crash time. What if you want to restore to an _earlier_ state — say, before a bad DELETE?

#### Continuous archiving

The database continuously ships WAL segments to archival storage. At any time you have:

* A full backup (base) from some earlier point.
* WAL records from the backup point to the present.

To recover to time T:

  1. Restore the base backup.
  2. Replay WAL records up to T.

This is **point-in-time recovery (PITR)**. PostgreSQL, Oracle, SQL Server all support it. MySQL InnoDB's binlog serves a similar role.

#### Replication

Streaming WAL to standby servers gives real-time replication. The standby replays the log as it arrives. If the primary fails, the standby takes over. We return to this in Chapter 18.

### 17.10 When Recovery Goes Wrong

#### Corrupted WAL

If WAL records themselves become corrupt, recovery cannot proceed. Every log record has a checksum; mismatched records stop recovery at that point (with prior records applied). The DBA is then faced with: recover to the last consistent point, or abandon recent transactions.

#### Catastrophic media failure

Disk itself fails. WAL and data pages are both unreadable. Recovery path: restore from backup + replay archived WAL up to the last archived segment. Transactions committed after the last archive are lost.

Hence: always archive WAL more frequently than the acceptable data-loss window.

#### Replication failures

The standby is behind; the primary fails. Switching to the standby loses recent transactions. Either accept the data loss or wait for the standby to catch up (which requires the primary to still be reachable, contradicting the failover scenario).

This is the CAP theorem's shadow on operational practice. See Chapter 18.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **WAL trades double-writing for sequential I/O and crash recoverability.** Every change is written twice (log + data page), but the log writes are sequential and batched via group commit, so the amortized cost is far lower than paying random-write latency on every data page.
* **Steal + no-force trades recovery complexity for runtime throughput.** Allowing uncommitted dirty pages to be evicted and not forcing committed pages to flush keeps the buffer pool flexible, at the price of needing both redo and undo passes at restart.
* **Frequent checkpoints trade background I/O for short recovery time.** More checkpointing smooths restart latency to seconds but steals IOPS from user queries; rare checkpoints give user queries free rein but risk minutes of recovery.
* **Synchronous commit trades per-transaction latency for durability.** Waiting for fsync before acknowledging bounds data loss to zero at the cost of millisecond-scale commit stalls; `synchronous_commit = off` buys throughput by accepting a small window of lost-but-acknowledged transactions.
* **Group commit trades individual-transaction latency for aggregate throughput.** Batching many transactions per fsync amortizes the sync cost across them, which raises system-level TPS but adds queueing delay to the earliest transaction in each batch.
* **Double-write buffers and full-page-image logging trade write amplification for torn-write safety.** Writing pages twice (or stamping a full image into the log) doubles the I/O for affected writes but guarantees that a crash mid-page never leaves unrecoverable corruption.

### 17.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State the three WAL rules in one sentence each.
  2. Describe "steal" and "no-force" and explain why their combination requires both undo and redo.
  3. What information is in a log record? Why is `prev_LSN` important?
  4. Describe the three passes of ARIES.
  5. What is a compensation log record, and why is it necessary?
  6. Explain fuzzy checkpointing. How does it differ from a sharp checkpoint?
  7. Why does a COMMIT always cost a disk sync?

#### Application Problems

  8. A database has the following log after a crash: 
         
         LSN 100: T1 BEGIN
         LSN 110: T1 UPDATE page P, x=10 → x=20
         LSN 120: T2 BEGIN
         LSN 130: T2 UPDATE page Q, y=5 → y=7
         LSN 140: T1 COMMIT
         LSN 150: T2 UPDATE page P, x=20 → x=25
         LSN 160: (crash)
         

After recovery, what are the values of x and y? Which records are redone and which undone?

Hint

Walk through the three ARIES passes in order. Which transactions committed before the crash, and which were in flight? For each log record, ask whether its transaction eventually committed — that determines whether the change stays (redo) or is reverted (undo).

  9. Draw the dirty page table and transaction table at the moment of crash in the above example.

  10. A server crashes with 1 GB of WAL since the last checkpoint. On SSD at 500 MB/s sequential read, estimate recovery time. What is the bottleneck?

Hint

Recovery must read the entire WAL since the checkpoint, and may apply page writes as it goes. Compute the sequential-read time first, then ask whether random-write I/O during redo, CPU cost, or something else could dominate.

  11. Explain why `SYNCHRONOUS_COMMIT = OFF` in PostgreSQL (or equivalent in other engines) can improve throughput 5–10× but violates durability.

  12. A transaction inserts 10 rows, commits, and crashes happen. For each scenario below, describe what happens after recovery: (a) commit record was flushed, no data pages were flushed. (b) commit record was not flushed, but some data pages were flushed. (c) neither commit record nor data pages were flushed.

Hint

For each scenario, ask two questions. First: does the log say this transaction committed? If yes, redo must restore its effects. If no, undo must roll back any data-page changes that leaked to disk. Trace the answer against WAL Rule 2.

  13. A torn write leaves a page half-old, half-new. Describe two mitigations (double-write buffer, full-page images). What cost does each impose?

  14. Design a backup strategy for a database with these requirements: RPO (recovery point objective) of 1 minute, RTO (recovery time objective) of 5 minutes, 100 GB database, 10 MB/s WAL generation.

Hint

RPO bounds how often you must archive WAL; RTO bounds how long replay can take. Work out how much WAL accumulates per minute, how long restoring a 100 GB base backup takes on your assumed disk bandwidth, and how often you therefore need fresh base backups to keep replay under 5 minutes.

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why does ARIES separate analysis, redo, and undo into three passes? Could they be combined into fewer passes? What invariant would break?

Hint

Each pass depends on something the previous one produced. What does redo need to know before it can start, and where does that information come from? What would go wrong if you tried to undo before finishing redo, given that redo can touch pages you are about to roll back?

  16. A database engineer suggests skipping the log for temporary tables ("they're not durable anyway"). Argue for or against this, considering what "durability" means in a WAL context and how it interacts with transactions that mix permanent and temporary writes.

  17. Group commit batches many transactions per fsync. What is the _latency_ cost of group commit for an individual transaction? When does group commit hurt?

  18. Recovery assumes the buffer pool is empty at start. What if a hot standby maintains its buffer pool warm with current pages? Could recovery be faster? What assumptions would have to change?

Hint

Redo exists to bring pages forward from their on-disk state to the crash-moment state. If some pages are already cached with more recent content, which ARIES steps become unnecessary for them? Think about how pageLSN is used to decide whether a redo record applies, and what "state at crash" even means when memory survived.

  19. Point-in-time recovery to time T requires WAL from a backup to T. What if T is _after_ the current log end (asking for future data)? What if T is _before_ the base backup (asking for older)? What safeguards should the engine enforce?

  20. Thread A's crash and recovery scenario restores to "200 enrollments committed, 50 in-flight rolled back." Describe what the user experience should be like for a student whose enrollment was in-flight. What should the application do to make this experience bearable?

### Chapter Summary

**Crash recovery** turns the abstract Atomicity and Durability promises into implementable engineering. The **write-ahead log (WAL)** is the central mechanism: every page modification is first described in a log record, and log records for committed transactions are flushed to disk before commit is acknowledged. **Three rules** — log every change, log ahead of page flushes, flush log before commit — guarantee recoverability.

The **steal + no-force** buffer policy combined with **WAL** gives maximum throughput but requires both **redo** (re-apply committed changes that weren't flushed) and **undo** (roll back in-flight transactions) during recovery. The **ARIES** algorithm does both in three passes: **analysis** (identify in-flight transactions and dirty pages from the log), **redo** (re-apply changes forward from the earliest dirty-page LSN), **undo** (roll back in-flight transactions using the prev_LSN chain, emitting CLRs for idempotency).

**Checkpoints** bound recovery time by flushing dirty pages and recording a consistent marker; **fuzzy checkpoints** do this without pausing the system.

The **fsync** story reveals that "durable" is a contract between the database, the OS, and the disk — implementations use page checksums, double-write buffers, and full-page images to defend against torn writes. **Group commit** amortizes fsync cost across concurrent transactions.

Thread A's registration crash recovers cleanly: committed enrollments persist (redo), in-flight transactions are rolled back (undo), and clients see consistent state. The ACID abstraction holds.

Chapter 18 leaves single-machine recovery and asks the next layer of questions: what happens when the database spans multiple machines, partitions split the network, and we must choose between consistency and availability? That is CAP, and distributed databases.

---

## Chapter 18: Distributed Databases and the CAP Theorem

Everything in the book so far has assumed a single machine. A DBMS process, a set of data files, a buffer pool, a WAL. One node, one clock, one source of truth. When that machine dies, the database stops.

Real production databases rarely live that way. A bank's customer data spans dozens of data centers. A social network's users span continents. A retail catalog is replicated into every region for latency. Even a modest application often runs with a primary and one or two standbys for availability.

Distribution multiplies every problem by orders of magnitude. Machines fail independently. Networks partition. Clocks drift. Two nodes can hold conflicting views of "the latest value" and cannot ask anyone to arbitrate. The theory that governs this is compact and sobering: **the CAP theorem** , stating that a distributed system cannot simultaneously offer strong Consistency, Availability, and tolerance to network Partitions. You get two of three, and the pair you give up shapes everything about the system.

This chapter covers the essential distributed database ideas: replication (primary/standby and multi-primary), partitioning (sharding), the CAP theorem and its practical interpretation, quorums and eventual consistency, and a glimpse of distributed transactions and consensus protocols. Thread C's bank account — replicated across two data centers, with a network partition in the middle — forces the CAP choice to become concrete.

We will not become distributed-systems experts in one chapter. We will build enough vocabulary to reason about scale, the tradeoffs real systems make, and why "distributed" is not just "single-node but with network calls."

### 18.1 Why Distribute?

Three reasons, each with different design implications.

#### Scalability

A single machine can hold terabytes of data and handle tens of thousands of transactions per second. Beyond that, you must distribute. Facebook, Google, Amazon — all processed data that fundamentally could not fit on one machine's hardware.

Distributing for scalability usually means **partitioning** : splitting the dataset across N machines, each handling a subset. A single query may touch many partitions (a scatter-gather) or ideally just one (a well-partitioned point query).

#### Availability

A single machine can crash. If that crash means "database down," your service is down. **Replication** — keeping copies of the data on multiple machines — lets the system continue when one fails. The question becomes: how many copies, how are they synchronized, and what do you do when some disagree?

#### Geography

A request from Tokyo to a Chicago server pays 200 ms of network latency. For interactive applications, that is unacceptable. Replicating data to a Tokyo data center cuts the latency but now you must keep regions consistent. Every strongly-consistent cross-region write pays another round trip.

These motivations pull the system design in different directions. A single-primary replication setup gives scalability-for-reads but not writes. Sharding gives scalability in both dimensions but complicates cross-shard transactions. Multi-master setups give low-latency writes in many regions but accept conflict resolution.

#### Common Misconception

"Distributed databases are just bigger single-node databases." They are fundamentally different systems. Every operation now deals with partial failure, clock skew, and consensus. A cross-region write is not "a local write with a network hop"; it is an entirely different algorithm.

### 18.2 Replication Models

#### Primary/standby (leader/follower)

The classic setup: one **primary** handles writes. One or more **standbys** replicate from the primary and handle reads.
    
    
    Client writes → Primary
    Primary ships WAL → Standby 1, Standby 2, ...
    Clients read ← Primary OR Standbys
    

Replication is typically via shipping the WAL (Chapter 17). Each standby applies the log as it arrives.

#### Synchronous vs asynchronous

* **Synchronous replication** : primary waits for at least one standby to acknowledge the WAL before committing. Durability survives primary failure.
* **Asynchronous replication** : primary commits without waiting. Standby may lag behind; a primary failure can lose recent transactions.

Synchronous is slower (commit pays a network round-trip); asynchronous is faster but lossy on failover.

#### Read-your-own-write consistency

A subtle problem: client writes to primary, then reads from standby. Standby has not yet received the write. Client sees stale data. Solutions:

* Always read from primary (throws away the read-scaling benefit).
* Route reads from recently-writing sessions to primary for a window.
* Version-stamp writes and have standby confirm the version on read.

This is the kind of operational nuance every real distributed database must address.

#### Multi-primary (multi-master)

Every replica accepts writes. Replicas asynchronously propagate their writes to others.
    
    
    Client A writes to Replica 1
    Client B writes to Replica 2
    Eventually each replica sees both writes.
    

Gains: no single point of failure; low-latency writes in every region. Losses: concurrent writes to the same key on different replicas must be reconciled. Conflict resolution is the central problem.

Resolution strategies:

* **Last write wins** (LWW): use timestamps. Prone to clock skew.
* **Version vectors / CRDTs** : data types specifically designed to merge safely. Some are monotonic counters, sets, ordered lists; no lost updates, but only for specific semantics.
* **Application-level** : the application decides how to merge. Shopping carts (union), inventory (hand-crafted).

Examples: DynamoDB (multi-master with LWW), Cassandra (similar), CouchDB (merge with revision trees), Redis (no native multi-master; external layers).

Multi-primary is operationally harder than primary/standby. Most teams choose primary/standby unless they have strong availability or latency requirements.

#### Leaderless (Dynamo-style)

No primary. Clients write to multiple replicas; reads query multiple replicas. A **quorum** rule decides success.

If N replicas, W writes required to confirm, R reads required to query:

* `W + R > N`: reads are **guaranteed to contact at least one replica** that participated in the latest successful write. Strong consistency — _with caveats_ (below).
* `W = 1`: writes fast; `R = N`: reads slow but strong.
* `W = N`, `R = 1`: writes slow; reads fast.

Tunable consistency. Cassandra, Riak, DynamoDB all expose this.

> **The "more or less" in W+R >N: why it is not strict linearizability.** The quorum rule guarantees that any successful read quorum _overlaps_ with the preceding successful write quorum by at least one node. That overlap replica has the latest value. But:
> 
>   1. **The read may return the value from the lagging replicas** , not the up-to-date one, unless the system does **read repair** (comparing the returned values and picking the latest by timestamp/vector clock). Cassandra does read repair asynchronously; a single read that does not include the up-to-date replica in its R-set will return stale data even though the latest write landed somewhere.
>   2. **Concurrent writes without a leader cannot be linearized by W+R >N alone**. If two clients both write `x=1` and `x=2` simultaneously, each reaching W replicas, the quorum overlap sees both — and the system must merge them (LWW, CRDT, or application conflict resolution). Linearizability requires a total order, which quorum alone does not provide.
>   3. **Sloppy quorums** (Dynamo's term) relax the rule under partition: if the "home" replicas for a key are unreachable, writes go to other nodes with hinted handoff. The W+R>N guarantee does not hold during the sloppy-quorum window.
> 

> 
> The precise guarantee of W+R>N is therefore: **"if a client sees a successful write acknowledgment, a subsequent read with the same quorum configuration will _be able to find_ the latest write, provided the system performs conflict reconciliation correctly."** Strong, but weaker than linearizability in the Lamport sense. True linearizability requires consensus (Paxos / Raft, §18.6).

### 18.3 Partitioning (Sharding)

#### Definition: Partitioning

Dividing data across multiple nodes (partitions, shards) so that each row lives on exactly one partition based on a partition key.

#### Partition strategies

* **Range partitioning** : rows with keys in range [a,b)[a, b)[a,b) go to partition P1. Good for range scans; risks hotspots if keys cluster.
* **Hash partitioning** : hash(key)mod N$\text{hash}(\text{key}) \mod Nhash(key)modN picks the partition. Even load, no range scans.$
* **Directory-based** : a lookup table from key to partition. Flexible but an extra service to maintain.

#### Consistent hashing

A refinement of hash partitioning. Nodes and keys are hashed onto a ring; each key goes to the first node clockwise. Adding/removing a node only remaps a fraction of keys, not all. Dynamo, Cassandra, Redis Cluster all use variations.

#### Cross-partition operations

A query against one partition is fast and local. A query spanning partitions requires:

* **Scatter-gather** : send to all partitions, collect results.
* **Cross-partition joins** : expensive; often materialized as denormalized data or co-located partitioning.
* **Cross-partition transactions** : require 2PC or consensus (Section 18.6).

Partition choice determines which queries are fast. A bank partitioned by account_id: balance queries are local. A query for "all customers in California": scatter-gather.

Bad partition keys create hotspots: a celebrity's account, a timestamp that always increments to the same partition, a user ID that spikes during an event. Industry practice includes deliberately randomizing within the key to spread load.

#### Common Misconception

"Sharding solves scalability." Sharding solves scalability for queries that align with the partition key. For queries that don't, sharding makes everything slower than a single-node database. Get the partition key right, or suffer.

### 18.4 The CAP Theorem

Eric Brewer stated the conjecture in 2000; Gilbert and Lynch formalized it in 2002.

#### Definition: CAP theorem

In the presence of network partitions (P), a distributed system must choose between strong Consistency (C) and Availability (A). You cannot have all three simultaneously.

#### What do C, A, P mean precisely?

* **Consistency (C)** : all reads return the most recent write. Formally, this is **linearizability** — every operation appears to take effect instantaneously at some point between its invocation and response, and the operations are totally ordered. This is _not_ the same as ACID's "consistency" (which refers to constraint satisfaction) and is _not_ the same as serializability (which orders transactions, not individual operations). CAP's C is stronger than ACID's C and different from ACID's I (isolation).
* **Availability (A)** : every request receives a non-error response. The system is always up and responsive.
* **Partition tolerance (P)** : the system continues to operate despite arbitrary network failures between nodes.

> **Linearizability vs. Serializability.** These are frequently confused, even in professional literature:
> 
>   * **Linearizability** is a property of individual read/write operations: every operation appears to take effect at a single instant in real time, visible to all concurrent clients. No operation sees a "stale" value after a fresher value has been observed anywhere. It applies to single objects (a key, a register).
>   * **Serializability** is a property of multi-operation transactions: the result of running a set of transactions concurrently is equivalent to some serial order of those transactions. It applies across multiple objects and multiple operations.
> 

> 
> A system can be serializable without being linearizable (e.g., transactions are correctly ordered relative to each other, but a read may observe a stale value before a committed write propagates to all replicas). It can be linearizable on single objects without full serializability (e.g., single-key reads are always up-to-date, but multi-key transactions can exhibit write skew). Spanner provides both: it is linearizable (by TrueTime + Paxos) _and_ externally consistent (serializable across partitions). Most databases provide one or the other; very few provide both at global scale.

#### Why the theorem holds

Suppose two nodes A and B are separated by a partition. A client writes X=1 to A. A client reads X from B.

* If B returns "X = 1" (linearizability), A must have communicated with B — contradicting partition.
* If B returns "X = 0" (the old value) or the latest it knows — not consistent.
* If B refuses to answer — not available.

Under partition, you choose one of: stale read, no response, inconsistent response.

#### Not really a theorem about steady state

CAP applies _during_ a partition. When the network is healthy, systems can be strongly consistent and available. The theorem says: when a partition occurs, you must choose. A healthy system can behave either way; the designer picks the behavior.

#### CP, AP, CA systems — and why the labels mislead

* **CP** : when a partition occurs, the side that cannot reach a majority refuses to serve writes (and sometimes reads). The majority side continues, strongly consistent. The minority side is _unavailable_ until the partition heals. Examples: CockroachDB, Google Spanner, etcd, ZooKeeper, PostgreSQL with synchronous replication to a majority.
* **AP** : when a partition occurs, _every_ node continues to serve both reads and writes. The price is that different partitions may accept conflicting writes and diverge; reconciliation (last-write-wins, CRDTs, application-level merging) happens when the partition heals. Examples: Cassandra (configurable), DynamoDB, Couchbase in its eventually-consistent mode, original Dynamo.
* **CA** : only possible without partition. A single-node database is CA. "CA distributed systems" do not exist in practice because any distributed system must eventually experience a partition.

> **A common misreading of the CP classification.** Saying "CockroachDB is CP" is shorthand; it does **not** mean "CockroachDB is always available as long as it is consistent." It means: **under partition, if a node cannot form a majority quorum with its peers, that node stops serving writes.** The majority partition continues. So a CP system is _partially unavailable_ under partition — the minority partition goes dark. The distinction between CP and AP is not "one is available and one is not"; it is "what each does with the minority side of a partition." CP abandons the minority to preserve linearizability; AP keeps the minority serving but gives up linearizability.
> 
> This is why, in practice, CP systems achieve very high availability (often >99.99%): partitions large enough to cut a majority out are rare, and the common case (a single failed node) never loses majority.

#### PACELC — the refinement that matters in steady state

Daniel Abadi's 2010 extension captures a crucial nuance: "In case of **P** artition, choose **A** or **C** ; **E** lse (when there is no partition), choose **L** atency or **C** onsistency."

CAP describes what happens during partition. But partitions are rare. **Most of the time, the network is healthy** , and the design question is different: when a client writes to a replicated system, must the write be acknowledged by all replicas (high latency, strong consistency) or by the local replica only (low latency, possibly stale reads elsewhere)? PACELC classifies systems with a four-letter tag:

System | PACELC | What it means  
---|---|---  
Spanner | **PC/EC** | Partition → consistency; normal → consistency (willing to pay the TrueTime wait).  
CockroachDB | **PC/EC** | Same — consistency first, always.  
PostgreSQL (single-region sync replication) | **PC/EL** | Partition → consistency; normal → favors latency (async replicas possible).  
MongoDB (default write concern) | **PA/EL** | Partition → availability (writes continue on majority side); normal → latency (ack'd at primary).  
Cassandra (`CONSISTENCY ONE`) | **PA/EL** | Partition → availability; normal → latency (acknowledge from nearest replica).  
Cassandra (`CONSISTENCY QUORUM`) | **PA/EC** | Partition → availability _on the majority side_ ; a client talking to the minority side fails. Normal → consistency (wait for quorum ack).  
DynamoDB (default) | **PA/EL** | Partition → availability; normal → latency.  
  
**The "E" half is where most real tradeoffs happen.** "Do we wait for all three replicas to acknowledge, or just one?" determines write latency in the common case. CAP never captured this because it is framed only around partitions. PACELC makes it explicit.

#### Thread C under partition

A bank account replicated across Chicago and Tokyo. The Trans-Pacific link drops.

* **CP** : reject withdrawals in Tokyo until partition heals. The business continues but with unavailability in one region.
* **AP** : allow withdrawals in both. Both see balance 1000 USD. Each withdraws 800 USD locally; balance is 200 USD in each region. When the partition heals, the two replicas see conflicting states. Reconciliation must handle it — typically by computing the total spent (1600 USD) against the true balance (1000 USD) and forcing a corrective action (flag the account, charge overdraft).

The bank's choice reflects business priorities. Most banks choose CP — they will rather reject transactions than let customers overdraw unknowingly. Some consumer systems (like "buy now, reconcile later") choose AP.

### 18.5 Eventual Consistency

#### Definition: Eventual consistency

A weak consistency model in which, if no new writes arrive, all replicas will eventually converge to the same value.

This is the consistency guarantee of AP systems. It says nothing about _when_ replicas will agree, and nothing about what any given read might return.

#### Stronger eventual guarantees
