* **Read-your-own-writes** : you will never see a value older than what you wrote.
* **Monotonic reads** : successive reads will not go backward in time.
* **Monotonic writes** : your writes apply in order.
* **Causal consistency** : writes that logically depend on prior reads are seen in order.

These are intermediate between eventual and strong; many systems support them through client-side tracking.

#### Conflict-free replicated data types (CRDTs)

A data type that can be merged deterministically regardless of update order. Examples:

* **G-Counter** (grow-only counter): increment-only; merge = take max of each replica's count.
* **OR-Set** (observed-remove set): tombstones preserve deletion order.
* **LWW-Register** : last-writer-wins with timestamps.

CRDTs solve the merge problem for specific data shapes. They're not general-purpose — you cannot store arbitrary application state as a CRDT — but when the data fits, they eliminate the "which write wins" question.

Redis's CRDB offers CRDTs; Riak had them; Automerge and Yjs power collaborative editing (Google Docs, Figma use variations).

### 18.6 Distributed Transactions and Consensus

When a transaction modifies data on multiple partitions and must succeed or fail atomically, we need **distributed transactions**.

#### Two-phase commit (2PC)

A coordinator orchestrates a vote:

  1. **Prepare phase** : coordinator asks each participant "can you commit?" Each participant writes a PREPARED record in its WAL and answers yes or no.
  2. **Commit phase** : if all yes, coordinator tells all participants to COMMIT. If any no, coordinator tells all to ABORT.

Guarantees atomicity across partitions. But:

* **Blocking** : if the coordinator crashes after phase 1, participants are stuck in PREPARED state with locks held. They can't commit without the coordinator; can't abort either.
* **Latency** : every commit costs two round trips plus WAL syncs on every participant.
* **No availability during coordinator failure**.

2PC is still used (many DBMSs implement it for cross-database XA transactions) but is not preferred in modern distributed systems.

#### Three-phase commit (3PC)

Adds an intermediate "pre-commit" phase to reduce blocking. Works under some failure models but not arbitrary network partitions. Rarely used in practice.

#### Consensus protocols — Raft, Paxos

For availability _and_ consistency, we need algorithms that make progress under failures. **Paxos** (Lamport, 1990) and **Raft** (Ongaro/Ousterhout, 2014) are the dominant solutions.

Both elect a leader from a quorum of nodes; committed log entries are replicated to a majority; leader changes are orderly. If a minority of nodes fails or partitions away, the majority continues.

Spanner, CockroachDB, etcd, Consul, ZooKeeper — all use Paxos variants or Raft. Modern distributed databases build on these primitives.

Consensus gives strong consistency at the cost of latency (replicate to majority before commit). It is the backbone of every CP-by-design distributed database.

#### Thread C in Spanner-like system

Account X in three replicas. Customer withdraws 800 USD. The write is routed to the leader, which proposes the change; the change is replicated to two followers; when majority (2 of 3) acknowledges, the leader commits; the reply returns.

If one replica is partitioned: the other two still form a majority. Writes continue. Strong consistency maintained.

If two replicas are partitioned: majority no longer formed. Writes _block_. CP chose availability loss.

### 18.7 Google Spanner — A Concrete CP System

Spanner, Google's geographically distributed database, achieves strong consistency across continents. Its secret: **TrueTime** , a globally-synchronized clock with bounded uncertainty.

Each transaction gets a commit timestamp in TrueTime's uncertainty interval. A transaction must wait out the uncertainty before acknowledging commit — that is, it must wait until its timestamp is guaranteed to be in the past. Typical wait: 7 ms.

> **Where does the 7 ms come from?** TrueTime's uncertainty bound ε has two main contributors:
> 
>   1. **Network latency to the TrueTime master (~1–4 ms, dominant)** : each data center has a handful of TrueTime _master_ servers that are directly connected to GPS receivers and atomic clocks. A Spanner node contacts its local TrueTime master to get a timestamp bound. The intra-data-center network round trip is typically 1–4 ms. This is the biggest contributor to ε.
> 
>   2. **Clock oscillator drift between masters (~ < 1 ms)**: GPS receivers achieve ~100 ns absolute accuracy; the high-stability oscillators (OCXO/atomic resonator) in the TrueTime masters drift at ~1 μs/second or less, introducing negligible additional uncertainty when polled frequently. GPS/atomic clock precision is well under 1 ms — far smaller than the network latency term.
> 
> 

> 
> Together, these produce an average ε of ~4 ms and a worst-case of ~7 ms in Google's data centers. Google's implementation targets ε < 7 ms as an operational bound; during GPS signal loss, the daemon falls back to local atomic clocks and if ε exceeds the limit, the daemon panics and blocks commits rather than risk violating the guarantee.
> 
> The consequence of a larger ε is a longer commit wait — if ε spikes to 10 ms, commits slow accordingly. This is why Google monitors ε carefully and over-provisions TrueTime masters. HLC-based systems (CockroachDB, YugabyteDB) avoid this by using logical clocks and consensus timestamps; they do not have the commit-wait overhead, but also do not get Spanner's "one-shot global read at a wall-clock time" capability.

This gives Spanner linearizability without two-phase commit for single-partition operations, and with structured 2PC for cross-partition. The cost: specialized hardware (atomic clocks + GPS receivers in every data center) and disciplined operations. Few organizations can replicate it.

Spanner's approach demonstrates that CP in the cross-continent setting is technically possible; it just requires very tight engineering. Most systems accept eventual consistency because the engineering cost is too high.

#### Why commit-wait is the key trick

A subtle but crucial point: how does Spanner avoid the usual CP → high-latency tradeoff? It does not avoid it — it transforms it. Traditional consensus-based CP systems (CockroachDB, etcd) round-trip to a majority of replicas _before_ acknowledging commit, adding network latency. Spanner also round-trips Paxos to majority, but it _additionally_ waits for its timestamp to pass the TrueTime uncertainty window. The two costs overlap: while waiting for Paxos acks, the clock is ticking toward certainty. The visible commit latency is max(Paxos round trip, TrueTime ε) rather than their sum.

What Spanner _buys_ with this machinery is **externally-consistent reads without coordination**. A reader anywhere in the world can, with a single local read, know whether a transaction committed before some absolute wall-clock time — no round-trip to a leader, no quorum read. For global analytics over transactional data, this is transformative. For pure OLTP workloads, the gain is smaller, which is why CockroachDB and YugabyteDB — which use HLC (hybrid logical clocks) instead of TrueTime — compete effectively without the specialized hardware.

#### The consensus latency floor

Any CP system that replicates across regions pays a **latency floor** equal to the round-trip time to the furthest replica in the majority quorum. For three regions (e.g., Virginia, Ireland, Tokyo), the majority is any two; Virginia↔Ireland is ~80 ms, Virginia↔Tokyo is ~150 ms. A write reaching the two closest replicas commits in ~80 ms. This floor is **physical** — bounded by the speed of light in fiber — and cannot be optimized away. It is _the_ reason CP is rarely used for latency-sensitive global workloads.

AP systems do not have this floor because they acknowledge writes from the nearest replica only, accepting the consequence of stale reads at distant replicas. The choice between PC/EC and PA/EL often reduces to: "can my users tolerate 80+ ms write latency?"

### 18.8 Operational Reality of Distribution

Distribution adds new failure modes every developer must internalize.

* **Split-brain** : two nodes each believe they are the leader. Usually caused by ambiguous failure detection. Solutions: fencing tokens, explicit quorum-based leadership.
* **Clock skew** : two nodes disagree on time. Can cause timestamps to mis-order events. NTP synchronization helps but only to ~10 ms; Spanner's TrueTime is the only industrial-strength fix.
* **Slow nodes** : one replica responds slowly, dragging down quorum. Health checks and timeouts matter.
* **Back pressure** : one partition is overloaded; the cluster needs to shed load gracefully rather than cascading failures.
* **Failover drills** : the DBA team must regularly practice primary failovers. Untested failover often fails when needed.

Distributed systems are not a drop-in replacement for single-node systems. They introduce an entire category of operational concerns that single-node DBAs never face.

### 18.9 Choosing the Right Distribution Model

A quick decision tree for a new system:

  1. **Will data fit in one machine now and next year?** → Single node with replication for availability.
  2. **Do queries follow a natural partition key (user_id, tenant_id)?** → Shard. Each partition is effectively single-node; minimal distributed complexity.
  3. **Do writes need strong consistency across regions?** → CP distributed system (Spanner, CockroachDB, YugabyteDB). Pay the latency.
  4. **Can we tolerate eventual consistency?** → AP system (Cassandra, DynamoDB). Free bidirectional writes; cheap.
  5. **Is this OLAP / analytics?** → Specialized column-store with eventual or batch consistency (BigQuery, Redshift, Snowflake).

Thread C's bank: option 3. Expected latency: 10–20 ms per commit across regions. Acceptable for banking workflows but not for chat applications.

Thread A's university: probably option 1 or 2. The registration database fits on one machine; a primary + standby for failover is sufficient.

Thread B's analytical join: option 5. A column-store where consistency is per-day is fine for analytics.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **CAP forces a choice between consistency and availability under partition.** When the network splits, you either refuse requests to keep replicas in sync (CP) or answer them knowing replicas may disagree (AP); partition tolerance is not optional in any real distributed system.
* **PACELC extends the cost to normal operation.** Even with a healthy network, a strongly consistent write must cross a quorum of replicas, so systems that prize low latency deliberately weaken consistency during steady state, not just during partition.
* **Single-leader replication trades write scalability for operational simplicity.** One writer means no write conflicts and easy reasoning, but the leader is a throughput ceiling and a failover hazard; multi-leader lifts the ceiling at the cost of conflict resolution.
* **Leaderless (Dynamo-style) tunability trades clean semantics for knobs.** `R + W > N` gives strong reads when you want them and fast reads when you don't, but clients now carry the responsibility of choosing per-operation consistency — a constant source of subtle bugs.
* **Synchronous replication trades commit latency for RPO=0.** Waiting for the standby acknowledgment pays a network round-trip on every commit in exchange for losing no committed data on failover; asynchronous replication reverses the bargain.
* **Consensus (Raft/Paxos) trades latency for non-blocking progress.** Replicating each write to a majority costs a round-trip but survives minority failures without human intervention — unlike 2PC, which blocks on coordinator failure.
* **Sharding trades cross-partition expressiveness for horizontal write scale.** A good partition key makes most queries local and linear; a bad one makes cross-shard joins and transactions the hot path and eats every scalability gain.

### 18.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. State the CAP theorem in one sentence. Under what condition does the tradeoff apply?
  2. Distinguish primary/standby replication from multi-primary. Name one scenario where each is preferable.
  3. Explain "eventual consistency" precisely. Does it forbid any data loss?
  4. What is a quorum? For N=5 replicas with W=3 and R=3, is the system strongly consistent? Why?
  5. Describe two-phase commit. What can go wrong if the coordinator crashes between phase 1 and phase 2?
  6. What is a CRDT, and why is it useful for multi-master replication?
  7. Summarize PACELC in one sentence.

#### Application Problems

  8. A social media feed replicates across three continents. Writes are local; reads see any replica. Describe: which consistency guarantees you can offer, which anomalies are possible, and how you might inform users when their post is visible.

  9. A bank with two data centers replicates account balances synchronously. A network partition isolates one data center. Describe the user experience in both data centers under a CP strategy and an AP strategy.

  10. A shopping cart stored in Cassandra uses LWW for conflict resolution. Describe a scenario where a user loses an item from their cart silently. Propose a conflict-free schema for the cart.

  11. A payment system uses MongoDB with majority-write-concern (waits for writes to be on majority replicas). Under what partition scenarios does it remain available? When does it stop accepting writes?

Hint

"Majority" means strictly more than half the replicas. For a given replica count N, which partitions still leave a majority reachable from the primary? Which partitions isolate the primary from more than half the set, and what does the primary do when it notices?

  12. A video streaming service has 100 million users. Each user's watch history grows linearly with use. Propose a partitioning strategy. Which queries are local; which are scatter-gather?

  13. Design a quorum configuration for a distributed database with 7 replicas that must survive 3 simultaneous failures without data loss. What values of N, W, R satisfy this?

Hint

"Survives 3 failures" means writes must still reach enough live replicas to be durable, and reads must still see the latest write. Translate those two conditions into inequalities on W and R given N=7, then check that `R + W > N` also holds.

  14. An e-commerce site replicates across US-East and US-West. A partition lasts 10 minutes. During the partition, each region processes 10,000 orders. Describe the reconciliation problem that arises when the partition heals.

Hint

Distinguish between orders that touch disjoint data (each user only ordered in one region) and orders that touch shared data (inventory counts, coupon usage, uniqueness constraints). Which class is trivial to merge? Which class may have irreconcilable conflicts, and what business rule decides the winner?

#### Think Deeper

  15. Why does the CAP theorem say you must give up _one_ of C/A/P but most real systems give up _partial_ amounts of each? Frame this in terms of PACELC and degrees of availability.

  16. Spanner uses TrueTime — a specialized clock service with bounded uncertainty. Design an alternative that achieves strong consistency without physical clocks. What does your design trade off?

  17. A startup argues "we're small; we don't need distribution." Two years later they are down for 12 hours because their single server's disk failed. Design a minimal availability story that would have avoided the downtime. What is the first step?

  18. Consider a system with multi-master replication across three regions and LWW conflict resolution. Construct a sequence of writes where the "correct" final value is lost despite no failures. What does this say about LWW?

  19. Two-phase commit has a blocking failure mode. Paxos/Raft avoid it by requiring majority consensus for leadership changes. Why can the majority always make progress? What is the cost of "no leader for a brief moment" in a Paxos system?

Hint

Think about why two majorities of the same set must overlap in at least one node. What does that overlapping node remember from the previous leader's writes, and how does the new leader use it? Then consider what clients experience during the election window — rejects, retries, or something else?

  20. Thread C's bank account at scale: 100 million accounts replicated across 5 global regions, each expecting 5000 USD worth of traffic per day. Design the partitioning and replication strategy. Estimate the commit latency per region. Which of CAP do you give up, and why is that the right choice for a bank?

Hint

Start by deciding where each account "lives" — is partitioning by account ID enough, or do you need a home region per account? Then compute the commit round-trip: how many replicas must ack, and what is the speed-of-light floor between the furthest two? Finally, ask what the bank loses if it picks AP and whether regulators would accept it.

### Chapter Summary

**Distribution** is motivated by scale, availability, and geography — each with different design implications. **Replication** (primary/standby, multi-primary, leaderless) keeps copies of data across machines; **partitioning** (sharding) splits the dataset across machines.

The **CAP theorem** states that under network partition, a distributed system must choose between **consistency** and **availability**. **PACELC** refines this to include the latency cost during normal operation. Real systems are classified as **CP** (relational DBs, Spanner, CockroachDB) or **AP** (Cassandra, DynamoDB); no truly distributed CA system exists because partitions are inevitable.

**Eventual consistency** is the weak baseline of AP systems; stronger guarantees — read-your-writes, monotonic reads, causal — are available through client-side tracking. **CRDTs** provide conflict-free merging for specific data shapes.

**Distributed transactions** cross partitions; **two-phase commit** gives atomicity but has blocking failures. **Consensus protocols** (Paxos, Raft) underpin modern strongly-consistent distributed databases, making progress under minority failures.

Thread C's bank, replicated across continents, must choose CP (reject withdrawals during partition) or AP (allow both, reconcile later). Most banks choose CP. Thread A's university is fine on a single node. Thread B's analytics fits best in a column-store warehouse.

Chapter 19 examines one such store: **column-oriented OLAP systems** like DuckDB, ClickHouse, and Snowflake, which fundamentally reorganize storage for analytical queries and can answer Thread B's original 20-minute query in milliseconds on a column store — for completely different reasons than indexes.

---

## Chapter 19: Column Stores and OLAP

Every chapter before this one, at the physical level, has discussed **row-oriented** storage. A row of a table is a contiguous byte sequence in a page; rows are the unit of I/O; indexes point at rows. This is the right choice for OLTP: the transactional workload fetches whole rows, updates a few attributes, and moves on. Row-orientation matches the access pattern.

But the OLTP assumption does not fit analytical workloads. An OLAP query like
    
    
    SELECT country, AVG(price) FROM sales WHERE year = 2025 GROUP BY country;
    

reads three columns (`country`, `price`, `year`) out of perhaps 50. On row storage, the engine must read the entire row — all 50 columns' worth of bytes — even though 94% of them are ignored. If the table is 10 GB, you read 10 GB just to get 600 MB of useful data. The bandwidth waste is enormous.

**Column-oriented** storage addresses this directly. Each column is stored together, in a separate file or page range. Reading three columns means reading three column files, not 50. The I/O shrinks proportionally. Combine that with specialized compression, vectorized execution, and optional in-memory formats, and the same query that takes 20 minutes on a row store runs in 100 milliseconds on a column store.

This chapter explores the column-store revolution, why it happened, how it works, and which systems use it. Thread B's 20-minute query finds its third answer — not indexes, not optimization, not query rewriting, but a _storage layout_ that matches the analytical workload.

### 19.1 Row-Store vs Column-Store — The Physical Difference

Consider a tiny table:
    
    
    Orders (order_id INT, customer_id INT, product_id INT, price DECIMAL, date DATE, country CHAR(2))
    

Four rows:
    
    
    1 | 42 | 7 | 19.99 | 2025-01-15 | US
    2 | 17 | 7 | 29.99 | 2025-01-15 | DE
    3 | 42 | 5 | 19.99 | 2025-02-01 | US
    4 |  8 | 9 |  9.99 | 2025-02-02 | JP
    

#### Row-store layout

Pages hold complete rows:
    
    
    Page 1:
      [1, 42, 7, 19.99, 2025-01-15, US]
      [2, 17, 7, 29.99, 2025-01-15, DE]
      [3, 42, 5, 19.99, 2025-02-01, US]
      [4,  8, 9,  9.99, 2025-02-02, JP]
    

A query that wants just `country` still reads all page bytes.

#### Column-store layout

Each column in its own region:
    
    
    order_id:    [1, 2, 3, 4]
    customer_id: [42, 17, 42, 8]
    product_id:  [7, 7, 5, 9]
    price:       [19.99, 29.99, 19.99, 9.99]
    date:        [2025-01-15, 2025-01-15, 2025-02-01, 2025-02-02]
    country:     [US, DE, US, JP]
    

A query for `country` reads only the country region. A query for `country, AVG(price)` reads country and price. Nothing else.

#### The implication at scale

Orders table: 10 million rows, 50 columns, 10 GB total (~200 bytes per row). Query: `SELECT country, AVG(price) FROM Orders WHERE year = 2025 GROUP BY country;`

* **Row store** : reads 10 GB.
* **Column store** : reads 3 columns out of 50. Country + price + year ≈ 600 MB. 17× less I/O.

At ~3 GB/s SSD bandwidth: 3.3 seconds row-store, 200 ms column-store. Plus compression (next section) can take column-store down to ~50 MB for these three columns; ~15 ms.

#### Common Misconception

"Column store is faster because of compression." Compression helps, but the _primary_ win is I/O elimination — reading only the needed columns. Compression is a secondary amplifier that turns a 10× win into a 50× win.

### 19.2 Compression — Why It Works on Columns

Columns compress radically better than rows because all values in a column are of the same type and often share structure.

#### Run-length encoding (RLE)

A sorted column like `date` in our Orders table is often runs of the same value.
    
    
    date: [2025-01-15, 2025-01-15, 2025-02-01, 2025-02-02]
    

Encoded as:
    
    
    (2025-01-15, 2) (2025-02-01, 1) (2025-02-02, 1)
    

Saves enormously for date columns, categorical columns, status flags — anywhere consecutive rows tend to match.

#### Dictionary encoding

For columns with few distinct values, build a dictionary:
    
    
    country values: [US, DE, US, JP]
    Dictionary: {0: US, 1: DE, 2: JP}
    Stored column: [0, 1, 0, 2]
    

`country` used 2 bytes per row; now uses log₂(distinct_count) bits. Average reduction: 3–10× for text-ish categorical data.

#### Bit-packing

An integer column with values 0 to 7 fits in 3 bits. Instead of 4 bytes per value:
    
    
    [3, 7, 1, 5, 2, 6, 0, 4]
    

pack into 3 bytes total. For low-cardinality integer IDs, savings are 4–10×.

#### Frame-of-reference / delta encoding

Column values are close to each other: instead of storing the raw values, store deltas from a base.
    
    
    timestamps: [1700000000, 1700000005, 1700000012, 1700000018]
    Base: 1700000000
    Deltas: [0, 5, 12, 18]
    

Deltas are small; fit in fewer bits.

#### The combined effect

A well-designed column store combines these techniques per column, automatically choosing based on data characteristics. Reported compression ratios on real datasets: 5–20×. A 1 TB row-store table becomes 50–200 GB in a column store. Cloud warehouses sell on this property alone.

#### Vectorized decompression

Chapter 11 introduced vectorized execution — processing batches of 1024 tuples at a time. Compressed columns can often be decompressed _into_ vector registers without materializing the full decoded column. SIMD instructions apply predicates on compressed data directly (comparisons on dictionary codes, for instance). Another 2–5× speedup.

### 19.3 The Cost: Writes

Column stores are superb at read-heavy workloads. They pay for it on writes.

#### Inserting a row

In a column store, inserting one row means appending one value to each of N columns. Each column lives in its own file/region. Naively: N page writes for one row. In row store: 1 page write for one row.

Real column stores mitigate this:

* **Batch inserts** : only allow inserts in large batches (typical: 10,000+ rows). Each batch becomes a new "row group" containing N per-column buffers.
* **Delta stores** : buffer recent inserts in a small row-store table; periodically convert to columnar. Queries union-scan both.
* **Append-only semantics** : no UPDATE, no DELETE — only INSERT or re-creation. Makes storage much simpler.

#### Updates and deletes

Columnar data is compressed and ordered for analytical access. Updating a single row means decompressing the row group, modifying one value, recompressing. Expensive.

Two common strategies:

* **Logical delete + insert** : mark the old row as deleted; append a new row. Deletes via a "visibility bitmap." Periodic compaction rewrites without deleted rows.
* **Immutable data + time travel** : every "update" creates a new immutable file; the system tracks which file is current. Examples: Snowflake's time-travel feature, Delta Lake, Iceberg.

For OLTP workloads (many small updates), these costs are prohibitive. Column stores are not a replacement for row stores in transactional settings.

#### Tradeoff

* **Row store** : fast writes (one page per row), slow analytical reads (whole row per access).
* **Column store** : slow writes (one page per column), fast analytical reads (only needed columns).

The workload determines which is right. Modern "HTAP" systems (Hybrid Transactional/Analytical) combine both — the next section.

### 19.4 Modern Column-Oriented Systems

#### DuckDB

An in-process column store (think "SQLite for analytics"). Single file, library-style, runs inside your Python/Jupyter process. Vectorized execution, compressed storage. Designed for up to tens of GB on a single machine. Very widely used in data science.

#### ClickHouse

Open-source distributed column store from Yandex. Optimized for time-series and log analytics. Handles petabyte-scale. Writes append via "MergeTree" (a log-structured store that periodically compacts).

#### Snowflake, BigQuery, Redshift

Cloud data warehouses. Storage is columnar (in variants of Parquet or proprietary formats). Compute and storage are decoupled — you pay for storage once, spin up compute clusters to query. All three support full SQL and transactions; all three are analytical.

#### Apache Parquet, ORC, Arrow

Not databases but **columnar file formats**. Parquet is the de facto standard for columnar files in data lakes. ORC (Optimized Row Columnar) is similar. Arrow is the in-memory columnar format; many engines use Arrow for exchange.

These formats are not bound to one engine — DuckDB, Spark, Polars, Presto, Trino all read Parquet directly. This is a major shift: storage is commoditized, compute is chosen per-query.

#### PostgreSQL extensions

Citus and Hydra add columnar storage to PostgreSQL for specific tables. The same database serves both OLTP (row-store tables) and OLAP (column-store tables). HTAP within one engine.

#### Vertica and the HP heritage

The original commercial column store, developed by database pioneers. Still in use at enterprises. Architectural template that many modern systems follow.

### 19.5 Column Stores and MVCC

How does MVCC work in a column store?

#### Version columns

Add xmin and xmax to every row group. Transactional read filters by snapshot visibility. The per-column storage can include a hidden "visibility" column.

#### Immutable storage + deletion bitmaps

Most modern column stores use immutable file segments. Deletes are logged in a bitmap ("row 37 of segment 12 is deleted as of xid 4291"). Queries read data + apply bitmap filter. Old data survives for time-travel queries; periodically garbage-collected.

> **Garbage collection is itself a write.** In a row store, vacuuming dead row versions modifies individual heap pages in place — targeted writes to specific pages. In a column store, there is no "in place" update. When a row group contains too many deleted rows to be worth keeping, the garbage collector must **rewrite the entire row group** — reading all surviving rows from every column in the group and writing new column files. For a row group with 100,000 rows and 50 columns of 8 bytes each, that is ~40 MB of reads and ~40 MB of writes, even if only a handful of rows were deleted. This makes column-store GC an expensive batch operation, not an incremental background task. Systems like Snowflake and Delta Lake schedule GC as periodic maintenance jobs (`VACUUM` in Delta Lake, retention-based expiry in Snowflake) rather than as continuous background work. The operational implication: deleting a row from a column store may not visibly reduce storage until the next GC cycle rewrites the affected row groups.

#### Compare with row-store MVCC

Row-store MVCC (Chapter 16) mixes versions within heap pages. Column-store MVCC typically separates data (immutable) from visibility metadata (mutable bitmap). This matches the column-store pattern: data written once, read many times, updated rarely.

Time-travel queries — "show me this table as of yesterday at 3 PM" — become natural: just apply the bitmap as of that time.

### 19.6 Thread B — The 20-Minute Query on a Column Store

Thread B's query:
    
    
    SELECT s.name, c.title, i.name
    FROM Students s
    JOIN Enrollments e ON s.id = e.student_id
    JOIN Courses c ON e.course_id = c.id
    JOIN Instructors i ON c.instructor_id = i.id
    WHERE s.year = 4;
    

In a row store without indexes: 20 minutes. In a row store with indexes: 2 seconds. In a column store: ~100 ms on a modest machine.

#### Why

* Column stores read only needed columns. For this query: `s.name`, `s.year`, `s.id`, `e.student_id`, `e.course_id`, `c.id`, `c.title`, `c.instructor_id`, `i.id`, `i.name`. Perhaps 10 columns out of 30+. Each column ≈ 8 bytes per row.
* Data is compressed. Dictionary-encoded `year` column: 800,000 rows × 2 bits = 200 KB.
* Vectorized execution: predicates on year column applied SIMD over dictionary codes. 100 MB/s of throughput per core turns into 10 GB/s of logical tuples.
* Hash joins run on dictionary-coded keys. No text comparisons; integer equality on compressed codes.

A modern machine — 32 cores, SSD, 64 GB RAM — answers this query on a 100 MB columnar representation in < 100 ms. The row-store with indexes (Chapter 10) answered in 2 seconds, which is already very fast; the column store is another 20× on top.

#### Why not use column stores for everything?

Writes. A single-row INSERT on a column store with 50 columns requires 50 column-region updates. Batching mitigates this but latency for individual inserts is high. OLTP workloads (one row per user request) are poor fits. Column stores shine on bulk loads and immutable data.

### 19.7 OLAP Queries and Workload Characteristics

#### Definition: OLAP (Online Analytical Processing)

Workloads characterized by:

* Large scans over big tables.
* Aggregations and groupings.
* Few concurrent users (relative to OLTP).
* Batch or interactive analytics.
* Read-heavy, write-rare.

#### Definition: OLTP (Online Transaction Processing)

Workloads characterized by:

* Small, point-located queries.
* Reads and writes of individual rows.
* Many concurrent users.
* Strict latency requirements.
* Mixed read/write.

Row stores fit OLTP. Column stores fit OLAP. Many real systems have both workloads — "HTAP" or "operational analytics" — requiring either two databases (replicated) or one database with both row and column formats.

#### Star schemas and snowflake schemas

OLAP databases often use a **star schema** : one large **fact table** (sales, events, measurements) surrounded by smaller **dimension tables** (customers, products, dates). Fact tables grow fast; dimension tables are small and relatively static.
    
    
    Fact: Sales(date_id, product_id, customer_id, store_id, quantity, price)
    Dimensions: Dates, Products, Customers, Stores
    

Queries join the fact table to dimensions, filter, and aggregate. Column stores are particularly well-tuned for this — the fact table is the target of most I/O, and star-schema queries access only a few dimension columns per query.

**Snowflake schemas** normalize dimensions further (dates → year, month, week; products → category, subcategory). Less common; adds joins.

### 19.8 Aggregations and Materialized Views in OLAP

Analytical queries are expensive. A common optimization: **pre-compute** aggregates.

#### Materialized aggregates

Store `daily_sales_by_country` as a separate table, refreshed nightly. Queries read the small aggregate table instead of scanning the fact.

Column stores often extend this to **cubes** — precomputed aggregates across many group-by combinations — and to **streaming incremental refresh** , where new fact rows update aggregates continuously.

#### Auto-materialized views

Modern warehouses (Snowflake, BigQuery) sometimes detect common query patterns and automatically materialize them. Transparent to the user; periodically refreshed.

#### Tradeoff

Precomputation makes reads instant but requires refresh. Staleness is the cost. A real-time dashboard refreshed every 30 seconds might use a continuous pipeline; a monthly board report uses a nightly batch.

### 19.9 Partitioning in OLAP

OLAP tables are often partitioned by **time** : one partition per day, week, or month. A query with `WHERE date BETWEEN ...` reads only the relevant partitions (**partition pruning** , Chapter 12).
    
    
    Partition 2025-01:
      [january data]
    Partition 2025-02:
      [february data]
    ...
    

Benefits:

* Queries touching recent data ignore old partitions.
* Old partitions can be archived or compressed more aggressively.
* Bulk-drop: `DROP PARTITION 2020-01` is O(1), much faster than `DELETE ... WHERE date < ...`.

Partitioning is the reason you can query 10 years of time-series data in a few hundred milliseconds: the engine reads only the last week's partitions.

### 19.10 When to Use a Column Store

* **Pure analytics on large data** : data warehouses, BI dashboards, log analysis. Clear win.
* **Mixed workload with high read ratio** : consider HTAP (Postgres + column extension, or SQL Server columnstore indexes).
* **Write-heavy OLTP** : stay with row store.
* **Time-series data** : column stores with time-partitioning are the best option.
* **Data lake exploration** : Parquet files on object storage, queried by DuckDB or Spark. No database server needed.

#### Common Misconception

"Column stores replace row stores." They are complements. Amazon runs its consumer site on row-store databases (DynamoDB, Aurora) and reports/analytics on Redshift (column). Most serious data systems combine both.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Column-oriented storage trades write-path efficiency for read-path selectivity.** Scanning only the columns a query needs eliminates most I/O for analytical workloads; the cost is that inserting one row touches every column region, so writes are an order of magnitude slower than in a row store.
* **Heavy compression trades CPU decode cost for bandwidth.** Dictionary, RLE, bit-packing, and delta encoding shrink column bytes by 5–20×, and modern SIMD pipelines decode on the fly — but queries that actually need to materialize many distinct raw values spend real CPU, and compression tuning becomes part of the schema design.
* **Vectorized execution trades per-tuple generality for batch throughput.** Processing 1024 tuples at a time amortizes function-call and instruction-decode overhead; queries with heavy per-tuple branching, user-defined functions, or scalar iterators lose some of this win.
* **Immutable files plus deletion bitmaps trade update latency for append-only simplicity.** Rewriting a row means re-encoding a row group, so column stores favor logical delete + insert with periodic compaction — great for bulk loads and time travel, painful for OLTP-style small updates.
* **OLAP layout trades OLTP performance for analytical performance.** Column stores are not a drop-in replacement for row stores; real systems usually keep both and connect them with CDC or ETL, paying for two engines because neither covers the whole workload.
* **Time-partitioning trades metadata complexity for partition pruning.** One partition per day or month lets queries skip 99% of historical data and makes retention a metadata drop, at the cost of managing hundreds of partitions and watching out for small-partition overhead.

### 19.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Explain the fundamental difference between row-oriented and column-oriented storage with an example.
  2. Name three compression techniques commonly used in column stores and give an example column type each is good for.
  3. Why do column stores pay a cost on writes? Describe one mitigation strategy.
  4. What is a fact table in a star schema? Why are fact tables good candidates for columnar storage?
  5. How does MVCC work differently in a column store compared to a row store?
  6. Describe partition pruning and give an example of a query that benefits.

#### Application Problems

  7. A 10 TB table has 100 columns; a typical analytical query reads 5 columns. Estimate the I/O cost on row storage and column storage. Include compression assumptions.

Hint

Row storage must read the entire table regardless of which columns the query needs. Column storage reads a fraction proportional to the columns selected, then shrinks further by a compression ratio. What fraction of 10 TB does the column store actually read under a realistic 5× compression assumption?

  8. You have a time-series table of sensor readings: `(sensor_id, timestamp, value, battery, temperature)` — 1 billion rows/day. Design a storage layout and partitioning scheme. Which database would you use?

  9. Thread B's 20-minute query: describe step by step why it runs in ~100 ms on a column store. Identify the three largest savings (I/O reduction, compression, vectorization) in percent of total time.

  10. A star-schema data warehouse has a Sales fact table (10 TB) and five dimension tables (10 MB each). A typical query joins Sales to two dimensions. Why are dimension tables often kept in memory, while the fact table is on disk?

  11. A startup is building a user-event analytics dashboard with 10M events/day. Recent data is hot (last 7 days); old data is cold (up to a year). Design a storage strategy that optimizes both query latency and cost.

Hint

Think in two tiers. What engine or format serves sub-second queries on the last 7 days, and what cheaper layout holds the other 358 days where query latency can be seconds? How does data move from hot to cold, and how does a single dashboard query transparently hit the right tier?

  12. A data scientist loads a 50 GB CSV into DuckDB for analysis. Queries run 10× faster than on her PostgreSQL OLTP server with the same CPU. Explain the specific architectural differences driving the speedup.

  13. You need to update one row in a 1 TB column-store table. Describe what the engine must do and roughly how expensive it is compared to the same update on a row-store.

Hint

Columns are compressed in row groups, and row groups are immutable in most modern engines. What must happen to update one value inside a row group — in place, or via the "logical delete + insert" pattern? Compare the bytes touched and the write amplification against a row store's single page update.

#### Think Deeper

  14. HTAP systems promise row-level OLTP performance and column-level OLAP performance in one engine. Describe architecturally how this is achieved (not specific products, but general approaches).

  15. Parquet files are immutable once written. How does a data lake handle updates? Compare the approaches of Delta Lake, Iceberg, and Hudi at a high level.

  16. A column store scans 1 GB of compressed dictionary-encoded data to answer a query. If the underlying data would have been 10 GB uncompressed, and the CPU decompression rate is 2 GB/s, estimate the query time on a 3 GB/s disk versus a 10 GB/s network attached storage. Which is the bottleneck?

Hint

Compute three independent times: how long to read 1 GB from each storage, and how long CPU takes to decompress 1 GB into 10 GB of logical data at 2 GB/s. The slowest of these three caps the query. Which storage medium moves the bottleneck off disk and onto the CPU?

  17. Argue that "big data" systems like Hadoop were successful for a specific reason: they normalized the column-store + columnar-file + distributed-query pattern and made it runnable on commodity hardware. What would have to be true for the pattern to have succeeded as a _closed_ proprietary product?

  18. Modern cloud warehouses (Snowflake, BigQuery) separate compute and storage. Explain why this separation is compatible with column-store architecture but awkward for row-store OLTP systems.

  19. Design a system where row-store tables and column-store tables coexist, with a single query engine serving both. What architectural primitives (query planner, storage manager, transaction coordinator) need adjustment?

  20. Thread B's query in a column store runs in 100 ms. Suppose the dataset grows 1000× (billion rows). Would the column-store time scale linearly (100 seconds)? Or is there a scaling mechanism that keeps it closer to 100 ms? (Hint: partitioning, parallelism.)

Hint

Work through which parts of the query scan 1000× more data and which do not. If you add 1000 cores of parallelism, what fraction of the work parallelizes cleanly? What parts — merging aggregates, final sort, network shuffles — refuse to scale past some bound, and how do they set the floor on latency?

### Chapter Summary

**Column-oriented storage** reorganizes tables so that each column lives together on disk. For **analytical** queries — which typically read a few columns out of many — column stores reduce I/O by factors of 10–100× relative to row stores. Combined with **columnar compression** (RLE, dictionary, bit-packing, delta) and **vectorized execution** , the speedup often exceeds 20× end-to-end.

The cost is on **writes**. Inserting one row means writing N column regions; updating or deleting a row is expensive. Column stores are **write-once, read-many** in design. Mitigations include batched inserts, delta stores, immutable files with deletion bitmaps, and compaction.

**Modern column stores** include DuckDB (embeddable analytical engine), ClickHouse (distributed log/time-series analytics), Snowflake/BigQuery/Redshift (cloud warehouses), and columnar file formats like Parquet that decouple storage from compute.

**Star schemas** with a large fact table and small dimensions suit column stores well; **time-based partitioning** lets queries prune old data quickly. **Materialized aggregates** precompute expensive rollups.

Thread B's 20-minute query, which became 2 seconds with indexes and 200 ms with a good optimizer in Chapter 12, answers in **100 ms on a column store** — for completely different architectural reasons. Each chapter has offered a different lens on the same question; collectively they explain the diversity of database designs.

Chapters 20–21 explore the rest of the non-relational landscape: **document stores** , **key-value stores** , **wide-column** stores, and **LSM-tree** -based engines. Chapter 22 asks how to choose between all of them — **polyglot persistence**.

---

## Chapter 20: Document and Key-Value Stores

The relational model, for all its elegance, forces the application to squeeze its data into tables and foreign keys. That works beautifully for the university registration system. It works awkwardly for a product catalog where one product has ten optional attributes and another has three. It works poorly for a cache where the only thing you need is "give me the value for this key, fast." It does not work well for a graph of social connections.

Non-relational databases arose from these friction points. Three major families dominate in the 2020s: **key-value stores** (Redis, Memcached, DynamoDB as KV), **document stores** (MongoDB, CouchDB), and **wide-column stores** (Cassandra, HBase — covered in Chapter 21). Each family trades parts of the relational contract for some other benefit: schema flexibility, operational simplicity, horizontal scale, extreme throughput on narrow operations.

> **Terminology alert.** This chapter uses "wide-column stores" (Cassandra, HBase) to mean distributed databases that organize data by row and column families, with each row potentially having a different set of columns. This is _not_ the same as **column-oriented stores** (Chapter 19 — DuckDB, Snowflake, ClickHouse), which store data column-by-column for analytical workloads. The naming collision causes genuine confusion in the industry: "Cassandra is columnar" is a common but incorrect statement. Cassandra's physical storage is row-oriented within each SSTable partition; it is "column-flexible" (each row can have different columns) but not column-oriented for analytics.

This chapter covers key-value and document stores. The next chapter covers wide-column stores and the LSM trees that make them possible. Chapter 22 closes with the question every real system faces: **which database for which workload?**

We will not disparage these stores as "less than" relational. They are different tools. Many of the greatest applications of our era are built on careful combinations of all of them. Understanding the tradeoffs is what lets you choose.

### 20.1 Key-Value Stores — The Simplest Database

#### Definition: Key-value store

A database that maps opaque keys to opaque values. The only operations are `GET(key)`, `PUT(key, value)`, `DELETE(key)`, and sometimes `SCAN(key_prefix)`. No schemas, no queries, no joins, no secondary indexes in the pure form.

#### Examples

* **Redis** : in-memory, extremely fast (tens of thousands to millions of ops/sec), rich data types (strings, lists, sets, hashes, sorted sets), optional durability.
* **Memcached** : in-memory, simpler than Redis, caching layer only.
* **DynamoDB** : cloud-native distributed KV; also supports document-like nested attributes. Millions of ops/sec, pay-per-use.
* **RocksDB / LevelDB** : embedded KV libraries; LSM-tree-based. Used inside many other systems as the underlying storage engine.
* **etcd / Consul** : small KV stores optimized for configuration and coordination; strongly consistent via Raft.

#### Why so simple?

A single `GET(key)` is one of the few operations whose ideal implementation is so specialized that dedicating a database to it pays off. Caches, session stores, rate-limit counters, leaderboards — all answered with a `GET(key)` in microseconds.

#### Access patterns

* **GET** : O(1) with hashing, O(log N) with B-tree-like structures.
* **PUT** : O(1) or O(log N).
* **Range scans** : require ordered keys; not all KVs support them.
* **Multi-key operations** : transactional multi-get/multi-put requires more machinery.

The canonical KV does one key at a time. Redis adds atomic multi-key operations via its transactional MULTI/EXEC; DynamoDB has conditional updates and transactional writes; etcd guarantees atomic multi-key updates through the Raft log.

#### Common Misconception

"Key-value stores don't scale because they can't do joins." The lack of joins is the reason they _do_ scale. Every KV request hits exactly one key — which is exactly one partition. The system is embarrassingly parallel. A million keys spread across 10 nodes each handles 100,000 — with no coordination.

### 20.2 Redis — The Swiss Army Knife

Redis deserves a deeper look because it has become, in practice, the default in-memory cache layer of the web.

#### Data types

* **Strings** : byte sequences, up to 512 MB.
* **Lists** : ordered collections, O(1) push/pop on both ends.
* **Sets** : unordered, unique values.
* **Hashes** : key → field → value (like a one-deep nested map).
* **Sorted sets** : values ordered by a floating-point score.
* **Streams** : append-only logs with consumer groups.
* **HyperLogLog** : probabilistic cardinality estimator.
* **Geospatial** : geo-indexed coordinates.

Each has constant-time primitives matched to specific use cases. A rate limiter is a `SETEX user_id:rpm 60 0` plus `INCR`. A leaderboard is a `ZADD leaderboard score user_id` plus `ZRANGE leaderboard 0 10`.

#### Redis Stack and Modules

The core Redis types above cover cache and session workloads. For more complex use cases, **Redis Stack** bundles official modules that extend Redis's capabilities while keeping the in-memory, single-threaded architecture:

Module | Capability | Typical use case  
---|---|---  
**RedisSearch** (RediSearch) | Full-text search, vector similarity, geospatial queries | Search-as-a-cache layer; semantic search over embeddings  
**RedisJSON** | Native JSON document storage with JSONPath queries | Document cache without document-store overhead  
**RedisTimeSeries** | Time-series data with automatic downsampling and retention policies | IoT telemetry, metrics aggregation  
**RedisGraph** | Graph queries (Cypher syntax) over in-memory adjacency lists | Social network features, recommendation engines  
**RedisBloom** | Bloom filters, Cuckoo filters, Top-K, Count-Min Sketch | Probabilistic membership and frequency tracking  
  
These modules run in the same Redis process and use the same persistence mechanisms (AOF/RDB). They are useful when the data model is already in Redis and you want richer queries without adding another store. The tradeoff: modules add memory overhead and some introduce O(n) operations that can block Redis's single-threaded event loop under large datasets — monitor `SLOWLOG` carefully when using them at scale.

#### Persistence options

Redis is in-memory by design but offers optional persistence. The exact durability guarantee depends on which mode you enable and how you configure `fsync`:

Configuration | Data loss window on crash | Write overhead | When to use  
---|---|---|---  
No persistence (default in some deploys) | **Everything since last restart** | Zero | Pure cache; data reconstructable from primary store  
RDB snapshot only, `save 900 1` | Up to 15 minutes of writes | Periodic fork (minor pause) | Cache that survives planned restarts  
AOF `appendfsync no` | Whatever the OS buffered (up to seconds) | Low | Rarely useful — fsync behavior is OS-dependent  
AOF `appendfsync everysec` (AOF default) | **Up to 1 second** of writes | Moderate (one fsync/sec) | Most production Redis-as-primary deployments  
AOF `appendfsync always` | **At most the last unacknowledged write** | High (fsync every write) | Financial or critical state; 10× slower  
RDB + AOF (both enabled) | Same as AOF (AOF is the stricter) | AOF overhead + periodic RDB fork | Recovery speed + safety  
  
**Key insight: "durable" is a spectrum, determined by which mode you pick.**

* In `appendfsync always`, Redis `fsync`s the AOF **before** acknowledging the client — a committed write is on disk, like PostgreSQL's `synchronous_commit = on`. This is the only Redis mode that gives single-node durability comparable to a WAL-backed RDBMS.
* In `appendfsync everysec` (the default), Redis acknowledges the client **before** the next fsync — so a crash within the 1-second window can lose already-acknowledged writes. This is the common source of the folk claim that "Redis can lose committed data."
* In `appendfsync no`, Redis never calls fsync explicitly; the kernel flushes when it chooses. Durability depends on how much the OS has buffered — potentially tens of seconds.

Where Redis remains weaker than PostgreSQL even at `appendfsync always` is the **multi-node story**. PostgreSQL with synchronous replication + `synchronous_commit = remote_apply` guarantees the write is durable on at least one replica before ack. Redis's equivalent — `WAIT N timeout` — is advisory (the client _requests_ N replicas; if the timeout expires, the command still succeeds, no matter how many actually acknowledged) and Redis does not use consensus, so failover via Sentinel can lose writes that were on the old primary but not yet on the new one.

For strict durability — "a committed write will survive any single-node crash and any single replica loss" — you need Redis Cluster (sharding) + Sentinel (automated failover) + `appendfsync always` \+ client-side `WAIT` with a strict non-zero timeout and explicit failure handling. Even then, the durability guarantee is weaker than a well-tuned synchronously-replicated PostgreSQL. This is why Redis is a great cache and a constrained system of record: durability is achievable but must be assembled explicitly.

#### When Redis is the right answer

* Caching results of expensive database queries.
* Session storage for web applications.
* Rate limiting and counting.
* Pub/sub messaging.
* Real-time leaderboards, presence systems.

#### When Redis is the wrong answer

* Data that must be strongly durable (Redis's persistence is asynchronous).
* Data that exceeds RAM — Redis can spill, but not gracefully at very large scale.
* Complex queries (no joins, no aggregates).

#### Thread A's use of Redis

The university's registration system could use Redis for:

* Caching a student's enrollment list (lookup by student_id).
* Rate-limiting registration attempts ("no more than 10 enrollments per minute").
* Real-time seat counts for UI responsiveness (with periodic reconciliation to the authoritative PostgreSQL).

Not for the authoritative data. The source of truth stays in PostgreSQL; Redis is a fast cache.

### 20.3 Document Stores — JSON as the Table Row

#### Definition: Document store

A database that stores hierarchical, self-describing documents (usually JSON or BSON) instead of rigid rows. Each document has its own schema; documents in the same collection may differ in fields.

#### Examples

* **MongoDB** : by far the most popular. Replicated, sharded, rich query language.
* **CouchDB** : document + multi-master replication with conflict resolution.
* **Couchbase** : document + KV; commercial; high-throughput.
* **Firestore** : Google's serverless document store. Real-time updates.
* **DocumentDB (Amazon)** : MongoDB-compatible cloud service.

PostgreSQL and MySQL also have JSON column types that let them act partially like document stores; in many cases this dual-mode capability makes a separate document store unnecessary.

#### The document model

A document is a JSON object. A `users` collection might have:
    
    
    {
      "_id": "42",
      "name": "Alice",
      "email": "alice@example.com",
      "addresses": [
        {"type": "home", "street": "123 Main", "city": "SF"},
        {"type": "work", "street": "456 Market", "city": "SF"}
      ],
      "preferences": {"newsletter": true, "theme": "dark"},
      "created": "2025-09-01T00:00:00Z"
    }
    

Each user is self-contained. Related data (addresses, preferences) is **embedded** rather than split across tables. A single GET returns everything the application needs.

#### Advantages over relational

* **Schema flexibility** : add a new field to one document without migrating others.
* **Locality** : related data lives together. One query fetches a full aggregate.
* **No impedance mismatch** : the document maps directly to the application's object. No ORM layer translating rows to objects.
* **Rapid prototyping** : no migration step for new fields.

#### Disadvantages

* **Schema drift** : without enforcement, documents grow inconsistent over time. Some have field X, some don't. Queries must handle all cases.
* **Joins are weak** : MongoDB's `$lookup` works but is slower than relational joins. Joining across shards is especially expensive.
* **Duplicated data** : if a customer name is embedded in 1000 orders, updating the name requires updating 1000 documents.
* **Transaction boundaries** : until recently, MongoDB's transactions were per-document. Multi-document transactions (added in 4.0) exist but are less natural.

#### When documents win

* **Natural aggregates** : a blog post with its comments, a shopping cart with its items, a sensor reading with its metadata. Fetching the aggregate is one query.
* **Rapidly evolving schemas** : startups adding features weekly.
* **Sparse attributes** : products with many optional fields, where most products use few.

#### When relational wins

* **Cross-entity queries** : "find all students in 4th year taking computer science courses." Joining across independent aggregates is clumsy in documents.
* **Consistent transactions across entities** : "transfer money from account A to account B" is two document updates in MongoDB, and needs a multi-document transaction.
* **Strict referential integrity** : foreign keys enforced at the database level. Documents have no native FK.
* **Reporting and analytics** : column-store-style aggregations on rigid schemas.

#### Common Misconception

"Document stores are schemaless." They have _implicit_ schemas — the application knows what fields to expect. What they lack is _enforced_ schemas. This is flexibility in development and a landmine in production, where schema drift silently breaks old queries.

### 20.4 MongoDB in Detail

#### Storage engine

MongoDB defaults to **WiredTiger** , a B-tree-based engine with MVCC and compression. Documents are stored as BSON (binary JSON). Each collection lives in its own file.

#### Indexing

MongoDB supports secondary indexes on any field, including nested fields and arrays.
    
    
    db.users.createIndex({email: 1});
    db.users.createIndex({"addresses.city": 1});
    db.orders.createIndex({user_id: 1, created: -1});  // compound
    

Compound indexes and the leftmost-prefix rule apply just like in relational.

#### Queries

MongoDB's query language is JSON:
    
    
    db.users.find(
      {"addresses.city": "SF", age: {$gte: 18}},
      {name: 1, email: 1}
    );
    

Equivalent SQL:
    
    
    SELECT name, email FROM users
    WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM addresses WHERE user_id = users.id AND city = 'SF')
      AND age >= 18;
    

For many queries, the document form is simpler because the embedded address is part of the user document directly.

#### Aggregation pipeline

Complex queries use an aggregation pipeline:
    
    
    db.orders.aggregate([
      {$match: {status: "shipped"}},
      {$group: {_id: "$customer_id", total: {$sum: "$amount"}}},
      {$sort: {total: -1}},
      {$limit: 10}
    ]);
    

Roughly: match, group by customer, sum amounts, top 10. Turing-complete when you include `$lookup`, `$facet`, `$graphLookup`. In practice, aggregation pipelines resemble SQL with more verbose syntax.

#### Replication

Replica sets: primary + secondaries. Writes go to primary; secondaries replicate async. Read preference is configurable: primary, primary-preferred, nearest, etc.

Failover: if primary fails, secondaries elect a new primary (Raft-based). Writes can have varying "write concerns" — acknowledge locally, acknowledge by majority, acknowledge by all.

#### Sharding

MongoDB shards by a **shard key**. Documents with the same shard key stay on the same shard. Queries targeting the shard key are fast (one shard); queries without it scatter-gather to all shards.

Choosing the shard key is the single most important decision in MongoDB operations. A bad shard key — monotonic timestamp, low-cardinality enum — creates hotspots and makes scaling fail. A good shard key spreads load and aligns with query patterns.

#### Transactions

Before 4.0, only single-document transactions (atomic within one document). Post 4.0, multi-document transactions exist but incur significant performance cost and are typically used sparingly. MongoDB's philosophy has always been: _design your aggregates so transactions are seldom needed_.

#### Common Misconception

"MongoDB lost writes in the early days; it's unsafe." It did, historically, with default settings that acknowledged writes before any persistence. Modern defaults (majority write concern) are safe. The criticism became a meme; the engineering has long since caught up.

### 20.5 When to Combine Document + Relational

A common pattern in real architectures:

* **Relational for core entities with strict relationships** : users, accounts, payments. Use PostgreSQL or MySQL.
* **Document store for user-generated content, semi-structured data** : posts, comments, profiles with extensible attributes.
* **KV cache on top of both** : Redis for hot reads.

Or:

* **PostgreSQL with JSON columns** : for cases where you want _both_ relational rigidity and document flexibility in the same database. Used for "99% structured fields + occasional blob" scenarios.

The tooling reality: Postgres's JSONB type is so good that many applications that would have been MongoDB a decade ago now run on Postgres. The ecosystem has converged.

### 20.6 Graph Databases — A Brief Note

Graph databases (Neo4j, JanusGraph, Amazon Neptune) are a fourth NoSQL family worth mentioning. They store nodes and edges and optimize traversal: "friend of friend of friend," "shortest path from A to B," social network connections, fraud detection patterns.

Most applications don't need graph databases. When they do, relational alternatives (recursive CTEs, self-joins) quickly become unwieldy, and graph engines can answer 5-hop traversals in milliseconds that would take minutes in a relational system.

Thread A's prerequisite chain "CS101 requires MATH100 requires MATH50" is a shallow graph — recursive CTEs handle it. A social network with 10^10 edges needs specialized graph storage.

### 20.7 Choosing a Non-Relational Store

A quick heuristic:

* **Hot reads, simple keys, can tolerate cache-style semantics** → Redis.
* **Hierarchical data, flexible schema, one entity at a time** → Document store.
* **Time-series, append-only, tabular analytical queries** → Column store (Chapter 19).
* **Graph traversals, many-to-many dense relationships** → Graph database.
* **Everything else, especially if you need joins and transactions** → Relational.

Most real applications end up with at least two of the above. That is not a design flaw; that is the sign of a mature system.

#### Common Misconception

"NoSQL is more scalable than SQL." NoSQL systems often scale differently — horizontally by sharding, typically trading consistency for scale. But modern relational systems (Aurora, Spanner, CockroachDB) scale horizontally too. "Scalable" is no longer a clean distinguishing feature; the useful distinction is _data model_ and _access pattern_.

### 20.8 Transactions in Document Stores

MongoDB 4.0+ supports multi-document ACID transactions:
    
    
    session = db.getMongo().startSession();
    session.startTransaction();
    try {
      coll1.insertOne({...}, {session});
      coll2.updateOne({...}, {...}, {session});
      session.commitTransaction();
    } catch (e) {
      session.abortTransaction();
    }
    

Semantics: snapshot isolation, read-your-writes within the session, optional write concern (acknowledge by primary, majority, etc.).

**Cost: significantly higher than single-document writes.** The overhead has three specific components:

  1. **Per-statement log entries** : each read and write within the transaction is logged in WiredTiger's transaction log. A single-document write logs one entry; a multi-document transaction with 10 operations logs 10 entries plus transaction-begin/commit records. Log I/O increases proportionally.

  2. **Conflict detection at commit** : WiredTiger uses MVCC. At commit time, each write must check whether another transaction has modified the same document since the read snapshot was taken. Under high concurrent write load to the same documents, conflict detection causes frequent `WriteConflict` errors, which surface to the application as retryable errors. Unlike PostgreSQL's SSI which may silently retry, MongoDB surfaced `WriteConflict` as an exception until drivers added automatic retry; older code must handle it manually.

  3. **Lock escalation and abort/retry** : MongoDB acquires intent locks at database and collection level during multi-document transactions. High-concurrency workloads on the same collection can trigger waits. Long-running transactions that span multiple round-trips (client-side "interactive" transactions) hold these locks for the duration, blocking other writers.

Benchmark guidance: Published benchmarks and MongoDB engineering blog posts report significant throughput reductions — often several times lower — when workloads are fully converted from single-document to multi-document transactions. The exact cost depends heavily on concurrency, document size, and conflict rate; your workload may differ substantially. MongoDB's design philosophy has always been: _design your aggregates so transactions are seldom needed_. A well-designed document often contains its "transactional neighborhood" within one document, making transactions unnecessary.

Other document stores: CouchDB uses multi-version reads and does not offer multi-document transactions directly (relies on eventual consistency + application-level merging). Firestore offers transactions at per-document granularity with collision retry semantics.

### 20.9 When Schemas Matter

Document stores' "no enforced schema" is a double-edged sword.

#### The advantages

* Add a field to one document; old documents still work.
* Prototyping is fast.
* Each document is self-describing; readable by inspection.

#### The pitfalls

* A year later, you have 50 fields, some renamed, some deprecated, some in snake_case and some in camelCase.
* Queries must defensively handle missing or null fields.
* Schema migrations are deferred indefinitely; when finally done, they touch every document.

#### Schema validation (opt-in)

MongoDB 3.2+ offers schema validation: specify a JSON Schema per collection; reject documents that don't conform. You can choose the strictness: warn, log, reject.

This is the compromise: flexibility when you want it, enforcement when you need it.

#### Common Misconception

"Document stores don't need schemas." They don't _require_ them; you almost always need them anyway, conceptually. Whether the database enforces them or the application enforces them, somebody has to.

### Tradeoffs Summary

* **Document stores trade cross-entity query power for schema flexibility and locality.** Embedding related data in a single document makes "fetch this whole aggregate" a single read, but joining across aggregates is slower and messier than a relational join — the data model assumes most queries stay within one document.
* **Key-value stores trade expressivity for raw speed.** A pure `GET(key)`/`PUT(key)` interface is embarrassingly parallel and answers in microseconds, but every query pattern beyond a point lookup (ranges, secondary indexes, joins) must be rebuilt by the application or provided as a bolt-on feature.
* **Redis trades durability defaults for latency.** In-memory storage and "ack before fsync" buy sub-millisecond writes, but durability is a spectrum set by `appendfsync` and `WAIT`; treating Redis as a system of record without a primary store behind it is a recurring operational mistake.
* **Schemaless storage trades migration cost at write time for handling cost at read time.** Adding a new field needs no migration, but every reader must now tolerate missing, renamed, or inconsistent fields — schema drift becomes a permanent tax on query code, paid forever in place of a one-time migration.
* **Denormalized embedding trades update cost for read simplicity.** Storing a customer's name inside 1000 orders makes order reads trivial but turns a name change into a 1000-document write; know which side of the ratio dominates before picking the layout.
* **Cache-aside with TTL trades freshness for simplicity.** Short TTLs and explicit invalidation keep reads fast and usually correct, but there is always a window where the cache disagrees with the source of truth — systems that cannot tolerate that window need CDC-driven invalidation or read-through-primary fallback.
* **MongoDB transactions trade familiar ACID semantics for performance.** Multi-document transactions exist and work correctly, but they cost enough that the design guidance is "shape your aggregates so you rarely need them" — transactions become a last resort, not a default tool.

### 20.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Define key-value store. What is the minimum set of operations it supports?
  2. Describe three use cases where Redis is the natural choice, and three where it is not.
  3. Distinguish embedded data from referenced data in a document store. Give an example of when each is appropriate.
  4. Why do many document stores favor "design the aggregate boundary" over "use transactions"?
  5. Explain why choosing the shard key is the most important operational decision in MongoDB.
  6. Name three advantages and three disadvantages of schemaless design.

#### Application Problems

  7. Design a Redis schema for a rate-limiter that allows 100 requests per user per minute. What Redis commands implement increment-with-expiry?

Hint

Pick a key whose natural lifetime is one minute (think: per-user, per-time-window). Which two Redis commands together guarantee both "count this request" and "forget this counter when the window ends" atomically on the first hit? What do you check against 100 on each subsequent hit?

  8. Convert the following SQL query to a MongoDB aggregation pipeline:
         
         SELECT customer_id, SUM(total) FROM orders
         WHERE status = 'delivered' AND year = 2025
         GROUP BY customer_id
         ORDER BY SUM(total) DESC LIMIT 10;
         

  9. A social app currently stores posts, comments, and likes in separate PostgreSQL tables. The primary query is "show me post X with all its comments and like count." Propose a document-store version and evaluate the tradeoff.

  10. An e-commerce cart stores items, quantities, and timestamps. Describe two architectures — one where the cart is a document, one where it is split into tables — and explain which you'd choose for a site with 100,000 concurrent users.

  11. A hot user profile is read 1 million times per second but updated once per day. Design a caching strategy using Redis and the authoritative database. Account for cache invalidation.

Hint

With a 1M:1 read-to-write ratio, the cache can stay populated almost permanently. What TTL would you pick given the update frequency, and how do you guarantee that the one daily update flushes every cached copy? Consider whether the update path should push to Redis directly or let a CDC feed handle it.

  12. A blog platform is built on MongoDB. A new feature requires joining posts with authors and counting comments across all posts. Describe the difficulty and propose two ways to solve it.

  13. A system uses DynamoDB with a partition key of user_id. Some users have millions of events; most have few. What problem will arise, and how would you redesign the partition key?

Hint

DynamoDB distributes throughput by partition key. What happens to the handful of users with millions of events compared to the quiet majority? To spread the heavy users' load across more partitions, how would you append or hash something to user_id — and what must that change to the partition key cost you at read time?

#### Think Deeper

  14. Why is "schemaless" a marketing term more than a technical one? Compare explicit schemas in relational databases, implicit schemas in document applications, and evolving schemas in hybrid PostgreSQL+JSONB setups.

  15. MongoDB's document model discourages cross-document transactions by design. Describe how this constraint reshapes application architecture versus a relational application. Give an example where the constraint is liberating and one where it is painful.

  16. Redis is the fastest database in wide use. But it sacrifices durability by default. Argue when this is an acceptable tradeoff and when it creates operational risk.

  17. A growing team has their data in three systems: PostgreSQL (users, accounts), MongoDB (product catalog), Redis (session cache). A new requirement: list all users who purchased a specific product. Discuss how to implement this query and the cost of the polyglot architecture.

  18. Key-value stores trade expressiveness for speed. Propose a minimal extension to a KV store that enables 80% of common query patterns without sacrificing the speed of GET/PUT. (Hint: think secondary indexes, expressions, transactions.)

Hint

List the query patterns you actually want to support — "find by email," "list last N by time," "check-and-set a field" — and identify the single smallest extension that unlocks each. Which of those extensions can be implemented with just another KV namespace (indexes as keys) rather than a full query engine?

  19. PostgreSQL has JSON and JSONB column types that provide document semantics inside a relational engine. For a new project, argue for and against using Postgres with JSONB versus using MongoDB. Be specific about workload and team expertise.

  20. Imagine Thread A's university registration system is migrated from PostgreSQL to MongoDB. Which aspects work naturally and which become awkward? Sketch an alternative data model that plays to MongoDB's strengths.

Hint

Walk through each core query: "enroll a student in a course," "check seat capacity," "list a student's courses," "list a course's students." Which of these map cleanly to fetching or updating one document, and which inherently cross two aggregates? How do you collapse the relational schema into student-centric or course-centric documents, and what query does each choice make painful?

### Chapter Summary

**Key-value stores** map keys to values with O(1) access. **Redis** extends the model with rich data types and has become the default in-memory cache for web applications. Its speed comes from in-memory storage and simple access patterns; durability is opt-in and often secondary.

**Document stores** store hierarchical, self-describing documents (JSON/BSON) and encourage embedding related data within one document. **MongoDB** is the dominant document store; its secondary indexes, aggregation pipeline, replication, and sharding cover much ground, though joins and multi-document transactions are less idiomatic.

Document stores excel at **natural aggregates** , **flexible schemas** , and **rapid development**. They are weaker on **cross-entity queries** , **strict referential integrity** , and **reporting**. Schema drift is a real operational cost.

The modern reality is **polyglot persistence** : relational for core structured data, document for flexible user-generated content, key-value for caching, column-store for analytics, graph for dense relationships. Few serious systems use one database alone. Chapter 22 formalizes the choice among them.

Chapter 21 explores the third major NoSQL family — **wide-column stores** like Cassandra — and the **LSM-tree** engine that powers many modern write-heavy systems.

---

## Chapter 21: Wide-Column Stores and LSM Trees

In Chapter 19 we met column stores — systems that store each column contiguously so analytics can read only what they need. In Chapter 20 we met document stores and key-value stores, each with its own data model and its own idea of what "a record" is. This chapter introduces the **third** major NoSQL family: **wide-column stores** , systems like Apache Cassandra and Apache HBase. Despite the name, these are not column stores in the OLAP sense of Chapter 19 — the name is an accident of history, and part of what we will do in this chapter is untangle the confusion.

More importantly, wide-column stores are usually built on a storage engine that deserves a chapter of its own: the **LSM tree** (Log-Structured Merge tree). LSM trees are the write-heavy counterpart to the B+ trees of Chapter 10. Where a B+ tree updates pages in place and pays a random-write cost on every insert, an LSM tree buffers writes in memory, flushes them to disk as sorted runs, and merges those runs in the background. The result: enormous write throughput, at the cost of some read complexity and some write amplification from compaction.

We will spend most of this chapter on the LSM tree itself, because it is the most important idea in this chapter and because it appears far beyond wide-column stores — RocksDB, LevelDB, the storage engine under CockroachDB, the engine under many modern time-series databases, and the write path of Kafka all trace their lineage to LSM ideas. We will compare LSM trees to B+ trees along the axes that matter: read amplification, write amplification, and space amplification — a tradeoff that should feel familiar by now, because every engine in this book has been one point on some such triangle.

**Learning goals for this chapter:**

* Explain the wide-column data model as it appears in Cassandra and HBase, and why "wide-column" is a misleading name for a row-oriented OLTP store.
* Trace the write path through an LSM tree: memtable, WAL, flush to SSTable.
* Trace the read path through an LSM tree: memtable + SSTable levels, and the role of Bloom filters in pruning.
* Describe two major compaction strategies (size-tiered and leveled) and the tradeoff each makes.
* Reason quantitatively about write amplification, read amplification, and space amplification.
* Compare LSM trees to B+ trees and name workloads that favor each.
* Recognize tombstones as the mechanism LSM trees use for deletes, and understand why "delete" is actually a write.
* Describe Cassandra's partitioning, replication, and tunable consistency model.

* * *

### 21.1 Untangling the Name: Wide-Column Is Not Column-Oriented

Let us dispose of a source of confusion before it infects everything else.

* A **column store** (Chapter 19) organizes data by column on disk. It is optimized for OLAP: scan one column of a billion rows fast. DuckDB, ClickHouse, Snowflake, Parquet files, Amazon Redshift — these are column stores.
* A **wide-column store** organizes data by row on disk — specifically, by a partition key and then a clustering key — and its physical layout is nothing like a column store's. Cassandra, HBase, Google Bigtable, and Amazon DynamoDB (in its wide-column mode) — these are wide-column stores.

The "wide" in wide-column refers to the fact that different rows can have different columns, and that there can be **very many** columns per row, including columns created at write time. If you are used to thinking of a row as a fixed tuple matching a schema, a wide-column row is more like a dictionary — a map from column name to value, one map per row, and the keys of that map can vary from row to row.

> ### Definition: Wide-Column Store
> 
> A database in which each row is identified by a **partition key** (which determines which node stores the row) and a **clustering key** (which determines how rows within a partition are sorted).
