Each row is logically a map from column name to value, where the set of column names can differ from row to row. Storage is usually row-oriented within a partition, built on an LSM tree.

The confusion is historical. Google's 2006 Bigtable paper described the system as a "sparse, distributed, persistent multidimensional sorted map" indexed by `(row key, column key, timestamp)`, and called groups of related columns **column families**. The term "column-oriented" was sometimes used informally because rows could have many columns and a single query could ask about a subset of them. When Cassandra and HBase adopted Bigtable's model, they inherited the terminology. By the time analytical column stores had become their own distinct thing, the name was stuck.

For the rest of this chapter, "wide-column" always means the Bigtable/Cassandra family. When we want the OLAP sense, we will say "columnar" or refer back to Chapter 19.

#### Misconception

> ### Common Misconception
> 
> **"Cassandra is a column store, so it should be fast for analytics."** No. Cassandra's physical storage is row-oriented within each partition (and within each SSTable). A query like `SELECT AVG(price) FROM orders` has to touch every row, not just the price column, and has to consult multiple SSTables per partition. For genuine OLAP, use a columnar system (Chapter 19). Cassandra is built for high-write OLTP with eventual consistency and horizontal scale, not for scanning a column of a billion rows.

* * *

### 21.2 The Wide-Column Data Model

Let us make the model concrete with a Cassandra example. Here is a table for sensor readings:
    
    
    CREATE TABLE sensor_readings (
        sensor_id UUID,
        reading_time TIMESTAMP,
        temperature DOUBLE,
        humidity DOUBLE,
        battery_pct INT,
        PRIMARY KEY (sensor_id, reading_time)
    ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (reading_time DESC);
    

The `PRIMARY KEY` declaration has two parts:

  1. **Partition key** : `sensor_id`. Cassandra hashes this to decide which node stores the row. All rows for a given sensor live together on the same node (and its replicas).
  2. **Clustering key** : `reading_time`. Within the partition for a sensor, rows are stored sorted by `reading_time`, descending here.

A read like `SELECT * FROM sensor_readings WHERE sensor_id = ? AND reading_time > ?` is extremely fast: Cassandra hashes the `sensor_id` to locate the node, then does a range scan on the clustering key. A read that omits the partition key — say, `SELECT * FROM sensor_readings WHERE temperature > 100` — has to contact every node in the cluster. Cassandra will in fact refuse such a query unless you add `ALLOW FILTERING`, to force you to acknowledge you are doing something inefficient.

This is the central design lesson of wide-column stores: **queries are designed around the partition key**. Unlike a relational system where the query planner decides how to run your SQL, in Cassandra the physical data model is shaped by the queries you intend to run. You denormalize aggressively — often storing the same data in multiple tables partitioned differently — because join support is minimal and the way to make a read fast is to make sure everything the read needs lives in one partition.

> ### Definition: Partition Key and Clustering Key
> 
> The **partition key** is the portion of the primary key that determines which node owns a row. All rows with the same partition key live on the same set of nodes. The **clustering key** is the portion that determines the order of rows **within** a partition. A query that specifies the partition key and a range on the clustering key can be answered by reading a contiguous sorted run on a single node.

#### Column Families and Sparse Rows

In Bigtable and HBase, columns are grouped into **column families** — declared up front, stored together on disk, and tuned together (compression, caching, TTL). Within a column family, individual columns can be created dynamically. A row might have 3 columns in one family and 300 in another family, with no schema required to enumerate them. A query typically reads from one column family at a time.

Cassandra's modern CQL interface hides column families behind tables and looks more relational than Bigtable's original API, but the underlying file format still separates data by partition and stores each column's values as cells. The "wide" in wide-column comes through in the fact that each cell carries its own timestamp and its own tombstone (Section 21.5), independent of the rest of the row.

#### Thread A Mini-Example

The University Registration System from Thread A is not an ideal Cassandra workload, but we can carve off one piece that is: **session tracking** for the web frontend. Each user session has a UUID, and we want to store user activity events ordered by time within a session.
    
    
    CREATE TABLE sessions (
        session_id UUID,
        event_time TIMESTAMP,
        event_type TEXT,
        page_url TEXT,
        client_ip INET,
        PRIMARY KEY (session_id, event_time)
    ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (event_time ASC);
    

Reads are almost always of the form "give me events for session X in time range Y": perfect for the partition+clustering model. Writes are append-mostly: user clicks produce new events, we rarely update or delete old ones. This is exactly the shape of workload an LSM tree handles best.

* * *

### 21.3 The LSM Tree: Motivation

Before we describe what an LSM tree _is_ , let us describe what problem it solves.

Consider a B+ tree under a heavy write workload — say, 100,000 inserts per second, each with a random key. Each insert must:

  1. Locate the right leaf page (a few page reads, usually cached).
  2. Modify the page (logged to WAL, then marked dirty).
  3. Eventually, be written back to disk (a random-write I/O).

The random write in step 3 is the problem. On a spinning disk, random writes are dominated by seek time (~5–10 ms): you cannot get more than a few hundred random writes per second per spindle. On an SSD, random writes are faster but still cost more than sequential writes, and flash cells have a limited number of erase cycles — random write patterns cause **write amplification** at the flash translation layer, shortening device life.

B+ trees also have a space-amplification problem under updates: pages that were once full leave holes when keys are deleted, and you need background `VACUUM` (Chapter 16) or equivalent to reclaim space.

The LSM tree turns random writes into **sequential** writes by buffering everything in memory and flushing large sorted runs to disk. It pays for this with:

* **Read amplification** : a read might have to check several sorted runs to find the latest value for a key.
* **Write amplification** from compaction: the same data gets rewritten several times as runs merge.
* **Space amplification** : obsolete versions linger until compaction removes them.

As always, we are shuffling costs. The question is whether the shuffle suits the workload.

> ### Definition: Log-Structured Merge Tree (LSM Tree)
> 
> A write-optimized storage structure that: (1) accepts writes into an in-memory sorted buffer (the **memtable**); (2) flushes the memtable to disk as an immutable sorted file (an **SSTable**) when it fills; (3) merges SSTables in the background (**compaction**) to remove obsolete versions and keep the number of files bounded. Reads consult the memtable plus one or more SSTable levels, often using Bloom filters to skip SSTables that definitely do not contain the key.

* * *

### 21.4 The LSM Write Path

Let us trace a single write through an LSM tree.

#### Step 1: Append to WAL

Just as in Chapter 17, the first thing any durable store does on a write is append to a **write-ahead log** — a sequential file on disk, flushed (`fsync`) before the write is acknowledged. This is the durability anchor. If the process crashes after this point, the WAL lets us replay the write.

#### Step 2: Insert into Memtable

The **memtable** is an in-memory data structure that maintains sorted order — typically a skip list (Cassandra, RocksDB) or a red-black tree (LevelDB's original implementation). The skip list is a popular choice because it supports concurrent inserts with simple locking, and iteration in sorted order is straightforward.

An insert is an `O($\log n)` operation on the memtable. It is fast$ — microseconds — because it is all in memory and all sequential from the WAL's perspective.

The write returns to the client at this point. The durability guarantee is "the WAL is on disk"; the sorted copy in the memtable is a convenience for fast reads before the flush.

#### Step 3: Flush to SSTable

The memtable has a size threshold — typically 64 MB to a few hundred MB. When it fills, two things happen:

  1. The memtable becomes **immutable** (read-only). A new empty memtable starts accepting writes.
  2. The immutable memtable is written to disk as a **sorted string table (SSTable)** : a single file containing all its key-value pairs in sorted order, plus an index and usually a Bloom filter.

The flush is a single sequential write of a large file. This is the key performance trick: even though the application fired off millions of random inserts, the flush to disk is sequential.

Once the SSTable is durably on disk, the WAL entries corresponding to the flushed memtable can be discarded — they are no longer needed for crash recovery.

> ### Definition: SSTable (Sorted String Table)
> 
> An immutable on-disk file containing a sequence of key-value pairs sorted by key, along with an index that allows binary search or direct lookup of a key's offset within the file. Once written, an SSTable is never modified; updates and deletes produce new SSTables, and obsolete SSTables are removed by compaction.

#### The Accumulation of SSTables

Over time, more and more SSTables accumulate on disk. Each one is sorted internally, but different SSTables cover overlapping key ranges — two SSTables might both contain entries for key `K42`, one written yesterday and one written today. The later write shadows the earlier one. Readers must check all SSTables that could contain a key, pick the newest value, and return it. This is where read amplification comes from.

The number of SSTables must be bounded. That is the job of **compaction**.

#### Thread A Illustration

Return to the session-tracking table. Suppose each user generates ~200 click events over a session, and we have 10,000 concurrent users. That is ~2 million events accumulated per hour. With a B+ tree, each event is a random write into a page determined by `session_id`'s position in the index — 2 million random I/Os per hour, far beyond a spinning disk's budget.

With an LSM tree, all 2 million events go into the memtable, then flush sequentially to SSTables. Sequential writes on a commodity SSD comfortably sustain hundreds of MB/s; 2 million small events per hour is nothing. The LSM tree was designed for workloads like this.

* * *

### 21.5 Tombstones: Deletes That Are Writes

In a B+ tree, a delete removes the key from its leaf page (and may trigger rebalancing). In an LSM tree, **there is no in-place modification**. SSTables are immutable. So how do you delete?

You write a special record called a **tombstone** : a key-value pair where the value is a "deleted" marker with a timestamp.

> ### Definition: Tombstone
> 
> A special record in an LSM tree that marks a key (or a range of keys) as deleted. Reads that encounter a tombstone for a key treat the key as absent, even if older SSTables still contain a value. Tombstones are removed only during compaction, once all older SSTables containing the key have been merged and the tombstone is guaranteed to have done its job.

The tombstone sits in the memtable, then flushes to an SSTable, and propagates through compaction levels like any other record. When a read looks up a key, it checks SSTables newest-first; if it encounters a tombstone, it returns "not found" and stops — even if an older SSTable still contains a value for that key. Only when compaction has merged the tombstone with every older SSTable that might contain the key is the tombstone safe to discard.

Tombstones have a dark side. They take up space, they slow down reads (you still have to read them), and they accumulate if a workload does a lot of deletes without running compaction. Cassandra has a well-known anti-pattern where a queue-like table fills with tombstones as rows are deleted, and reads get slower and slower until the tombstones are compacted away. Operators learn to watch tombstone counts and tune compaction accordingly.

A second subtlety: tombstones have a **grace period** (`gc_grace_seconds` in Cassandra, default 10 days). A tombstone must be kept at least this long before it is removed during compaction. Why? Because in a distributed setting (Section 21.9), a replica that was offline when a delete was applied might come back and "resurrect" the deleted value by gossiping it to other replicas. The tombstone on the other replicas must still be there to intercept the resurrected write. The grace period bounds how long a replica can be offline before it must be rebuilt from scratch.

* * *

### 21.6 The LSM Read Path

A read must find the most recent value for a key — or determine it has been deleted. Here is the sequence.

#### Step 1: Check the Memtable

The memtable is in memory and sorted. A lookup is `O(\log n)` and returns instantly if the key is present. If found, return.

#### Step 2: Check SSTables, Newest First

SSTables are organized into **levels** (Section 21.7). For each SSTable that could contain the key, the read does:

  1. **Bloom filter check.** Each SSTable has a Bloom filter — a compact probabilistic data structure that answers "might this key be in the file?" with no false negatives (if it says no, the key is definitely not there) but some false positives. For typical parameters, a Bloom filter uses ~10 bits per key and gives a ~1% false-positive rate. If the Bloom filter says no, skip the SSTable.
  2. **Index lookup.** If the Bloom filter says yes, consult the SSTable's index (loaded into a block cache if possible) to find the key's offset.
  3. **Read the data block.** Fetch the relevant block from disk (or from the block cache).

The first SSTable where the key is found — or where a tombstone for the key is found — ends the search.

> ### Definition: Bloom Filter
> 
> A probabilistic data structure that stores a set of keys in a fixed-size bit array, allowing membership tests with no false negatives and a tunable false-positive rate. In LSM trees, each SSTable has its own Bloom filter, used to avoid reading SSTables that definitely do not contain the queried key. Typical parameters: 10 bits per key, ~1% false-positive rate.

#### Read Amplification

If a key is in the memtable, one lookup is enough. If a key is only in the oldest SSTable, and Bloom filters have 1% false positives, the read has to:

* Check memtable: miss.
* Check every SSTable in every level, consulting Bloom filters.

On average, with 10 SSTables and 1% false-positive Bloom filters, a read for a non-existent key touches ~0.1 SSTables beyond the Bloom-filter check — almost zero extra I/O. But a read for an existing key touches all SSTables newer than the one with the key, plus the SSTable with the key, because we must check newer ones first in case they have a newer version or tombstone.

**Read amplification** is the ratio of bytes read from disk to bytes returned to the client. For a point read in a well-tuned LSM tree with Bloom filters, read amplification is usually small (a few data blocks). For a range scan, it can be larger — you have to merge runs from multiple SSTables and a memtable, all of which contribute rows in overlapping key ranges.

> ### Definition: Read / Write / Space Amplification
> 
>   * **Read amplification** : bytes read from storage per byte returned to the client. Higher is worse.
>   * **Write amplification** : bytes written to storage per byte of application data. Higher is worse (more flash wear, more I/O contention).
>   * **Space amplification** : bytes of storage occupied per byte of live application data. Higher is worse (more disk used for obsolete versions).
> 

Every storage engine has all three, and they trade off against each other. LSM trees are typically low-write-amp / medium-read-amp / medium-space-amp; B+ trees are typically high-write-amp (because of random-page updates) / low-read-amp / low-space-amp.

* * *

### 21.7 Compaction: Keeping the Tree Bounded

Without compaction, SSTables would pile up forever. Compaction is the background process that merges SSTables, discards obsolete versions and expired tombstones, and keeps the number and total size of SSTables bounded.

Compaction is a **merge** in the classic external sort sense: read several sorted SSTables, merge their key sequences, write a new sorted SSTable, delete the inputs. For a given key, keep the newest value (or drop the key entirely if the newest "value" is an expired tombstone).

The question is _which_ SSTables to merge, and _when_. Two major strategies dominate.

#### Size-Tiered Compaction (STCS)

In **size-tiered compaction** , SSTables are grouped into tiers by size. When enough SSTables of similar size accumulate (typically 4 of them), they are merged into one SSTable of the next size tier. Tier 0 holds small flushed SSTables; tier 1 holds ~4× larger; tier 2 holds ~16× larger; and so on.

* **Pro:** Write amplification is low. Each byte of data is rewritten roughly `\(\log_4(N)\)` times as it migrates up the tiers.
* **Con:** Space amplification can be large. At any moment, a key might have up to 4 copies in tier 0, plus 4 in tier 1, plus 4 in tier 2 — multiple versions of the same key scattered across many SSTables. Read amplification is also higher: a point read might check many SSTables.
* **Con:** The final merge at the top tier is enormous and may require space equal to the size of the database, briefly doubling disk usage.

STCS is Cassandra's historical default and remains good for write-heavy, time-series-like workloads where obsolete versions clear out naturally.

#### Leveled Compaction (LCS)

In **leveled compaction** , SSTables are organized into levels. Level 0 is special — newly flushed SSTables, which may overlap in key range. Levels 1, 2, 3, … each contain many SSTables, but **within a level** , SSTables have disjoint key ranges. Level `\(i+1\)` is about 10× larger than level `\(i\)`.

When level `\(i\)` exceeds its size budget, one of its SSTables is picked and merged with all SSTables in level `\(i+1\)` that overlap its key range — typically ~10 SSTables, producing ~10 new SSTables in level `\(i+1\)` with no overlap.

* **Pro:** Space amplification is low — at most ~10% overhead from in-flight compaction. Read amplification is low — at most one SSTable per level contains any given key, except in level 0.
* **Con:** Write amplification is higher. A single key in level 0 migrates through each level, getting rewritten at each step; with 7 levels, each byte might be rewritten ~10–20 times before reaching the last level.

LCS is RocksDB's default. It is popular for workloads where read latency matters and where the storage cost of STCS's space amplification is unacceptable.

#### L0 Write Stalls — When Compaction Falls Behind

Level 0 is a special case in leveled compaction: newly flushed SSTables land here and are _not_ guaranteed to have disjoint key ranges. A read must check _all_ L0 SSTables (unlike levels 1+, where at most one SSTable covers any key). This means read amplification scales with the L0 SSTable count — more L0 files = slower reads. More importantly, if writes arrive faster than the compaction thread can promote L0 SSTables into L1, L0 grows unboundedly.

RocksDB implements a two-stage backpressure policy:

* **Slowdown** (`level0_slowdown_writes_trigger`, default 20 SSTables): once L0 exceeds this count, RocksDB artificially throttles incoming writes — inserting small sleeps — to give compaction a chance to catch up.
* **Stop** (`level0_stop_writes_trigger`, default 36 SSTables): if L0 continues to grow, RocksDB **blocks all writes completely** until compaction reduces L0 back below the stop threshold. From the application's perspective, write latency jumps from microseconds to potentially seconds or longer.

> **Operational implication.** An L0 write stall is not a crash and not an error — the database is still alive, reads still work (slowly), and writes resume once compaction catches up. But the latency spike is severe enough to violate SLAs in production. Mitigation strategies include: (1) provisioning faster storage or more compaction threads; (2) reducing the flush frequency (larger memtables); (3) monitoring L0 SSTable count as a leading indicator, not just write latency; (4) enabling `rate_limiter` to smooth compaction I/O and reduce interference with foreground operations. The same L0 accumulation problem can occur in Cassandra when SSTables pile up faster than compaction clears them, though Cassandra's backpressure mechanism differs (compaction throughput throttling rather than write stalls).

#### Other Strategies

* **Time-window compaction** (Cassandra): designed for time-series data. SSTables are grouped by write time; old time windows are never rewritten. Great for write-once-read-recent workloads.
* **Universal compaction** (RocksDB alternative): similar to size-tiered but with stricter space constraints.
* **Hybrid strategies** : some systems use LCS at lower levels and STCS at the top, getting both benefits at some cost in complexity.

#### Quantifying the Tradeoff

Let us make the amplification numbers concrete. Suppose we have 1 TB of live data, a memtable that flushes at 256 MB, and a 10× level ratio.

* **B+ tree, random-write workload** : each insert touches one leaf page (say 8 KB), dirtying it. With a 50% update pattern, we might write ~2 pages per insert (counting WAL). Per-key write amplification ~2.
* **LSM with leveled compaction** : 7 levels, each 10× the previous. Per-key write amplification ~10–20 over the key's lifetime, but all writes are sequential.
* **LSM with size-tiered compaction** : per-key write amplification ~4 (log₄ of the tier count), also sequential.

On SSDs, the LSM's sequential-write pattern is so much friendlier to flash that its higher write amplification at the logical level still wins on flash lifetime. This is why LSM engines dominate modern OLTP storage under write-heavy workloads.

* * *

### 21.8 Comparing LSM and B+ Trees

Let us pull the comparison together.

Axis | B+ Tree | LSM Tree  
---|---|---  
Point read | 3-4 page I/Os (root to leaf, cached) | 1 memtable + Bloom-filtered SSTable reads  
Range scan | Single leaf traversal | Merge across memtable + multiple SSTables  
Random insert | Random-page write | Sequential append (memtable + WAL)  
Bulk load | Sort and build bottom-up | Flush → compact  
Write amplification | ~1-3 | ~4-20 (strategy-dependent)  
Space amplification | Low (~30% for half-full pages) | 10%-50% depending on strategy  
Read amplification | Low | Higher, softened by Bloom filters  
Dominant I/O cost | Random writes | Sequential writes  
Crash recovery | Relatively fast (WAL replay) | Fast (WAL replay into memtable)  
Delete | In-place | Tombstone + compaction  
  
**Rule of thumb** : write-heavy workloads with not-too-skewed key access favor LSM. Read-heavy, update-in-place workloads with strict latency bounds favor B+. Almost every modern embedded KV store (RocksDB, LevelDB, Cassandra's internals, BadgerDB, Pebble) is LSM-based because modern storage favors sequential I/O and modern workloads generate many writes.

#### Misconception

> ### Common Misconception
> 
> **"LSM trees are always better than B+ trees — that is why everyone uses RocksDB."** No. LSM trees are better _for write-heavy workloads on modern SSDs_. For workloads dominated by small point lookups with tight tail-latency SLAs, B+ trees still compete well. SQL Server, Oracle, InnoDB, and PostgreSQL all use B+ trees (or close variants). The right engine depends on the workload — which is exactly what this book keeps saying.

#### Thread B Context

Recall Thread B — the 20-minute query that became 2 seconds after we added indexes (Chapter 10) and then 100 ms on a columnar engine (Chapter 19). Where does LSM fit? Mostly in the OLTP system _producing_ the data. The e-commerce orders that Thread B was aggregating were likely being written into a primary store with an LSM backend (a sharded Cassandra cluster, or a RocksDB-backed system) because the site's peak write rate — tens of thousands of orders per minute during a sale — benefits from the LSM's sequential-write profile. The data is then extracted and loaded into the columnar analytical store for the overnight query. Two engines, one pipeline, each optimized for its end of the workload.

* * *

### 21.9 Cassandra: Putting It Together

Apache Cassandra is the canonical open-source wide-column store. Let us see how the pieces we have discussed fit into its architecture.

#### Partitioning

Cassandra is a distributed hash table. Every node owns a range of hash values (a range of the **token ring**). To find the node owning a row, hash the partition key and look up the range on the ring. Each key is typically replicated to **N=3** nodes, placed at successor positions on the ring.

#### Replication and Tunable Consistency

Writes go to all N replicas; reads can contact any subset. Cassandra exposes the familiar **R + W > N** consistency tuning from Chapter 18:

* **Write consistency level W** — how many replicas must ack before the write returns. `ONE` = fast but low durability; `QUORUM` = 2 of 3, balanced; `ALL` = strong but dies if any replica is down.
* **Read consistency level R** — how many replicas must agree on a read. `ONE` = fast, might return stale; `QUORUM` = read-your-own-writes if `W = QUORUM`.

The application chooses R and W per operation. A session-tracking insert might use `W = ONE` for latency; a billing write might use `W = QUORUM` for safety.

#### Hinted Handoff and Read Repair

When a replica is down during a write, the coordinator node stores a **hint** — a record of the write, destined for the absent replica — and delivers it when the replica returns. This is Cassandra's antidote to temporary unavailability.

> **Hinted handoff has a time limit — and what happens after it matters.** Cassandra's default `max_hint_window` is **3 hours** (configurable; some deployments set it to 11 hours). If a replica is offline for _longer_ than `max_hint_window`, the coordinator stops accepting hints for it. Hints that arrived before the window closed are still delivered on reconnection. But **any writes that arrived during the gap between the window closing and the replica coming back are silently lost from that replica's perspective**.
> 
> When the replica returns after a long outage, it participates in reads and may serve **stale data** — data that is "missing" the writes that occurred during the hint-window gap. Reads with `QUORUM` or `ALL` may repair the stale replica through read-repair, but reads with `ONE` or local-DC consistency can return the old stale value indefinitely.
> 
> **Data loss scenario:** RF=3, one replica offline for 24 hours, `max_hint_window` = 3h. Writes acknowledged at `W=QUORUM` (2 of 3) arrive normally. The offline replica rejoins. For the 21-hour window between hour 3 and hour 24, the rejoined replica is missing ~21 hours of writes. A `R=ONE` read that hits that replica returns stale data. No error is raised; the application sees plausible-but-wrong results.
> 
> **Mitigation:** Use `nodetool repair` on the rejoined node to reconcile its data with peers via Merkle-tree comparison before reintroducing it to traffic. Many teams automate this in the node restart procedure. The `gc_grace_seconds` (default 10 days) must be longer than the maximum expected offline duration to prevent tombstone resurrection during repair.

When a read sees disagreement between replicas, the coordinator performs **read repair** : it returns the latest value to the client and, in the background, sends the latest value to the stale replicas to bring them up to date. Combined with periodic **anti-entropy** (a Merkle-tree comparison between replicas), this is how Cassandra achieves eventual consistency.

#### The Write Path Inside One Node

Writes on a single Cassandra node follow the LSM path we described:

  1. Append to **commit log** (Cassandra's name for its WAL).
  2. Insert into memtable.
  3. Ack to coordinator.
  4. Memtable eventually flushes to an SSTable.
  5. SSTables compact in the background.

This LSM-based write path is why Cassandra absorbs huge write volumes gracefully. The classic Cassandra workload — time-series data, IoT telemetry, event logs, audit trails — fits LSM perfectly.

#### Where Cassandra Fits Poorly

* **Complex joins.** Cassandra has none. Everything is denormalized.
* **Strong cross-partition consistency.** No multi-partition transactions (lightweight transactions exist via Paxos but are expensive).
* **Ad-hoc analytics.** Row-oriented storage is wrong for column scans. Export to a column store.
* **Secondary indexes at scale.** Cassandra has them, but they fan out across the cluster and are slow for large result sets.

The design is laser-focused on "highly available, write-heavy, partition-key-driven reads, eventual consistency." If that is your workload, Cassandra shines. If it is not, pick something else.

* * *

### 21.10 Other LSM-Based Systems

LSM is a storage engine idea that has escaped its wide-column origins and is now everywhere.

* **RocksDB** (Facebook, fork of LevelDB): an embedded KV store used as the storage engine under CockroachDB, TiKV, YugabyteDB, MyRocks (a RocksDB engine for MySQL), Kafka Streams' state stores, and many others. If a modern system says "LSM," it is often using RocksDB.
* **LevelDB** (Google): the original open-source LSM implementation; the ancestor of RocksDB.
* **Pebble** (CockroachDB): a RocksDB-compatible LSM written in Go, for teams that want a native Go engine.
* **BadgerDB** : a Go-native LSM KV store with separated value logs — stores large values outside the SSTables to reduce write amplification.
* **ScyllaDB** : a C++ rewrite of Cassandra that claims much higher per-node throughput, built on the Seastar shard-per-core runtime.
* **HBase** : Hadoop-ecosystem wide-column store, Bigtable-inspired, LSM-based, running on HDFS.
* **Bigtable and its cloud cousins** (Google Bigtable, Amazon DynamoDB's internals): production descendants of the original Bigtable paper.

Beyond storage engines, the LSM idea shows up in unexpected places — message queues (Kafka segments are immutable SSTables with a different API), time-series databases (InfluxDB, TimescaleDB's compressed hypertables), and even some log-structured file systems.

* * *

### 21.11 Bloom Filters in a Little More Depth

Because Bloom filters are so load-bearing in LSM read paths, they deserve a closer look.

A Bloom filter is a bit array of size `\(m\)` with `\(k\)` hash functions. To add a key, hash it `\(k\)` times and set those `\(k\)` bits. To test membership, hash and check those `\(k\)` bits — if any is zero, the key is definitely absent; if all are one, the key is _probably_ present.

The false-positive rate is approximately:

$p \approx \left(1 - e^{-kn/m}\right)^{k}$

where `\(n\)` is the number of inserted keys. With `\(m = 10n\)` bits (10 bits per key) and `\(k = 7\)` hash functions, `\(p $\approx 0.008\)`$ — about 1%. Doubling bits per key to `\(m = 20n\)` (and optimal `\(k = 14\)`) drops the rate to `\(p $\approx 6.7 \times 10^{-5}\)`$ — about 0.007%, a ~140× improvement in false-positive rate for a 2× memory cost. Whether that tradeoff is worth it depends on how expensive an SSTable read is relative to the extra RAM.

For a billion-key SSTable at 10 bits per key, the Bloom filter uses ~1.25 GB of memory — often held in RAM. 99% of non-existent-key lookups skip the SSTable entirely, costing just a hash and a memory probe. Without Bloom filters, LSM read performance would be unworkable.

More sophisticated variants — **Cuckoo filters** , **xor filters** — trade space and construction cost against false-positive rate. Bloom remains the default because it is simple, it supports streaming insertion, and it is well understood.

#### FP Rate Drift from the Design Target

A Bloom filter is designed for a specific expected cardinality `\(n\)`. The false-positive formula `\(p = (1 - e^{-kn/m})^k\)` shows why cardinality matters: `\(n\)` appears in the exponent. If the actual number of keys inserted deviates from the design target, the actual FP rate diverges significantly:

Inserted keys vs. design `\(n\)` | Effect on bit occupancy | Actual FP rate (10 bits/key design)  
---|---|---  
0.5× `\(n\)` (half expected) | Bits sparser than intended | ~0.01% — better than design, wastes memory  
1× `\(n\)` (on target) | As designed | ~1%  
2× `\(n\)` (double expected) | Bits fill up faster | ~10% — 10× worse than design  
5× `\(n\)` (5× expected) | Bits near saturation | ~40% — most lookups falsely positive  
  
**Why this matters in practice.** LSM SSTables are usually written with a Bloom filter sized for the number of keys in that SSTable. But the design assumptions can break down when:

* **Compaction merges SSTables** : leveled compaction produces larger SSTables whose combined cardinality may exceed what the new filter was designed for if the sizing code has a bug.
* **Cardinality estimation is wrong at flush time** : RocksDB estimates the memtable's key count at flush. If updates to existing keys have been counted as new keys, the filter is under-sized.
* **Long-lived SSTables grow stale** : in systems where SSTable retention is long and no compaction occurs, the same Bloom filter serves a growing number of logical keys (tombstones bring the "effective" cardinality down, but the filter was built on the original cardinality).

**Mitigation.** Monitor the actual FP rate in production via counters (RocksDB exposes `bloom_filter_useful` and `bloom_filter_full_positive`). If FP rate has drifted significantly above the design target, force a compaction to rebuild SSTable files with fresh, correctly-sized Bloom filters.

* * *

### 21.12 Thread A Revisited: Choosing Storage for Each Subsystem

Let us bring the University Registration System's storage decisions into one place, using everything we have seen.

Subsystem | Workload profile | Best engine | Why  
---|---|---|---  
Core registration (Students/Courses/Enrollments) | OLTP, joins, transactions | PostgreSQL (B+ tree) | Relational constraints, ACID transactions  
Session tracking (web frontend) | Append-only, session-partitioned, short retention | Cassandra (LSM) | Write-heavy, eventually consistent, naturally partitioned  
Audit log (who changed what grade) | Append-only, occasionally read | Cassandra or Kafka + Parquet | LSM absorbs writes; archive to columnar for analytics  
Cache for the course catalog | Sub-millisecond reads, small dataset | Redis (in-memory hash) | Speed; data is a projection of the primary store  
Grade analytics (dashboards) | OLAP, full-table scans by column | DuckDB or ClickHouse (columnar) | Column scans, compression  
  
No single engine is best for the whole system. This is the polyglot persistence idea we will formalize in Chapter 22.

* * *

### 21.13 Tradeoffs Summary

* **Wide-column stores trade joins and cross-partition transactions for horizontal write scale and high availability.** Design queries around the partition key or you lose all the advantages.
* **LSM trees trade read amplification and write amplification for sequential writes.** Modern SSDs love sequential writes; modern applications generate lots of writes; LSM usually wins for write-heavy OLTP.
* **Leveled vs size-tiered compaction** is itself a read/write/space tradeoff: LCS favors reads and space, STCS favors writes. Neither is universally right.
* **Tombstones are the cost of immutability.** They make deletes cheap at write time but expensive if not compacted.
* **Bloom filters are not optional** for LSM read performance at scale. Treat them as essential.

Back in Chapter 10 we saw B+ trees as the default index structure for OLTP. We now see their complement: LSM trees, the default engine for write-heavy OLTP, distributed KV, and wide-column stores. The next chapter asks the question every real project has to answer: **given all these engines, how do you pick?**

* * *

### 21.14 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Explain in one sentence why "wide-column store" is a misleading name for systems like Cassandra. How does Cassandra's physical layout differ from a true column store like DuckDB?

  2. Define: memtable, SSTable, WAL (in the context of an LSM tree). Which of these is mutable? Which is durable?

  3. What problem do Bloom filters solve in an LSM read path? Why does the 1% false-positive rate not cause correctness problems?

  4. Define **tombstone**. Why must a tombstone survive at least as long as the oldest SSTable that might contain the value it shadows?

  5. State the typical write-amplification formulas for size-tiered and leveled compaction. Which strategy has lower write amplification, and which has lower space amplification?

  6. In Cassandra, what is the partition key? What is the clustering key? Give one query that Cassandra will answer efficiently and one it will refuse without `ALLOW FILTERING`.

#### Application Problems

  7. You are designing a storage layer for a real-time chat app. Each user has millions of messages; reads are almost always "give me the most recent 50 messages in this conversation." Sketch a Cassandra table — choose the partition key, clustering key, and clustering order — and justify each choice.

  8. A Cassandra deployment has `gc_grace_seconds = 10 days`. An operator deletes 1 million rows on Monday but one replica is offline until the following Friday (11 days later). What will happen when the offline replica comes back online? What if the offline replica comes back on day 9 instead?

Hint

Tombstones are only safe to discard after `gc_grace_seconds`. Ask what each live replica still holds at day 9 vs day 11, and what the offline replica brings back with it. If the tombstone has already been garbage-collected elsewhere, what wins when the offline replica's stale values gossip out to its peers?

  9. You observe that your LSM-backed KV store's p99 read latency has ballooned from 2 ms to 200 ms over the past month, while p50 is unchanged. The memtable size and Bloom-filter configuration have not changed. What is a likely cause, and how would you diagnose it?

Hint

p50 unchanged but p99 exploding suggests a tail problem — something a minority of reads hit. Which LSM failure modes specifically inflate tail reads without changing the typical case? Think tombstones, compaction backlog, or SSTable count per level, and ask which metric would reveal each.

  10. An RocksDB instance using leveled compaction has 7 levels. Each level is 10× larger than the previous. Level 1 is 100 MB. How much data can the system hold before level 7 overflows? If the total on-disk data size is reported as 800 GB but the live (non-obsolete) data is 700 GB, what is the space amplification?

Hint

Level capacity is a geometric series: 100 MB, 1 GB, 10 GB, ..., scaling by 10× per level. Sum up to level 7. For space amplification, divide total bytes on disk by live bytes — what does the ratio tell you about how much disk is "wasted" on obsolete versions?

  11. Your Cassandra cluster is time-series IoT data with a 30-day retention policy. Size-tiered compaction never cleans up old time windows well — tombstones from expiring data accumulate. What compaction strategy would you switch to, and why?

  12. A developer writes:
         
         SELECT * FROM sensor_readings WHERE temperature > 100;
         

on a 500-node Cassandra cluster. The query fails with a warning about `ALLOW FILTERING`. Explain (a) why Cassandra refuses, (b) what happens if they add `ALLOW FILTERING`, and (c) how they should redesign the data model if this query is frequent.

  13. In a Bloom filter with `\(m = 10n\)` bits and `\(k = 7\)` hash functions, the false-positive rate is ~1%. Suppose you change to `\(m = 20n\)` and `\(k = 14\)` — doubling space. Estimate the new false-positive rate. Is doubling space worth it for a read-heavy workload?

Hint

Plug `\(m/n\)` and `\(k\)` into the false-positive formula `\((1 - e^{-kn/m})^k\)`, or use the rule of thumb that each extra bit/key roughly halves the false-positive rate. Then weigh the extra RAM against the avoided SSTable reads: what fraction of queries was being falsely directed into an SSTable before, and how expensive was each of those reads?

  14. Compare the crash-recovery procedure of an LSM tree to the ARIES procedure of Chapter 17. What is the same? What is different? Which is typically faster to recover, and why?

#### Think Deeper

  15. The "LSM vs B+" tradeoff is often summarized as "LSM for writes, B+ for reads." But MongoDB (from Chapter 20) uses WiredTiger's B+ tree by default. Why? Think about what MongoDB's workload typically looks like, and why "write-heavy" for MongoDB might mean something different than for Cassandra.

Hint

Cassandra's canonical workload is append-only events at tens of thousands of writes per second per node. What does a MongoDB application typically do at that same rate, and how often does it update rather than append? Think about document size, in-place updates, index churn, and tail-latency SLAs — which of these favor B+ even under "heavy writes"?

  16. Suppose storage becomes free and unlimited. Which amplification (read/write/space) stops mattering, and which still does? How might storage engine design change if only write and read amplification mattered?

  17. CockroachDB uses RocksDB (LSM) as its storage engine but exposes a SQL interface with full ACID transactions and distributed joins. That looks contradictory: LSM is the "NoSQL" engine, SQL+ACID is the "RDBMS" story. Explain how CockroachDB combines them. What does the LSM give up? What does the SQL layer add?

Hint

Remember that "SQL" and "LSM" live at different layers. What does the LSM actually know about — rows, keys, versions? Where are transactions and joins implemented, and how is MVCC encoded into keys so an LSM-native engine can support it? Ask what CockroachDB must pay in read amplification for the SQL guarantees to hold.

  18. Revisit Chapter 17's ARIES analysis. If you were designing crash recovery for an LSM tree from scratch, which parts of ARIES would you keep, and which would you simplify or drop?

  19. Tombstones are the LSM analog of MVCC's undo-log versions (Chapter 16): both allow concurrent readers to see a consistent view while writers append. Compare the two. What workloads make tombstones cheaper than undo records? What workloads make undo records cheaper?

  20. Design a hybrid storage engine that is "LSM on the write path, B+ on the read path." Sketch the architecture. Where would data flow? What would compaction look like? What problems does this create that pure LSM does not have?

* * *

### Chapter Summary

Wide-column stores like Cassandra and HBase occupy a specific niche: horizontally scalable, write-heavy, highly available, eventually consistent, with queries designed around the partition key. Their name is a historical accident; physically they are row-oriented within partitions, typically backed by LSM trees.

**LSM trees** are the most consequential idea in this chapter. They turn random writes into sequential writes by buffering in a memtable and flushing to immutable SSTables. Reads consult the memtable plus SSTable levels, with Bloom filters pruning most negative lookups. Compaction merges SSTables in the background; size-tiered compaction favors write throughput, leveled compaction favors read latency and space efficiency. The LSM engine now powers a vast swath of modern data infrastructure: RocksDB, Cassandra, HBase, Kafka, CockroachDB, TiKV, and many more.

Compared to the B+ tree of Chapter 10, LSM trades read amplification and write amplification (from compaction) for sequential-write friendliness. On modern SSDs this is usually a good bargain for write-heavy OLTP workloads. For read-dominated or latency-critical workloads, B+ trees still compete — which is why PostgreSQL, InnoDB, and Oracle have not switched. There is no universal winner; there is only workload.

The chapter ends a long arc of storage-engine chapters: Chapter 10 (B+ tree indexes), Chapter 19 (column stores), Chapter 20 (documents and KV), Chapter 21 (wide-column and LSM). Each engine solves a specific problem extremely well and solves other problems poorly. **Chapter 22 asks how to combine them.** Real systems almost never use just one engine; they use the right engine for each subsystem — _polyglot persistence_ — and the hard part is the joints where engines meet.

---

## Chapter 22: Choosing the Right Database — Polyglot Persistence

By this point in the book you have met a zoo of database engines. Relational stores with B+ tree indexes (Chapter 10). Column stores for analytics (Chapter 19). Key-value stores like Redis and document stores like MongoDB (Chapter 20). Wide-column stores like Cassandra and the LSM trees underneath (Chapter 21). On top of these sit concerns that cut across every engine: transactions, isolation, replication, sharding, consensus (Chapters 13-18).

The central question of this chapter is not "which database is best?" It is: **given a system with many different data needs, how do you choose the right engine for each, and make them work together?**

The answer most real architectures have converged on is called **polyglot persistence** : use multiple databases in the same system, each for the subproblem it solves best. A modern web application might have PostgreSQL for orders, Redis for session cache, Elasticsearch for search, Kafka for event streams, Cassandra for audit logs, and Snowflake for analytics — and that is not over-engineered, it is normal. Each engine earns its place by being dramatically better than the others at one thing.

But polyglot persistence is not free. Every additional datastore is a new failure mode, a new operational skill, a new consistency boundary, and a new joint in the plumbing between systems. This chapter is about choosing engines _and_ about knowing when to stop adding them.

**Learning goals for this chapter:**

* Articulate a decision framework for matching workloads to storage engines.
* Recognize the common workload archetypes and the engines that serve each.
* Understand the operational and consistency costs of adding a new datastore.
* Reason about data flows between stores (CDC, ETL, dual writes) and their failure modes.
* Evaluate realistic multi-store architectures and critique them.

* * *

### 22.1 The Decision Framework: Workload Axes

No rule of thumb matches workloads to engines perfectly, but there is a short list of axes that tend to drive most decisions. When designing or reviewing a storage layer, walk through these.

#### Axis 1: Read / Write Ratio

* **Write-heavy** (sensor telemetry, event logging, clickstream, audit): LSM-based engines (Cassandra, RocksDB, HBase) are designed for this. B+ tree engines struggle under random-write floods.
* **Read-heavy with occasional writes** (product catalog, user profiles, reference data): B+ tree engines (PostgreSQL, InnoDB) read fast; caches (Redis) make them faster.
* **Read-heavy analytical** (dashboards, BI, reporting): columnar engines (DuckDB, ClickHouse, Snowflake) win by 10-100×.
* **Balanced OLTP** (most transactional apps): B+ tree engines are the default for good reason.

#### Axis 2: Access Pattern

* **Point lookups by primary key** : any engine does this, but Redis and DynamoDB are purpose-built for it at sub-millisecond latency.
* **Range scans on sorted key** : B+ tree, LSM tree with appropriate key design, or a clustered index can all serve this.
* **Multi-attribute predicates with joins** : relational engines. Everything else forces you to denormalize.
* **Full-text search** : Elasticsearch, OpenSearch, or PostgreSQL's full-text search (good enough for small-to-medium corpora).
* **Aggregations across billions of rows** : columnar engines, full stop.
* **Nearest-neighbor over vectors** : dedicated vector databases (pgvector, Pinecone, Weaviate, Qdrant).
* **Graph traversals** : graph databases (Neo4j, Amazon Neptune) or relational recursive CTEs for modest depth.

#### Axis 3: Consistency Requirements

* **Strong serializable** : single-node relational (PostgreSQL, Oracle, SQL Server); or distributed systems willing to pay the cost (Spanner, CockroachDB, FoundationDB).
* **Snapshot isolation** : most relational systems. Adequate for most OLTP.
* **Read-committed / read-your-writes** : easy everywhere.
* **Eventual consistency** : Cassandra, DynamoDB, distributed caches.
* **Per-partition strong, cross-partition eventual** : wide-column stores by default.

#### Axis 4: Scale — Data Volume and Throughput

* **< 100 GB, <10k QPS**: single-node anything. Do not distribute until you have to.
* **100 GB to a few TB, 10k-100k QPS** : a well-tuned single-node PostgreSQL still works. Add read replicas before sharding.
* **Multi-TB OLTP, >100k QPS**: sharded relational (Vitess for MySQL, Citus for PostgreSQL), or distributed SQL (CockroachDB, Spanner), or NoSQL (Cassandra, DynamoDB).
* **Petabyte-scale analytics** : cloud data warehouses (Snowflake, BigQuery, Redshift).
* **Trillions of events** : streaming systems (Kafka + stream processors) plus specialized sinks.

#### Axis 5: Operational Complexity Budget

* **Small team, no 24/7 ops** : prefer single-node managed services. One PostgreSQL is easier than five engines.
* **Dedicated infra team** : can operate Cassandra, Kafka, Spark, Flink, etc. But each one costs a person-year of learning.

**A note on managed services.** Whether to run an engine yourself or use a managed service is as consequential as which engine to pick. Managed PostgreSQL (RDS, Cloud SQL, Neon, Supabase) removes storage management, OS patching, minor-version upgrades, and failover automation. ElastiCache does the same for Redis. DynamoDB and Snowflake are managed-only by design. For each candidate engine, verify whether a mature managed offering exists — and if it does, the default should be to use it unless scale, cost, or data-residency constraints make self-hosting necessary.

Self-hosting an engine the team has never operated before is one of the most reliable ways to create a 3 a.m. incident. A managed Cassandra (Astra DB, Amazon Keyspaces) can handle 90% of Cassandra workloads without the operational overhead. The 10% where self-hosted wins is almost always a cost or compliance argument at very large scale.

The most common design mistake is to choose an engine that matches the data perfectly but exceeds the team's ability to operate it. A slightly-wrong engine that the team knows cold beats a perfect engine they are learning as they go.

> ### Definition: Polyglot Persistence
> 
> An architectural approach in which a single application uses multiple data storage technologies — relational, document, key-value, columnar, graph, full-text — each chosen for the subproblem it solves best. The term was coined by Martin Fowler and became mainstream in the early 2010s.

* * *

### 22.2 Engine Cheat Sheet

A short catalog of engines and their sweet spots. This is a working reference, not a purchase order.

#### PostgreSQL

* **Engine type** : Relational, B+ tree, MVCC.
* **Strengths** : The most versatile single engine. SQL, transactions, JSON (JSONB), arrays, full-text search, geospatial (PostGIS), vectors (pgvector), triggers, materialized views, extensions. Strong durability. Mature tooling.
* **Weaknesses** : Scale beyond a large single node requires sharding (Citus) or replication gymnastics. Analytics across billions of rows are slow compared to columnar engines.
* **Use when** : Default for any new OLTP application under petabyte scale.

#### MySQL / InnoDB

* **Engine type** : Relational, B+ tree, MVCC.
* **Strengths** : Mature, widely deployed, extensive tooling. Vitess provides horizontal sharding. Generally lower per-node overhead than PostgreSQL.
* **Weaknesses** : Historically weaker on advanced SQL features and extensibility than PostgreSQL. Replication is more operator-hostile.
* **Use when** : You are already in the MySQL ecosystem, or Vitess-style sharding fits.

#### SQLite

* **Engine type** : Embedded relational, B+ tree.
* **Strengths** : Zero ops. File-based. Full ACID. Fast for single-process workloads. Runs on everything including phones.
* **Weaknesses** : One writer at a time (WAL mode helps but does not eliminate). Not a server.
* **Use when** : Embedded storage, per-user local state, desktop apps, edge devices, single-process services with modest concurrency.

#### Redis

* **Engine type** : In-memory KV, optional persistence.
* **Strengths** : Sub-millisecond latency. Rich data types (lists, sets, hashes, sorted sets, streams). Pub/sub. Great for caches, rate limiting, session storage, leaderboards, distributed locks.
* **Weaknesses** : Dataset bounded by RAM unless you use Redis Enterprise / tiered storage. Not a durable system of record by default.
* **Use when** : Cache, ephemeral or rapidly-changing state, real-time aggregations.

#### MongoDB

* **Engine type** : Document (BSON), B+ tree indexes, MVCC.
* **Strengths** : Flexible schema. Nested documents. Horizontal sharding built in. Good developer experience for applications whose data _is_ documents.
* **Weaknesses** : Joins weak; denormalization is the path. Cross-document transactions exist but are expensive.
* **Use when** : Your data is genuinely hierarchical and varies in shape; you want a single engine that scales horizontally and looks programmer-friendly.

#### Cassandra / ScyllaDB

* **Engine type** : Wide-column, LSM tree.
* **Strengths** : Massive write throughput. Horizontal scale. Tunable consistency. High availability through replication. Time-series workloads.
* **Weaknesses** : No joins. Query model forces schema-for-queries. Operationally complex (repair, tombstones, compaction tuning).
* **Use when** : Write-heavy workloads partitioned naturally by a key; time-series, event logs, audit trails.

#### DynamoDB

* **Engine type** : Managed wide-column / KV, LSM-based internally.
* **Strengths** : Fully managed. Predictable single-digit-millisecond latency. Autoscaling. Global tables for multi-region.
* **Weaknesses** : Cost at scale. Limited query flexibility. Cross-partition queries are painful.
* **Use when** : You are in AWS and want managed NoSQL without running Cassandra.

#### DuckDB

* **Engine type** : Embedded columnar analytical.
* **Strengths** : SQL, vectorized execution, reads Parquet and CSV natively. Zero ops. Fast local analytics.
* **Weaknesses** : Single-process; not a server. Not for OLTP.
* **Use when** : Analytics on a single machine, ETL scripts, data science notebooks, edge analytics. The "SQLite of analytics."

#### ClickHouse

* **Engine type** : Distributed columnar OLAP.
* **Strengths** : Extremely fast for analytical queries on billions of rows. Real-time ingestion. Good compression.
* **Weaknesses** : Weak on complex joins (though improving). Eventual consistency on deletes and updates.
* **Use when** : Real-time analytics dashboards, log analytics, observability at scale.

#### Snowflake / BigQuery / Redshift

* **Engine type** : Cloud data warehouses, columnar.
* **Strengths** : Managed, petabyte-scale, separation of compute and storage, tight integration with cloud ecosystems.
* **Weaknesses** : Cost. Latency for ad-hoc exploration. Vendor lock-in.
* **Use when** : Enterprise analytics, data lakes, BI, ML feature stores.

#### Elasticsearch / OpenSearch

* **Engine type** : Inverted-index search engine.
* **Strengths** : Full-text search, log analytics, faceted search, aggregations. Relevance ranking.
* **Weaknesses** : Not a primary store. Expensive for raw storage. Operational complexity.
* **Use when** : Text search, log/metric exploration, complex faceted queries.

#### Kafka

* **Engine type** : Distributed log. Not exactly a database, but behaves like one for streaming.
* **Strengths** : High-throughput append-only log. Durable, replayable, horizontally scalable. Backbone of event-driven architectures and CDC pipelines.
* **Weaknesses** : Not indexed; random access is terrible. Not a query engine.
* **Use when** : Event sourcing, stream processing, CDC, decoupling services.

#### Neo4j / Graph Databases

* **Engine type** : Property graph, adjacency-list storage.
* **Strengths** : Deep traversals, pattern matching (Cypher), recommendation engines, fraud detection.
* **Weaknesses** : Not built for tabular analytics. Sharding is hard for graph data.
* **Use when** : Genuinely graph-shaped problems with multi-hop traversals.

#### Spanner / CockroachDB / YugabyteDB

* **Engine type** : Distributed SQL, usually LSM + Raft/Paxos.
* **Strengths** : Horizontal scalability with strong consistency, geo-distribution, SQL compatibility.
* **Weaknesses** : Higher write latency than single-region relational. Operational subtlety.
* **Use when** : You truly need horizontal-scale + strong consistency + SQL. This is often overkill; verify you cannot solve the problem with a sharded PostgreSQL first.

* * *

### 22.3 Workload Archetypes and Recommended Stacks

Let us go the other direction. Given a workload archetype, what engines tend to appear?

#### Archetype 1: The Transactional Web App (most web apps)

* **Primary store** : PostgreSQL or MySQL. Holds users, orders, content, configuration, permissions.
* **Cache** : Redis. Session state, hot reads, rate-limit counters.
* **Search** (if applicable): PostgreSQL full-text for small corpora, Elasticsearch for large ones.
* **Object storage** (if files): S3 or equivalent, with URLs in the relational store.

That is usually the whole stack for the first few years. Anything more is premature.

#### Archetype 2: Real-Time Analytics SaaS

* **Ingest** : Kafka (or Kinesis, or equivalent) receiving events.
* **Hot analytical store** : ClickHouse or Druid serving real-time dashboards.
* **Cold warehouse** : Snowflake / BigQuery for long-term history and ad-hoc analysis.
* **Primary OLTP** : PostgreSQL for account metadata, billing, users.
* **Cache** : Redis for UI responsiveness.

The hot/cold split is the defining feature. Hot path answers "last 7 days" in <1 second; cold path answers "past 3 years, sliced six ways" in seconds to minutes.

#### Archetype 3: IoT / Time-Series

* **Primary ingest store** : Cassandra, InfluxDB, or TimescaleDB. Partitioned by device and time.
* **Stream processor** : Flink or Kafka Streams for windowed aggregations and anomaly detection.
* **Archive** : S3 + Parquet for long-term cheap storage.
* **Dashboarding** : Grafana over Prometheus (for metrics) or ClickHouse (for events).

Cassandra's LSM writes and time-window compaction fit telemetry beautifully. The 30-day retention TTL drops oldest data automatically.

#### Archetype 4: E-Commerce at Scale

* **Catalog** (read-heavy): PostgreSQL + Redis, or MongoDB for complex product variants.
* **Inventory** (correctness-critical): PostgreSQL with strict transactions, possibly cached via Redis.
* **Orders** : PostgreSQL. Transactions required.
* **Search** : Elasticsearch over the catalog.
* **Cart** (ephemeral): Redis.
* **Reviews / social** : MongoDB or PostgreSQL JSONB.
* **Recommendations** (graph-ish): Neo4j or a graph-processing layer over the warehouse.
* **Event stream** : Kafka for audit, CDC, and downstream consumers.
* **Analytics** : Snowflake, fed by CDC from the OLTP stores.

Yes, that is a lot of engines. Large e-commerce sites really do run stacks like this. Each engine is earning its keep by being the best tool for its slice of the problem.

#### Archetype 5: Social / Content Platform

* **User graph** : often sharded relational, sometimes graph DB.
* **Posts / timeline** : Cassandra or a sharded relational system. Heavy write volume, user-partitioned reads.
* **Messaging** : Cassandra or a custom LSM store, partitioned by conversation.
* **Notification fanout** : Redis queues + Kafka.
* **Search** : Elasticsearch.
* **Media** : Object storage (S3) with CDN.
* **Analytics** : Big warehouse + streaming.

#### Archetype 6: The Small Startup

* **Everything** : PostgreSQL.
* **Maybe** : Redis for a cache.

And this is a perfectly good architecture. Do not add engines until their absence is causing real pain. Every added engine adds an operational burden roughly equivalent to a team member. A startup with three engineers should not be running five datastores.

* * *

### 22.4 The Joints: How Data Moves Between Engines

The hardest parts of a polyglot architecture are not the engines themselves — it is what happens _between_ them. A write lands in the OLTP store; how does it get to the cache, the search index, the analytics warehouse, and the audit log, reliably and in a timely manner?

Three patterns dominate.

#### Pattern A: Dual Writes (Usually Wrong)

The application writes to both systems in sequence:
    
    
    db.save(order)         # write to PostgreSQL
    cache.set(order.id, order)  # write to Redis
    search.index(order)    # write to Elasticsearch
    

This looks simple and is the most common source of data drift in polyglot systems. Problems:

* **Partial failure** : the first write succeeds, the second fails, now the two stores disagree. There is no transaction across them.
* **Ordering across replicas** : two concurrent updates from different clients can land in different orders at different stores.
* **Retries** fix one problem and create another: now the write happens twice in some stores.

If you are doing dual writes you need idempotency keys, retry with backoff, a reconciliation job, and monitoring for drift. Even then, it is brittle.

**Is dual-write ever acceptable?** Yes, with preconditions. Dual-write is appropriate when:

  1. **Idempotency is trivially achievable** — e.g., both stores are keyed by a client-generated UUID and the operation is "upsert by ID."
  2. **The derived store is a cache or search index** , not a system of record. Drift there is an inconvenience, not a data-loss event; a reconciliation job sweeping the primary can heal it.
  3. **CDC is genuinely unavailable or prohibitively complex** — e.g., a legacy primary with no change-data feed.
  4. **You have invested in reconciliation infrastructure** : a daily diff job comparing the two stores, alerts when drift exceeds threshold, and an owned runbook for resolution.

Small, carefully-built dual-write systems work in production for years. The anti-pattern is _careless_ dual-write without any of those four disciplines — which is how most teams end up writing dual-write and why it shows up on every "anti-pattern" list. Reach for CDC first; if CDC is a poor fit, build dual-write with the four disciplines, not by hope.

#### Pattern B: Change Data Capture (CDC)

Instead of the application writing to each store, the **primary store is the source of truth** and a CDC pipeline propagates changes to derived stores.
    
    
    [App] → [PostgreSQL] --WAL--> [Debezium/Connector] → [Kafka] → [ES/cache/warehouse]
    

The pipeline reads the primary store's write-ahead log (Chapter 17) — PostgreSQL's logical replication, MySQL's binlog, MongoDB's oplog — and turns each committed change into a Kafka event. Downstream consumers update their local stores.

**Benefits** :

* One source of truth. All other stores are derived.
* Exactly-once or at-least-once semantics, depending on connector and idempotency design.
* Decoupling: adding a new downstream consumer (say, a new search index) does not touch the application code.
* Rebuilds possible: tear down a derived store and rebuild from Kafka or from a full snapshot + log tail.

**Costs** :

* Pipeline is now load-bearing infrastructure. Outages propagate.
* Eventual consistency: the search index lags the database by seconds, sometimes minutes.
* Schema evolution is harder — all downstream consumers must handle each new field version.

> ### CDC Consistency Contract: What Reads See During Lag
> 
> A CDC pipeline establishes a **read-your-writes + eventual consistency** contract for derived stores, with specific semantics that vary by consumer type:
> 
> **During normal operation (lag < a few seconds):**
> 
>   * A write committed to PostgreSQL appears in Kafka after the WAL record is read by Debezium — typically within 10–500 ms.
>   * The Elasticsearch consumer applies the event and makes it queryable — typically within another 100 ms–2 s.
>   * A user who writes a record and immediately searches for it in Elasticsearch may or may not see it. This is expected and must be communicated to users (e.g., "results may take a few seconds to appear").
> 

> 
> **During pipeline lag (seconds to minutes, under backpressure or connector restart):**
> 
>   * The derived store is **read-stale** : queries return results based on the last committed offset in Kafka, not the current state of PostgreSQL.
>   * The lag is **monotonic within a partition** : Kafka's total order within a partition means you will never see event N without having seen all events before N. However, for consumers reading multiple partitions, events from different partitions may arrive out of wall-clock order.
>   * **Reads from the primary are always current.** Application code that needs fresh data must read directly from PostgreSQL (or the authoritative store), not from the CDC-derived index.
> 

> 
> **During pipeline outage (connector down or Kafka unavailable):**
> 
>   * The derived store is **frozen** : no new writes propagate. Reads return arbitrarily stale data. The last-applied offset is known (Debezium stores offsets in a Kafka topic); on reconnection, the pipeline replays from that offset.
>   * The primary store is unaffected — it continues to accept writes normally.
> 

> 
> **Design implication.** Application code must be written with the CDC lag contract in mind: never read from a derived store when the operation requires the latest committed value (e.g., "does this item already exist?"). Read from the primary for write-path checks; use derived stores only for read-path queries where stale-by-seconds is acceptable.

> ### Definition: Change Data Capture (CDC)
> 
> A pattern in which changes to a primary database (inserts, updates, deletes) are captured from the database's own write-ahead log and streamed — typically via Kafka or a similar message bus — to downstream consumers that maintain derived views (caches, search indexes, warehouses, other services). The primary store is the source of truth; all others are eventually consistent projections.

#### Pattern C: Event Sourcing

Instead of storing current state and deriving the log, store the **log** and derive current state. Every user action is an event appended to Kafka (or a purpose-built event store). Consumers materialize views — a current-order table, a search index, an analytics cube — from the event stream.

**Benefits** :

* Complete history is native. Auditability, reproducibility, "what did the system look like at time T?"
* Polyglot stores are all consumers of the same log.

**Costs** :

* Query patterns are harder — everything is derived, so changing a view requires re-reading the log.
* Schema evolution and event versioning are ongoing concerns.
* Up-front investment is high.

#### Pattern D: ETL and Batch

For analytics, most systems still run a nightly ETL: bulk-extract from OLTP, transform, load into the warehouse. Simple, robust, and appropriate when real-time is not required. Many companies use CDC _and_ batch ETL: CDC for operational derived stores (caches, search), batch ETL for the analytical warehouse.

#### Choosing a Pattern

* **Dual writes** : acceptable for caches and search indices when idempotency + reconciliation + drift monitoring are all in place; avoid for systems of record.
* **CDC** : default for modern polyglot systems with real-time derived views.
* **Event sourcing** : for audit-heavy domains (finance, healthcare) or when "history is the point."
* **ETL batch** : for analytical warehouses where latency of hours is fine.

Most real architectures blend these. CDC for the user-facing parts. Batch ETL for the warehouse. Dual writes tolerated in a few places with guilt.

* * *

### 22.5 Consistency Across Engines

The moment you have more than one datastore, you have a new consistency question: **what happens when two stores disagree?**

Some disagreements are benign. The search index lags the OLTP store by a few seconds — the user might search for an item they just created and not find it yet. Annoying but usually acceptable, especially if the UI shows the item in "your recent activity" directly from the OLTP store.

Some disagreements are not benign. The cache shows a price of 10 USD; the OLTP store says the price was just updated to 15 USD. If checkout reads the cache, customers pay 10 USD for an item now priced at 15 USD. Real money, real refunds, real anger.

Countermeasures:

* **Read-your-writes via source-of-truth fallback** : after a write, the UI reads from the OLTP store for a short window, not the cache.
* **Version stamps** : every entity has a version number. Cache entries and search index entries carry the version they were built from. If a client is willing to pay the latency, it can verify the version against the OLTP store.
* **TTLs** : caches expire, so drift heals automatically. Short TTLs are cheap insurance.
* **Explicit invalidation** : writes invalidate cache keys. CDC-driven invalidation is more reliable than application-driven.
* **Reconciliation jobs** : a background task that compares OLTP and derived stores, flagging discrepancies.

#### Misconception

> ### Common Misconception
> 
> **"If we pick strongly consistent engines everywhere, the system is strongly consistent."** No. Consistency is a property of the _system_ , not of the components. Two strongly consistent databases connected by eventual-consistent replication form an eventually-consistent system. The weakest link wins.

* * *

### 22.6 The Cost Side: What Each Engine Adds

A new engine is not free. Here is the full cost of ownership, not just the license.

  1. **Operational burden.** Deployment, upgrades, monitoring, backups, capacity planning. Figure one to two engineer-years of learning per serious engine.
  2. **On-call surface area.** Each engine can fail in its own way at 3 AM. Runbooks multiply.
  3. **Consistency plumbing.** CDC, reconciliation, version stamps, idempotency — each engine connects to at least one other.
  4. **Cost of skills.** Hiring someone who knows Cassandra well is harder (and pricier) than hiring someone who knows PostgreSQL. "Managed services" help but do not eliminate this.
  5. **Observability overhead.** Each engine has its own metrics, traces, and logs. Dashboards for each. Alerts for each.
  6. **Data-model cognitive overhead.** Developers must remember which data lives where, which consistency guarantees apply, which queries work in which engine. In teams with turnover, this knowledge gets lost.

A rule of thumb: a new datastore should save more engineering time (or unlock more revenue) than it costs in ongoing operations. If you cannot articulate what the new engine does that your existing engines cannot, you probably do not need it.

#### When to Consolidate

A sign you have too many engines: multiple engines hold overlapping data with bidirectional sync. You are fighting drift constantly. Often you can collapse two engines into one — PostgreSQL with JSONB can replace a lightweight MongoDB usage; PostgreSQL full-text search can replace an underused Elasticsearch; a well-indexed relational table can replace an ad-hoc KV store.

* * *

### 22.7 Case Study 1: A Realistic E-Commerce Stack

Let us walk through a real-ish architecture and critique it. The company is mid-size: ~1M daily active users, ~100k orders/day.

#### The Stack

* **Primary OLTP** : Sharded PostgreSQL (Citus or manual sharding by customer region).
* **Session and cart cache** : Redis cluster.
* **Product search** : Elasticsearch.
* **Recommendations** : Neo4j (purchase graph) + precomputed recommendations in Redis.
* **Event bus** : Kafka, with Debezium CDC from PostgreSQL.
* **Real-time fraud detection** : Flink consuming Kafka.
* **Analytics** : Snowflake, fed by CDC via Kafka Connect and by nightly batch ETL.
* **Media** : S3 behind a CDN.

#### Data Flows

  1. **User places an order.** App writes to PostgreSQL in a transaction (inventory decrement, order insert, payment record). CDC emits the changes to Kafka.
  2. **Cart clears.** App deletes the cart key from Redis.
  3. **Search index updates.** A Kafka consumer updates Elasticsearch with new stock levels within seconds.
  4. **Fraud scoring.** A Flink job reads the order event, computes features, writes a score back via another Kafka topic. App reads the score before shipping confirmation (with a short timeout).
  5. **Warehouse ingest.** Kafka Connect streams orders, users, and inventory changes to Snowflake. A nightly batch reconciles and runs quality checks.
  6. **Recommendation refresh.** Nightly batch in Snowflake computes new recommendations, writes to Neo4j and pushes hot user recommendations to Redis.

#### Strengths

* PostgreSQL is the unambiguous source of truth for anything money-related. No dual writes to money.
* CDC decouples downstream systems. Adding a new consumer (say, a loyalty-points service) does not touch the checkout code.
* Fraud and recommendations run on their own engines without slowing OLTP.

#### Risks

* The Kafka pipeline is the most critical piece of infrastructure. A full pipeline outage stalls search freshness, recommendations, fraud scoring, and warehouse ingest. Monitoring and SLAs for Kafka are as important as for the database.
* Snowflake costs can balloon; requires vigilance.
* The Neo4j graph may not pay its way if recommendations are simple enough to compute in a batch job and cache in Redis. A sign to consider consolidation.

#### A Scaled-Down Version

For a smaller version of the same company at 10× less traffic:

* PostgreSQL (single primary + read replica).
* Redis (session + cache + precomputed recommendations).
* Elasticsearch only if full-text search is core; otherwise PostgreSQL full-text.
* Kafka only if CDC is worth it; otherwise direct dual-writes-with-idempotency for search.
* Analytics: DuckDB over nightly dumps, or a single-node Snowflake. No Flink. No Neo4j.

Fewer engines, same functional outcome, much less ops.

* * *

### 22.8 Case Study 2: A Financial Services System

A trading platform for retail investors. 500k users, ~10k trades/second at market open.

#### Constraints

* **Regulatory** : every trade must be auditable. Immutable log required.
* **Correctness** : a user must never be able to sell shares they do not own. Strong consistency on account balances.
* **Latency** : order submission to confirmation in <50 ms at p99.
* **Durability** : no data loss, even for in-flight trades during a crash.

#### The Stack

* **Order processing core** : In-memory event-sourced system backed by Kafka (durable log) and periodic snapshots. Every state change is a Kafka event.
* **Account balances** : PostgreSQL with strict serializable transactions for balance updates. Could be Spanner or CockroachDB if geographic distribution is needed.
* **Market data feed** : Time-series database (InfluxDB or kdb+) for real-time prices.
* **Historical trades** : Kafka (forever) + Parquet in S3 for cheap long-term storage.
* **Analytics and reporting** : Snowflake, ingested from Kafka.
* **User profile / auth** : PostgreSQL.

#### Why Event Sourcing Here

Financial systems have strong requirements for auditability: regulators want to know the exact state of the system at any past moment. Event sourcing makes this natural — replay the log up to time T, reconstruct state. Materialized views can be rebuilt from the log at any time. This is the textbook use case for event sourcing and it is a ton of work to set up; the stringent requirements justify the cost.

#### What Would Go Wrong

* If PostgreSQL goes down during a trade, the event-sourced log has the trade recorded, but the account balance view is stale. The system needs to either fail the trade (safe) or accept that balances lag (probably unacceptable for trading).
* If Kafka goes down, order submission stalls entirely. Kafka becomes the system's single most critical dependency — multi-AZ replication, aggressive monitoring, vendor support.

* * *

### 22.9 Case Study 3: A Learning Platform (Thread A, Matured)

Let us come back to Thread A — the University Registration System — and imagine how it would evolve as the university grows into a large online-learning platform serving millions of learners.

#### Current (Chapter 1-7 baseline)

A single PostgreSQL database holding Students, Instructors, Courses, Enrollments, Grades. Maybe a Redis cache.

#### Matured: 10M learners, 100k concurrent users during exam periods

* **Student / course / enrollment data** : PostgreSQL (primary-replica, large node). Still the source of truth for registration records.
* **Session state** : Redis cluster. Session tokens, rate limiting, login attempt counters.
* **Assignment submissions** : PostgreSQL for metadata; S3 for the files themselves.
* **Video lectures** : S3 + CDN.
* **Learning activity telemetry** (every click, every quiz interaction): Cassandra, partitioned by `session_id`. Massive write volume, eventually consistent.
* **Search** (find a course, an instructor, a topic): Elasticsearch, fed by CDC from PostgreSQL.
* **Recommendations** : A nightly batch in DuckDB or Snowflake, results pushed to Redis.
* **Analytics / reporting for administrators** : Snowflake or a columnar system (ClickHouse), fed by CDC + batch.
* **Audit log** (FERPA/GDPR compliance): Kafka (hot) + S3 Parquet (cold archive).
* **Messaging between learners and tutors** : A wide-column or document store partitioned by conversation.

This is a dozen engines. It looks like a lot, and it is. But notice: each engine solves a specific subproblem, and removing any one of them breaks a specific capability. The platform's previous architecture (PostgreSQL for everything) cannot absorb the telemetry write rate; cannot search the catalog quickly; cannot hold a decade of video; cannot generate real-time analytics dashboards.

#### The Engineering Team

A platform like this has a data infrastructure team of 5-10 people whose main job is to keep the joints working: CDC pipelines, schema evolution, cost management, reliability. That is the real cost of polyglot persistence, and it scales with the number of engines roughly linearly.

* * *

### 22.10 Anti-Patterns

A few patterns that look reasonable but usually do not work.

#### Anti-pattern 1: "We will use a NoSQL database for flexibility, and add transactions later."

You cannot bolt on transactions. Whatever invariants your system needs — balance consistency, inventory correctness, unique constraints — need to be in the design from the start. Starting with a loose store and "adding rigor later" almost always means accepting data that violates the invariants forever. If you need strong consistency, pick an engine that provides it.

#### Anti-pattern 2: "We will just use MongoDB for everything."
