MongoDB can serve many use cases reasonably, but its weaknesses (joins, multi-document transactions, analytics) become the whole team's weaknesses when everything is in it. Systems that stayed on MongoDB for everything often add PostgreSQL later for the relational subset.

#### Anti-pattern 3: "Let's introduce Kafka to decouple services."

Kafka is valuable when you have real streaming needs (CDC, event-driven integrations, replayability). Adding Kafka purely to "decouple" services often creates an eventually-consistent distributed system where a synchronous API call would have been fine. The decoupling benefit is real but the consistency cost is real too.

#### Anti-pattern 4: "We'll cache everything in Redis."

A cache is not a primary store. Caching everything means your durable system of record is unclear — if Redis loses its data, what do you rebuild from? Caches should be derived, rebuildable, and TTL-bounded.

#### Anti-pattern 5: "We picked a distributed SQL database so we do not have to think about sharding."

Distributed SQL (Spanner, CockroachDB) _does_ make horizontal scale easier, but it also makes local operations slower (cross-region latency, consensus overhead) and it makes schema changes more complex. If your data fits on one large node, one large node is usually simpler and faster. Distributed SQL earns its place when you genuinely cannot fit on one node.

* * *

### 22.11 A Decision Checklist

When considering whether to add a new engine to the stack:

  1. **What specific workload are we serving that existing engines cannot?** Be precise. "Performance" is not an answer; "p99 latency <2 ms at 100k QPS" is.
  2. **Can we scale or tune an existing engine to meet this need?** Read replicas, partial indexes, JSONB, pgvector, PostgreSQL full-text, materialized views — how far does the existing engine go?
  3. **What is the data flow between the new engine and the existing ones?** How does data get in? How does it stay consistent? What is the recovery story?
  4. **What is the operational cost?** Who is on call? What happens when it goes down? Who knows how to tune it?
  5. **Is there a managed service?** If yes, it dramatically reduces operational cost.
  6. **What is the exit plan?** If this engine does not work out, how do we migrate off?

If every question has a clear answer, adding the engine is probably right. If any question is shrug-shrouded, stop and rethink.

* * *

### 22.12 Tradeoffs Summary

* **More engines → more capability and more cost.** The cost scales with the number of joints between engines, which grows super-linearly.
* **One big engine → simpler ops, lower peak performance.** A tuned PostgreSQL solves more problems than most teams think. Do not branch out until you have a real reason.
* **CDC is almost always the right way to propagate data.** Dual writes are a trap. Event sourcing is the nuclear option.
* **Consistency is a system property.** Strong consistency in every component does not give you a strongly consistent system; you still have to design for the integration points.
* **The weakest link determines overall availability and consistency.** A 99.9% available component is fine; five of them in a chain gives 99.5%, which is ~44 hours of downtime per year.

The next chapter — the last of the book — stitches every concept together on a single realistic system design. **Chapter 23** treats you as a database designer who must combine SQL schema, indexes, transactions, concurrency control, replication, partitioning, and engine choice to solve an end-to-end problem. It is the capstone: everything in one place.

* * *

### 22.13 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. Define polyglot persistence in your own words. What is the minimum number of datastores that counts as "polyglot"?

  2. Name three specific costs of adding a new datastore that do not appear on its product page.

  3. What does CDC stand for? Which property of a database's write-ahead log makes CDC possible?

  4. List three workload axes from this chapter and, for each, give one type of engine that optimizes for one end of that axis.

  5. Why are dual writes considered an anti-pattern for keeping two stores consistent? Name two specific failure modes.

  6. Give one real architectural reason to use event sourcing, and one reason _not_ to.

#### Application Problems

  7. A team is running a PostgreSQL database with ~500 GB of data and ~20k QPS, and they are considering adding Cassandra "for scale." What three questions would you ask before approving this change?

Hint

"For scale" is not a workload. What specific query or write pattern is straining PostgreSQL today, and can that one pattern be peeled off instead of migrating everything? Also ask who will operate Cassandra on-call and how the CDC/consistency story between the two stores will be maintained.

  8. For each of the following subproblems, recommend a primary engine and a cache (if any): (a) a leaderboard for a 50M-user game, updating every second; (b) a product catalog with 100k SKUs, read-heavy; (c) a real-time location tracker for 500k delivery vehicles, writing every 5 seconds.

  9. You discover that your production cache (Redis) and your primary store (PostgreSQL) disagree: the cache shows a user as "active" when the database shows "deleted." The discrepancy has persisted for 3 hours. (a) What are two likely causes? (b) How would you prevent this class of drift in the future?

  10. Design a CDC pipeline from PostgreSQL to Elasticsearch for a products table. Describe: (a) how changes are captured from PostgreSQL; (b) what Kafka topic structure you would use; (c) how the consumer handles updates and deletes; (d) what happens if the consumer is down for 6 hours.

Hint

For (a), which PostgreSQL feature turns committed changes into a replicable stream? For (b) and (c), think about whether one topic per table is enough, and how the consumer must translate INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE events into ES index operations idempotently. For (d), what bounds how long Kafka can buffer before the consumer falls off the retention window?

  11. Your team is debating between (a) storing user profile JSON in MongoDB vs (b) storing it in PostgreSQL JSONB. List three concrete questions whose answers would tip the decision one way or the other.

  12. A startup's architecture: PostgreSQL (OLTP), Redis (cache), Elasticsearch (search), MongoDB (product catalog), Cassandra (events), Snowflake (analytics), Neo4j (recommendations), Kafka (events). Their team is 8 engineers. (a) Which two engines would you most strongly argue to remove or replace? Explain. (b) Which operational capability is most likely underinvested?

Hint

Eight engines for eight engineers means ~1 person-year of operational knowledge per engine — almost none. Which engines overlap functionally with PostgreSQL (JSONB, full-text, recursive CTEs, extensions) and could be collapsed? For (b), count how many of those eight engines have no dedicated owner for upgrades, backups, or on-call — the one that is load-bearing and unowned is the answer.

  13. Your analytics team wants real-time revenue dashboards that currently lag by 24 hours. The OLTP store is PostgreSQL; the warehouse is Snowflake fed by nightly batch. Design a change that gets dashboards to <1 minute staleness without affecting OLTP performance. Identify the tradeoffs.

Hint

Nightly batch is easy but slow; querying OLTP directly is fast but hurts production. What intermediate tier (streaming ETL, a real-time analytical store like ClickHouse, or CDC into Snowflake streams) buys seconds-fresh data without scan load on PostgreSQL? What does each option cost in operations and in consistency guarantees?

  14. An e-commerce site has this checkout flow: app writes order to PostgreSQL, then writes to Redis "cart cleared" marker, then publishes to Kafka for downstream (email, analytics). If the process crashes after PostgreSQL but before Redis, the cart still shows items when the user reloads. How would you fix this? Consider (a) transactional outbox, (b) CDC-driven invalidation, (c) read-from-source-of-truth after write.

#### Think Deeper

  15. Revisit Anti-pattern 1 ("add transactions later"). Why is it so hard to retrofit strong consistency onto a loose store? Give a specific example in which code works today but would silently produce wrong data if you add concurrent writers without transactions.

  16. Kafka is described in this chapter as "the backbone of modern polyglot persistence." Steelman the opposite view: what architectural argument would you make for a system that avoids Kafka entirely? When would that be the right call?

  17. The case studies in this chapter show architectures with 5-12 engines. The chapter also says "start with PostgreSQL and add only when needed." Where is the crossover point? Sketch a few signals that indicate an organization has outgrown its current stack.

Hint

Think in terms of specific symptoms, not abstractions: a query that times out no matter how you index it, a write rate that saturates WAL I/O, a search pattern that tsvector can't tune for, an analytics scan that locks OLTP for minutes. Each symptom points at a different "next engine." Which three symptoms would you list first, and what engine does each unlock?

  18. The Chapter 18 CAP/PACELC framework and this chapter's polyglot framework feel closely related. Compose them: for each engine in your recommended stack, label its CAP/PACELC position. What does this tell you about the system as a whole?

Hint

Label each engine's behavior under partition (CP or AP) and under normal conditions (latency- or consistency-favoring). Then ask: if your OLTP is CP but your cache is AP and your analytics tier is eventually consistent, what is the _system's_ guarantee to the user? Which component dictates the weakest link?

  19. Read-your-writes across a polyglot system is a subtle guarantee. Design a read-your-writes protocol that works across PostgreSQL (primary) and an eventually-consistent search index. What mechanism tracks "which writes has the user made"?

  20. Imagine a future where one engine — call it "everything-store" — can match every specialized engine's performance on every workload. Would polyglot persistence disappear? Think about whether the multi-engine pattern is driven by performance, or by other factors (team specialization, blast radius, cost allocation, schema discipline).

* * *

### Chapter Summary

Polyglot persistence is the modern answer to the fact that no single engine is best at everything. Relational engines excel at transactional correctness; column stores excel at analytics; KV stores excel at sub-millisecond lookups; LSM-based wide-column stores excel at write throughput; search engines excel at text; graph databases excel at deep traversals. Real systems combine them, each engine earning its keep.

The hardest part of a polyglot architecture is not the individual engines — it is the joints between them. Change data capture (CDC) is the pattern most systems converge on: the OLTP store is the source of truth, its write-ahead log drives an event stream, and downstream consumers maintain derived views. Dual writes are the common trap. Event sourcing is the heavy-duty option when the log itself is the point.

Every added engine carries costs: operational burden, on-call surface, consistency plumbing, cognitive overhead. A new engine should save more than it costs. Small teams should run one engine (usually PostgreSQL) as long as possible. Big teams with real diverse workloads genuinely benefit from a dozen engines, but they pay for it in data-infrastructure staffing.

There are no universal rules, but there is a decision framework: match workload axes (read/write ratio, access pattern, consistency, scale, ops budget) to engine strengths; design the joints before you admire the components; treat the system's consistency as weakest-link. The next and final chapter puts all of this to work on a single end-to-end design exercise.

---

## Chapter 23: Putting It All Together — A Full Design Review

This is the final chapter. It does not introduce a new engine, a new algorithm, or a new theorem. Instead, it asks you to use every idea in the book at once, on a single realistic system design problem, and to defend your decisions.

The premise: you are the founding database engineer at a mid-sized company that runs an online learning platform (call it **Thread A** , grown up). The product has survived its scrappy early days on a single PostgreSQL instance. It is now time to redesign the data layer for the next three years of growth. Your job is to produce a design document that covers schema, indexes, transactions, isolation levels, replication, partitioning, caching, analytics, and the CDC/ETL plumbing that binds them together — and to explain, for every choice, which tradeoff you are taking and why.

We will walk through the design in the same order you would tackle it in practice. At each step we revisit the relevant chapter's concepts and apply them. The goal is not to arrive at the single correct answer — there isn't one — but to practice the kind of end-to-end reasoning that separates "picked an engine" from "designed a system."

**Learning goals for this chapter:**

* Translate a business description into data requirements.
* Design a normalized relational schema with appropriate indexes and constraints.
* Reason about transactions, concurrency, and isolation in context.
* Plan for growth: read replicas, sharding, caching, analytical stores.
* Specify the integration patterns (CDC, batch ETL, event streams) between stores.
* Anticipate failure modes and describe recovery procedures.
* Critique a completed design for the tradeoffs it has (and has not) accepted.

* * *

### 23.1 The Problem Statement

**LearnQuest** is a Socratic-learning platform for university students. Instructors publish courses made of reading materials, quizzes, projects, and live tutoring sessions. Learners enroll, progress through lessons, submit assignments, take quizzes, and interact with an AI tutor that reads their submission history to personalize help. Instructors grade submissions (sometimes with AI assistance), and administrators run reporting across courses, institutions, and time.

#### Current State (End of Year 0)

* One PostgreSQL instance, 1 TB of data, 5k concurrent users at peak.
* Single application server cluster. All reads and writes to the primary DB.
* Redis used as a session cache.
* Weekly analytics dashboards built from a nightly `pg_dump` loaded into a DuckDB for ad-hoc queries.

#### Projected State (Year 3)

* **10 million learners** , **50k concurrent users** at peak (exam periods hit hard).
* **1 billion quiz attempts per year**. Each attempt is a small row.
* **500 million pages of reading progress events** per month.
* **50 TB of assignment submissions** (PDFs, text, small images) by year 3.
* **AI tutor** makes ~10 DB lookups per conversation turn; plans for 100M turns per month.
* **Administrators** want real-time dashboards of course performance, instructor activity, and system health.
* **Regulatory** : FERPA compliance in the US, GDPR in the EU. Need per-user data export and deletion.

Everything else in the problem follows from those numbers. The existing single PostgreSQL cannot absorb a billion write events a year from quizzes, nor serve 50k concurrent reads at the latencies users expect during an exam.

#### Constraints You Cannot Negotiate

* Strong consistency for grades and enrollment (no "you took the quiz twice because the cache disagreed").
* Grade records immutable post-finalization; changes must be audited.
* All user data deletable on request within 30 days (GDPR).
* 99.9% availability SLA for the learner-facing application.
* On-premise data residency for EU learners (a single EU region).

* * *

### 23.2 Step 1: Identify the Entities and Their Access Patterns

Every database design begins here. Forget engines for a minute; write down the entities, their relationships, and how they are queried.

#### Entities

* **Institution** — a university or school that owns courses.
* **User** — a learner, instructor, or administrator. Has roles.
* **Course** — a curriculum belonging to an institution.
* **Lesson** — a chapter within a course (reading + videos).
* **Quiz** — a set of questions attached to a lesson.
* **Question** — belongs to a quiz.
* **Assignment** — a larger project attached to a course.
* **Enrollment** — binds a learner to a course.
* **Submission** — a learner's answer to a quiz question or assignment.
* **Grade** — a score attached to a submission or assignment.
* **Progress Event** — "learner X opened lesson Y at time T"; telemetry.
* **Tutor Conversation** — AI-tutor chat history for a learner.
* **Audit Event** — admin or instructor action, for compliance.

#### Access Patterns, by Volume and Urgency

Pattern | Volume | Latency | Notes  
---|---|---|---  
Learner opens a course; show lessons + progress | 50k RPS peak | <100 ms | Hot read  
Learner submits a quiz answer | 5k RPS peak | <500 ms | Write, must be durable  
Instructor views class dashboard | 1k RPS | <1 s | Aggregation  
AI tutor fetches recent submissions for a learner | 10k RPS | <50 ms | Hot read, small result  
Admin runs cross-institution analytics | 10 RPS | seconds to minutes | Complex OLAP  
Background telemetry (page views, clicks) | 50k RPS sustained | best-effort | Huge write volume  
Enrollment / course registration | 1k RPS during peak | <2 s | Transactional  
Grade finalization | 100 RPS | <5 s | Transactional, audited  
  
Immediately two workload clusters stand out: **OLTP-like** (small transactional writes, fast reads, strong consistency) and **high-volume telemetry** (append-only, write-heavy, eventually-consistent tolerable). A third cluster — **analytics** — sits off to the side with very different requirements.

This three-way split drives the architecture.

* * *

### 23.3 Step 2: The Core Relational Schema

Start with what Chapter 3 called the "default engine": a relational database. Design the schema for OLTP entities. We will worry about scale later.
    
    
    CREATE TABLE institutions (
        institution_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        name TEXT NOT NULL,
        region TEXT NOT NULL,      -- 'US', 'EU', 'APAC'
        created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE users (
        user_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        email CITEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
        display_name TEXT NOT NULL,
        institution_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES institutions,
        role TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (role IN ('learner','instructor','admin')),
        created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
        deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE courses (
        course_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        institution_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES institutions,
        title TEXT NOT NULL,
        description TEXT,
        owner_instructor_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES users,
        published BOOLEAN NOT NULL DEFAULT FALSE,
        created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE lessons (
        lesson_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        course_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES courses,
        order_index INT NOT NULL,
        title TEXT NOT NULL,
        body TEXT NOT NULL,
        UNIQUE (course_id, order_index)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE enrollments (
        enrollment_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        user_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES users,
        course_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES courses,
        enrolled_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
        status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('active','dropped','completed')),
        UNIQUE (user_id, course_id)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE quizzes (
        quiz_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        lesson_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES lessons,
        max_score NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,
        created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE quiz_questions (
        question_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        quiz_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES quizzes,
        order_index INT NOT NULL,
        prompt TEXT NOT NULL,
        answer_key JSONB NOT NULL,
        UNIQUE (quiz_id, order_index)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE submissions (
        submission_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        user_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES users,
        quiz_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES quizzes,
        started_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
        submitted_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
        status TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (status IN ('in_progress','submitted','graded'))
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE submission_answers (
        submission_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES submissions,
        question_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES quiz_questions,
        answer JSONB NOT NULL,
        PRIMARY KEY (submission_id, question_id)
    );
    
    CREATE TABLE grades (
        grade_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        submission_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES submissions UNIQUE,
        grader_id UUID REFERENCES users,
        score NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,
        finalized_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
        created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
    );
    

#### Design Decisions

* **UUIDs as primary keys.** Cross-region assignments and offline writes are easier with non-sequential keys. We pay some index-locality cost (Chapter 10) but gain portability. An alternative is ULIDs, which are UUIDs with sortable prefixes — better for index locality.

* **Separate`submissions` and `submission_answers`.** One row per submission, many rows per answer. Normalized.

* **Grade immutability.** The `grades` table stores the final score. Historical changes go to an `audit_events` table (not shown) — we never UPDATE grades without also writing an audit row. This is enforced in application code; we could also use a before-update trigger.

* **`deleted_at` on users, not hard delete.** Soft delete is better for foreign-key integrity and for GDPR's 30-day window (the actual hard delete is a scheduled job).

> **FK caveat: soft-delete does not exclude deleted rows from FK checks.** The FK constraint `user_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES users` says "this `user_id` must reference an existing row in `users`." A soft-deleted user still has a row in `users` (only `deleted_at` is set), so the FK is satisfied. This means:
> 
>     1. **Application code, not the database, must filter out soft-deleted users.** Every query that should exclude deleted users must add `WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` (or equivalent). Forgetting this condition is a common bug: an enrollment lookup can silently return a deleted user's data.
>     2. **New writes to child tables are not blocked.** A programming error that creates an enrollment for a soft-deleted user will succeed — the FK passes. Add an application-layer check or a partial unique index to prevent this.
>     3. **Hard delete must happen in FK dependency order.** When the GDPR pipeline eventually hard-deletes the user row, it must first delete or nullify all FK references across `enrollments`, `submissions`, `grades`, and all other child tables. Otherwise Postgres raises a foreign-key violation.
>     4. **Soft-delete alone does not satisfy GDPR right-to-erasure.** A soft-deleted row is still physically in the database, still accessible to anyone with direct database access, and still subject to legal discovery. The `deleted_at` flag hides the user from the application but does not erase their data. Full right-to-erasure compliance requires the hard-delete pipeline (§23.10) to eventually remove or anonymize every row carrying PII — the soft-delete is only a temporary, privacy-preserving placeholder within the 30-day window.

* **CITEXT for email.** Case-insensitive uniqueness without custom collation.

#### Indexes

Beyond the primary-key indexes that Postgres creates automatically, we need several secondary indexes. Chapter 10 taught us that indexes are not free — each one slows writes — so we add them where query patterns justify them.
    
    
    -- Fast lookup of a learner's enrollments
    CREATE INDEX idx_enrollments_user ON enrollments (user_id, course_id);
    
    -- Fast lookup of a course's enrollments (for class roster)
    CREATE INDEX idx_enrollments_course_active ON enrollments (course_id) WHERE status = 'active';
    
    -- Fast lookup of a learner's submissions for a course
    CREATE INDEX idx_submissions_user_quiz ON submissions (user_id, quiz_id, submitted_at DESC);
    
    -- Fast lookup of recent ungraded submissions for an instructor
    CREATE INDEX idx_submissions_ungraded ON submissions (status, submitted_at) WHERE status = 'submitted';
    
    -- Fast lookup of lessons in order for a course
    CREATE INDEX idx_lessons_course_order ON lessons (course_id, order_index);
    
    -- Instructor's courses
    CREATE INDEX idx_courses_owner ON courses (owner_instructor_id);
    

Note the **partial indexes** (Chapter 10) on `idx_enrollments_course_active` and `idx_submissions_ungraded`. They index only a slice of the table and are dramatically smaller than full indexes. The ungraded-submission queue has millions of historical rows but only thousands of "to-grade" rows at any time; a partial index on `WHERE status = 'submitted'` is tiny and hot.

#### Constraints We Are Leaning On

* Foreign keys between every entity pair. Caught bugs will surface loudly instead of accumulating orphans.
* `CHECK` constraints on `role`, `status` so invalid values never land.
* `UNIQUE` constraints on `(user_id, course_id)` for enrollment, and on `submission_id` for grades (one grade per submission).

The relational system's strength is that these invariants live in the schema, not in application code spread across services. That is the first reason to prefer a relational engine for the system of record.

* * *

### 23.4 Step 3: Transactions, Concurrency, and Isolation

Now walk through the hottest write paths and decide on isolation.

#### Path 1: Learner Submits a Quiz
    
    
    BEGIN;
      INSERT INTO submissions (submission_id, user_id, quiz_id, started_at, status)
        VALUES ($1, $2, $3, $4, 'in_progress')
        ON CONFLICT (submission_id) DO NOTHING;
      -- ... learner answers questions, one at a time (UPSERT submission_answers)
      UPDATE submissions SET submitted_at = now(), status = 'submitted'
        WHERE submission_id = $1 AND status = 'in_progress';
    COMMIT;
    

Most of the transaction is small and touches only the learner's own rows. **READ COMMITTED** is fine here — no cross-row invariants to protect. The ON CONFLICT clause handles the "user refreshed during submission" case.

Watch out for the Chapter 14 isolation pitfalls: if the quiz is timed and the check "is the timer still running?" is done in application code between the `INSERT` and the `UPDATE`, a racing admin could extend the timer mid-submission. Solution: include the deadline check in the UPDATE's WHERE clause (`AND now() < (SELECT deadline FROM quizzes WHERE quiz_id = \$3)`), turning it into a read-modify-write inside a single statement that the database guards for us.

#### Path 2: Enrollment Creation (Capacity-Limited Course)

Some courses have enrollment caps. This is Thread A's classic problem from Chapter 1: two learners racing for the last seat.
    
    
    BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
      SELECT current_count INTO cnt FROM course_capacity WHERE course_id = $1 FOR UPDATE;
      IF cnt >= max_cap THEN
        ROLLBACK; RETURN error;
      END IF;
      INSERT INTO enrollments (...) VALUES (...);
      UPDATE course_capacity SET current_count = current_count + 1 WHERE course_id = $1;
    COMMIT;
    

* **SELECT FOR UPDATE** takes a row-level exclusive lock on the `course_capacity` row. Concurrent enrollers block until the first transaction commits (Chapter 15).
* **REPEATABLE READ** in PostgreSQL uses snapshot isolation; the FOR UPDATE turns the read into a lock to prevent the write skew that would otherwise let two transactions each read `cnt = 49` and both insert (Chapter 16).
* Alternatively, a `CHECK (current_count <= max_cap)` constraint plus simple `INSERT ... WHERE` would let the database reject the overflow via a conflict check. Both approaches work; pick one and be consistent.

This is the pattern we saw in Chapter 13's "two users one account" story, but in reverse: multiple writers contending for a scarce resource.

#### Path 3: Grade Finalization

Grade finalization is auditable. Every change must appear in `audit_events`. We use a single transaction to guarantee atomicity:
    
    
    BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;
      UPDATE grades SET score = $1, finalized_at = now()
        WHERE grade_id = $2 AND finalized_at IS NULL;
      INSERT INTO audit_events (...) VALUES (grade_id, grader_id, 'finalize', old_score, new_score, now());
    COMMIT;
    

Serializable here is cheap: the transaction touches two rows and the contention is minimal. If we had higher volume we would drop to READ COMMITTED and rely on the `finalized_at IS NULL` check to prevent double-finalization. For this volume, serializable is safer and costs nothing measurable.

#### Summary

Use READ COMMITTED as default; escalate to REPEATABLE READ or SERIALIZABLE only for the small set of transactions that have cross-row invariants (course capacity, grade finalization). This is exactly the guidance from Chapter 14: isolation levels are a per-transaction choice, not a system-wide one. Pay for strength only where the invariant demands it.

* * *

### 23.5 Step 4: Growing the Single Database

A single PostgreSQL node can go much further than most teams think — especially with modern hardware (64-core, 512 GB RAM, NVMe). Before introducing sharding or distributed SQL, exhaust the single-node options:

#### 1\. Read Replicas

Most of our read volume — dashboard views, lesson-reading — tolerates a few seconds of staleness. Route these reads to replicas.
    
    
                 [Primary]  ← writes
                    |
             streaming replication
               /        \
        [Replica-1]  [Replica-2]  ← dashboard reads, search, CDC source
    

A primary + 2 replicas in the same region doubles read capacity and provides failover. Replication is synchronous for low-latency replicas and asynchronous for distant ones, per the tradeoff of Chapter 18.

Two caveats:

* **Read-your-own-writes**. A learner submitting a quiz and then loading the results page must not hit a lagging replica that shows "no submissions yet." Solution: reads _from the learner's own session_ go to the primary for a short window (say, 10 seconds) after any write. The application tracks a per-session "read primary until" timestamp.
* **Replica lag monitoring**. If a replica falls behind by more than a few seconds, the application pulls it out of the load balancer.

#### 2\. Connection Pooling

50k concurrent users do not mean 50k open database connections. PgBouncer (or a similar pooler) multiplexes many application sessions onto a modest pool of backend connections. Without this, you will be OOM-killing Postgres long before you hit any interesting scale issue.

PgBouncer offers three pooling modes with very different tradeoffs:

Mode | Backend returned to pool | Safe for… | Breaks…  
---|---|---|---  
**Session** | When the client disconnects | Everything — full session state is preserved | Nothing; but one backend per connected client defeats the purpose of pooling under high concurrency  
**Transaction** | After each `COMMIT` or `ROLLBACK` | Most OLTP workloads | Session-level features: advisory locks (`pg_advisory_lock`), `SET` variables, named prepared statements, `LISTEN/NOTIFY`  
**Statement** | After each individual statement | Almost nothing — breaks any multi-statement transaction | All transactions with more than one statement; effectively unusable for normal applications  
  
**Most teams use transaction mode.** It allows a pool of 50–200 backend connections to serve thousands of application threads. The cost is that session-level state does not survive across transactions: advisory locks must be replaced with `FOR UPDATE` row locks, `SET LOCAL` must replace `SET`, and server-side prepared statements must be disabled (`server_side_binding = no` in PgBouncer config) or handled carefully.

Session mode is appropriate when your application relies heavily on session-level features and connection count is not a bottleneck. Statement mode is almost never appropriate for PostgreSQL applications.

#### 3\. Partitioning

PostgreSQL supports **declarative partitioning**. The `submissions` and `progress_events`-adjacent tables can be range-partitioned by date:
    
    
    CREATE TABLE submissions (
        submission_id UUID PRIMARY KEY,
        user_id UUID NOT NULL,
        quiz_id UUID NOT NULL,
        started_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
        submitted_at TIMESTAMPTZ,
        status TEXT NOT NULL
    ) PARTITION BY RANGE (started_at);
    
    CREATE TABLE submissions_2025_q4 PARTITION OF submissions
        FOR VALUES FROM ('2025-10-01') TO ('2026-01-01');
    

Benefits: older partitions can be vacuumed and archived separately, query planner can prune partitions, dropping an old partition is a metadata operation rather than a DELETE of millions of rows. This alone extends single-node scale substantially.

#### 4\. Sharding (Only If Necessary)

At year 3 scale we probably do not need horizontal sharding for the core relational entities — 10M users at ~100 KB each is ~1 TB, comfortable on one node. But if enrollment and submission growth demands it, shard by **institution_id** : each institution's data lives in one shard, and cross-institution queries (only administrators run these) go through a fanning query layer. Citus is the low-friction path in the PostgreSQL ecosystem.

* * *

### 23.6 Step 5: The High-Volume Write Path — Telemetry

Now for the part that the relational store cannot absorb: progress events.

* 500M progress events / month ≈ 200 writes per second sustained, peaking higher.
* 1B quiz attempts / year ≈ 32 writes per second, peaking at 10× during exams.
* AI tutor conversation turns: 100M / month ≈ 40 writes per second sustained.

Raw counts look modest, but each of these workloads has bursty peaks 10-30× the average, and they compete for the same primary's IOPS budget. More importantly, the queries against progress events are different from OLTP:

* "Show a learner's last 30 days of activity" — partition-key-friendly
* "Compute active-user counts by course" — analytical
* "Stream recent events to the AI tutor" — real-time, learner-partitioned

These are textbook fits for different engines, not one.

#### Subsystem 1: Progress Events — Cassandra

Progress events are:

* Append-only (never updated).
* Partitioned naturally by user (or by user+course).
* Queried as "recent events for this learner" or aggregated in analytics.

Fit: **Cassandra** (or ScyllaDB), per Chapter 21. Schema:
    
    
    CREATE KEYSPACE learnquest
      WITH replication = {
        'class': 'NetworkTopologyStrategy',
        'us_east': 3,    -- RF=3 per region: survive any 1 node failure while holding LOCAL_QUORUM
        'eu_west': 3     -- RF=3 in eu_west satisfies GDPR data residency (EU rows stay in EU)
      };
    
    CREATE TABLE learnquest.progress_events (
        user_id UUID,
        event_time TIMESTAMP,
        event_type TEXT,
        course_id UUID,
        lesson_id UUID,
        payload TEXT,
        PRIMARY KEY (user_id, event_time)
    ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (event_time DESC)
      AND default_time_to_live = 15552000;  -- 180 days
    

**Why RF=3 per region, not RF=1?** Our 99.9% availability target (≤ 8.76 hours/year downtime) requires surviving single-node failure without read unavailability. With RF=3 and consistency level `LOCAL_QUORUM` (reads/writes need 2 of 3 replicas), we tolerate one node loss per region without availability loss. RF=1 would mean every node failure causes a partial read outage — unacceptable. RF=3 is the Cassandra-standard minimum for production; RF=5 is used when write amplification cost is acceptable and stronger fault tolerance is needed.

**Cross-region replication** (`NetworkTopologyStrategy`) also addresses GDPR data residency: EU user events replicate only within `eu_west`, enforced at the application layer by restricting reads/writes to the local DC for EU-origin users.

TTL removes old events automatically; we do not need to run DELETE. For longer retention, a Kafka Connect sink exports events to Parquet in S3.

#### Subsystem 2: AI Tutor Conversations — Cassandra or PostgreSQL

Each learner may have many conversations. Reads are "last N messages for this user" — a clustering-key range scan.

If volume stays at 100M turns/month, PostgreSQL handles it fine with a partitioned table. If it grows to 1B/month, move to Cassandra. Start in PostgreSQL (less operational weight) and migrate only if we hit a real limit. Chapter 22's advice: don't add engines speculatively.

#### Subsystem 3: Cache — Redis

Redis serves:

* User session data (30-day TTL).
* "Hot" course/lesson metadata (1-hour TTL, invalidated on write via CDC).
* Rate limiters (per-user, per-IP).
* Pre-computed leaderboards for gamified courses (ZSET per course).

Redis cluster in each region. Data is derived — if Redis loses everything, we rebuild from PostgreSQL with a warm-up script. Redis is a cache, not a system of record.

* * *

### 23.7 Step 6: Search — When and What

Learners search the catalog ("find me a course on machine learning"). Instructors search submissions. Administrators search audit logs.

**Start with PostgreSQL full-text search.** For a 10k-course catalog, it is more than enough:
    
    
    ALTER TABLE courses ADD COLUMN search_vector tsvector
        GENERATED ALWAYS AS (to_tsvector('english', title || ' ' || coalesce(description, ''))) STORED;
    
    CREATE INDEX idx_courses_search ON courses USING gin (search_vector);
    

If the catalog grows to millions of courses, or search requirements become complex (typo tolerance, faceted filters, relevance scoring), migrate to **Elasticsearch**. Keep CDC from PostgreSQL to ES so the application never dual-writes.

* * *

### 23.8 Step 7: Analytics — The Warehouse

Administrators want real-time dashboards of course engagement, instructor activity, learner retention, and system health. Chapter 19's columnar engines are the right fit.

Two tiers:

  1. **Real-time analytics** : ClickHouse. Fed by CDC from PostgreSQL (for OLTP data) and by Kafka Connect from Cassandra (for telemetry). Serves dashboards with <5 second freshness.
  2. **Deep analytics and long-term storage** : Snowflake. Nightly batch from ClickHouse + S3 Parquet archives. Used by the data team for ad-hoc analysis, ML feature engineering, and regulatory reports.

Why both? ClickHouse is fast and cheap at its volume but expensive at petabyte scale; Snowflake separates compute and storage, so decades of cold data are cheap. The two-tier pattern is common.

#### Thread B Revisited

Back in Chapters 7, 10, 11, 12, and 19 we walked a single query — "top 10 products by revenue this week" — from 20 minutes on a row store to 100 ms on a columnar engine. The LearnQuest analogue is "top 10 courses by new enrollments this week" or "learner cohort retention over 90 days." These run on ClickHouse, answered in 1-2 seconds against billions of events.

The OLTP store is not asked these questions. Ever. If an administrator opens a dashboard and we query PostgreSQL, we are doing something wrong. All dashboards go through the analytical tier.

* * *

### 23.9 Step 8: Recovery, Replication, and Disaster

Chapter 17's WAL and ARIES gave us durability on a single node. Chapter 18 extended it to distributed systems. For LearnQuest:

#### Backup Strategy

* **Continuous archive** of PostgreSQL WAL to S3 (tools like pgBackRest or WAL-G). Point-in-time recovery to any second of the last 30 days.
* **Nightly base backup** of PostgreSQL for fast restores.
* **Cassandra snapshots** nightly, incremental diffs hourly. Cross-region replica provides real-time protection; snapshots protect against logical corruption ("someone ran the wrong DELETE").
* **ClickHouse / Snowflake** are derived, so they need not be backed up; they can be rebuilt from Kafka + S3.
* **S3 for submissions** : cross-region replication + versioning. Object storage is the cheapest and most durable tier in the system.

#### Failure Scenarios

* **Primary PostgreSQL fails.** Synchronous replica promoted automatically. RTO <1 minute; RPO zero (synchronous replication).
* **Region fails (AWS us-east-1 goes down).** Route traffic to EU or APAC region. EU learners see no disruption (per data residency, their data was there all along). US learners see degraded service until region recovers.
* **Logical data corruption.** Point-in-time restore a copy of PostgreSQL, inspect, merge back surgically. Hours of work; worth it for correctness.
* **CDC pipeline failure.** Downstream stores fall behind but self-heal when pipeline resumes (Kafka retains events for 7 days). Derived views show stale data in the meantime; application shows a banner.
* **Cassandra partition loss.** Other replicas serve reads; failed replica rebuilds from peers ("nodetool repair"). Availability maintained throughout.

* * *

### 23.10 Step 9: Security, Privacy, and Compliance

Not a database course concern, exactly, but every real database design must accommodate:

* **Encryption at rest** (disk-level, built into cloud managed databases).

* **Encryption in transit** (TLS everywhere, no exceptions).

* **Row-level security** in PostgreSQL for multi-tenant isolation (one institution cannot query another's data even if application code has a bug):
        
        ALTER TABLE enrollments ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
        CREATE POLICY enrollments_tenant_isolation ON enrollments
            USING (institution_id = current_setting('app.current_institution_id')::UUID);
        

* **GDPR user deletion — the multi-engine problem.** When a user requests deletion, data must be removed from _every_ engine that holds it. Naive sequential deletion is fragile: a failure at step 4 leaves PostgreSQL deleted but Cassandra still retaining the user's events. We use an **idempotent, checkpointed pipeline with compensating semantics** to make the process resumable and auditable.

**Design principles:**

    1. **Idempotent steps.** Each step can be re-run safely. Deleting an already-deleted row is a no-op, not an error. Cassandra tombstones are already idempotent (re-writing a tombstone is cheap and harmless).
    2. **Checkpoint per engine.** A `gdpr_deletions(user_id, engine, status, attempt_count, last_error, completed_at, sla_deadline)` table in PostgreSQL records progress. Restarting the job reads this table and skips completed engines.
    3. **No compensating rollback on partial failure.** You cannot "un-delete" data you've already wiped — deletion is intrinsically one-way. Instead, failures **block forward progress** and alert a human; partial-deletion state is an operational problem but is GDPR-permissible as long as deletion completes within the 30-day window.
    4. **Audit trail outlives the data.** The audit log retains `{user_id_hash, deletion_requested_at, each_engine_completed_at, final_status}` — enough to prove compliance, nothing about the user.
    5. **SLA-aware retries.** The 30-day GDPR window is a legal deadline, not a suggestion. Each engine's retry policy sets an internal deadline well inside the window (e.g., 25 days) so there is a 5-day human-intervention margin. Retries use exponential backoff; when `attempt_count >= 5` _or_ `now() > sla_deadline`, the job pages on-call and pauses that engine's step until human resolution. Under no circumstances does the pipeline retry silently past the legal deadline.

**The pipeline** :
    
    Step 0: Insert gdpr_deletions rows (one per engine: postgres_pii, postgres_analytics_anon,
                                         cassandra_events, redis_cache, warehouse, s3_blobs).
    Step 1: PostgreSQL — soft-delete (deleted_at = now()) immediately. Safe, reversible
            within the 30-day window if the user changes their mind.
    Step 2-N: Daily scheduled job iterates over gdpr_deletions WHERE status='pending':
              a. For each engine, call the engine-specific idempotent delete routine.
              b. On success: status='completed', completed_at=now().
              c. On failure: increment attempt_count, log last_error.
                 If attempt_count >= 5, status='failing', page on-call.
              d. After all engines reach 'completed': write audit record,
                 hard-delete PII from PostgreSQL (the last step — after this,
                 the user_id is no longer usable for lookup).
    

**Why this ordering?** PostgreSQL PII is the _last_ thing deleted, not the first. Until every downstream engine confirms deletion, we need the `user_id` to locate the user's records in those engines. Hard-deleting PostgreSQL first would leave orphaned data in Cassandra we can no longer locate. The soft-delete in Step 1 protects privacy immediately (the user is effectively deleted from the application's point of view), while hard PII deletion at the end ensures all downstream wipes succeed.

> **Cascade scope: the user row is not the only PII.** GDPR's "right to erasure" applies to _all_ personal data, not just the `users` row. The pipeline above lists engines (Cassandra, Redis, warehouse) but must also enumerate derived data _within_ PostgreSQL itself that carries user PII:
> 
>     * `submissions(user_id, ...)` — which quiz a user took and when. The row must be deleted or anonymized.
>     * `grades(grader_id, ...)` — who graded a submission. If the deleted user was a grader, `grader_id` references them.
>     * `enrollments(user_id, ...)` — which courses the user was enrolled in. The enrollment rows carry the user's learning history.
>     * `submission_answers(...)` — the user's actual answers (free text potentially containing PII).
>     * Any application-level tables not shown here (forum posts, profile comments, support tickets, A/B test assignments).
> 
> The FK constraints (`REFERENCES users`) ensure referential integrity while the user row exists — but they do **not** automatically cascade deletion. When the user row is hard-deleted in the final step, Postgres will raise a foreign-key violation unless all child rows have been deleted first (or the FK is declared `ON DELETE CASCADE`/`ON DELETE SET NULL`). The pipeline must enumerate every FK child table, decide whether to hard-delete or anonymize each row, and execute in dependency order: delete `submission_answers` before `submissions`, delete `grades` before wiping `graders`, etc.
> 
> **Anonymization vs. deletion.** Some derived rows may need to be _anonymized_ rather than deleted. An entry in `grades` that records a grade given _by_ the deleted user (as grader) is not itself PII for the deleted user — it belongs to the student being graded. Setting `grader_id = NULL` (where the FK allows it) anonymizes the row without losing the grade. The appropriate choice must be made case-by-case based on whether the row's PII is _about_ the requesting user or merely _related_ to them.

**Failure modes covered** : Cassandra replica offline (the tombstone replicates later via hinted handoff); Redis cluster failure (retry); warehouse unreachable (retry; audit record deferred until it succeeds). Failure _not_ covered: an engine permanently lost without backup — this is a bigger operational incident than GDPR alone and must be escalated.

* **FERPA** : grades and enrollment records are educational records under US law; access must be authenticated, audited, and role-scoped. Row-level security plus an application audit layer handle this.

* **Data residency** : EU users' OLTP data lives in an EU-region PostgreSQL that is _not_ replicated to US regions. Global administrators querying cross-region use a cross-region query layer that respects residency constraints.

* * *

### 23.11 The Full Architecture Diagram

Putting it all together:
    
    
                           [ App servers (N per region) ]
                                    |
                                    v
                 +-------------------------------------+
                 |       [ PgBouncer connection pool ] |
                 +-------------------------------------+
                                    |
              +---------------------+---------------------+
              v                                           v
    [ PostgreSQL primary ]                          [ Read replicas ]
        (OLTP core)                                 (dashboard reads)
              |                                           |
              |  WAL                                      |
              v                                           |
        [ Debezium CDC ]                                  |
              |                                           |
              v                                           |
           [ Kafka ]-----+------+------+--------+         |
              |          |      |      |        |         |
              v          v      v      v        v         |
      [ ClickHouse ] [ ES ] [ Snow- ] [ Redis ] [ Cassandra maintenance? ]
         (analytics) (srch) ( flake)  (invalidation)       (also feeds
                                                            Kafka via 
                                                            CDC bridge)
    
      Telemetry write path:
      [ App ] --> [ Cassandra cluster ] (per region) --> [ Kafka ] --> [ ClickHouse + S3 Parquet ]
    
      Submissions storage:
      [ App ] --> [ S3 ] (metadata in PostgreSQL)
    
      Redis cluster in each region (session, cache, rate limiter)
    
      AI tutor: reads from Cassandra/PostgreSQL per turn, writes conversation turns to a partitioned PG table.
    

Let us count engines: **PostgreSQL** (primary OLTP), **Cassandra** (telemetry + tutor at scale), **Redis** (cache + session), **Kafka** (CDC bus + event backbone), **ClickHouse** (real-time analytics), **Snowflake** (long-term warehouse), **Elasticsearch** (search, optional), **S3** (blob storage). Eight engines if you count them all.

Is that too many? At 10M users with 50k concurrent and the workload shapes described, no — each engine is solving a distinct problem. At 10k users we would have none of this: one PostgreSQL and Redis. The architecture must match the scale.

* * *

### 23.12 Tradeoffs Index: What Every Choice Cost

A habit worth building: when you finish a design, walk through the tradeoffs accepted. What did you give up for each choice?

* **UUIDs over sequential IDs** → lost a bit of B+ tree locality; gained cross-region portability.
* **Soft delete over hard delete** → more disk usage, more query complexity (`WHERE deleted_at IS NULL` everywhere); gained FK integrity and a 30-day privacy window. Note: soft delete alone is not GDPR-compliant — the hard-delete pipeline (§23.10) is what closes the loop.
* **CDC over dual writes** → added Kafka as critical infrastructure; gained single source of truth and replayability.
* **Read replicas with session-pinning** → complexified the read path; gained 3× read capacity.
* **Cassandra for telemetry** → introduced LSM operational complexity, eventually consistent; gained ~100× write throughput without hurting OLTP.
* **ClickHouse for real-time analytics** → another engine; gained <5s dashboard freshness.
* **Snowflake for deep analytics** → cost; gained ad-hoc flexibility and petabyte capacity.
* **Row-level security** → slight query overhead; gained defense-in-depth for multi-tenancy.
* **Synchronous replication** → slightly higher write latency; gained RPO zero.

No line item is "free." If we ever say "this is all upside, no cost," we have not thought hard enough.

#### Misconception

> ### Common Misconception
> 
> **"A good architecture is one where nothing ever goes wrong."** No. A good architecture is one where the things that do go wrong fail gracefully, are detected quickly, and can be recovered from. Every system accumulates incidents; the design's quality is measured by what they look like when they happen. Plan for partial failure.

* * *

### 23.13 What We Did Not Cover (and Why)

To keep this chapter focused, several real concerns got pushed aside. If you were doing this exercise for real, you would need to address them too:

* **Schema migrations.** ALTER TABLE in production on a billion-row table is a significant operational risk — one every database engineer encounters.

**Why ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN is dangerous.** In PostgreSQL versions before 11, `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN col TYPE DEFAULT expr` triggered a full table rewrite: every row was rewritten to include the new column's default value. On a 1-billion-row table this could take hours, holding an `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE` lock the entire time — blocking all reads and writes.

**PostgreSQL 11+ instant ADD COLUMN.** Since PostgreSQL 11, `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN col TYPE DEFAULT constant` where the default is an immutable constant (a literal integer, text, or timestamp value — not a function call) is instantaneous: the default value is stored in the catalog, not written to each row. Existing rows see the default value without a physical rewrite. This is the most important migration improvement in recent PostgreSQL history. Any default that involves a function call — `now()` (which is `STABLE`, not `IMMUTABLE`), `gen_random_uuid()`, `random()` — does not qualify for the fast path and still triggers a full table rewrite.

**The copy-swap pattern (for rewrites that cannot be avoided).** Create a new table with the desired schema; backfill data in batches while the original remains live; use a trigger to replicate writes to the new table during backfill; once caught up, acquire a brief lock, replay the delta, rename the tables. This achieves near-zero downtime at the cost of significant complexity and temporary storage.

**Tooling.**

    * **`pg_repack`** : a PostgreSQL extension that automates the copy-swap pattern for table rewrites and index rebuilds. Works for most non-DDL rewrites. Requires superuser or `repack` role.
    * **`pt-online-schema-change` (pt-osc)**: Percona's trigger-based tool designed for MySQL. Not natively compatible with PostgreSQL — listed here because it frequently appears in migration discussions, but PostgreSQL teams should use `pg_repack` instead.
    * **`gh-ost`** : GitHub's binlog-based tool for MySQL — not applicable to PostgreSQL. Listed here because it appears frequently in migration literature; PostgreSQL teams should use `pg_repack`.

**Safe migration checklist:** (1) test on a production-size snapshot and measure lock duration; (2) use `lock_timeout` and retry rather than holding indefinitely; (3) apply `statement_timeout` to the ALTER; (4) schedule during low-traffic windows even for "instant" operations; (5) always have a rollback plan.

* **Testing.** Unit tests for application logic; integration tests against a real PostgreSQL; load tests; chaos testing.

* **Observability.** Metrics (Prometheus), logs (Loki / Elastic), traces (OpenTelemetry). Dashboards for every engine.

* **Cost management.** Cloud databases get expensive; continuous cost monitoring matters more than you think.

* **Onboarding and documentation.** New engineers need to understand the data model; a diagram in a README is not enough.

* **Capacity planning.** Grow storage and compute ahead of demand; the worst outages happen when you hit a limit you did not see coming.

* **API design on top of all this.** The database schema is not the API; an application layer shapes queries and enforces authorization.

Each of these could fill a book. The database design is the foundation; everything else is built on top.

* * *

### 23.14 Reviewing Your Own Design: A Checklist

When you have produced a data architecture, run it through this checklist:

  1. **Schema integrity.** Are all business invariants enforced by constraints, not just application code? Foreign keys? CHECK constraints? UNIQUE constraints? If a bug corrupts the application, does the database stop it?
  2. **Isolation correctness.** For each transaction, does the chosen isolation level actually prevent the anomalies that matter? Have you considered write skew specifically?
  3. **Index usage.** Does every frequent query have a supporting index? Does every rarely-used table lack its unused indexes? Run `EXPLAIN` on every important query.
  4. **Growth plan.** If load doubles, what breaks first? If it grows 10×? 100×? Have you specified what you would do at each inflection?
  5. **Backup and recovery.** Describe, step by step, what happens when the primary database dies at 3 AM. RTO? RPO? Who is paged?
  6. **CDC and derived views.** How do downstream views recover when CDC is down for hours? Can you rebuild any derived store from scratch?
  7. **Consistency across engines.** For each pair of engines, what is the consistency guarantee between them? Is it enough for the user experience you are promising?
  8. **Security and compliance.** Row-level security? Audit logging? User deletion procedure? Encryption? Data residency?
  9. **Cost.** For each engine, what does it cost at current scale, at 10× scale, at 100× scale?
  10. **Operational load.** Do you have staff (or managed services) for every engine? What is the on-call rotation?

A design that passes this checklist is not guaranteed to succeed. But a design that fails it is guaranteed to cause pain, and the pain will be worse the later you notice.

* * *

### 23.15 Exercises

#### Concept Check

  1. List the three workload clusters this chapter identified in LearnQuest and name the engine(s) chosen for each.

  2. Why is a partial index (e.g., `WHERE status = 'submitted'`) often dramatically smaller than a full index? Under what access patterns does this matter?

  3. Explain read-your-own-writes in the context of read replicas. Why is it a concern, and how does session-pinning solve it?

  4. What is a CDC pipeline, and which database property makes CDC possible in PostgreSQL?

  5. Why does this architecture use both ClickHouse and Snowflake for analytics, instead of one or the other?

  6. Give two specific reasons why Cassandra is chosen for progress events and not PostgreSQL.

#### Application Problems

  7. Extend the schema to support **peer review** : a learner reviews another learner's submission and gives feedback. What tables, columns, and indexes would you add? What constraints would you enforce?

  8. A new feature requires that a learner can "fork" a course — create a personal copy and modify lessons. Propose a schema change. Think about storage cost (do we copy rows or use a parent-child relationship?) and about how queries for "all lessons a learner has access to" would be expressed.

  9. Consider the quiz submission transaction (Section 23.4, Path 1). Rewrite the transaction so that a learner cannot submit two concurrent attempts at the same quiz. Use a database-level mechanism, not application-level.

Hint

Database-level means the constraint survives any application bug. Which of these tools would enforce "at most one in-progress attempt per `(user_id, quiz_id)`": a UNIQUE constraint over a partial condition, an advisory lock, `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, or a serializable transaction? Which is the simplest that still works under concurrent inserts?

  10. An instructor wants to grade submissions in bulk: "mark all ungraded submissions for quiz X as correct." Write the SQL. What isolation level would you use? What happens if a learner submits a new attempt during the bulk operation?

Hint

Start with the UPDATE that sets the score for `WHERE quiz_id = X AND status = 'submitted'`. Then ask whether a new submission landing mid-statement should be graded as part of this batch or excluded. The answer determines your isolation level — which level lets the bulk write see only the snapshot from its start, and which would risk a phantom?

  11. Design the GDPR deletion pipeline in more detail. List every engine that holds data for a user, what gets deleted from each, and how you coordinate the deletion across them (what happens if one step fails?).

Hint

Walk through each engine in the architecture (OLTP, telemetry, cache, warehouse, search, audit, blob storage) and write down what "delete user X" means there — full row, tombstone, anonymize, or retain-with-audit-reference. What ordering ensures you can still locate data after early steps succeed? What record-keeping makes the whole pipeline idempotent and resumable if step 4 of 7 crashes?

  12. The cache invalidation strategy in this chapter uses CDC: when PostgreSQL updates a row, a Kafka event triggers Redis to invalidate the cache. Describe two failure modes of this approach and how you would detect them.

  13. The analytics tier uses ClickHouse fed by CDC. An administrator notices a dashboard number looks wrong (revenue from yesterday shows 10k USD lower than the OLTP source). Walk through your debugging approach. Where could the discrepancy come from?

Hint

Trace the data flow in reverse: dashboard query → ClickHouse table → Kafka consumer → Kafka topic → Debezium → PostgreSQL WAL. At each stage, what could drop, double, or reorder rows? Think about lag (is yesterday's revenue still being written?), filter bugs (timezone, status flags), and schema drift (a new `refund` column the consumer ignored).

  14. The architecture uses synchronous replication for the local PostgreSQL replica and asynchronous for geographically distant replicas. Explain why this hybrid is appropriate. What happens if we made all replication synchronous? All asynchronous?

#### Think Deeper

  15. The chapter argues for starting with PostgreSQL and adding engines only when needed. Imagine you are hired to review LearnQuest's year-1 architecture (one PostgreSQL, one Redis), and the team asks "should we add Cassandra now, since we are going to need it eventually?" What would you tell them? When is the right time to introduce a new engine?

  16. The AI tutor makes ~10 DB lookups per conversation turn at 40 turns/second average — 400 lookups/second sustained. Each lookup fetches a small slice of the learner's recent history. Where should this data live? Critique three options: (a) read from PostgreSQL directly, (b) read from a dedicated Cassandra table, (c) read from a per-learner Redis cache warmed from CDC.

Hint

Evaluate each option on three dimensions separately before combining:

**p99 latency** (the tail, not the average): PostgreSQL under sustained 400 RPS on primary adds index load and can spike at p99 when autovacuum or checkpoint fires. Cassandra at `R=ONE` hits the nearest replica with ~1–2 ms local latency but p99 can spike during compaction. Redis is typically the best here: <1 ms p99, since the data is fully in-memory.

**Throughput ceiling** : PostgreSQL's connection pool (e.g., PgBouncer) is the constraint — 400 RPS of small reads is well within a well-tuned primary, but adds ~5–10% to the primary's total load. Cassandra's per-partition design scales horizontally; 400 RPS spread across 10M learners is trivially distributed. Redis's single-threaded event loop handles >100K ops/sec on a single node; 400 RPS is not a concern.

**Consistency window** : (a) always-current (reads from primary); (b) eventually consistent by at most the CDC lag (seconds to minutes); (c) stale by whatever CDC lag + cache TTL. For a tutoring session where "the learner's recent history" must include the last few exchanges, even seconds of staleness could cause the AI to re-ask questions the learner just answered. For history beyond the last ~30s, staleness is acceptable.

The question's real challenge: what does "blast radius" mean for each option — if the engine fails, what breaks, and how many users are affected simultaneously?

  17. Revisit Chapter 18's CAP / PACELC framework. For each engine in this architecture, place it on the CAP triangle or the PACELC matrix. Does the overall system have strong consistency? Where does it?

  18. The design uses "eventually consistent" derived views (search, analytics). A product manager asks for "real-time" dashboards — meaning <1 second freshness. What changes in the architecture? What new risks appear? (Hint: think about how fast CDC can propagate, and what happens under load.)

  19. The design uses row-level security for multi-tenancy. An alternative is per-tenant databases (each institution has its own PostgreSQL instance). What are the tradeoffs? At what scale does each approach break down?

  20. Revise the architecture to support **offline-first learning** : learners can study on a phone in airplane mode for days, then sync when they reconnect. What engines change? What conflict-resolution strategy would you use? Would you consider CRDTs (Chapter 18)? Why or why not?

Hint

List the operations a phone does offline: progress events, quiz answers, notes, bookmarks. Which are append-only (merge-friendly) and which update shared state (conflict-prone)? For each, ask whether a CRDT semantics fits the data shape — and where last-writer-wins, per-user partitioning, or server-authoritative reconciliation would be simpler than a CRDT.

* * *

### Chapter Summary

This chapter brought every idea in the book to bear on a single system. The sequence mirrors the sequence of real design work: identify entities and access patterns; design the core relational schema with appropriate indexes and constraints; choose isolation levels transaction by transaction; scale the single database as far as it will go with replicas, partitioning, and pooling; introduce specialized engines (Cassandra, Redis, ClickHouse, Snowflake, Elasticsearch, S3) only where the workload truly demands them; glue them together with CDC so the OLTP store stays the single source of truth; plan for failure, privacy, and compliance before they become crises.

The discipline every chapter in this book argued for — **name the tradeoff** — is the only way to survive a design like this. Every choice has a cost. You are paying write latency for synchronous replication, operational complexity for Cassandra, consistency lag for CDC-fed views, storage for soft deletes. Good architecture is not an absence of costs; it is a set of costs chosen deliberately because the benefits outweigh them, and documented so that the next engineer (possibly you, three years from now) can reopen the decision as conditions change.

There is no universal "right" architecture for LearnQuest, and there is none for your system either. The goal of this book — and the goal of this capstone — is to give you the vocabulary, the frameworks, and the intuitions that let you reason through the design rather than guess at it. Database systems are a study in tradeoffs. Every ACID promise has a cost; every NoSQL flexibility has a cost; every index speeds reads and slows writes; every cache is a new source of drift. The engines change every five years; the tradeoffs do not. Learn the tradeoffs, and the engines become tools, not mysteries.

**Thread A's registration system is behind us. Thread B's slow query is behind us. Thread C's two-users-one-account mystery is behind us.** In their place you have something more valuable than any of them: the ability to draw the right system on a whiteboard, critique your own design, and know which tradeoffs you have accepted and why.

That is what a database engineer does.
